Dina — Implementation Notes

The ACTIVE section tracks the current stream. Everything under Archive shipped earlier; its blow-by-blow lives in git history.

ACTIVE — Commerce & Procurement Pack PHASE 0 + CORE DONE

Spec: docs/COMMERCE_PROCUREMENT_PLUGIN_ARCHITECTURE.md (version f53d5e84e6055c0c). Delivery is phase-by-phase per the spec's §24. This pass = Phase 0 (protocol spine) + the §23 required Core work. NOT in this pass: Buyer/Supplier runner plugins (Phase 1 pack), catalog AppView (Phase 2), managed runtime (Phase 3), payment (prohibited in v1).

What shipped

  • @dina/commerce-protocol (new zero-dep package) — canonical Money/Quantity + closed unit vocabulary v1; §9.1 arithmetic (exact BigInt rationals, ONE half-even rounding, integer totals, bounds fail not wrap); ten domain-separated digests (dina:commerce:v1:<domain> preimage); full negotiation + lifecycle wire types with validators (quote CAS chains, replacesQuoteDigest lineage, immutable maxUses, all-or-none orders, projection-extends, kind-narrowed acks, status graph + genesis + cumulative snapshots + restore fences, cancellation, six-outcome reconcile, epoch records); frozen conformance vectors incl. malformed battery, magnitude-bound accept/reject pairs, and an end-to-end-linked status chain.
  • @dina/core commerce layer — migration v31: commerce_order_refs (dual unique keys + pre_effect/effect_started phases), commerce_quote_heads/uses (signing CAS + provisional holds), commerce_status_heads, commerce_receipts, commerce_epoch_watermarks, plugin_drain_authorizations. Engines: admission (§9.9 precedence, durable frozen rejections, per-outcome hold settlement, phase-scoped crash recovery), lifecycle (status signing, atomic cancellation race, reconcile with fail-closed held-evidence verification), epoch service (fail-closed until established, CAS-serialized restores, capacity voiding). Receipts + operational tables in .dina export per §16.2.
  • Plugin substrate — host_operations manifest field (scope-hash re-consent on widening; existing hashes untouched); extension-operation registry with deny-before-validation; provider-ingress bridge (D2D query → plugin lane → provider-kind claim → validated completion → response bridge); drain authorizations validating prior-CID tasks against their AUTHORIZED pinned values, drain-kind limited to pre-rebind tasks.

WHERE THIS STANDS — read this first

A consolidated view. Everything below this section is a chronological log of individual findings and fixes; this is the picture they add up to.

What works end to end today

The retailer↔manufacturer arc runs, driven in tests through the real composition path — real SQLite, the real commerce runtime, a real plugin runner, and the SAME ingressResultTransformer both boots wire, so what a buyer receives in a test is what a buyer receives in production. 6 journey suites / 53 tests:

  • Discovery → fan-out → quotes → ranking → order → acceptance → delivery, in one sequence.
  • Capacity exhaustion; a competitor refused against Sancho's quote; a replayed submission answered from Core's record.
  • A mid-order plugin update, with the lane closing afterwards.
  • Restart agreement; ChairMaker losing an order and recovering it from Sancho; restore-fencing before the chain moves again.
  • ERP fulfilment with a late webhook refused; a failing credential surfaced despite a settings row calling it fine.
  • Cancellation adjudicated by Core, with a competitor refused at the subject gate.

WBS reality — the counts are not the story

docs/COMMERCE_WBS.md reads 77 DONE / 11 PART / 12 TODO / 1 BLOCKED across 67 commerce modules and 62 commerce test files. Treat the DONE column with suspicion. The single most valuable thing this review round established is that several DONE rows describe engines that were correct, well tested, and called by nothing: the status chain (7.x), the cancellation engine, update continuity, and the §15.2b supplier approval payload. Three of those are now wired. That is the dominant defect class in this codebase and the WBS cannot see it, because a row goes green when the engine is built.

Genuinely incomplete, in priority order

GapConsequence today
§15.2b supplier approval lane — no approval card, no owner decision route orderAcceptance:'review' is REFUSED at configuration time rather than silently rejecting every order. §18.6 counterproposal controls are absent from the inbox for the same reason. Also blocks finalizePendingCancellation, so a pending_review cancellation cannot be closed.
Status chain, buyer side — genesis only on acceptance; no buyer-side ingestion or chain verification A rejected or countered order gets no terminal genesis, and a buyer cannot verify sequence, predecessors or forks. The supplier side now signs on fulfilment.
Major-update DISPATCH (C7-08's second half) Authorization is now correct — continuity only across a major, only for the three lifecycle handlers. But DrainAuthorization stores no protocol version or handler identity, and buildContinuityEnvelope still targets the current install lane, so a prior major's continuations execute against current code.
Infrastructure-blocked rows — hosting control plane, live catalog AppView, a real ERP, Postgres Cannot be closed on this machine at all. Recorded as blocked rather than pending.

Open questions — these need you

  1. Should I build the §15.2b approval lane, or is refusing the setting the right shipping state? Building it is a card, an owner route, reservation holding, and a mobile surface. I refused the setting because a half-built approval path is worse than an absent one, but that is a product call about whether suppliers need human order review at launch.
  2. Is the buyer expected to verify the status chain in v1? The supplier signs it correctly now. Buyer-side verification is a meaningful build, and if v1 buyers simply trust the supplier's signed records it can wait.
  3. Do you want major-version plugin updates supported at launch? If a major bump is out of scope for v1, the dispatch half of C7-08 does not need building and the authorization split already prevents the dangerous case.
  4. The dual review has not converged and I cannot finish it alone. Claude's continuity reviewer works. Codex is reachable only by starting a fresh thread each time — its original continuity thread hung and needs /codex:status and probably /codex:cancel from you. Cold audit (the skill's terminal state) requires both.

Standing rule this effort has earned

If a fixture needs a digest, an id, or a manifest, get it from the thing that makes them. Six separate false-green tests this round came from hand-built stand-ins that contradicted the protocol — acknowledgements with no version, admission outcomes missing the acknowledgement field, a two-field replacement quote, a fabricated cancellation digest. In every case the production code was right and the fixture was the lie.

Design decisions and spec interpretations

  • Separate package (§6 topology): commerce protocol versions independently of the core wire protocol; same conventions (nullable-error validators, injected Sha256Fn, frozen vectors, dep-hygiene gate — src/ imports relative paths only). canonicalJson is a deliberate copy of @dina/protocol's JCS subset; frozen vectors on both sides turn copy drift into a test failure.
  • §9.1 "exact under the declared unit conversion" read as: the CONVERSION must be exact (kg/g, l/ml, same-code); the quantity/priceBasis ratio may be fractional — the integer-quotient reading would falsify the spec's own "per-unit pricing always does" clause. Case↔each is refused, never guessed.
  • Choices the spec left open (flagged below for confirmation): unit vocabulary v1 = each/case/pallet/g/kg/ml/l, custom unit codes rejected; bounds = 15-digit minor units / 12-digit quantity integers; currency validated as shape only (no ISO table pin); termsDigest input = charges + estimates + paymentTerms + validUntil; reasonCode = open bounded snake_case vocabulary; timestamps = canonical ISO UTC (Z, fraction absent or 3 digits); totals reject an intermediate-negative running sum.
  • Deviation from house export policy: operational commerce tables (heads, counters, order refs, watermarks) ARE exported — §16.2 requires it; restoring receipts without them would reset counters or fork heads. The stale-backup residual is fenced by the commerce epoch, not by exclusion.
  • Partial quotes are legal (a quote need not answer every request line, §19); all-or-none binds ORDERS to quotes. Genesis status state is DERIVED from the recorded acknowledgement — callers cannot claim a different resolving event.
  • host_operations enters the scope hash only when non-empty, so every pre-commerce manifest and frozen digest vector keeps its hash while widening still forces re-consent (absent == empty == no brokered authority).

Item map

  • CMP-1…6 — protocol spine: scaffold/numerics, arithmetic, digests, negotiation types, lifecycle types, frozen vectors. DONE
  • CMC-1…6 — Core: stores + migration v31, admission engine, lifecycle engine, extension-op registry, provider-ingress bridge, drain + epoch fencing. DONE
  • CM-FINAL — three-tier test plan + adversarial workflow pass + full monorepo gate. DONE

Validation

Adversarial workflow pass: six finder dimensions, 26 agents, every finding independently skeptic-verified — 20 raised, 3 refuted, 17 confirmed and fixed. The critical one: held-evidence re-adoption accepted a CONTENT digest (a hash anyone can compute) as authenticity proof, letting a forged acknowledgement mint a real fulfilment obligation; now an injected verifyHeldEvidence that fails closed. Also fixed: cancellation could decide an effect_started reservation (double-win); held status receipts did not disqualify never_received; delivered → disputed was unbounded at signing; drain entries under-pinned params/data-scope; an over-wide ingress schema-snapshot smuggling channel; two host_operations validation gaps; three vector under-pins; four missing/weak tests.

Final gate (serial, after the last fix): commerce-protocol 198 · protocol 724 · core 6,208 · brain 3,557 · core-server 2,825 · mobile 3,293 — 16,805 tests green; typechecks clean on all seven targets; lint/format clean on touched files; diff confined to 46 intentional paths.

Remaining integration (next phases)

  • Phase 1 pack wiring: Brain-side submitter routing ServiceHandler.handleQuery into the ingress bridge (seam mirrors the existing reasoning submitter); PDS fetch/publish callbacks for the epoch service; Buyer/Supplier manifests + runners.
  • Phase 2: catalog pointer/snapshot records (CAS sequencing) + the commerce AppView (ingest, index, search, relationship projection, §25.4 tests). Settle spec Open Question 2 (snapshot transport) first. CatalogItem/relationship-claim validators already exist at the protocol layer.
  • Phase 3: managed business runtime.

Owner decisions (both previously blocking, now settled)

  1. Post-restore quote capacity (X-10). §16.2 says admission against a restore-voided quote returns quote_superseded with “a freshly signed head at the new epoch”, and separately that “capacity is never resurrected from a backup”. It never says what capacity that fresh head carries — and that is a money question. Decision: Core never synthesizes a replacement. It holds no commercial authority (Kernel-not-Platform), and it cannot know how much of the original allowance was really consumed: the backup’s use counter is precisely the number a stale restore makes untrustworthy. A supplier-side seam owns the re-offer. With no seam wired, admission refuses with quote_voided and no digest — naming the voided head would have the buyer re-approve forever against something that can never go live again. This is a deliberate, recorded deviation from the spec’s literal wording: the spec asks Core to do something Core is not permitted to do.
  2. Scope of this pass. The two reviewers read §24 oppositely — a genuine specification ambiguity, not reviewer error. Decision: the broad reading. This pass owes the extension-operation broker, the provider installer journey, the update-rebind coordinator and validated archive restore, alongside the Phase 0 spine and the §23 Core work. Both reviewers now share this as a frozen rubric, which removed the four-item disagreement in round 3.

Owner decisions, 2026-08-07 (binding)

  1. Wire version. 1.x is explicitly unfrozen until the §24 Phase 0 exit criterion is met; it then freezes at 1.0. The HeldEvidence change was incompatible, but nothing is deployed and no external peer exists, so burning a major on pre-release churn buys nothing — and further Phase 0 changes are expected (units, catalog records, missing vectors). Consequence: no conformance claim may be made, and no third party may target the wire, until that note is lifted. This must be stated in packages/protocol/docs/conformance.md, not only here.
  2. Quotes do not reserve stock (spec §27 Q6, now closed). max_uses caps how many orders a quote family admits; real inventory stays supplier/ERP authority. Core cannot honour or verify a stock reservation, and modelling one would put a commercial commitment inside the kernel. This confirms the built semantics — recorded so a later reader does not mistake capacity for inventory.
  3. Unit vocabulary v1 is a closed list; custom units are rejected (spec §27 Q4, now closed). Widening after the freeze is additive and safe; a permissive v1 cannot be narrowed without breaking peers. Supplier-declared units with conversion evidence were rejected because two implementations can disagree on a conversion, which breaks the byte-identical arithmetic §9.1 depends on.
  4. Archive carries operational tables as EVIDENCE; restore never adopts their counters. §16.2 requires quote/status heads and use counters in the archive, while the house posture treats them as live authority — a genuine contradiction. Resolution: export them so an archive is a complete record, and make the restore ceremony always void capacity and re-fence chains rather than trusting restored counters. Consequence: the WS-4.2 preflight no longer has to establish that counters are trustworthy — only that the archive is intact and authentic. Capacity and chain state are re-derived every time. This is both smaller and safer than the spec-faithful reading, and it removes the "validated counters" failure mode entirely.

Architecture decision — why a refactor, not more patches

Four review rounds kept finding second-order breaks: a correct, tested fix in one place broke a rule enforced in another. Twice the damage came from my own remediation — the §11.2 ownership gate silently disabled §16.2 disaster recovery, and persisting valid_until supplied the last ingredient for reviving an expired pre-backup quote family. That is not bad luck four times; it is a structural signal.

The smells, measured:

ruleplaces enforcing itowner
“capacity is never resurrected from a backup”4+none
“does this order exist”14 call sitesnone
“wire records are snake_case”12 as castsconvention

Named: shotgun surgery (one idea, many edit sites — the cost of a change tracks how many sites you remember, and I remembered three of four); anemic domain model (rows plus discipline, so a quote family cannot refuse an illegal operation because it is not a thing); lying casts (the compiler told not to look at exactly the boundary that needed checking); and a non-exhaustive switch over parsed JSON (the type system asserting a fact about untrusted data). The tests were the only net holding these together, and seven of them turned out to hold nothing.

Recorded because I got the reasoning wrong first: I declined the deeper fix partly because it was a day’s work and would invalidate four review rounds. Neither is a reason. Reviews are cheap to re-run; wrong structure is not cheap to live with. The only legitimate constraint is avoiding rigidity.

Agreed design (refined against a Codex review of the proposal)

Plain validated data crosses boundaries. Pure domain aggregates own local state rules. Transactional application services coordinate cross-aggregate changes. Raw persistence primitives are not available to production callers.

Four corrections to my first proposal, all accepted:

  • Uniqueness across (buyer_did, purchase_order_id) and (buyer_did, idempotency_key) is a database invariant. One CommerceOrder cannot own a rule about all orders.
  • Deciding an order and settling its quote hold spans two aggregates plus receipts. It belongs to a transactional application service, not to CommerceOrder — my version would have pushed quote knowledge into the order.
  • Typed results, not throws, for business outcomes. Expired, consumed, superseded and illegal-transition are normal results that travel to the buyer as wire reason_codes. Corruption and impossible state (a failed settlement inside a committed decision; a head row whose receipt is missing) throw and roll back. Conflating the two is arguably what produced the ignored settleUse boolean.
  • Hiding the repositories is the whole point. QuoteFamily.advance() is worthless while casAdvanceHead() stays callable — the next call site simply bypasses it. Success is measured by a grep returning empty, not by a passing test.

One discipline added on top: application services coordinate and persist, and must not re-derive aggregate rules. A service that re-checks epoch or capacity “to be safe” has scattered the rule again and become a god-object with extra steps.

Wire types stay plain serialisable data with validators — they are hashed byte-exactly, frozen in conformance vectors, and must be reproducible by a Go/Rust/Swift port. Turning them into classes would fight both. No unit-of-work ceremony over the existing synchronous DatabaseAdapter and explicit TxRunner: that is rigidity, not correctness.

Refactor log

ARCH-0a — QuoteFamily aggregate, quote ledger hidden (done)

packages/core/src/commerce/quote_family.ts. The supplier-side quote family is now an object that refuses illegal operations, and CommerceQuoteLedgerRepository is no longer reachable from any production caller. Three engines took the store instead: admission, epoch, lifecycle.

rulebeforeafter
capacity never resurrected after restoreone expiry-filtered sweep at restoreisStale() inside the aggregate, checked by every mutating path
admission precedence (§9.9)seven inline branches in admitOrderone admits() method that returns the record it judged
capacity check + holdactiveUseCount then holdUse, two statementsone hold(), check-and-act together
settlement CASboolean discarded at three call sitessettle() throws CommerceIntegrityError, transaction rolls back

Decisions worth knowing:

  • Refusals are values; corruption throws. Expired, consumed, superseded, voided, stale-epoch and duplicate are ordinary outcomes that become wire reason_codes, so they are returned. A settlement CAS that cannot fail failing, or a head row that will not read back after being written, throws — those mean the ledger and the order references disagree, and committing half a decision is worse than aborting.
  • The aggregate does not re-check the protocol verifier. Revision numbering, audience, request binding, in-chain epoch regression and immutable max_uses belong to verifyQuoteRevisionExtends. My first version re-implemented two of them locally — the same scattering, one layer down — and answered with a vaguer message than the real one. It now calls the verifier and carries its sentence through in a detail field. Only one rule is the aggregate's alone: epoch monotonicity, which compares the head to Core's restore history and is invisible to a verifier that only sees two revisions.
  • detail is operator text, never wire text. The buyer-facing reason_code is derived from the refusal name alone, through a total Record<QuoteRefusal, string> — so a new refusal cannot reach a counterparty unhandled. stale_epoch maps to quote_voided: to the buyer they mean the same thing, this family is gone.
  • Duplicate is decided before capacity. Capacity-first told a caller retrying its own order that the quote was consumed — by the caller's own hold.
  • hold() checks expiry itself rather than trusting that admits() ran first. An aggregate whose rules only hold when methods are called in the right order is the discipline this refactor exists to remove. It judges against the head's window, which the family keeps current; admits() judges the record the order names, which may be an honoured superseded revision with a different deadline.
  • An instance is a one-transaction snapshot of the head row. Stated in the class doc because nothing in the type system says so.

Debt this leaves, recorded rather than hidden: the restore sweep still calls voidUnexpired, which by construction cannot see an already-expired family. It is kept because the restore is an event that must leave a receipt with a real count — the fence itself is now epoch monotonicity, which needs no sweep and cannot miss a row.

Verification: nine mutations, each asserting its anchor exists before removing it — a silent no-op mutation reads as a passing suite. All nine went red. Two (stale_epoch at admission, settlement rollback) were caught only by the aggregate's own unit tests until cross-layer tests were added; both now fail in two suites. Grep confirms no production file outside the aggregate names registerHead, casAdvanceHead, holdUse, settleUse, voidUnexpired or activeUseCount. Gate: tsc clean, eslint clean, core 6,271 passing.

Two false-green tests were caught during this item. The first asserted the validity window advanced with the head without ever reading it — fixed by exposing validUntil and asserting it. The second asserted duplicate_use on a single-use quote, where capacity is exhausted by the first hold, so the refusal it named was unreachable.

WS-0.4 — audience binding as a required argument (done)

QuoteFamily.register(deps, quote, expectedBuyerDid). The buyer a quote is supposed to be for is now an argument the caller must supply, refused inside the aggregate as foreign_audience. Both inline comparisons are deleted.

Why this shape. The rule had already escaped once: the counterproposal path was bound in round 2 and the post-restore re-offer path was missed, found only in round 3. A comparison at a call site is a rule every path must remember; an argument is a rule every path must satisfy. Adding the parameter made the compiler enumerate all sixteen call sites — which is the property being bought, not a side effect.

Deliberate no-op. The ordinary signing path passes quote.buyer_did, which compares a value to itself today. That is intentional and marked in the code: it is the seam where the still-open request-receipt binding will supply a real expectation (the buyer named on the retained quote request), and meanwhile it forces every future caller to answer the question rather than inherit no check.

Tests: 151 pass. Mutation — deleting the guard fails four tests across both registration paths, up from two when the rule lived at one call site. tsc and eslint clean. Next: WS-0.3, the StatusChain aggregate, which is the same argument applied to a rule that has now escaped three times.

WS-0.3 — StatusChain aggregate (done)

packages/core/src/commerce/status_chain.ts. Chain CREATION, ADVANCEMENT and FENCING are now three operations on one object, and CommerceStatusHeadRepository is unreachable from production. Each operation carries its own restore prerequisite: create refuses when the ORDER predates the epoch, advance refuses when the HEAD does, and fence requires a higher epoch because fencing is how a restored chain rejoins.

The rule this closes escaped three times. Missing from signStatusUpdate; then missing from signStatusUpdateInTx once added to the first; then, shared as a predicate, unable to express chain creation at all — the predicate takes a head epoch, and at genesis there is no head. That last one is worth stating plainly, because I got it wrong myself and published a table asserting genesis was safe: the state that matters after a restore is the BUYER's copy, not the local row. A restored supplier whose head row was lost signs a second sequence-0 record, the buyer holds two, and the fence cannot repair it — unavailable before a genesis, blocked afterwards by the fork check. Local absence is the restore symptom, never proof of safety.

Design decision: two bars, not one. Re-adoption first stamped the order with previousEpoch(current) to make creation refuse. A test caught that as a no-op at epoch 1, where there is no lower epoch — the sentinel only worked from epoch 2. That was a workaround wearing a rule's clothes. "Was re-adopted from a counterparty's evidence" and "belongs to an older generation" are different facts, so they are now a boolean (reconciliation_required) and an epoch (admitted_epoch) with separate refusals. The flag holds at every epoch.

Duplicate removed rather than tested. The aggregate also re-checked "a fence needs a strictly higher epoch". Mutation showed deleting it changed nothing, because setFence enforces that itself in both implementations and the interface documents it as the STORE's guard. Re-checking a rule that already has an owner is the same scattering this aggregate exists to end, pointed one layer down. Deleted, with the reasoning recorded in place so it does not come back.

Tests: 157 commerce, 6,298 core. Mutations — genesis restore gate (4 red), advance restore gate (4), transition legality (2), re-adoption bar (2), re-adoption stamp (2). All caught.

Process failure worth recording: a greedy multi-line regex intended to delete one 15-line method matched from the first /** in the file and deleted 965 lines of lifecycle_engine.ts. Restored from the commit and re-applied the migration as 15 exact-string edits, each asserting a unique match. This is the fourth loose-anchor incident in this work and the first destructive one. Rule going forward: no regex spanning lines, ever — exact strings with asserted uniqueness, including for deletions. The commit an hour earlier is what made recovery cheap.

WS-0.2 — CommerceOrder aggregate + §12.8 atomicity (partial)

packages/core/src/commerce/commerce_order.ts. The order owns its own lifecycle (reserved → decided, pre_effect → effect_started), whether a transition is legal now, and what a replay answers.

What it deliberately does NOT own. Uniqueness across (buyer_did, purchase_order_id) and (buyer_did, idempotency_key) stays in the repository: one order cannot enforce a rule about all orders, and the unique indexes already do. Nothing about the quote lives here either — deciding an order AND settling its capacity hold spans two aggregates, so it is coordinated, not absorbed. Pushing quote knowledge into the order is how the two got tangled originally.

§12.8 race closed. Acceptance and its status genesis were two transactions. A cancellation arriving in the gap saw a decided order with no chain and answered refused_policy; the same cancellation a moment later could cancel. The reason code depended on timing rather than on the order. They now commit together: the admission engine takes an injected createAcceptedGenesisInTx, supplied by the composition root so neither engine learns the other's internals, and a genesis failure throws so the acceptance rolls back. An accepted order with no chain — the state the race exploited — is no longer reachable.

Tradeoff, stated plainly. The reviewers asked for an AdmissionService as a separate transactional coordinator. What landed is the atomicity requirement met through a narrow injected seam, with the engines still owning their transactions. The correctness defect is closed and mutation-verified; the file-level extraction is not done. Recorded as WBS 0.2b rather than described as complete.

Tests: 161 commerce, 6,302 core. Mutations — genesis not created on accept (4 red), genesis failure swallowed instead of rolling back (2 red). Both caught.

WS-0.5 — the boundary made real (done)

packages/core/src/commerce/runtime.ts composes the commerce stores once. Production code receives QuoteFamilyStore, StatusChainStore and CommerceOrderStore; the five mutable per-repository globals are gone, and both boot paths install one runtime instead of five raw repositories.

Why this mattered more than it looked. I previously told the owner "the ledger is hidden — grep-verified". That was true of call sites and false of the boundary: the raw repositories were still exported from the package root AND installed into mutable globals, so any future caller could reach past every aggregate rule with no compiler or test failure. A grep proves a fact about today; it says nothing about the design.

The guard is now static, not behavioural. __tests__/commerce/boundary.test.ts asserts over the SOURCE: no production file outside an owner calls a raw mutator, no per-repository globals exist, no engine declares a raw repository dependency, and only the composition root constructs SQLite repositories. Behavioural tests cannot express this — they test what the code does, and the risk here is what a future caller could do. It fails on the commit that reintroduces a bypass rather than on the incident that exploits one.

It immediately found two things I had missed while believing the item was finished: the fifth global (watermarks.ts) was still there, and both engines still declared orderRefs: CommerceOrderRefRepository even though CommerceOrder existed. That is the guard doing exactly the job a grep could not.

Design decision: lazy identity. supplierDid and currentEpoch are thunks, because storage initialises before identity and before the commerce epoch is published. Capturing a value at composition would freeze a placeholder into every quote the node ever authenticates. Both throw until established, which is the §16.2 fail-closed posture rather than an oversight.

Tests: 414 commerce + plugins, 6,306 core, workspace typecheck clean. Next: WS-0.6 (separate authorization from existence at the ingress gate) and 0.7 (type the wire boundary), then WS-3 provider install and the composition root that first makes commerce reachable.

WS-0.6 — authorization separated from existence at the ingress gate (done)

The §11.2 gate treated all three order-scoped capabilities identically: prove you own an existing order reference, or be refused. That was right for two of them and broke the third.

The defect, which was mine. order_reconcile exists to resolve outcome_unknown (§12.7/§16.2): the buyer submitted an order and never learned whether it landed. The case that matters most is the one where the supplier holds NO reference — it crashed before the durable write, or restored a backup taken before the order arrived. Requiring an existing reference made exactly that case unanswerable. My own §11.2 remediation silently disabled the disaster recovery it sat next to.

The fix is a distinction, not a bypass. Two capabilities are entitlement-by-possession (order_status, cancel_order): you may ask about an order you own, so the reference IS the proof and absence stays a denial. One is entitlement-by-evidence (order_reconcile): authorization comes from the buyer-bound payload, which must name the authenticated sender, and whether a reference exists is the handler's answer rather than the gate's business.

Why this leaks nothing. A reconcile for someone else's order is keyed on the authenticated sender and also answers never_received, so "no such order" and "not your order" stay indistinguishable — the endpoint is not an existence oracle. That is exactly why the same relaxation would be wrong for order_status, and a test now pins that contrast rather than leaving it as reasoning in a comment.

Tests: 17 ingress, 6,309 core, workspace typecheck clean. Mutations — restoring the reference requirement for reconcile (1 red), dropping the payload buyer binding (2 red), dropping the existence check for status (4 red). All three branches load-bearing.

WS-3.1 — provider-kind capabilities are installable (done)

NODE_SUPPORTED_FEATURES now ships kind.provider. Until this, the manifest vocabulary existed but the §14 compatibility gate rejected it, so a Supplier plugin could not be installed at all — and every commerce engine behind it was unreachable in production no matter how well tested. The spec calls this a Phase 1 exit dependency (§11.2a); it was the single largest reason the green suite overstated the state.

Why this is one line and not a new subsystem. Provider capabilities execute on the same lane tools use — plugin:<install_id>, identical claim-token, lease, retry and pinned-schema discipline — and the claim guard already computes requiredKind = envelope.service_ingress !== undefined ? 'provider' : 'tool', refusing a provider envelope on a tool-only consent and the reverse. The ingress bridge, binding resolution and response path were built in CMC-5. The install gate was the last door, not a missing floor.

The paired test matters more than the passing one. A manifest declaring ['provider', 'notify'] must still be refused with kind.notify missing and kind.provider present in neither list — otherwise "we opened one kind" and "we stopped checking kinds" look identical from a green suite.

Tests: 64 install-service, 6,311 core, workspace typecheck clean. Mutation — removing kind.provider fails 2 tests. Next: the composition root wiring (WS-3.6) and the ingress journey, which together make the first retailer/manufacturer scenario runnable.

Procurement scenario — Sancho buys from ChairMaker (done)

packages/core/__tests__/commerce/procurement_scenario.test.ts. Eight cases across both storage backends, walking the supplier-side spine as a journey rather than as isolated rules: quote → order → accept → prepare → partially fulfil → dispatch → deliver, plus restart, capacity exhaustion, audience binding, restore fencing, and both reconcile cases (processing and never_received).

Why a scenario at all. Every other commerce test drives one engine and asserts one rule, and that suite stayed green through thirty defects. A journey test is worth having precisely because it does not know which rule it is checking — it notices when the journey stops working, which is the failure mode unit tests are structurally blind to.

Scope stated so the tick is not over-read. No catalog publication or AppView discovery (WS-5, unbuilt), no plugin runner or D2D transport (engines are driven directly, as the composition root will drive them), no external ERP. This is the supplier-side spine end to end — NOT the §25.6 manual acceptance journey, which additionally needs two real Dinas, a published catalog, and a revocation step.

Two authoring errors worth recording, because both were the test being wrong about the spec rather than the code being wrong. delivered is a discriminated-union arm that FORBIDS a line snapshot (§9.11) — dispatched already carried the final quantities, and repeating them would let two records disagree. And a competitor attacking an audience-bound quote must name ITSELF as buyer; an order naming Sancho is caught earlier still, as a caller/payload mismatch, so my first version tested a different (weaker) thing than it claimed.

Tests: 16 scenario cases (8 × 2 backends), 6,327 core, workspace typecheck clean. Mutation against the scenario ALONE — acceptance/genesis atomicity (6 red), §15.5 replay lookup (2), §9.8 audience binding (2), capacity exhaustion (2). The scenario is load-bearing on its own, not a narrative over rules other tests already cover.

WS-1 protocol defects — arithmetic order and calendar dates (done)

Totals were order-dependent, which breaks §9.1 interop. computeTotal rejected any INTERMEDIATE negative running value, so subtotal 100, subtract 200, add 500 was refused while the same three charges reordered summed to 400 and passed. §9.1 specifies a plain integer sum, so two conforming implementations iterating a charge set differently must agree — otherwise the byte-identical guarantee is false for any invoice where a discount precedes a surcharge, which is an ordinary shape. Non-negativity is now a property of the RESULT.

The existing test asserted the wrong behaviour and had to be replaced rather than adjusted: it explicitly pinned "order of add/subtract cannot rescue an intermediate negative". A test encoding a non-authoritative interpretation is worse than no test, because it converts a defect into a defended decision.

And then my own fix contained the same smell. I added an explicit final non-negativity check to computeTotal; a mutation showed deleting it changed nothing, because minorUnitsToString already refuses a negative bigint. Removed — re-checking a rule that has an owner is the scattering this refactor exists to end. The test now pins the OWNER's message, so a future duplicate cannot satisfy it while the real guard is deleted.

Frozen vectors, including a rejection. Three new cases: charge_order_discount_first and charge_order_surcharge_first must produce identical totals, plus final_total_negative_is_invalid. The vector runner now handles expected-rejection cases, because a permutation pair is meaningless if an implementation can also accept what this one refuses.

validateIsoUtc accepted impossible dates. Date.parse('2026-02-30T00:00:00Z') succeeds and silently means 2 March. A timestamp that means a different day than it reads is worse than one that is rejected: it is digest-covered, so two implementations disagreeing about whether to normalise produce different bytes for the same input. Now round-trips the parsed instant and refuses a mismatch — with a real leap day (2028-02-29) and a legitimate month end still accepted, so the check is not a blanket refusal.

Tests: 208 commerce-protocol, 6,327 core, workspace typecheck clean. Mutations — final non-negativity owner (1 red), calendar rollover guard (1 red). One duplicate deleted rather than tested.

WS-1.5 — buyer-side quote verification (done)

verifySignedQuoteForBuyer previously bound only the request DIGEST. That proves the supplier SAW the request; it proves nothing about whether the quote's lines correspond to it. A quote could reuse a genuine digest while inventing line ids, misnaming requested_product, or substituting a different exact variant where the buyer said none — and the buyer's Dina would authorise the wrong goods against a quote that verified cleanly.

Five bindings added, each mutation-verified: expiry against a canonical acceptance time; request_id agreement; every quoted line must correspond to a requested line (a quote may price a SUBSET, but may not invent); exact product identity by scheme AND value, never name similarity; and substitution authority, which defaults to none and requires substitution_evidence when granted.

Design decision: subset yes, invention no. A supplier that cannot supply every line still gives a usable answer, so a partial quote is legal. What is not legal is a line the buyer never asked for — that is the §20.4 bait-and-switch surface, and it must not rely on the owner noticing by eye.

Context now carries the retained REQUEST, not just its digest. That is a wire-adjacent API change to BuyerQuoteContext, and it is the point: a verifier that cannot see what was asked cannot check that the answer matches. It also carries at_iso, because "is this quote still live" is a question about a moment, and a verifier without a clock silently answers "always".

Tests: 213 commerce-protocol, 6,327 core, workspace typecheck and eslint clean. Mutations — expiry, line correspondence, product identity, substitution authority, substitution evidence. All five red. One mutation initially crashed the suite rather than running (removing a guard made a variable possibly-undefined); redone type-soundly, since a crashed suite is not a survival.

WS-1.4 and WS-1.10 — discriminant both ways, and the freeze made honest (done)

Relationship discriminant. validateProductRelationshipClaim checked "a DID object needs a DID relationship" but not the inverse, so manufactured_by with a ProductRef object passed — a claim that a product is manufactured BY ANOTHER PRODUCT would enter the relationship projection and compose inherited manufacturer standing along an edge that means nothing. Both directions now enforced; mutation-verified.

The pre-freeze note now lives where a port would read it — packages/commerce-protocol/docs/conformance.md, added to the package's published files so a consumer actually receives it. Recording it only in these notes would have satisfied the task and helped nobody: a Go or Rust implementor reads the package, not my working notes.

It states plainly that 1.x is unstable until Phase 0 exits, that no implementation may claim conformance, that records signed before the freeze may not verify after it, and that changes land without a version bump — with the three incompatible ones (HeldEvidence, computeTotal, BuyerQuoteContext) listed in a changelog. It also lists the Phase 0 exit criteria as checkboxes with the open ones unticked, and declares which §25.1 vector categories are still missing rather than implying full coverage.

Five tests keep the document honest. A prose warning nobody checks decays — the version drifts, the freeze happens informally, or the note gets deleted in a tidy-up. The tests assert the doc names the version the code emits, carries the declaration while the version is 1.x, records the incompatible changes, lists checkable exit criteria, and admits the vector gaps. Deleting the warning is a decision that asserts the wire is stable; it should fail loudly here rather than quietly in someone else's port.

Tests: 219 commerce-protocol, workspace typecheck and eslint clean. Remaining in WS-1: exact conversation-version pinning (1.7), the catalog Phase-0 record contract (1.8), and the remaining §25.1 vector categories (1.9).

WS-1.7 — a conversation pins an exact version, not a major (done)

The defect. A reservation recorded pinned_major, and every generated record — acknowledgement, status update, receipt — hard-coded the build's COMMERCE_PROTOCOL_VERSION. So a buyer who proposed at 1.1 against a 1.1 quote received a 1.0 acknowledgement and a 1.0 status chain, and the mismatch passed the check because only the major was compared. §9.13 says a conversation is conducted at one version throughout. Ours drifted at the first record the supplier emitted.

The fix. pinned_major became pinned_version, carrying the exact string from order.protocol_version at reservation. Every emitter now takes the pinned version as an argument rather than reading a module constant: buildAcknowledgement, buildStatus, recordResult, and both signing paths. majorMatches became versionMatches and demands exact equality. The major is still derived from the pin where drain accounting genuinely wants a major (substr(...instr(...)) in SQL, split('.')[0] in memory) — that is a different question and keeps its own answer.

Where the build version still belongs. Nothing in the engines imports COMMERCE_PROTOCOL_VERSION any more; the two leftover imports were dead and are gone. Deciding whether an incoming record's version is one this build can parse is a different question from which version this conversation speaks, and it stays owned by checkProtocolVersion.

Two tests I wrote were false green, and the fix was structural. The first seeded a fixture at 1.0 — equal to the build version, so a record emitted at either would be indistinguishable and the mutation survived. The second asserted only that "some error" came back, which the wrong error satisfies. Overriding protocol_version on an already-built record does not work either: the version is digest-covered, so an override produces a record that fails signature verification for an unrelated reason and the test goes green for the wrong cause. The helpers now build at the target version — seedAdmittedOrderAtVersion('1.1') and makeCancellation(order, protocolVersion) — and the assertions discriminate accept from reject.

Verification: three mutations, each 2 tests red — exact-version match, status emits the pin, acknowledgement emits the pin. Tests: 6,331 core passing, workspace typecheck and eslint clean. Remaining in WS-1: the catalog Phase-0 record contract (1.8) and the remaining §25.1 vector categories (1.9).

WS-3.6 — the engines were built by nobody (part)

What I found. CommerceAdmissionEngine, CommerceLifecycleEngine and CommerceEpochService are constructed nowhere outside a test file. Both boots install the commerce STORES and stop. So every assertion in the six hundred-odd commerce tests was about objects that production never made: no path could admit an order or sign a status, and no test could notice, because each test builds its own engines. Wiring a subsystem up to its own doorstep and no further is a failure mode unit tests are structurally blind to.

Engines now come from the composition root. createCommerceRuntime returns admission and lifecycle alongside the stores, so both boots get them with no boot-side change — the payoff of having one root rather than a wiring recipe each boot repeats. The root is also the only place that can tie createAcceptedGenesisInTx, which is what makes acceptance and its status genesis one transaction (§12.8); admission must not learn about status chains and the lifecycle engine must not learn about admission, so nothing below the root can make that knot.

Identity became a thunk in both engines. They took supplierDid as a captured string while currentEpoch next to it was already a function — and that inconsistency was the reason the engines could not be composed at storage-init time at all. Identity resolves after storage, so a captured DID is either unavailable or empty. Read per use, an absent identity throws at the moment of use and the operation fails closed, which is what §16.2 asks for.

One transaction runner per database, by construction. tx.ts said "boots MUST construct exactly one runner per Tier-0 db and pass the SAME instance", and both boots did not: each passed a raw (fn) => db.transaction(fn), and the run plane later built a second runner and overwrote the registration. Two depth counters over one database is the nested-BEGIN crash the counter exists to prevent; it stayed invisible only because nothing yet nested across the two. Commerce writes to that same database and is composed long before the run plane exists, so the luck was about to run out. tier0TxRunner(db) now derives the runner from the database, so asking twice returns the same object. A rule each caller had to remember became a fact.

A typed answer for "commerce is not configured". Both refusals arrive as thrown errors, which is right for a signing path and wrong for a caller that only wants to know whether to offer commerce at all — that caller would have to catch an exception and read its message. commerceAvailability() answers not_installed / no_identity / no_epoch / available.

Honest status: commerce is wired but still switched off. Nothing installs CommerceEpochService — it needs a CAS-capable PDS publisher, which is WS-2.4 — so on a real boot availability() reports no_epoch and every commerce operation refuses. That is the DoD's "disabled cleanly when unconfigured" half, and it is the correct §16.2 posture, but it is not commerce running end to end. I am recording 3.6 as PART, not DONE.

Tech debt removed while here. The procurement scenario built THREE engines — a plain admission engine, a lifecycle engine, and a second "atomic" admission engine — and used the plain one for exactly one call, registerSignedQuote, which the wired engine does identically. It was testing a configuration that no longer ships. Collapsed to one admission engine matching production.

Verification: 8 new composition-root tests over a real SQLCipher file, plus a boundary test forbidding engine construction outside the root. Five mutations, each caught and none crashing the suite: genesis closure unwired (2 red), runner not shared (1), timeout default removed (1), availability always true (2), and an engine constructed in a stray production file (1, named by file). Tests: 6,340 core, 2,825 server, 3,293 mobile; workspace typecheck and eslint clean.

WS-2.4 — the epoch service had no repo (part)

Why commerce was still off after WS-3.6. The epoch service takes its repo as two injected callbacks, a live read and a CAS write, because the PDS client is app-level wiring. Nothing supplied them, so the service was never built, getCommerceEpochService() returned null forever, and every signing path refused.

The PDS client could not do the CAS. PDSPublisher.putRecord sent no swapRecord, which is a blind overwrite — fine for a service profile, wrong for the one record whose entire purpose is to serialize concurrent writers. Two nodes restoring from the same backup would both believe they published epoch N+1, and two divergent generations of quotes would exist under one business identity. putRecord now takes an optional swap, PDSPublisherError.casLost distinguishes a lost race from a refusing repo, and getRecord was added (the class had no read at all) because a swap needs the CID of the thing it replaces.

Fail-closed in three places. An unreachable repo throws rather than reading as "no record" — treating it as absence would publish a genesis over a live chain. A record that fails validation throws for the same reason. And the genesis writes with swapRecord: null, so it lands only if nothing is there.

Three of my first five mutations survived, and two were real false greens. The "restorer that lost the race" test never produced a race: establishAfterRestore re-reads at the top of every attempt, so the second node simply saw the new value and there was no conflict to lose. The fake PDS now has a one-shot hook that moves the live record between a reader's read and its publish, which is the only window in which a writer can actually lose. The "corrupt record" test asserted the record was unchanged — but the CAS would have rejected the genesis anyway, so the test passed while the validation did nothing. It now asserts no write was even ATTEMPTED.

One guard I could not reach, and said so in the code. The check that refuses to swap against a predecessor this node never read is unreachable through the service, which always passes back the record the closure just cached — I tried overlapping restores and could not construct a case. It stands as an assertion on the EpochPublisher contract and now THROWS rather than returning false, because "retry" cannot fix a caller handing us a predecessor we never saw. The comment says plainly that it is unexercised rather than implying coverage.

Wired at boot, and only on success. The server calls it where the PDS publisher is already wired. When establishment fails the service is left UNINSTALLED, so commerceAvailability() reports no_epoch — installing a service whose currentEpoch() throws would report the same thing less clearly. A node without DINA_PDS_PROVISION has no repo, so commerce is simply off there.

Still open: no periodic revalidation of the live epoch (a forgotten pre-restore node signs at its cached epoch indefinitely), and the mobile boot has no epoch wiring, so commerce is server-only today. Verification: 9 new tests against a fake PDS that enforces swap semantics; mutations for swap-omitted (3 red), swap-key-not-sent (3), CAS-loss-not-retried (1) and corrupt-read-as-absent (1). Tests: 6,340 core, 2,834 server, 3,557 brain; typecheck and eslint clean.

Two process notes. I ran git stash to check whether two lint errors pre-dated my change — a git write I should not have made without asking; it restored cleanly and git show HEAD:file answers the same question. And an eslint --fix aimed too broadly reformatted seven unrelated files; reverted, and the diff is this item's files only.

WS-3.2 — the listing rule and the answering rule disagreed (part)

The defect. validateServiceListing has accepted a complete plugin binding as an execution plane since the plugin substrate landed, so a commerce listing bound to a Supplier plugin saves and publishes cleanly. ServiceHandler.handleQuery knew only two planes — an agent binding and a Tier-1 instruction — and answered capability_not_executable to every query such a listing received. One rule decided what could be published and a different rule decided what could be answered. That is also why the provider-ingress bridge had no caller: there was no code path that could ever have reached it.

Injected, not imported. Brain now recognises the plugin plane and hands the query to a ProviderIngressSubmitter supplied by Core — the same seam shape as ServiceReasoningSubmitter. The handler decides WHICH plane answers; Core owns what that plane does. Brain learns nothing about installs, manifest CIDs, or workflow tasks.

Defaulted in the shared runtime, not at each boot. buildHomeNodeServiceRuntime resolves the submitter from the wired WorkflowService, so both boots get it with no boot-side line to forget. An option each composition root had to remember is exactly how a plane comes to validate, save, publish, advertise itself, and then answer unavailable on the one node where somebody forgot. A node with no workflow service gets null and plugin-bound capabilities refuse.

Both execution paths, not just the easy one. A review-policy capability goes through approval first, and executeAndRespond now routes to the plugin after the operator approves. Handling only the auto path would have left a silent hole: the approval succeeds, the query falls into the delegation path the operator never configured, and the requester waits out its TTL while everything looks fine.

Refusals are relayed, and stay non-disclosing. The typed ingress code reaches the requester; Core's message does not, because it is written for an operator reading logs and an order-scoped denial must not become an oracle for enumerating order ids.

Three test problems found by mutation. Two mutations I first ran did not survive, they CRASHED the suite (unused-variable and unreachable-code errors), which reports as "0 total" and is not evidence of anything; redone shape-preserving. A third genuinely survived: nothing tested the runtime DEFAULT — both my tests passed an explicit submitter or none, so deleting the default entirely changed nothing. Fixed by registering a workflow service and watching the refusal code change from plugin_lane_unavailable to a real ingress verdict. And my empty-string binding test covered one of three symmetric fields; the other two guards were dead. Made table-driven rather than trusting symmetry — the same reasoning that left three guards unexercised earlier in this work.

One assertion I wrote was wrong about the system, and the system was right. I expected install_unavailable from a node with no installs; the bridge answered order_subject_denied, because §11.2 subject authorization runs BEFORE binding resolution so an unauthorized sender cannot probe install state through the typed codes either. Corrected the test to the real ordering.

The completion half, closed. The existing full-loop test stopped at the bridge CONTEXT, because the harness stubs responseBridgeSender. That proves the correlation survives the plugin lane and says nothing about what the peer receives — so the half of §11.2a step 4 a buyer actually cares about was unexercised. Five tests now drive the REAL sender: a conforming result answers the buyer; a result the pinned schema rejects becomes result_schema_violation and the drifted payload never travels; a missing required field is a violation rather than a partial success; unparseable runner output still answers, so the buyer stops waiting instead of learning nothing until its TTL; and an explicitly tagged unavailable is forwarded verbatim rather than dressed as success.

Two more mutations crashed rather than survived — the same trap as before, both from TypeScript narrowing (inverting a null check makes the following field access a compile error). Redone shape-preserving: disable validation by flipping the status it triggers on, and defeat ingress recognition by parsing an empty envelope. All five completion mutations then came back red.

3.2 is now DONE. Verification: 15 new tests (7 handler, 3 runtime, 5 completion), 13 mutations caught across the two halves. Tests: 6,345 core, 3,567 brain, 27 home-node, 2,834 server, 3,293 mobile; typecheck clean, no new lint errors.

WS-3.3 — a listing could publish a binding that never resolves (done)

What was missing. validateServiceListing lives in @dina/protocol and checks the binding's SHAPE — all three fields together, or none. It cannot check more, because that package is deliberately dependency-free and has no install registry. So nothing stopped a provider saving and publishing a listing that named an install this node does not have, pinned a manifest CID the install no longer runs, or named a capability the manifest never declared. Each of those advertises a capability on the AppView and then answers install_unavailable to every buyer who acts on it. For a buyer, a discovery result that cannot be fulfilled is worse than not appearing at all.

Where the check belongs. validateServiceConfigForSave in Core — its own docstring already says it is the shared save boundary for every editor and agent surface, and Core is where the install registry lives. The codes are declared in the protocol package because they are part of the contract every surface reports; the CHECK cannot live there. Five refusals, each with its own code and its own operator-facing sentence: unknown install, non-active install, stale CID, undeclared capability, and a TOOL capability published as a service.

That last one is a consent boundary, not a typo check. A tool capability answers Dina's own questions; a provider capability answers a PEER's. Publishing a tool as a service would route a stranger's query into a capability the owner consented to for something else entirely.

Save-time checking does not replace query-time checking. A listing valid at save can go stale afterwards — the install gets paused, or updated to a new CID. Ingress keeps its own identical checks; this one exists so the failure surfaces to the OPERATOR while they are editing, rather than to a buyer weeks later.

My test fixtures were wrong three times before the code was. Missing category, then an invalid category for that capability, then a bare request_quote that is neither an official capability nor a namespaced custom one. Each was a real listing rule doing its job. The fourth failure was mine too: I hand-rolled a manifest that activate accepted but getById could not read back, so the install vanished silently — fixed by using the manifest shape the ingress suite already proves round-trips. And the tool-capability test failed until I gave the second install its own device DID, because activate refuses when another active install already owns a device — which would have reported "not active", the right answer to a different question.

Verification: 8 tests, 7 mutations caught. Two mutations crashed rather than survived (removing a null guard makes the following field access uncompilable under strict TS); for those, the reported CODE was mutated instead, which is what the tests actually pin. Tests: 6,353 core, 724 protocol, 3,567 brain, 2,834 server, 3,293 mobile; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-4.2 — the restore fence had no trigger, and I had just made it reachable (part)

Say the bad part first: my own WS-2.4 change opened this. Before it, nothing installed the epoch service, so currentEpoch() threw and commerce refused everything — a restored node was safe by accident. Installing the epoch service made establish() run on every boot, and establish() ADOPTS the live epoch. A node restored from a backup would come up, adopt the unchanged epoch, and find every restored quote head matching it. The backup's use counters would be live again: capacity already spent, spendable a second time. That is the resurrection §16.2 exists to prevent, and I put the last piece in place for it two items ago.

Why nothing caught it. establishAfterRestore() — which increments the epoch, voids unexpired capacity, and writes the fence receipt — is fully implemented and well tested, and had no caller anywhere. The same shape as the engines and the ingress bridge: correct code, reachable by nobody.

Why a marker rather than a check. Nothing in restored data distinguishes it from data this node wrote itself — that is what a faithful restore MEANS, and the commerce tables are in the archive deliberately (restoring receipts without heads and counters would re-serve quotes against reset counters or re-sign forked heads). The knowledge that a restore happened exists for exactly one moment, inside the import, and has to be written down before it is lost.

Written in the import's OWN transaction. A marker written after the import committed could be lost to a crash in between, leaving resurrected counters with no fence pending — the precise state this prevents, reachable by unlucky timing. Inside the same transaction, restored capacity and the obligation to void it are one atomic fact.

Three deliberate asymmetries. The marker is set unconditionally, with no attempt to detect whether a given archive carried commerce rows: an empty commerce set costs one wasted epoch increment, a missed one lets capacity be spent twice. It is left set when the fence cannot complete, so a node that could not reach its repo retries next boot instead of quietly adopting the live epoch later. And an unreadable kv_store reads as "fence owed" — "no marker found" is the answer that resurrects capacity, and a database we cannot query is not evidence that this node is safe to trade from.

It does not travel. The marker describes THIS node's pending obligation, not the backup's content. Exported, a backup taken while a fence was owed would demand a second fence on a different node for an event that already happened here.

Recorded as PART, not DONE. The owner decision says preflight must prove the archive is intact and authentic — row counts, digests, required-table coverage, cross-table invariants — and that is not implemented; restore still trusts the archive's structure. What IS closed is the half the decision calls out as mandatory regardless: counters are never adopted. Verification: 7 tests (5 boot-side, 2 archive-side), 5 mutations, all caught first attempt. Tests: 6,355 core, 2,839 server, 3,293 mobile; typecheck clean, no new lint errors.

WS-4.4 — a cancellation could decide an order whose decision was lost (done)

The hole. The cancellation race treats reserved as "not yet decided" and lets the cancellation win: it refunds the hold and signs a terminal cancellation_won genesis. After a restore that reading is unsound. An order restored as reserved may have been accepted AFTER the backup was taken — the buyer holds an acknowledgement this node no longer has. Deciding it now forks the chain against a record the buyer can prove, and refunds capacity that was actually committed.

The same hole through re-adoption, which is worse. Re-adoption rebuilds the reference from the buyer's held acknowledgement, so the order arrives back in reserved having never been decided by THIS node's records at all.

It answers nothing and records nothing. The guard fires before the receipt write. The old path wrote a cancellation receipt and THEN failed at the signer, leaving durable evidence of a decision never made; now a cancellation retried after reconciliation still gets a real answer. The buyer's route to certainty is order_reconcile, the capability designed for exactly "I do not know what happened".

Deliberately not pending_review. The result union has no "unresolved" arm, and pending_review was the tempting fit — but it claims a human is looking at the order, which is false, and it would let finalizePendingCancellation decide the very order that cannot be decided.

One existing test changed meaning, and I did not weaken it. A test asserted the chain-level refusal message; the order-level guard now fires first, so the message names the order rather than the fence. I updated it and ADDED the stronger assertion the change earns: no receipt is written. The first mutation run also showed the reconciliation-flag guard surviving — no test covered cancelling a re-adopted order — so that case is now covered too.

Verification: 1 new test, 1 updated, 2 mutations each caught after the gap was closed. Tests: 6,357 core; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-4.3 — the fence had no gate (part)

I built a one-way door and then locked it harder. Re-adoption stamps reconciliation_required, which bars chain creation — and, as of WS-4.4, bars cancellation too. Nothing anywhere ever cleared that flag. A re-adopted order was frozen permanently: it could never sign a status, and after my last commit it could never be cancelled either. A rule with no exit is not a fence, it is a wall.

The bar is the order proposal, and the digest is the whole proof. The buyer presents the proposal it holds; Core requires its digest to equal the one on the reference, which came from this supplier's OWN signed acknowledgement. So the check binds the recovered document to something the supplier already committed to — a buyer cannot substitute a larger order, because the digest would not match. The proposal is then STORED, which is what makes the clearing honest rather than a flag flip: the node can describe the order's lines again, which is exactly the capability it lost.

Deliberately not an operator confirmation. The obvious alternative asks the owner "did we ship this?" before clearing. But a re-adopted order has no status chain at all, so there is no fulfilment to reconcile — the first status it can sign is the genesis, which states what the acknowledgement already says. Asking a human to confirm something the records establish trains them to click through, which costs more safety than it buys.

A SQLite-only failure the dual-backend harness caught. The in-memory repository passed while SQLite returned an empty proposal: the new column was in the schema and the UPDATE, but not in the SELECT's explicit column list. A single-backend test would have shipped it.

Mutation found one piece of dead code and one honest pair. The explicit buyer-DID comparison survived deletion, because orders.load is keyed on (buyerDid, purchaseOrderId) — the lookup IS the ownership test, the same entitlement-by-possession the ingress gate uses. Deleted rather than kept as a weaker second copy of a rule, since the proposal is attacker-supplied and the key is not. The aggregate's flag check and the repository's CAS also each survived alone — but removing BOTH fails, so they are layered rather than duplicated: one gives a precise refusal, the other makes two concurrent ceremonies resolve to one winner. Documented as such.

PART, not DONE: there is no owner-facing surface (route or UI) and no bulk sweep over all non-terminal orders — the ceremony is per-order and caller-driven. Verification: 4 new tests, 4 mutations caught, 2 redundancies analysed rather than assumed. Tests: 6,365 core, 2,839 server, 3,293 mobile; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-4.5 — "business records survive" is not satisfied by rows in a table (part)

The finding. uninstall had no commerce awareness. Every commerce lifecycle capability — order_status, cancel_order, order_reconcile — reaches its answer through the backing install's binding, so removing that install while orders are open leaves the records intact and UNREACHABLE. The buyer can never learn the outcome of an order the supplier committed to. §16.4 says business records survive; surviving in storage is not the promise.

Refuse rather than warn. The uninstall throws a typed error carrying the open count, so the operator is told what to do — resolve those orders by delivering, cancelling or reconciling them — rather than being handed a wall.

delivered is deliberately NOT terminal for this count. It becomes terminal only once its dispute window elapses, and a chain inside that window is still an open obligation. Counting it as finished would let an uninstall strand exactly the orders a dispute is most likely to concern.

Conservative on purpose, and I want this flagged. The count is NODE-WIDE, because an order reference does not record which install served it. On a node running one Supplier plugin — the only shipped shape — that is exact. On a multi-plugin node it can refuse an uninstall that would have been safe. I chose that direction because refusing costs an operator a conversation while permitting costs a buyer their recourse, but recording the order → install attribution would make it precise, and that is worth doing before multi-plugin nodes are real.

Mutation caught the usual gap. Three counting mutations went red immediately; the GUARD ITSELF survived, because nothing drove uninstall. Adding that test surfaced two API facts I had assumed wrong: an unknown install id returns early (correctly — nothing to strand), and without a device-revoke callback the teardown stops at paused rather than deleting the row.

PART: pause and update semantics (§16.3, §16.5) are not addressed, and the WBS makes 4.5 depend on the update-rebind coordinator (3.7), which is not built. Verification: 5 new tests, 4 mutations caught. Tests: 6,370 core, 2,839 server, 3,293 mobile; typecheck and lint clean.

The procurement scenario found a defect my unit test could not (4.3 correction)

What happened. I added a Sancho/ChairMaker journey for the hardest restore case: ChairMaker restores a backup taken BEFORE the order arrived, so the order is not stale on that node — it is ABSENT, and only Sancho's copy proves it happened. Re-adoption recovers it, the order is frozen, the ceremony unfreezes it, and trading resumes.

It failed at the last step with status: order receipt missing — store integrity failure. My WS-4.3 ceremony stored the recovered proposal on the order REFERENCE, in a column I added for the purpose. The code that needs the proposal — signStatusUpdate, checking cumulative line snapshots — reads the RECEIPT store. So the ceremony cleared the flag, reported success, and left the order still unable to move. My unit test only advanced as far as the genesis, which does not read the proposal, so it passed.

The fix removed the column rather than adding a second reader. The receipt store is already the durable home of every commerce document, keyed by digest; a second copy on the reference is the duplicate-owner smell I have deleted three times elsewhere in this work. The ceremony now writes the proposal as the order receipt, and the schema column, repository field, and row plumbing I had added are gone.

Why this is the argument for scenario tests. Every unit involved was correct and passing. The defect lived in the seam between "where the ceremony writes" and "where the lifecycle reads" — invisible to any test that exercises one side. It is the same shape as the seven wiring gaps found earlier in this work, and it was caught by walking the whole journey once.

WS-4.3 is more complete than it was, and the note above it should be read with this correction. Tests: 6,372 core (211 commerce), 2,839 server, 3,293 mobile; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-3.4 — the extension-operation broker (part)

The problem §3.4 solves. A plugin runner is untrusted code out of process that must be able to ask for effects only Dina can perform — search the AppView, send a D2D message — without ever holding the authority to perform them. The answer is a four-step protocol, and this is its state machine: propose, permit, execute, verify.

Durable first, because every step boundary is a crash boundary. The proposal is recorded before anything is permitted, so a crash leaves a readable row rather than an intention that evaporated. executing is written BEFORE the effect, so a crash mid-effect is discoverable through listExecuting() instead of being retried into a double send. That is also why outcome_unknown is a first-class terminal state rather than an error: an effect that MAY have happened is a different fact from one that certainly did not, and only the first forbids a retry.

Two orderings that are load-bearing. Deny-before-validation: an operation the capability never declared is refused without consulting the registry or touching params — those params are not input this node should be parsing. And params are validated BEFORE the row exists, because a recorded proposal is something the owner may be asked to permit, and it should never be possible to be asked about params nobody checked.

Schema digests pinned at proposal time. The registry's schemas change when an adapter updates. A proposal permitted under one contract must be verified under that same contract, or an update silently reinterprets a decision the owner already made. A result that violates the pinned schema is recorded failed and the drifted payload never lands.

Refusals are recorded, cancellation is bounded. A silent drop leaves the runner retrying for ever and the owner with no record of a decision they made. A runner may withdraw before a decision but never after execution begins — an effect that may already have happened cannot be un-asked.

PART, and the honest gap is the one this work keeps finding: NOTHING CALLS IT. There is no typed host operation to execute (3.5), no owner-facing permit surface, and no sweeper wired over listExecuting(). I am recording that plainly rather than marking DONE, because "built and unreachable" has been the defect in seven of the eleven items before this one. Verification: 13 tests, 7 mutations, all caught (one of my first mutations was a no-op — an appended comment rather than a behaviour change — and was redone). Tests: 6,385 core, 2,839 server, 3,293 mobile; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-3.5 — typed host operations (part)

One sentence is the whole rule: the runner supplies the PARAMS, Dina supplies the AUTHORITY. That is enforced structurally rather than by checking — the context an executor receives carries the validated params and the asking install's identity, and has no field for a credential, a session, or a key. A runner cannot smuggle authority through a payload because there is nowhere to put it. Everything an executor needs in order to ACT is closed over at composition time by the boot that owns those things.

A thrown executor settles outcome_unknown, never failed. A socket can die after the bytes have left, so a throw cannot distinguish "never happened" from "happened and the wire died". Calling it failed invites a retry that sends twice. The asymmetry is deliberate and costed: a false "unknown" costs an operator a manual check, a false "failed" costs a duplicate effect. Executors that CAN characterise a failure return failed themselves and stay retryable.

"Bounded" is a security property, not a performance note. An unbounded search lets a runner page an entire index through a channel the owner approved for one lookup. The cap is applied in Core after the search returns, and the request carries no limit field at all — so there is nothing for a runner to widen.

Mutation distinguished a layered pair from dead code, again. The dispatcher's `permitted` pre-check survived deletion, because the broker's own CAS catches the same case. Defeating BOTH lets an unpermitted proposal execute (11 tests red), so they are layered rather than duplicated: the pre-check reports which state blocked it and avoids parsing params for a proposal that cannot run, the CAS makes two concurrent dispatchers resolve to one winner. Kept and documented, where the redundant buyer check in WS-4.3 was deleted — the difference is whether removing both changes behaviour.

PART: only the bounded search exists. D2D send, publication candidate and connector broker are not written, and no boot registers an executor — so 3.4 and 3.5 are now reachable from each other but still not from a running node. Verification: 9 new tests (22 in the file), 8 mutations caught. Tests: 6,394 core, 2,839 server, 3,293 mobile; typecheck and lint clean.

Composing the host-operation plane, before it became the eighth orphan (3.4/3.5)

I had just built another unreachable pair. The broker and the dispatcher reached each other and nothing else. That is the defect this work has found in seven of the thirteen preceding items, and I was one commit from adding an eighth — so I stopped and wired it rather than moving to WS-3.7.

One helper, both boots. createPluginHostRuntime builds the broker and the dispatcher already knowing each other, and both storage inits call it where they already composed the operation registry. The alternative — an option each composition root remembers to pass — is precisely how the provider-ingress bridge, the commerce engines, and the epoch service each came to exist with no caller.

And a static guard, because a convention is not a mechanism. Nothing outside the owning modules may construct either class; a boot or route that builds its own gets a differently-wired pair and nobody notices until an effect goes missing. Proven non-vacuous by dropping an offending file in and watching the guard name it.

3.4 and 3.5 are now composed on a running node. Still open and recorded as such: no owner-facing permit surface, no sweeper over listExecuting(), no runner path that calls propose, and only the bounded search operation exists. Verification: 3 new tests (25 in the file), boundary guard proven non-vacuous. Tests: 6,397 core, 2,839 server, 3,293 mobile; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-3.7 — the update-rebind coordinator (part)

A plugin update is three writes that must be one. Prior-CID authorizations, the install's new CID, and every listing pinned to the old CID. Each pair being out of step is an ordinary interleaving, not a crash, and each has a distinct cost: a CID that moves without its listings takes the supplier silently off the market (ingress refuses every query as binding_stale); listings that move without the CID fail the same way from the other side; a CID that moves without authorizations terminalizes in-flight tasks and stops a buyer's open order being answerable. So the unit of change is the transaction.

The claim is testable because the two tables share a database. service_configs and plugin_installs both live in Tier-0, so the listing rewrite is synchronous SQL inside the install's own transaction rather than a second write hoping to land. Tests drive real SQLite for exactly this reason — a pair of in-memory maps would let the atomicity claim be made without being earned.

Design decision: the in-memory listing cache adopts only after commit. service_config answers reads from a process-local map. Updating that map inside a transaction that can still roll back produces the same split-brain the transaction exists to prevent, pointing the other way. So the rewrite returns a committer and the coordinator runs it once the rows are durable. A new adoptServiceConfigInMemory exists for this and deliberately skips validation: the caller has already committed the row, and re-validating here could reject a listing the database has accepted, leaving memory and disk disagreeing with no way back.

Authorizations carry the PRIOR schemas, not the current ones. A drained task is judged against the contract it was created under. Reading the live manifest at claim time would let an update retroactively change what an in-flight task was allowed to return. The test fixture's new manifest renames both schema fields so this is visible rather than asserted.
