Two entries per prior capability, not one: drain covers tasks already claimed and expires; lifecycle_continuity admits NEW prior-major lifecycle calls and has no expiry, because §9.13 serves them until the orders are terminal and no clock knows when that is. It is released explicitly.

A mutation that survived was worth more than the six that died. Making the rebind publish to the cache inside the transaction changed nothing, because the only rollback any test exercised (a lost CAS) aborts before the rebind runs. The discipline was untested for the ordering where it actually matters. Adding "the transaction fails AFTER the rebind" killed it. Seven mutations verified in total; one earlier attempt was a no-op I caught by asserting the substitution count rather than trusting a green run.

Tradeoff: stores resolve per use rather than at construction. The install registry and the drain store are wired at different points of different boots — installs in storage/init.ts, drains in boot_service.ts on mobile. A coordinator that captured them would hold a null on whichever boot wires them later. Resolving per use and refusing stores_unavailable when either is missing removes the boot-order coupling entirely, and matches the decision already taken for the supplier DID.

Open, and stated plainly: nothing calls this yet. applyUpdate and setPendingUpdate had no production caller before this work — the owner-facing update discovery and consent flow has never been built, and that flow is the real owner of the boot wiring. I chose not to install a global singleton no boot reads, because a wired orphan is still an orphan. Also open: the Tier-0 runner is re-entrant, so calling apply from inside a larger transaction would let the cache adopt a rebind the outer transaction could still roll back — documented on the class, not defended in code. Verification: 11 tests, 7 mutations killed, typecheck and lint clean.

WS-3.8 — prior-major lifecycle continuity (part)

The spec is unusually direct here, and it is worth quoting the shape: a lifecycle request names a purchase order, Core reads that order's pinned major from the order-reference record and dispatches to that major's retained handler set, and "the current negotiation manifest is never consulted." So the routing key is the ORDER, not the listing. After an update the listing has already been rebound to the new CID (WS-3.7) — following it would answer a buyer under a contract their order was never opened against.

Deviation: I pin by the serving MANIFEST CID, not by the protocol major. The spec says major; the drain-authorization table is keyed (install_id, prior CID, capability); and a plugin manifest declares no protocol major anywhere, so there is nothing to derive a major from without inventing a new manifest field and a mapping that could disagree with the CID. A CID implies exactly one major, so pinning by CID is strictly finer-grained and uses the key both sides already share. `commerce_order_refs` gains `serving_manifest_cid`; "released per major once its last order is terminal" becomes "per prior CID". Flagging this as the one place I departed from the letter of §9.13.

Design decision: the serving CID is a CALLER-supplied admission input, not a node-wide setting. A node may run several supplier plugins. A single "current manifest" read at admission would stamp an order with whichever plugin happened to be asked about, and route its later lifecycle requests into another plugin's schemas — a correctness bug, not merely over-refusal. It is optional because a supplier need not be plugin-backed at all; absent, the order records '' and no prior-manifest routing applies, which is right, since nothing served it under a contract that could be superseded. Re-adopted orders (§16.2, rebuilt from a counterparty's held evidence) record '' deliberately: the evidence names the protocol version but not the manifest, and guessing the current one would claim a contract this node cannot vouch for.

Every authority field on a continuity envelope comes from the authorization. Params schema, result schema, approved scope hash, action class, effects idempotency, context bound — all recorded at rebind from the prior manifest. The claim guard performs the mirror check and re-derives the same fields from the same row, so the two sides cannot drift. A capability that has left the current manifest entirely is still served, which is the whole point.

Refusing is the honest failure. When no continuity lane was retained, ingress returns a typed lifecycle_continuity_unavailable rather than falling back to the current manifest. A fallback would parse the buyer's request against a contract their order was never opened under, and "answered wrongly" is worse than "not answered". A drain entry alone does not admit a NEW lifecycle request — drain covers work already claimed at the rebind moment; only continuity admits new arrivals.

A schema guard caught my fixture, again. Six tests failed with install_unavailable after an update that reported success. The cause: rowToInstall quarantines any row whose current_version column disagrees with its pinned manifest's own version, and my fixture bumped the column while reusing a manifest that still said 0.1.0. The guard was right and the fixture was wrong — the third hand-built plugin fixture this work has gotten wrong, which is itself the argument for how strict these mappers are.

Open: nothing yet calls releaseContinuity when the last prior-manifest order goes terminal — that needs a non-terminal count per CID, and the store today counts by protocol major (countReservedByMajor). And no submit_order wire path exists, so outside tests every order records an empty serving CID and the continuity branch is unreachable in production until that path is built. Verification: 6 new tests (28 in the file), 6 mutations killed, typecheck and lint clean.

Deep review of 3.7 / 3.8, and closing the release gap (§9.13)

Three things the review found in my own code, all fixed. A duplicated priorCid !== '' && priorCid !== install.currentCid that two branches could drift apart on — now named needsPriorManifest once. An as PluginTaskEnvelope['action_class'] cast that was pure noise, because the envelope field is already string. And the real one: nothing ever released a continuity lane.

Releasing a lane early is worse than never releasing it. §9.13 closes a lane "once its last order is terminal" — a clock cannot know that, and neither could my first version, which released on request. If it fires while an order is open, that buyer's next order_status is refused for an order the supplier is still obliged to. So releaseContinuity now consults a count and returns { released, openOrders }: it refuses while any order that manifest served is still open, and says how many, so the caller can retry after the next settles.

The count is keyed on the manifest, not the major. countReservedByServingManifest(cid) joins the two halves — the order records which manifest served it, the authorization is keyed on that same CID. The pre-existing countReservedByMajor could not do this job: it counts protocol majors, and a manifest declares none. The counter is INJECTED into the coordinator rather than imported, because a plugin coordinator has no business reaching into the order store; an absent counter reads as zero, so a node with no commerce runtime can still close a lane it could never have used.

The scenario the goal asked for. Added to the retailer/manufacturer journey: Sancho orders while ChairMaker runs manifest 1; ChairMaker ships manifest 2 with an order still open; the lane REFUSES to close and reports one order outstanding; the order settles; the lane closes and the authorization is gone. It runs on both the in-memory and SQLite backends, and it is the first test that walks 3.7 and 3.8 together as one story rather than as two mechanisms.

Verification: 2 further mutations killed (release ignoring the count; an absent counter treated as blocking). Full gate: core 6,417, server 2,839, typecheck and lint clean. One open edge remains and is unchanged: no submit_order wire path exists, so outside tests every order records an empty serving CID and the continuity branch is unreachable in production until that path is built.

The submit_order wire path (§9.9, part)

This closes the gap that made everything above test-only. Until now no production code ever wrote an order reference, so serving_manifest_cid was always empty and no lifecycle request could ever route to a prior manifest — the §9.13 machinery was correct and unreachable. Provider ingress now admits an inbound order in compiled Core BEFORE any runner is asked, stamping the install's current CID as the manifest that served it.

Where the authority line falls, and why there. Idempotency, quote capacity and the reservation record are Core's; whether to take the business is the plugin's. A plugin that could accept twice, or accept against capacity Core had already spent, would be committing the owner to something with no durable record behind it. So a replayed submission returns its original acknowledgement and a refusal is Core's to make — neither reaches the plugin, which is what stops a buyer's retry storm from becoming a dispatch storm on the runner lane.

No commerce runtime fails closed (commerce_unavailable) rather than dispatching unadmitted. That is the same reasoning as the store guard in the rebind coordinator: the partial state where an effect happens without its record is the one worth refusing outright.

Two of the four mutations crashed instead of surviving, which is not evidence and I have now been caught by it enough times to check the count every run. Replacing a condition with if (false) makes the following code unreachable and the suite reports Tests: 0 total. Redone shape-preserving — emptying the capability set, and changing the refusal's code rather than deleting the branch — both died properly.

Open: the runner's accept/reject answer is not yet fed back into decideOrder. Admission reserves and dispatches; closing the round trip is the next seam, and until it lands an accepted order still needs the owner or a test to call decideOrder directly. Verification: 9 new tests (35 in the file), 4 mutations killed; core 6,424, server 2,839, typecheck and lint clean.

Closing the order round trip (§9.9, part)

A buyer must never receive the runner's JSON. An acknowledgement is a signed commercial record: it settles the quote hold, opens the status chain, and becomes the receipt both sides reconcile against later. Bridging the plugin's raw answer would hand the buyer a claim nothing signed, leave the hold unsettled, and leave no chain to ask about. So the runner DECIDES and Core RECORDS — transformInboundOrderResult intercepts a completed submit_order and replaces the result with what decideOrder produced.

Design decision: an unreadable answer leaves the order UNDECIDED. Every coercion available here — treating an unknown shape as acceptance, or as rejection — is a guess about whether a supplier meant to commit. The safe reading of "I cannot tell" is to record nothing and let the decision sweeper own it. The tests assert that no decision reaches the engine on any malformed answer, which is the part worth pinning: the refusal is only meaningful if it also declines to write.

Two failure directions, deliberately asymmetric. If the seam throws, the runner's own answer is sent — a completion that already landed must not vanish because the transform failed. If the seam runs but Core cannot record the decision, the runner's answer is likewise sent, and the order stays open. Neither case fabricates an acknowledgement.

The buyer is the authenticated sender, always. The purchase order id comes from the dispatched params but the buyer DID comes from the transport-authenticated envelope, so a runner naming someone else's buyer cannot redirect a decision onto an order it was never asked about. Mutation-verified by making it prefer the params field.

Tradeoff: the workflow layer gained a hook rather than an import. ingressResultTransformer is injected, so the workflow layer still knows nothing about commerce — it owns only the ordering, transform then send. It is wired once in workflow_plane.ts, which both boots go through, rather than at each boot: the option every root must remember is the option one of them forgets.

Correction, same session: I first refused counterproposal on the grounds that Core could not verify a replacement quote. That was wrong — decideOrder already checks counter lineage (§9.9) and registers the quote through the family, which is exactly where §9.8 audience binding is enforced. So all three §9.9 outcomes are now carried; the seam checks shape only, because a second opinion here could only disagree with the one that counts. Verification: 23 tests in the file, 6 mutations killed; core 6,447, server 2,839. Mobile: one failure on the first run that I could not reproduce in two subsequent clean runs — I could not capture which test, so it is recorded as an unexplained flake rather than a pass.

WS-1.8 — the catalog publication wire contract (§10.2/§10.3, part)

The spec states one trust chain and the module is that chain: supplier repo proof → current snapshot pointer → immutable snapshot metadata → canonical payload root → bounded catalog pages. Every hop recomputes, which is what makes the feed host transport rather than authority — a modified page fails its digest, a swapped payload fails the root, a forged pointer fails the chain.

Design decision: catalog commitments are NOT §9.12 digest domains. §9.12 fixes ten record digests and the frozen conformance vectors pin every one; that list is a closed vocabulary for negotiation and lifecycle RECORDS. A page digest and a payload root are content commitments over published bytes — a different thing with a different lifetime. Adding them to the frozen list would have broken the vectors and quietly widened a deliberately closed set, so they get their own dina:commerce:catalog:v1: prefix and a test asserts the two domains cannot collide.

Tradeoff: an ordered commitment, not a Merkle tree. A tree would let a verifier check one page against the root without fetching the others — a real benefit, but only if inclusion proofs ship with it, and §10.2 bounds catalogs precisely so a consumer CAN fetch every page. Publishing a tree without proofs would advertise a guarantee the wire format does not carry. A tree stays additive if paging ever outgrows the bound.

Two mutations SURVIVED, and both found something real. Sorting the page digests before committing broke no test — the payload root's order-sensitivity was undefended, even though the docstring claimed order was committed. And removing page_index from the page digest broke nothing either, which exposed a wrong claim in my own comment: I had described the index binding as closing a reordering attack. It does not. Pages with different content already have different digests, so a reorder of those is caught by content alone; pages with identical content are interchangeable by definition, so swapping them changes nothing a consumer could observe. The binding is a correctness property — a page record cannot be served from a slot it does not claim — and the comment and test now say exactly that instead of overclaiming.

Update, same session: a PUBLISHER now exists (commerce/catalog_publisher.ts), so the contract has a producer. It runs the CONSUMER's own verifyCatalogPointerAdvance on its own output — one rule, checked by the same code on both sides, so a producer cannot drift from the verifier and a fork is refused before anything reaches the wire. Two further decisions the tests forced: an over-large catalog is REFUSED rather than truncated (a silently shortened full-state snapshot omits products with nothing in the record to say so), and WITHDRAWAL IS TERMINAL for a catalog_id — a tombstone names no snapshot, so nothing can link to it, and relaunch means a new id. That last one only surfaced because a test tried to publish after withdrawing and got a confusing digest-mismatch error; the rule is now explicit with its own message.

§10.3 feed hardening, built as PURE POLICY. A catalog feed URL is supplier-controlled input aimed at our own network stack — the classic SSRF shape, and the spec says to treat it as hostile. The module performs no I/O, deliberately: the dangerous part of a fetcher is the decision, not the socket, and a decision function can be tested against every address form an attacker would try. Covered: HTTPS-only as an ALLOW-list (not a deny-list — file:, gopher: and friends are all ways to read something local), credentials-in-URL refused, private/loopback/link-local/CGNAT/multicast literals blocked in v4 and v6 including the IPv4-mapped form, redirect targets re-validated exactly as the original, a content-type allow-list, and BOTH a decompression ratio and an absolute cap because neither implies the other. Anything it cannot parse is blocked rather than guessed at.

The limit of it, stated in the module itself: a URL check cannot stop DNS rebinding. A name that resolves publicly at validation can resolve to 169.254.169.254 at connect. So isBlockedAddress must ALSO run against the address actually connected to, on every hop, and a fetcher that only validates the URL has not implemented this policy. A mutation taught me something here too: killing the IPv4-mapped unwrap broke only one test, because a real URL parser normalises [::ffff:127.0.0.1] to the hex form and the conservative fallback catches that instead. Both forms are now pinned separately, since different code blocks each.

Update, same session: an INGESTER now consumes all of it (commerce/catalog_ingest.ts), so neither the policy nor the verifiers can be routed around. The design point: the injected transport is DUMB. A transport that took a URL and returned bytes would let a caller fetch first and ask questions after — and the interesting failure is exactly the request that should never have been made. So the ingester clears the URL before the first call, hands the transport something already approved, and requires it to report the address it ACTUALLY connected to so that can be re-checked on every hop. The pointer stays the authority: a snapshot that is internally perfect but is not the one the pointer names is refused, because substituting one publication for another is what the pointer exists to stop. Tests drive the real publisher's output through the ingester, so the two halves check each other rather than fixtures either invented. Open: the AppView side (Jetstream ingest, PostgreSQL projection, xRPC search) is unbuilt — this is the verification pipeline, not the index.

Deep review of the catalog work found two real gaps, both now fixed. CATALOG_FEED_LIMITS.maxMillis was declared and never read — a dead constant that made the module LOOK like it enforced a time cap. And there was no aggregate byte budget: a snapshot may legitimately name a thousand pages, so "each page is under 8MB" is not a limit on anything an attacker cares about — a compliant feed could cost gigabytes. Both are now one budget shared across the whole ingest, checked BEFORE each request so a spent budget stops the next call rather than paying for it first. A mutation confirms the budget is shared rather than per-fetch, which is the version of this bug that would look correct in review.

A third candidate I looked at and rejected: the snapshot rkey is the 64-char hex digest. That is a valid ATProto rkey (charset and length both fine) and it is content-derived as §10.2 requires, so there is nothing to fix — noting it because "looks unusual" is not the same as "is wrong", and the check was worth making.

Verification: 63 policy + 20 ingest tests, 12 mutations killed across the catalog work; core 6,543 green, typecheck and lint clean. §10.3's feed-fetch hardening (HTTPS only, bounded redirects, DNS re-resolution, private-address denial, byte/time/page caps, decompression limits, no ambient cloud credentials) is an AppView fetcher concern and is unbuilt; the bounds constants here are the wire half of it only. Verification: 27 tests, 5 mutations run — 3 killed outright, 2 survived and were fixed. commerce-protocol 246 tests green; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-0.7 — rehydration through the ingress validator (part)

The asymmetry this closes. A record arriving on the wire was validated before anything acted on it. The SAME record read back from the receipt store was JSON.parse(…) as PurchaseOrderProposal — a promise to the compiler and a check of nothing. Five sites did this, and every one of them fed decision logic written on the assumption that ingress had already vetted the value.

Why it is worth closing even though the store is not an attacker. This is an integrity problem, not a security one, and the difference is in how it fails. A receipt written by an older build, truncated by a half-finished migration, or corrupted on disk flows straight into a commercial decision — and the first symptom is a wrong outcome rather than an error. Re-running the validator turns that into a loud refusal at the point of reading. It matters that the validator RE-DERIVES the digest: a test states this explicitly by showing a tampered order that is structurally perfect, carries the original digest field, and is still refused.

Design decision: the helper returns the failure rather than throwing. Each caller knows what an unreadable record means in its own context, and they genuinely differ — the decision path reports a store-integrity error, loadRetainedQuote treats it as "not retained" because its contract is "the quote a digest names, or null", and the recovery sweeper SKIPS that row so one corrupt receipt cannot stop every other timed-out order from being decided. A throw would have flattened three correct behaviours into one.

Update, same session — the ingress casts are gone too. Rather than assertion functions (asserts value is T), which read badly at call sites and cannot be composed, the wire package gained readPurchaseOrderProposal / readSignedQuote: they validate and TYPE in one step, returning the record or the reason it is not one. The existing validate… functions delegate to them, so there is ONE implementation and the two shapes cannot drift about what "valid" means. Every as PurchaseOrderProposal / as SignedQuote in src/commerce is now gone.

A static guard keeps them gone, in boundary.test.ts, because the rule is about what a future caller COULD write and no behavioural test can express that. Proven non-vacuous by reintroducing a cast and watching it fail. One documented exception: order_decision.ts passes a counterproposal's replacement quote straight to decideOrder, which registers it through the quote family — the one gate that owns audience binding — and a second check there could only disagree with the one that counts.

The decisionOutcome default arm — and a claim I had to withdraw. I added it, wrote a comment saying held evidence comes from a counterparty so an unknown kind could reach the switch, and built a test to prove it. The test failed, and the reason is that the claim was false: every caller either builds the acknowledgement locally or runs validateOrderAcknowledgement first, which rejects an unknown kind before the switch ever sees it. The default arm is NOT reachable today. I deleted the test rather than contrive a path to it, and rewrote the comment to say what the guard is actually for: the protocol adding a FOURTH acknowledgement kind. The validator would accept it immediately, this switch would not, and without a default it returns undefined typed as a result — a silent wrong answer to a buyer, produced by a change in another package that compiles cleanly here. Worth keeping, worth describing accurately; a comment that oversells a guard is worse than no comment.

Verification: 9 rehydration tests + 1 static guard, 4 mutations killed; commerce-protocol 246, core 6,553, server 2,839, typecheck and lint clean. WS-0.7 DoD is now met.

Catalog conformance vectors, and a hash contract I got wrong (§25.1)

The catalog wire contract had no frozen vectors, so a Go or Swift port could implement §10.2 with no way to check it agreed. Added conformance/vectors/catalog.json: the three commitments plus five pointer-chain cases. The refusal STRING is pinned, not merely the fact of refusal — two implementations that both reject a rollback for differently-worded reasons diverge the first time an operator reads a log. Verified as a real gate by perturbing the frozen root and watching CI fail, then regenerating.

Building the vector exposed a genuine defect in my own catalog work. catalog_publication.ts had invented a second hash contract, (input: string) => string, in a package whose established Sha256Fn is (Uint8Array) => Uint8Array. Nothing failed while the only callers were tests I had written to match — but the generator, which uses the package's own fixture hash, could not call it at all. Two hash contracts inside a package whose entire job is byte-agreement is exactly the quiet inconsistency that makes a later port disagree. One contract throughout now, callers and fixtures aligned.

The lesson worth keeping: a vector is not only a compatibility artifact, it is a SECOND CALLER. Writing one made a design flaw visible that four passing test files had hidden, because those tests and the module had agreed on the wrong thing together. Verification: 4 new vector tests (commerce-protocol 250), core 6,553; typecheck and lint clean.

The vector generator was deleting frozen conformance cases (§25.1, fixed)

The most important thing I found this session, and I found it by accident. After adding new vectors I ran git status on a file I had not meant to touch and saw arithmetic.json modified: 79 lines deleted. The committed artifact held four §9.1 total-cases; generate.ts produced one. Three had been added by hand and the generator never taught them — so running the command the file's OWN HEADER documents silently destroyed charge_order_discount_first, charge_order_surcharge_first and final_total_negative_is_invalid. Those pin that a discount before a surcharge totals the same as the reverse, and that non-negativity is a property of the RESULT rather than of the running value.

The generator's header says "never regenerate to fix a red vector test — that is the drift the vectors exist to catch." The drift was already there, pointing the other way: the artifact was AHEAD of its generator, and the documented workflow was the thing that would lose it.

Fixed by COMPUTING the three cases, not transcribing them. A hand-copied expected value would have frozen my transcription instead of the implementation's behaviour — the same failure the vectors exist to prevent, one level up. So permute() runs computeTotal for each charge order and emits whatever comes back, and the negative case asserts the total is refused before emitting anything. Verified by regenerating twice and checking the result is semantically identical to the committed file (json.load equality, all four case names present).

One residual difference, stated rather than hidden: the committed arithmetic.json had no trailing newline and the generator adds one, as it does for every other vector. That is a whitespace normalisation which changes no vector content. I had flagged this generator as needing your decision; it did not — "the documented command destroys conformance cases" is tech-debt to fix, not a judgement call, and I should have just fixed it. Verification: commerce-protocol 273 (83 in the vector suite), core 6,553; typecheck and lint clean.

The conformance doc's vector inventory was unguarded (§25.1)

Adding three vectors left docs/conformance.md claiming three when six existed. Five tests already guard that document — the version, the pre-freeze declaration, the changelog, the exit criteria, the gaps paragraph — and none of them looked at the vector TABLE. So the file a port reads to learn what is covered understated coverage, silently, and only a human reading both would have noticed.

Fixed the table, then added the missing guard: compare it against the directory, in both directions. A missing row understates coverage; a row for a vector that no longer exists promises coverage the repository cannot deliver, which is the worse of the two.

My first version of that guard was wrong, and the mutation caught it. It searched the whole document for each filename and passed while the table was broken, because I had also mentioned units.json in the prose below. The comment said "in the table"; the code said "anywhere in the file". Rewritten to read only table rows, then verified against both failure directions — dropping a row fails, inventing one fails.

Worth naming the pattern, since it has now happened three times this session: a check I wrote, a comment I wrote to justify it, and a test I wrote to prove it can all agree with each other and still be wrong together. Mutating the thing under test is what separates them. Verification: commerce-protocol 274; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-2.5 + WS-2.6 + WS-1.6 — the fence path was a way around the deadline (§9.11, §16.2)

The restore fence is how a supplier that lost records rejoins a chain the buyer can still prove. It has a verifier on each side: the supplier signs one (signRestoreFence), the buyer accepts it (verifyRestoreFence). Reading them side by side, the two sides did not agree about anything.

The buyer enforced two rules the supplier did not. It checks the fenced fulfilment against the ORDER's accepted lines, and it checks the fence's state is legal from the predecessor. The supplier copied both forward verbatim and never loaded the order at all. That is not a harmless duplication gap: it means Core signs a record the buyer must reject, and the order strands — the precise outcome the fence exists to prevent. A restore is also the one moment the two halves can genuinely disagree, since the order reference and the status receipts are separate tables and can come back from different backup vintages.

The epoch bar had the same shape. Both sides require the fence to raise the epoch, but against different heads: ours locally, theirs at the buyer. After a restore the buyer's head sits ABOVE ours, on records we lost, and its epoch comes with it — so clearing our own bar says nothing about clearing theirs. The bar is now the higher of the local head and the highest epoch among the verified presented receipts, which for a conforming buyer is its head.

And NEITHER side applied the dispute deadline. verifyStatusSuccession refuses delivered → disputed once the window closes. verifyRestoreFence allowed the same move and never looked at the window, because it took no clock at all. A supplier could therefore dispute an order whose window shut months ago by marking the record restore_fence: true — a recovery mechanism used to escape a deadline, which is the shape of every privilege escalation. The verifier now takes the RECEIVER's at_iso, required rather than optional, because an omitted clock that skips the check reproduces exactly the bug. Recorded in the conformance changelog as breaking.

WS-2.5, found while in the file. loadHeadStatus proved the loaded record was the one the head NAMES and never that it belongs here. The receipt store is keyed by digest across every order this node has handled, so a head row pointing at another order's status loads clean, then drives transition legality and becomes the cumulative-lines floor for a successor signed on a DIFFERENT order. Now bound to buyer, order and supplier.

Mutation record. Ten run. Killed: fence lines check disabled; epoch bar dropping the presented receipts; each of the three identity fields separately; the deadline keyed on the wrong predecessor state; the deadline bound to the supplier's own updated_at; and all three >→>= boundary flips. Two were redone after landing as no-ops or crashes.

Two findings came out of mutations rather than review. The first >= flip SURVIVED: nothing pinned whether the deadline instant is inclusive, though THREE functions answer that question and a port reading them separately could implement two one way and the third the other — an order terminal on one side and still disputable on the other, over one millisecond. Now pinned in all three. The second: deleting the buyer_did and supplier_did comparisons both survived, because the one test I wrote exercised only the order id. Each is a distinct escape and each now has its own case.

One arm of the new fence check is INERT and says so in the code rather than looking like a guard doing work: the cumulative-regression comparison can never fail while the fence copies the predecessor's lines verbatim. It is passed anyway so the call stays byte-for-byte the buyer's, because §16.2 permits a fence to legally advance rather than restate, and on the day this engine takes that option the arm goes live with no edit. Verification: commerce-protocol 275, core 6,566, core-server 2,839; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-2.3 — a parked cancellation review could be closed but not resolved (§12.8)

When a cancellation cannot be decided immediately it parks as pending_review, and finalizePendingCancellation is the only way out. Finalizing to cancelled recorded a terminal result and moved nothing at all — no decision, no refund, no chain. Core told the buyer the order was cancelled while its own chain stayed live and could go on to sign dispatched, and the held quote capacity stayed held for good.

Worse, §12.8 REQUIRES a cancelled result to name the status head it ruled on, and that digest arrived as an optional caller parameter. Omit it and the record fails the buyer's own validator; pass the wrong one and the ruling is bound to a head this engine never ruled on. Either way the record was durable and terminal, so idempotency replayed it forever and finalization refused to reopen a review that was no longer pending. One missing field ended the conversation permanently.

A caller cannot supply a fact it does not own, so the parameter is gone and the digest comes from a head this path just wrote. A review can be parked in TWO places and each has its own winning shape: parked BEFORE genesis (reserved, external effect in flight) means decide, refund, sign the cancellation_won genesis; parked AFTER genesis (policy asked for a review) means transition the head to cancelled. Both now do exactly what the live path does for the same outcome, through one shared settlement rather than a second copy of sixty lines.

Why it went unnoticed: the one existing test finalized only to refused_already_dispatched, which correctly decides nothing. The whole defect lived in the branch no test entered — and writing the missing test immediately exposed a second parked shape I had not accounted for, so the first version of the fix would have broken the case the existing test covered.

Mutation record. Four run, three killed: recording a post-genesis win without moving the chain; recording a pre-genesis win without settling; binding the result to the wrong head. The fourth SURVIVED — deleting the new validate-before-write guard broke nothing, because with every field now derived there is no input left to be wrong. Kept anyway and labelled in the code as a backstop no current input reaches, since the bug it answers was a caller supplying a field it did not own, in a function that writes durable, terminal, unreopenable evidence.

A separate live defect fell out of chasing that survivor. versionMatches carried a pinnedVersion === '' → true hatch for "legacy rows carry no pin". No such row can exist (pinned_version is NOT NULL with no default, and the only writer stores the order's validated version), and if one appeared the hatch opened the wrong way: it DISABLED §9.13 pinning for precisely the row whose version nobody knows, and fed an empty protocol_version into every record built from it. Removed; an unknown pin now refuses. Verification: commerce-protocol 275, core 6,572, core-server 2,839; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-2.2 — the recovery sweeper was correct, unreachable, and leaked (§9.9 step 3)

recoverAdmissions() is what turns an abandoned pre_effect reservation into a real rejected(decision_timeout) and gives its quote capacity back. Nothing called it. The only callers in the whole repository were its own tests, so on a running node no reservation ever timed out: every abandoned order held capacity indefinitely and the buyer's order_reconcile answered received_processing for ever.

Inside it, a second leak. The sweeper loaded and re-validated the order proposal first and skipped the row if either step failed. That reads as prudence and has no floor: the row stays in the expired set, so it is reconsidered and skipped on every future sweep, and the hold never returns. Silently — the sweep reported only its successes, so an operator saw "0 timed out" whether nothing was due or everything was stuck.

A rejected(decision_timeout) needs nothing from the proposal. Its three fields are on the order reference, the order digest got there from admission so the record is still correctly bound, and the outcome commits to nothing commercially. So the sweeper now builds the acknowledgement from the reference and resolves the row instead of stepping over it. What it genuinely cannot resolve is reported as stuck, separately from errors: a sweep that finds nothing and a sweep that can fix nothing must never read the same.

The parameter type was part of the cause. buildAcknowledgement asked for a whole PurchaseOrderProposal while reading three fields of it, which is what made the sweeper believe it needed one. Narrowed to a Pick. A parameter type that overstates its needs teaches callers to fetch more than they must.

Wiring. A new CommerceAdmissionSweeper is started in wireWorkflowPlane, so both boots get it from one place rather than two copies that drift. It resolves the commerce runtime PER TICK instead of capturing it: commerce composes after storage, is absent on a node with no published epoch (§16.2), and is torn down on identity change, so "no commerce" is an ordinary quiet tick rather than a boot-order problem.

Mutation record. Three against the WIRING, all killed: constructing the sweeper without starting it, resolving the global to null, and deferring the first sweep to the interval. That last set is the point — a unit test of the sweeper would have passed happily while nothing constructed it, which is exactly how the engine method sat unreachable in the first place. So the wiring assertion runs against the REAL wireWorkflowPlane on a real boot.

Correcting the WBS. 3.6 claimed "both boots get the engines with no boot-side change". That is not true of mobile: apps/mobile/src/storage/init.ts only ever calls installCommerceRuntime(null) on teardown and never creates one, so commerce is server-only today. The sweeper is wired in the shared plane and will start working on mobile the day a runtime is composed there; recorded rather than quietly left as-is. Verification: commerce-protocol 275, core 6,584, core-server 2,841, home-node 27; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-2.7 — I tried to move `order_status` into Core and backed it out (§9.11, §9.13)

Recorded because the attempt is the finding, not because it shipped. The reasoning was: the status chain is supplier-signed and Core holds the key, so a runner asked for a status could at best return an unsigned claim about state. Core should answer it directly, and should do so before the plugin-binding checks — a buyer must be able to learn the state of an order this node already accepted even after the supplier uninstalls the plugin, or a supplier could strand a buyer by uninstalling.

It built cleanly, typechecked across the workspace, and broke six tests in the §9.13 prior-manifest routing block. Those tests were not stale: they encode a deliberate design from WS-3.8, and the thing they encode is what I had missed. The published SERVICE schema for `order_status` is the SUPPLIER's, declared in the manifest, and the buyer pinned it. Core answering with a shape of its own choosing returns something the pinned schema does not describe — and it removes the supplier's ability to enrich a status with data only it has, a carrier reference or a tracking link. §9.13's continuity lane exists precisely because the answer's SHAPE belongs to a manifest.

So the change was wrong and the tests were right. Reverted in full: the Core-answered route, the second success variant on ProviderIngressResult, the widened responder body, the rehydrateOrderStatus reader, and the narrowing helper. None of those pieces stands on its own once the route is gone, and leaving a built-but-uncalled reader behind would be the exact defect I spent WS-2.2 removing.

What I now think WS-2.7 actually is. Its DoD reads "subject-authorized read; non-buyer receives a non-disclosing rejection" — which is AUTHORIZATION, and WS-0.6 already delivered it. I read a handler into it that the spec does not ask for. The real concern underneath — a runner reporting a state Core never signed — is genuine but belongs on the completion path, where Core already validates the runner's result against the pinned schema and could additionally cross-check a claimed status against its own chain. That is a smaller, different change, and I have left it as an open item rather than smuggling it in here.

Worth naming: six failing tests were the only thing between a plausible argument and a wire-contract violation. The change had a clean typecheck, a clean lint, and a story I found convincing while writing it. Verification after revert: core commerce + ingress 407, typecheck clean, no residue of the reverted route in any file.

WS-3.9 — Buyer and Supplier manifests + the runner SDK (§6, §8.1, §8.2)

Two reference manifests and the SDK a runner uses to claim work and answer it. The manifests live in src/, not in a test: a manifest is a published contract, and a pair that only existed inside a .test.ts is a pair nothing else could install, review or diff — the scenario would pass while the artefact §24 asks for did not exist.

The validator found four things I had invented. action_class: 'commit' and privacy_class: 'business' are not words the §5 vocabularies contain; capability ids must be reverse-DNS, and the regex allows hyphens but not underscores, so com.dinakernel.commerce.submit_order is rejected while …submit-order is fine; and a runner capability must declare effects.idempotency. None of that was visible by reading — only by asking the validator, which is why the test runs the real one rather than checking fields by hand.

The one interesting declaration is the buyer's place_order: idempotency: 'unsupported'. Placing an order is an effect on ANOTHER party's node, and §15.5 asks that party to deduplicate — but this buyer cannot verify that they do. Declaring supported would authorise Core to auto-retry on the strength of a promise nobody here can keep, and a duplicated order is exactly the harm §12.7 exists to prevent. unsupported means an interrupted attempt surfaces as outcome_unknown and is reconciled honestly rather than replayed hopefully.

The SDK is explicitly NOT a security boundary and says so at the top of the file. A runner is out-of-process, holds its own key and can call WorkflowService directly; Core re-validates at the bridge regardless. It exists so the correct path is the easy one and so a runner's own mistake surfaces where the runner can act on it, rather than as a rejection the buyer sees.

I made the exact bug the SDK exists to prevent, while writing it. The first version validated params against schema_snapshot.params. That field does not exist — the envelope's schema_snapshot IS the capability's result schema, which the claim guard enforces by canonical comparison. So the check read undefined, skipped itself, and looked like a guard while being nothing. The real claim guard caught it. Params turn out to be validated twice inside the trust boundary already (at enqueue and at claim check 3g), so the SDK now does not re-check them and says why.

A second finding, from a test that would not pass. The SDK originally swallowed the claim guard's terminalized list, so a runner whose lane is quiet because its work is being REJECTED looked identical to one with nothing to do. Now reported on every claim outcome — the difference between diagnosing a consent mismatch in a minute and staring at an empty queue.

A theme worth naming, since this is the third time today. The SDK's malformed-envelope branch is unreachable: the claim guard parses with the same function and terminalizes first. Rather than delete it (the parser returns | null, so it must be handled) or leave a passing test implying the path is live, the code says it is unreachable and the test asserts what actually happens. Same treatment as the inert lines arm in the restore fence and the validate-before-write backstop in cancellation. Mutations: 4 run, 4 killed — disabling the result check, failing a job instead of leaving it claimable, collapsing outcome_unknown into a plain failure, and swallowing the terminalized list. Verification: core 6,605, core-server 2,841; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-11.3 — the retailer→manufacturer journey, through the plugin lane (§25.3)

procurement_lane_scenario.test.ts runs an order the way a real one goes: inbound query → provider ingress → Core admits in compiled code → task on the install's private lane → ChairMaker's runner claims through the SDK and answers → Core replaces that answer with the acknowledgement IT signed → Sancho receives it. Every seam is the production one, including the WS-3.9 reference manifest, which is installed rather than stubbed — if that pack could not be installed the journey could not start.

Seven scenarios: the happy path, a decline, a replayed submission, an order against spent capacity, outcome_unknown, a runner answer the pack's own schema rejects, and a stranger probing an order Sancho owns.

It immediately found a real defect in what I shipped an hour earlier. My supplier manifest declared the decision field as decision; Core's seam (readDecision) reads kind. Nothing local objected — the manifest validated, the SDK accepted an answer shaped to it, the schema check passed. The failure appeared only at the end of the journey, where the buyer received the runner's raw words instead of a signed acknowledgement and the order was left undecided. A conforming supplier plugin could never have completed a sale.

The rename alone would have been a patch, so the fix is structural: readDecision is now exported as readSupplierDecision — the shapes it accepts ARE the plugin contract, not test plumbing, and keeping it private is what let the two sides disagree with nothing able to notice. A test in reference_manifests.test.ts now drives the real reader against the manifest's declared enum, including the shape the manifest USED to declare, so the guard is a guard rather than two files matching strings.

One assertion of mine was wrong and worth recording: I expected the runner's supplier_order_id to be scrubbed from the wire. It is not, and should not be — §9.10 carries it because only the supplier knows it. The property that matters is narrower: every field carrying AUTHORITY (both DIDs, the order digest, the accepted quote digest, and the acknowledgement digest itself) comes from Core's record, and validateOrderAcknowledgement re-derives that digest. A runner cannot fabricate it, which is what makes it safe to let one answer on this path at all.

An assertion also had to be rewritten because the commerce runtime hides the quote ledger on purpose (ARCH-0a): "did the declined order's capacity come back" is now answered by whether a second order can be admitted against a one-use quote, not by peeking at a counter. The boundary pushing back on a test is the boundary working. Verification: core 6,613, core-server 2,841; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-5.3 — the publication leakage gate (§12.1, §23)

The last thing between a supplier's private data and a permanent public record. This gate is different in kind from every other validator in the pack: a rejected order can be resubmitted and a bad quote expires, but a catalog snapshot is published to a PDS, indexed by an AppView, and content-addressed — it does not un-publish. A credential that reaches one is disclosed permanently, to everyone. So a false negative costs unboundedly and a false positive costs a supplier renaming a column, and every judgement call below goes the refusing way.

Two independent rules, and neither subsumes the other. A closed public-field vocabulary stops internal_cost, supplier_notes and erp_connection by not KNOWING them — which is why it survives a field name nobody has thought of yet. A secret-shaped-value detector then covers the residual case the vocabulary cannot reach: a token pasted into description, which is a field a catalog is supposed to have. Rule 1 alone publishes a key in a legal field; rule 2 alone publishes a cost column that looks like nothing in particular.

The gate lives INSIDE `buildCatalogSnapshot`, before pagination and before any digest. A gate a caller must remember to run is a gate the next caller will miss, and this is the one place in the pack where being missed is unrecoverable. Running it there also means a refused catalog leaves no computed artefact a later caller could mistake for a publishable one.

Findings name the FIELD and never the value. An error message is written to a log, and a log is one more place a secret can come to rest — echoing it would turn one leak into two. A mutation that appended the offending value to the detail was killed by a test that greps the whole verdict for the secret.

Design decision worth your eye — `sku` and `mpn` are in the vocabulary. Adding the gate immediately failed twelve existing catalog tests whose fixtures use a bare sku. That is a real gap in my list, not a fixture problem: a SKU is printed on the packaging. But it IS a second way to say what product: {scheme, value} says canonically (§9.3), which is the sort of duplication that later diverges. I admitted both rather than force every honest CSV import to fail for a reason unrelated to leakage, and I am recording the tension rather than pretending it is resolved. If you would rather the canonical form be the only one, that is a one-line change here plus an import mapping.

Open question: list_price is currently publishable. My reading is that an advertised list price is the supplier's own choice to disclose, and a real price still comes from a signed quote (§9.8) — so this is advertising, not a term. If §14.3 competitor-resistance should keep even list prices off a public index, remove the three price fields and the gate does the rest.

Six mutations, six killed: removing the gate from the publisher; opening the vocabulary; emptying the pattern list; checking only the top level; truncating the report silently; echoing the value into the finding. 26 new tests. Verification: core 6,639; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-5.2 — supplier catalog import (§12.1, FR-S2, FR-S3)

A supplier's catalog already exists, in a spreadsheet. Any design that starts by asking them to re-enter it in a form gets a ten-row catalog for the demo and nothing afterwards — so the importer takes the CSV they have and tells them exactly what is wrong with it.

One rule shapes the whole module: a bad row is never dropped. The tempting behaviour — import what parses, skip the rest, report a count — is wrong in a way that is hard to see. A catalog snapshot is FULL STATE (§10.2): publishing it says "this is everything I offer". A supplier whose 40-row file imported 37 has published a catalog that silently omits three products, and nothing in the record says so. Buyers do not see an error; they see a supplier who does not stock the thing. So an import yields every row or none, and the findings name which rows and why. Unknown COLUMNS are reported for the same reason — a column silently ignored is a column the supplier believes they published.

Two judgement calls that look like fussiness and are not. Whitespace is trimmed but case is NOT folded: a trailing space is a spreadsheet artefact and refusing it would be hostile for no safety gain, while a vocabulary that folded case would have two spellings for one unit — and two spellings is how implementations come to disagree about how much was ordered. And defaultScheme is REQUIRED rather than defaulted, because guessing between gtin and sku picks the product's IDENTITY, and §9.4 forbids merging identity by name.

Variant parents resolve after every row is read, so a spreadsheet listing a variant above its parent imports cleanly. Refusing on row order would be an accident of how the supplier sorted their file, not a fact about their catalog.

The importer does NOT check for leakage, deliberately. buildCatalogSnapshot owns that gate and owns it alone, so there is one place a leak can be stopped and no second copy to drift from it. A test pins the division: a secret in a KNOWN column (`description`) passes import and is refused at publication. Two gates for one rule is two places to disagree.

Six mutations, six killed: partial import; ignoring unknown columns; merging duplicate identities; opening the unit vocabulary; ignoring unit scale; resolving every variant parent. Two initially crashed on unreachable-code type errors and were redone shape-preserving. 17 tests; one of my own cases was wrong (I expected a trailing space to be refused). Verification: core 6,656; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-6.1 + 6.2 — hard filters, then a deterministic score (§13.2–§13.4, FR-B6, FR-B7)

How Sancho picks between the quotes he collected. Two stages, and the ORDER is the rule: hard filters remove offers that do not meet the stated requirements, and only what survives is scored. §13.2 is explicit about this and the reason is not tidiness — a weighted score can always be dragged across a hard requirement by making one factor extreme enough. A requirement that participates in a score is not a requirement. The test that pins it gives a FREE offer that cannot supply the quantity and checks it never appears at all.

No LLM in the arithmetic path (FR-B6). Integer basis points throughout, ties broken on quote id rather than arrival order, and the score recomputable from the components the buyer was shown. A model may write the requirements and read the explanation back; it never touches the comparison, because a ranking that cannot be recomputed is one nobody can dispute.

Every removal is reported. "Why isn't ChairMaker here" is the first question anyone asks of a shortlist, and a comparison that cannot answer it is one the buyer cannot trust. Currency is never converted to make an offer comparable — a rate applied here would be this node inventing a commercial term. An unreadable expiry reads as EXPIRED, not as "no expiry".

§13.4, the rule I care most about here: missing data is explained, never substituted. A new supplier has no PeerLens history. Scoring that absence as zero makes the trust system a barrier to entry; scoring it as full marks rewards having no history at all. So the factor is left out of THAT offer's score, the weight actually applied travels with the score, and the omission is named. Two tests hold both failure directions: a newcomer must not lose to a badly-rated incumbent on identical price and lead time, and must not beat an excellent one either.

Design decision, and I got it wrong first. Price and lead time are RELATIVE — "cheapest" is only meaningful against the other offers, so they normalise across the shortlist. Trust is ABSOLUTE: 4000 means the same thing whoever else happened to quote, and normalising it would stretch the least-bad supplier in a weak field up to full marks, telling the buyer that a mediocre record is excellent because the competition was worse. My own test expected the top offer to score 10000, which only holds if trust is normalised too. The consequence is worth knowing when reading a score: an offer winning every relative factor still cannot reach 10000 unless its rating already is.

Open question. The weights (price 60%, lead time 25%, trust 15%) are fixed in v1 rather than buyer-tunable. Tunable weights are a real feature and also the thing that would make two Dinas rank the same offers differently — so they wait until there is somewhere to record the chosen weights alongside the decision. Say if you would rather have them configurable now.

Six mutations, six killed: scoring before filtering; a missing rating scored as zero; trust normalised like price; ties broken on input order; the score not normalised by the weight applied; removals dropped silently. Two needed redoing shape-preserving. 24 tests. Verification: core 6,680; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-5.9 — bounded quote fan-out (§12.3, §20.17)

A buyer asks "who can supply 500 oak chairs". The obvious implementation queries every supplier the index returns, which turns ONE local action into N outbound D2D messages to N independent nodes. At that point the buyer's Dina is a traffic amplifier: one tap produces load proportional to how many suppliers exist, and a peer repeating it in a loop has a distributed flood with someone else's name on the envelopes.

So the cap is a hard ceiling, not a default. A caller asking for more is CLAMPED rather than refused — refusing tempts a caller into a workaround, and the workaround will not have a cap at all. The amplification test runs the planner at 10, 100, 1,000 and 10,000 candidates and asserts the outbound count never moves.

One query per SUPPLIER, not per listing. Six listings from one manufacturer would otherwise take six of the eight slots, turning a cap meant to protect the network into one that narrows the buyer's comparison.

Trust orders but never excludes. A supplier nobody has rated yet sorts last and still gets asked if there is room. Excluding them at fan-out would make the trust system self-fulfilling — no queries, so no orders, so no reviews, so no trust, so no queries — and that is a barrier to entry dressed as a safety feature. Selection is deterministic down to the DID, so the buyer's fan-out is never a function of someone else's pagination.

A mutation showed one of my guards was doing nothing. Replacing Math.max(0, …) with a huge negative bound broke no test: a negative cap already selects nothing, because length >= cap is true from the start. Rather than delete the clamp or leave it looking load-bearing, I found what it actually protects — the REPORTED capApplied, which a caller puts in front of the owner when explaining a short shortlist, and which should not read -1. Now asserted, and the code says which half is behavioural and which is presentational.

Six mutations, six killed after that fix: ceiling downgraded to a default; negative cap unbounded; per-listing fan-out; the buyer querying itself; ordering falling back to input order; unrated suppliers excluded rather than sorted last. 17 tests. Verification: core 6,697; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-6.6 — competitor and probing resistance (§14.3, §20.10)

A competitor does not need to breach anything to hurt a supplier: they need only ask. A hundred quote requests at varying quantities draw the price curve, the volume breaks, and which products can actually be sourced. Every individual answer was legitimate — the harm is in the aggregate, which is why no single-request check can see it.

Two defences, answering different halves. A per- counterparty BUDGET over a window is the only thing that sees the aggregate. A UNIFORM refusal stops the refusal itself becoming the oracle — without it a prober stops reading the answers and starts reading the shape of the "no", learning the catalog before the budget even runs out. A budget with distinguishable refusals leaks early; uniform refusals with no budget leak to anyone patient. Neither is the other's fallback.

The wire refusal is a single constant, not a message built per case. Two refusals differing by one word are two a prober can tell apart, and every "helpful" detail — which product, whose budget, what window — is a bit of the catalog handed over. The real reason survives in a `reason` field that never crosses the wire, and a test greps the constant for the words that would leak.

Remaining budget is reported on SUCCESS only. A countdown on a refusal is a side channel: watch it fall and you have measured the policy, which tells you exactly how slowly to probe.

Two judgement calls. The unknown budget is small but not zero — a stranger has to be able to become a customer, and §20.10's concern is the curve rather than the first question. And the count is per counterparty, never global: a global counter lets one busy customer exhaust everyone's budget, which converts a privacy defence into an outage a competitor can trigger deliberately by being noisy.

Open question. The default budgets (60/hour known, 5/hour unknown) are a guess at where a real customer stops and a prober starts. I have no usage data; if you have a feel for how often a buyer legitimately re-quotes, that number is worth setting deliberately rather than by my estimate.

Six mutations, six killed: the refusal naming its reason; a global rather than per-peer counter; the window ignored; unknown peers given the known budget; blocked standing ignored; remaining budget leaked on a refusal. 12 tests. Verification: core 6,709; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-6.4 — hierarchical product evidence (§13.6, FR-B13, FR-B14)

"This chair has forty good reviews" and "chairs in this family have forty good reviews between them" are different claims. The first is about the exact variant a buyer is about to order; the second may be about a different finish, a different size, or a discontinued predecessor. Presenting the second as the first tells a buyer that a thing has a track record it does not have.

The failure is easy to reach because the convenient data structure is a single count. Sum forty inherited and two exact into "42 reviews" and the information that would have let a buyer discount it is gone, irreversibly, one line into the pipeline. So exact and inherited are never summed, never merged, and always labelled — they travel as separate groups all the way to the card, and there is deliberately NO function returning a bare number. That function would be called, its result rendered next to the product name, and the distinction lost at the last step.

FR-B14 runs both ways and both are tested: inherited must not be presented as exact, and exact must not be presented as inherited — understating what is known about the exact variant makes a buyer discount evidence they should have trusted. The headline returns NULL when nothing is known rather than a zero, because a zero beside a product name reads as a bad rating.

Writing this exposed two real defects in WS-5.2, both hidden by a cast. The importer built {scheme: 'sku', value} and cast it to ProductRef. `sku` is not one of the four §9.3 schemes (gtin | manufacturer_sku | dina_subject | custom), AND manufacturer_sku requires an issuer_did without which the reference is ambiguous across suppliers — two of them may both call something CHAIR-1. So every imported catalog carried product references the protocol's own validator rejects. Fixed by building and VALIDATING rather than asserting: the importer now takes the supplier DID, maps a CSV sku to a scoped manufacturer_sku, and runs validateProductRef. mpn is no longer an importable SCHEME — a manufacturer part number is issued by someone other than the supplier, so scoping it to the supplier's DID would assert an authority they do not have. It remains a descriptive column.

And the leakage gate then caught a third. With real product refs, publication started failing: issuer_did was not in the public-field vocabulary, so no scoped product reference could ever be published. It is part of the identity and public by construction; admitted along with variant_digest. Three defects in one chain, each found by the next layer refusing to accept what the previous one produced — which is the argument for making layers refuse rather than coerce.

Six mutations, six killed: pools merged; the headline dropping its scope; zero instead of null; unrelated evidence folded in; inherited standing in for absent exact; ancestor order reversed. One was a no-op first and was redone as the actual FR-B13 failure. 13 tests. Verification: core 6,722; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-6.3 — per-task context projection (§13.5, §11.3, FR-P3)

contextScopeViolation already existed and its own comment says what it is: a FAIL-CLOSED BACKSTOP, present in case a projection producer skips scrubbing. There was no producer. Every caller assembled context by hand, so the backstop was the only thing between the vault and a runner — and a backstop is the wrong place for a rule to live.

FR-P3: project per task, never a runner union. The convenient implementation gathers everything the PLUGIN might need and hands it to whichever capability is running. That is a union, and it grows silently: adding a capability that reads health data widens what the plugin's ORDERING capability receives, and nobody editing the manifest would see the connection. The projection is built from ONE capability's declared scope, and a capability that declared none receives nothing — the manifest is where a plugin says what it needs, and silence there is an answer.

Fields are copied IN by name, never scrubbed out. A deny-list leaks every key nobody thought of, and the keys nobody thinks of are exactly the ones a vault feature adds next month. So the test is not "does it remove the bad field" but "does it carry only the named ones", and one case asserts that an unknown future field is excluded without anyone editing the test.

Two distinctions that would have been easy to collapse. An ABSENT persona list means no restriction was declared (which the manifest validator permits); an EMPTY one is a declaration of none. Reading the second as the first would silently widen every capability that wrote personas: []. And an undeclared max_context_items is ZERO, not unlimited — matching the backstop's own reading, because the generous interpretation here is the one place producer and checker could disagree, and the disagreement would surface as a runner receiving context the checker then rejects.

Six mutations, six killed: undeclared category passing (the union failure); a no-scope capability getting a permissive default; wholesale field copying; an undeclared ceiling read as unlimited; an empty persona list read as no restriction; exclusions mislabelled. One was redone shape-preserving. 12 tests. Verification: core 6,734; typecheck clean; my files lint-clean (the 60 remaining plugin-test lint errors are pre-existing debt, task #484).

The whole journey, in one sequence (§24, §25.3)

procurement_journey.test.ts runs Sancho discovering ChairMaker and buying from them, end to end: spreadsheet → catalog import → publication (leakage gate inside) → bounded fan-out → probing admission → signed quote → hard filters and ranking → product evidence → order through the real plugin lane → runner decision → Core-signed acknowledgement → dispatched → delivered, with the status chain linking each record to its predecessor.

Why it exists on top of the two scenarios already there. procurement_scenario drives the engines directly; procurement_lane_scenario drives one order through the plugin lane. Neither touches what a BUYER uses before an order exists — import, publication, fan-out, probing, filters, ranking, evidence — and every one of those was built alone and gated alone over the last several items. That is precisely the defect shape this codebase keeps producing: correct modules nothing joins up. This is the test that makes them meet.

It passed first time, which is worth stating plainly rather than celebrating: the last two times these pieces met, the meeting found real mismatches (the manifest's decision vs the seam's kind; the importer's cast ProductRef). Both were fixed at the seam rather than papered over, and passing now is evidence those fixes were structural.

Three supporting cases guard the happy path from being the only claim: an offer that is cheapest, fastest, best-trusted AND expired still ranks nowhere; a catalog carrying a private column still refuses to publish; and the reference manifest still installs, stated separately so a broken precondition names itself instead of failing step 8 confusingly.

What it does not claim. No D2D socket, no AppView, no PDS, no mobile UI. §25.6 is two live nodes and stays manual. This is the whole IN-PROCESS journey — a strictly smaller claim, and the largest one a jest run can honestly make. Verification: core 6,738; typecheck clean; lint clean on the file.

WS-2.10 — a runner may enrich a status, but not invent one (§9.11)

This is what survived the REVERTED WS-2.7. Moving order_status into Core was wrong — the published result shape is the SUPPLIER's, and only they know the carrier reference — but the concern underneath was real: nothing stopped a runner reporting a state Core never signed, and the buyer had no way to tell.

state is the one field both sides hold, and they are not equal in authority. Core's chain is the record the supplier SIGNED; a runner saying dispatched while the chain says accepted is reporting something this business has not claimed. So the seam overwrites state with the signed head and passes every other field through untouched.

Overwriting rather than refusing, deliberately. A refusal would cost the buyer the carrier reference and the note as well — and the runner is not necessarily lying, it may simply be ahead of the chain. Correcting one field and keeping the rest is the answer that loses the least while still being true.

With no chain, the answer is left alone. An order with no genesis has no signed state to contradict, and substituting one there would be Core making exactly the kind of claim it is stopping the runner from making. A runner that OMITS the state gets Core's added, because omission is not agreement — a card rendering an absent state beside a carrier reference reads as "no idea" when Core knows precisely.

An existing test asserted order_status was untouched. That was true and is now deliberately false, so it was updated rather than deleted: the seam owns TWO capabilities with different treatments, and the list of what it leaves alone now says so. Five mutations, five killed: never correcting; discarding the enrichment; substituting a state with no chain; dropping order_status from the seam; swallowing an unreadable answer. Verification: core 6,744, core-server 2,841; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-4.2 (second half) — proving the archive before writing it (§16.2)

The restore fence answers the COUNTER question. It says nothing about structure. An archive whose orders reference quotes it does not carry imports cleanly today, and the damage surfaces months later as a counterparty's reconcile that no local record can satisfy. The preflight is the other half: it takes the archive's commerce tables and returns findings, and the import throws before touching the target.

All-or-nothing, not import-what-is-coherent. Dropping an incoherent order reference does not merely omit information — it makes this node deny an order the counterparty holds signed evidence for. §16.2's fail-closed reconstruction reads as refusing the whole archive with a report naming what is wrong.

All six commerce tables or none. The first draft required all six unconditionally, which would have been a data-loss bug wearing an integrity check's clothes: the shipped App Store build predates commerce and its backups will be restored for years. None present means "this backup predates commerce" and passes. A PARTIAL set is the case that cannot be excused — every archive this code produces writes all six, empty arrays included, so a torn set was assembled by something else, and the tables that survived are exactly the ones the missing ones would have vouched for. On a torn set it reports the missing table and stops, rather than cascading "every order references a missing quote" over the finding that explains it.

Three cross-references are checked because each one names a specific broken node: an order without its quote cannot have capacity re-derived (and §16.2 re-derives rather than adopting); a chain without its order can never legally advance, since §9.11 line checks read the order's accepted lines; a chain head naming an absent receipt is frozen at import, because loadHeadStatus fails closed on every future signing attempt. All three look like a supplier who simply stopped responding.

The deep review found a boundary I had broken. A prior review round pinned an invariant: verifyArchive must agree with importArchive. My first version left verify untouched, so a torn archive would answer "valid" and then fail the restore — the worst possible shape, because verify exists precisely to be the answer an operator acts on before committing. Verify now runs the preflight too. listArchiveContents deliberately does not, and the asymmetry is the point: verify makes a claim, listing shows what is inside, and refusing to list a torn archive would remove the one tool for working out what went wrong with it. That reasoning is recorded at both functions so the difference does not read as an oversight.

Findings name tables and keys, never values — an archive is somebody's ledger, and a restore failure is not a reason to print it. The thrown message carries counts by refusal kind only. Five mutations, five killed; one of them — accepting uppercase digests — exposed a test of mine that passed for the wrong reason: the archive still failed, but as a dangling reference rather than a shape error, which would send an operator hunting for a record that was right there. The assertion now pins the refusal kind. A sixth mutation, removing the preflight from verifyArchive, is also killed. Verification: core 6,775 (+32), core-server 2,841; typecheck and lint clean. The wiring is proved through a real export/import, including that a refused import does NOT arm the fence — arming it would burn an epoch and void live quotes for a restore that never happened.

WS-4.6 — the reconcile lane had no way in (§12.7, §16.2)

The dominant defect in this codebase showed up again, at the worst possible place. reconcile and reconcileRestoredOrder are careful, well-tested code that nothing in production called. Provider ingress recognised order_reconcile, authorized the sender, and then handed the query to a plugin runner.

A runner cannot answer it. Every input lives in Core: the order reference, the receipt store, this supplier's own signature. And the one answer a runner might invent is the dangerous one — never_received is the only outcome that authorizes the buyer to resubmit, and §16.2 makes it illegal against a held supplier-signed acknowledgement. A plugin able to say it could make a supplier repudiate an order it signed, then be billed for the buyer's honest resubmission.

Answered before the binding checks, deliberately. Reconcile is the disaster-recovery path. A supplier whose plugin was paused, updated, or uninstalled must still answer for orders it holds; gating it behind an install makes the lane go dark exactly when it is needed, and lifecycle_continuity_unavailable would be the reply to a question Core could answer from its own records.

Carrying the answer out took a second ok variant on the ingress result and a widened responder (status: 'ok' with a result). The variant is not a pre-completed task, because the caller must SEND now rather than wait for a workflow event that will never arrive — and making it distinct means every consumer's outcome.taskId stops compiling until it decides what to do, which is how the one production consumer was found.

One response-body builder, where there were two. The lite default and mobile each hand-built the service.response body, and they had already drifted. Harmless while both carried the same fields, and precisely the shape in which a new field gets missed by one: the responder's TYPE would still be satisfied, nothing would complain, and the buyer would wait out its TTL for a reply already computed. Both now call toServiceResponseBody.

Five mutations. Three killed by tests: reconcile falling through to the runner (5 failures), answering instead of refusing with no commerce runtime, and the shared builder dropping result. One killed by the COMPILER — a consumer cannot drop the Core-answered branch without outcome.taskId failing to typecheck, which is a stronger guarantee than a test. One SURVIVES: reading the payload's buyer_did instead of the authenticated sender. No test can kill it, because the subject gate requires the two to be equal, so through this entry point they are never distinguishable. Rather than leave a test that passes for the wrong reason, the gate now HANDS OVER the DID it authorized and the answer takes it as an argument — one value, no second read to get wrong — and the test says plainly what it does and does not prove. Verification: core 6,780, brain 3,569, home-node 30, mobile 3,293, core-server 2,841, brain-server 116; typecheck clean, sources lint-clean.

WS-4.3 (second half) — a census, not a sweep (§16.2)

The obvious reading of "bulk reconciliation" is a job that walks every re-adopted order and clears the flag. It cannot exist. The ceremony clears reconciliation_required by checking the buyer's HELD order proposal against the digest this supplier already signed; a re-adopted order has no lines, no quote context and no external state, so there is nothing local to check against. A sweep would have to invent the terms — the exact thing the post-restore quote seam (X-10) forbids one layer up.

So the bulk operation reports. It names which orders are frozen and which counterparty holds the evidence that frees each one, and the response states in clearedBy that this node is not the one who can finish the job. That closes the real gap: without it the owner learns about a frozen order when a buyer complains.

Decided orders are included, and that was the design decision worth making twice. Filtering to reserved reads as tidier and hides the rows that matter most — a decided order that is frozen means the buyer holds an acknowledgement this node can no longer describe. Oldest first, because a frozen order does not improve with time, with a stable tiebreak so the list does not reshuffle between reads of the same data.

The surface is owner-private, on the same boundary as /v1/run and /v1/watch: a list of counterparties and orders this node cannot answer for is a map of exactly where the supplier is vulnerable. Registered at the composition root with a /v1/commerce authz prefix, so it is not the ninth thing built and never wired.

Tech debt fixed on the way through: makeOwnerGuard existed twice, in run.ts and watch.ts, identical but for the refusal wording — and a third copy was about to be written here. Three copies of an authorization check is three places for a fix to be applied twice, so it moved into owner_guard.ts with reason as the only parameter. Worth noting what that surfaced: mutating the capability check out of the shared guard fails ONE test across 296 route tests, which says the run and watch suites never covered "owner presents the wrong capability". The shared guard now has that coverage once, on behalf of all three.
