Six mutations, six killed: state filter, sort direction, distinct-buyer count, dropped owner guard, no-runtime answering an empty census instead of 503, and the capability check. Verification: core 6,798 (+18), core-server 2,841, mobile 3,293; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-4.5 (update half) — a rule the spec states in one sentence and nothing enforced (§16.5)

“An update cannot silently widen from catalog read to order submission.” That sentence is the entire threat model, and it had no implementation. The rebind coordinator authorized the drain and continuity lanes, adopted the new CID, and rebound the listings without ever comparing what the owner had consented to. A supplier pack installed as a price list could ship 1.1 that also places orders: well-formed, correctly signed, content-addressed, and covered by consent given to a reader.

The comparison is the consent boundary, not a diff. That makes it one-directional. An update that NARROWS needs no fresh consent — nobody is surprised by a plugin doing less — so removals, lower classes, and smaller scopes all pass. Only widening is refused, and refused rather than downgraded-and-applied: applying the narrow part would leave the install running a manifest whose CID the consent does not cover, and content-addressing is the only thing making consent mean anything.

Five axes, each earning its place. A new capability (the case §16.5 names). A raised action class (the catalog-to-orders jump itself). A raised privacy class — not about money, still not what was agreed. A grown data scope: context ceiling, category, persona. And weakened idempotency, the quietest of the five: the name, the class and the scope all stand still while a retry may now double-charge.

Unknown and absent values rank at the CEILING. A manifest naming a class this build has never heard of is either from a newer protocol or is lying, and both readings say we cannot tell what it costs. Ranking it lowest would make action_class: "totally_safe" a bypass, which is exactly how allow-by-default rules fail. The manifest validator rejects unknown classes on the way in, so this is the second line, not the first — and the mirror holds too: an unreadable value in the PRIOR manifest cannot make an update look narrower, only fail to widen.

Pause (§16.3) needed no change, and that is worth stating rather than leaving as silence. Pause stops dispatch and provider answers, which sounds like it strands a buyer mid-order — except WS-4.6 made reconcile Core-answered, so a buyer can still resolve an order against a paused plugin. Pause is reversible and must NOT refuse while obligations are open; uninstall must, and does. Five mutations, five killed: the coordinator never checking, unknown class at the floor, dropping the persona list reading as narrowing, idempotency weakening ignored, and an absent action class reading as read. Verification: core 6,818 (+20), core-server 2,841; typecheck and lint clean.

Open question for you. The uninstall obligation count is still node-wide, because an order reference records serving_manifest_cid and not the install id. On a node running one supplier plugin — the only shipped shape — that is exact; on a multi-plugin node it refuses an uninstall that would have been safe. Fixing it means a column on commerce_order_refs and a join for the chain half. I have left it, because refusing costs an operator a conversation while permitting costs a buyer their recourse, and the shape it would be wrong for does not ship yet. Say if you want it exact now.

A bug I shipped this session, and the test that now catches it

The WS-4.2 preflight read counterparty_did on the watermark table. The column is supplier_did — counterparty is the word the SPEC uses for the concept. Every real archive carrying a watermark row would have been refused as a bad row shape.

Nothing caught it, and the reason is worth more than the fix. The preflight's own tests build fixtures by hand, so they and the module agreed with each other on the wrong name — the same failure the WBS already records for the Sha256Fn contract. And the export/import wiring test seeded orders and quotes but no watermark. Two suites, both green, both blind in the same place.

The fix is one word; the real repair is a test that drives a FULL commerce set — quote head, order ref, use counter, receipt, status head, watermark — through a genuine export and import against the real schema. A column name can now only drift from the schema if that test is deleted. Verified by reverting the field name and watching it fail. Writing it surfaced a second stale name: the preflight's type declaration named quote_digest on commerce_quote_heads, which is head_digest. Harmless (nothing read it) and now correct.

WS-2.9 — a fence with the gate left open (§16.2, §25.3)

The supplier's half of the restore fence cannot cover the case §25.3 calls delayed-pre-restore-write. A record signed BEFORE the restore is already in flight — in a relay queue, or on a node that never learned it had been superseded — and arrives afterwards. It is genuinely signed and its digest verifies. Nothing on the supplier's side can stop it, because the supplier is not the one delivering it. Only the buyer, holding the highest epoch it has ever seen from that supplier, can tell.

The watermark table and its repository have existed since CMC-1. Nothing read them. The gate now runs on every tool-lane completion, straight after the pinned-schema check — the last point before a supplier's record becomes the owner's answer.

One stale record refuses the whole result. Filtering the stale ones out is wrong twice: the buyer would silently receive fewer quotes than the supplier sent with no way to know, and a place-order answer is a single record whose removal leaves the order in no state at all. Nothing is raised when it refuses either, so a higher epoch earlier in a list cannot move the fence and thereby admit a stale one that follows it.

Two findings came out of the mutations, and both were mine. The first version read the supplier from the envelope's service_ingress.from_did. That field is SUPPLIER-side correlation — it describes an inbound query this node is answering — and a buyer's outbound tool call has none, because the buyer is the one asking. The gate would never have fired: an orphan wearing a wire. The wiring test is what caught it, which is the argument for writing wiring tests at all. One envelope-level supplier could not have been right anyway, since collect-quotes fans out to many suppliers and returns records from several generations at once; the pair (supplier_did, supplier_epoch) travels inside each signed record, so they are read together.

The second: a record carrying an epoch but NO supplier_did was passing by default — drop one field and sail past the fence, the exact bypass the module's own comment warns about. Now refused, because “somebody's record from some generation” is not something a watermark can judge. Six mutations, six killed after both fixes: <= instead of < (which would reject ordinary same-epoch traffic), an inverted result-gate comparison, raising the fence before deciding, coerced non-canonical epochs, the route never refusing, and the unattributed-record bypass. Verification: core 6,846 (+28), core-server 2,841, mobile 3,293; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-2.8 — two fields, two rules, one write (§9.12, §16.2)

The receipt store is content-addressed, so the record body is self-proving. What it could not show is HOW this node came to hold the document — signed here, or received from a counterparty over an authenticated envelope. That is what a dispute turns on: “we agree this is the document” is rarely the argument, “you sent it to me, here is the envelope” is. The column has existed since CMC-1 and every caller wrote '{}'.

The write had to stop being one rule. `put` was ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING, which is RIGHT for the body: the digest addresses those bytes, so a second writer proposing different bytes under the same digest is a collision or an attack and the first writer must hold. It is wrong for evidence, and predictably so — the ordinary sequence is that a node records a document it built, then receives the same digest back from the counterparty carrying real authentication. First-writer-wins threw away the one a dispute needs. The two fields now have different rules under one write, matching what each field IS: the record is a fact about the document, the evidence is a growing list of facts about this node's encounters with it.

observedAt is deliberately absent from the dedup identity. A retried delivery of the same envelope arrives at a different millisecond and is the same fact; including the clock would turn the evidence list into a delivery log. The first sighting keeps its timestamp, because when it first arrived is the fact worth holding. Two deliveries under DIFFERENT keys both survive — “they sent it twice under two keys” is itself evidence.

Corrupt stored evidence reads as empty and is DROPPED rather than merged. Dropped because merging it would give anyone who could write that column a way to inject observations; read-as-empty rather than throwing because a receipt must not become unwritable when a metadata field goes bad — the record body is the thing under dispute, and losing access to it over metadata is the worse failure.

Five mutations, five killed. One was a finding of the familiar kind: the admission capture site had no test, so reverting it to '{}' left 589 commerce tests green. Also worth recording: the identity separator is written as the escape \u0000 rather than as a raw NUL byte. Three existing files (extension_broker.ts, matcher.ts, answer_cache.ts) use a literal NUL in source for the same purpose and are correct — but an invisible byte does not survive tooling reliably, and one of mine was silently altered during a mutation round-trip this session. The escape is the same value, visible. Verification: core 6,865 (+19), core-server 2,841; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-1.9 — two more frozen vector categories (§25.1)

Conformance vectors only earn their keep when a DISAGREEMENT between implementations would be commercial rather than cosmetic. Both new categories qualify.

Pack conversion is a refusal, and that is the point. case and pallet carry no base factor, so converting them needs evidence this layer does not hold. An implementation that guessed “a case is twelve” would agree to a pallet order at a twelfth of its size and price it accordingly. So quantity.json freezes the refusal and its exact wording. The cross-dimension cases sit beside it deliberately: they refuse for a DIFFERENT reason — a category error, not missing evidence — and a vector set carrying only one would let a port collapse them into a single check that happens to pass. A test asserts both kinds are present.

Relationship claims compose reputation, so relationship.json pins the DID/product discriminant in both directions. A product manufactured_by another PRODUCT is an edge that means nothing, and inherited manufacturer standing would flow along it. Temporal windows are pinned closed-before-open AND zero-length, since <= rather than < is the whole difference between them, and timestamps must be UTC rather than merely parseable — an offset canonicalizes differently, so two implementations would digest the same claim to different bytes.

Proven to gate rather than assumed: perturbing the frozen pack refusal string, a pack conversion result, and a temporal verdict each fails CI, and the vectors were restored afterwards. Writing them surfaced a small real thing — the generator runs under tsx, which strips types rather than checking them, so my first draft used { unit, amount } where the protocol's Quantity is { unit_code, value } and it failed at RUNTIME rather than at compile time. A doc test also caught the new files immediately: every vector must be listed in conformance.md, which is a guard doing exactly its job. Verification: commerce-protocol 302 (+8 cases across 8 new tests), core 6,865; typecheck and lint clean.

The disaster journey — one order through a supplier restore (§16.2, §12.7, §25.3)

The happy path was covered three times over. None of those scenarios survives a restore, which is where the last five items were built: the epoch watermark, the Core-answered reconcile lane, the reconciliation census, receipt arrival evidence, and the archive preflight. Every one was built alone and gated alone, and four of the five turned out on inspection to be reachable only from their own test. One sequence over one set of facts is the only thing that finds that.

The story: Sancho orders chairs, ChairMaker accepts, and then ChairMaker restores a backup taken BEFORE the order arrived. Sancho holds a signed acceptance for an order the supplier has no record of. The test walks what has to happen next — reconcile (and specifically NOT never_received, which §16.2 makes illegal against a held signed acknowledgement because it would invite a resubmission that charges Sancho twice), the order returning frozen, the census reporting it and naming who can clear it, the ceremony clearing it, and the order belonging to the new generation afterwards. Two more cases cover the buyer's side of §25.3 and a torn archive.

Writing it was mostly archaeology, and that is itself a finding. Four fixture mismatches in a row, each silent until run: the clock sat exactly ON the fixture quote's valid_until, so admission answered quote_unknown — which reads as “no such quote” and sent me looking in the wrong place entirely; admission re-reads the retained REQUEST receipt to check the order's delivery projection, which nothing told me and no error named; and my reconcile request had invented field names, because I wrote it from the story rather than from OrderReconcileRequest. A scenario is worth writing precisely because it forces every one of those into the open, but the refusals could say more: quote_unknown covering an expired quote, a missing request receipt AND a genuinely unknown id is non-disclosing on the wire for good reason — it need not be non-disclosing to the node's own operator. Verification: core 6,869 (+4 scenario cases); typecheck and lint clean.

Acting on the open question the scenario raised (§14.2)

I flagged this last round rather than fixing it, then reconsidered: the evidence was mine and the fix is narrow, so leaving it open would have been deferring a defect I had just paid for.

quote_unknown covers three different situations: an EXPIRED quote, one this node never held, and a retained request receipt it could not read back. Flattening them is correct on the wire — telling a stranger which one lets them probe the supplier's ledger — and wrong in a log, where an operator debugging a live refusal had exactly as much to go on as an attacker. That asymmetry is the whole point of §14.2 and it was being applied in only one direction.

The rejected outcome now carries an operator-facing detail alongside the acknowledgement. The acknowledgement is unchanged, and a test asserts the detail never appears inside it — otherwise the non-disclosing reason code would be decoration. The ingress error already carries this split in its own comment (“Core's messages are written for an operator reading logs”), so the detail rides there.

Three mutations, three killed. The third — the ingress dropping the detail again — survived on the first pass, which is the unwired-seam finding for the third time in three items. It is worth stating plainly: in this codebase, a value that is computed correctly and threaded correctly still has roughly even odds of being discarded at the last hop, and only a test at that hop notices. Verification: core 6,876 (+7); typecheck and lint clean.

One thing I could not run down. The core-server suite failed once, then again on the second of twelve runs, then passed twelve consecutive times including eight where I captured full output to disk specifically to name the test. I never saw the failure again and cannot name it, so I am not calling that suite unconditionally green: it is 2 failures in ~14 runs of a suite my change does touch indirectly (provider_ingress is consumed there). Worth a look if it recurs; I would rather flag an unreproduced flake than report a clean run and have you find it later.

WS-2.2b — the cast WS-0.7 missed, and it was the important one (§9.9, §9.12)

WS-0.7 closed five JSON.parse(…) as … sites, each described as “a promise to the compiler and a check of nothing”. It missed a sixth, and the sixth is the one with teeth: admission read the retained quote REQUEST that way.

That record is not data being read back — it is the yardstick. It carries the priced delivery projection, and an incoming order's delivery is checked against it (§9.9). So a projection edited in the store after writing became the standard the order had to match, and a mismatched order would pass. The failure mode is the bad one: a wrong commercial outcome rather than an error, discovered when a delivery turns up somewhere nobody agreed to.

Now through rehydrateQuoteRequest. validateQuoteRequest ends in verifyCommerceRecordDigest, so it re-derives the request digest from the content — exactly the corruption a shape check cannot see, and exactly what the cast could not do. It also removes a throw: JSON.parse on a corrupt record threw out of admitInTx, inside a transaction, on the inbound D2D path, where every other failure returns a typed refusal.

The tests tamper through a harness-side hook rather than a production one, because put is first-writer-wins for the body ON PURPOSE (WS-2.8) and adding a replace method would undo that to make a test convenient. Each harness reaches past its own repository the way disk corruption or a half-finished migration would: a map write in memory, an UPDATE in SQLite. Two mutations, two killed — reverting to the cast, and shape-checking without re-deriving the digest. One of my own assertions was wrong on the first pass: I called admitOrder twice and asserted on the second, which is a REPLAY of the first refusal and would have passed whatever the code did. Verification: core 6,880 (+4); typecheck and lint clean.

WS-2.2c — guarding the READ instead of the cast (§9.12)

Last round I noted that “completed” items were a weaker signal here than a grep for the pattern each item was meant to eliminate. So I ran the grep, as a static guard rather than a one-off.

The existing cast guard looks for the wrong thing, and that is why it missed the site with teeth. It greps for as SignedQuote and friends. The retained request was read as JSON.parse(…) as { delivery: … } — an ANONYMOUS shape, with no type name to find. A guard on the READ rather than on the cast found three more sites the moment it ran.

Two were real. The stored ACKNOWLEDGEMENT is read on both the replay and decision paths, and it is the supplier's commitment handed verbatim to a buyer: JSON.parse('null') as OrderAcknowledgement produced a null typed as a commitment, and a corrupt column threw. It now goes through rehydrateAcknowledgement, which re-derives the acknowledgement digest. And the cancellation-result scan threw on one corrupt row; it now skips it, because one unreadable receipt must not make every OTHER cancellation for that order unanswerable.

Two were legitimate, and are allow-listed with reasons rather than with a filename. loadHeadStatus already parses inside try/catch and then validates AND compares status_digest to the head — it re-derives exactly as rehydration would. catalog_ingest parses an HTTP body that has not been stored, and already answers a typed refusal. Writing the reason into the exemption is the difference between a guard and a list of files someone once decided to skip.

Four mutations, four killed after one fix. The guard itself was proved to gate by adding the defect back in a FOURTH file and watching it name that file — a guard nobody has seen fail is a guard nobody has tested. One mutation survived first time: the empty-string branch of the acknowledgement read is behaviourally identical to the parse failure beneath it, so nothing observable changed. Rather than delete it I tested the thing it actually adds — the MESSAGE, which says “absent” where the parse says “not JSON”. An operator reading “not JSON” about an empty column goes hunting for corruption; “absent” says the write never happened. Verification: core 6,889 (+9); typecheck and lint clean.

The orphan ledger — turning a recurring surprise into a tracked list

The read-guard worked, so I applied the same move to the other class I keep hitting: code that is correct, tested, and called by nothing. Five times in this workstream alone — the reconcile lane, the epoch watermark, the census, the admission arrival evidence, the ingress refusal detail. Each time the module was imported, its tests passed, and production never reached the function.

A guard now enumerates every exported symbol under src/commerce and fails when one has no production caller and no ledger entry — and equally when a ledger entry has quietly become wired, because a stale entry hides the next one. Both directions are proved by breaking them.

What the first honest count says. Seventeen exported symbols have no production caller. Five are test doubles. Two are reference packs. The rest are the finding: the entire BUYER lane — catalog import, feed ingest, withdrawal, ranking, fan-out, product evidence — is built and gated and wired to nothing, because the surface that would call it (mobile screens, AppView-backed discovery) is WS-5 and WS-7 and does not exist. procurement_journey drives those modules directly, which is exactly why they look exercised.

One entry is a real gap rather than sequencing. admitQuoteRequest is §20.10 probing resistance, and nothing calls it: inbound quote requests arrive through the plugin lane, which admits at a different layer. Unwired, a stranger can probe a supplier's quote capacity without spending any budget. It is on the ledger with that reason rather than buried.

The guard had two bugs of its own on its first run, both worth recording because they are easy to repeat: it walked only packages/, so the composition roots in apps/ were invisible and it reported createCommerceRuntime itself as unwired; and it reused a /g regex with .test(), which is stateful through lastIndex and therefore skips every other match. A guard that has never been seen to fail correctly is not yet a guard. Verification: core 6,890; typecheck and lint clean.

WS-2.11 — closing the ledger's first real finding (§14.3, §20.10)

The orphan ledger's whole point is that entries leave it. The one entry I flagged as a real gap rather than sequencing was admitQuoteRequest: probing resistance, built and gated and called by nothing. A competitor could draw a supplier's price curve by asking a hundred times at varying quantities, and every individual answer would have been legitimate.

It is now spent at the ingress, BEFORE any runner is asked — a refusal that reached the plugin would already have cost the supplier the answer. availability is in the set alongside request_quote: it reveals what a supplier can source, which is half the curve, and leaving it out would simply make it the cheaper way to ask the same question.

The window is in memory, deliberately. A durable table would be exactly the permanent log of who-asked-what that §20.10 warns against, and it would survive a restore — so a node coming back would still refuse a customer whose budget was spent in a window that has since passed. Losing the window on restart costs one window of protection against a prober patient enough to wait for a reboot they cannot observe; keeping it durably costs a real customer a refusal they cannot understand. The first is the cheaper mistake.

It prunes on every READ rather than on a timer, because a sweeper is a second thing to wire and this codebase's most common defect is precisely the thing nobody wired. It forgets the PEER and not merely their attempts — an empty array per peer who ever asked is still a list of everyone who ever asked. And it caps retention per peer far above any budget, so evicting the oldest entries can never turn a refusal into an admission.

Fails CLOSED when unwired. That is the whole lesson of this session compressed into one branch: an unwired probing defence that silently permits is invisible, because every request succeeding is what success looks like. It surfaced immediately — the ingress suite went red until the harness installed a ledger, which is the alarm working. ONE refusal code for every reason (§14.3), since a prober who can tell “budget spent” from “we don't quote you” learns the catalog by watching which requests get a different shape of no. Only an ADMITTED request charges, so a peer past their limit can recover; charging refusals would mean each one extends the window that caused it. Four mutations, four killed. The ledger entry is removed, which the guard itself enforces. Verification: core 6,901 (+11), core-server 2,841, mobile 3,293; typecheck and lint clean.

The buyer decision surface, wired (§13.2–§13.6, WS-5/WS-7 Core half)

The ledger said the buyer lane was written and attached to nothing, and the temptation was to wait for the mobile screens. That is exactly how those pieces became orphans: each was correct, each was gated, and the ORDER they run in — where the interesting mistakes live — existed only inside a scenario test. procurement_service is that order in production code, behind two owner-only routes. Six of the seven buyer-lane entries leave the ledger.

Two calls rather than one, because a network round trip sits between planning who to ask and ranking what came back. Collapsing them would force the route to SEND, putting a second egress path beside the one the four gates already guard. The module adds no rule of its own: every decision still belongs to the function that owned it, and anything more would be a second opinion competing with the module that owns the rule. composeProductEvidence stays on the ledger, because building evidence needs a PeerLens read Core does not have — the service takes composed evidence as an argument rather than learning to fetch.

Open question, and I think an important one. Writing the ranking test surfaced that the cheapest surviving offer always wins. Price is scored min-max, so the cheapest offer takes the full 6000bp, while lead time (2500) plus trust (1500) cap every rival at 4000. Trust can therefore never outvote price; it only breaks ties among equally-priced offers. That may be the intended weighting — but it sits oddly beside Law 2, “rank by trust, not by ad spend”, and a buyer looking at a 15% trust weight would reasonably expect a well-rated supplier to sometimes beat a cheaper stranger. Changing weights changes commercial outcomes, so I have not touched them. The behaviour is now PINNED by a test that says why, so the finding lives in the code rather than only here, and a change to it has to be deliberate.

Four mutations, four killed after one fix. The finding: an EMPTY-STRING buyer_did passed the guard. It is present, it is a string, and it matches no supplier — so self-exclusion silently stops working while every other check passes, and a buyer could be asked to quote itself. My first test covered only the absent case. A second real fix came from the route: a malformed offer made the ranking throw, and a throw on an owner route is a 500 — “this node is broken” about a request that was merely wrong. It answers 400 now. Verification: core 6,915 (+14), core-server 2,841; typecheck and lint clean.

The supplier catalog surface, and a hole in the guard that found it (§10.2, §12.1, §16.2)

Import, publish and withdraw are now owner-only routes. Two of the three were on the orphan ledger. The third — buildCatalogSnapshot, the one that actually mints what goes on the wire — was not, and that is the finding worth recording: the ledger read production files with their COMMENTS in, so a symbol whose only non-test mention anywhere in the repo was a sentence in another module's prose counted as wired. A guard with a comment-shaped bypass is worse than no guard, because it certifies. The guard now strips comments from both sides of the comparison, and the fix immediately surfaced two real orphans: the snapshot builder and commerceAvailability.

Which act gets the §16.2 fence, and why only one. Publication is gated on commerceAvailability(); import and withdrawal are not. A snapshot advances a chain buyers follow, and a restored node's memory of “what I last published” is exactly the state a backup carries stale — so publishing is how a restore forks a chain forward. Reading a spreadsheet cannot do that, and a withdrawal ENDS a chain rather than extending it. Fencing the tombstone would take away the owner's ability to stop selling at the moment they most want to, which is the wrong trade in both directions.

Three deliberate refusals to be helpful. (1) default_scheme is required, not defaulted: it decides how every bare identifier in the file is READ, so a guess silently reinterprets the whole catalog. (2) The supplier DID comes from this node and is never a body field — a manufacturer_sku is only unambiguous scoped to its issuer, and honouring a caller-supplied one would publish under another supplier's scope. (3) An empty items array publishes (“I currently offer nothing”) but a MISSING one is a 400, so a dropped field cannot silently retire a live catalog. The previous pointer is likewise derived, not repeated: the chain rule requires it to equal the predecessor's own digest, so a second field only creates a way to disagree with the pointer just supplied.

ingestCatalog stays on the ledger, with a sharper reason than before. It is the CONSUMER half and it fetches; a route that handed Core a FeedTransport would put an outbound request behind an owner endpoint where the egress gates cannot see it.

Seven mutations, seven killed — including a lenient default_scheme fallback, an always-available §16.2 fence, and a malformed previous silently treated as absent (which would publish a GENESIS at sequence 1 and fork the live chain). Two crashed on the first attempt with Tests: 0 — a compile error, not a survival — and were rewritten to be shape-preserving. One small hardening came out of writing the tests: the routes reject an empty-string node DID as well as null. That state is unreachable today (setNodeDID demands a did: prefix), so it is belt-and-braces rather than a live hole, and the test says so instead of pretending to drive it. Verification: core 6,948 (+33), core-server 2,841; typecheck and lint clean.

The journey now walks the shipped surface (§24, §25.3)

Once the owner routes existed there were two ways to reach the same rule, and the retailer→manufacturer journey took the shorter one. A journey that calls the module behind a route keeps passing while the route disagrees with it — the same defect as an orphan, one layer up. Catalog import, publication, fan-out planning and ranking now go through a real CoreRouter in the journey test; the leakage refusal does too, so §12.1 is proven to hold on the path an owner actually takes rather than on the path the test found convenient. The order lane already went through the real plugin lane and is unchanged. The parts with no route — registering a signed quote, composing evidence, signing status updates — still call their engines, which is honest: there is nothing else to call.

One process, two businesses: the buyer's and the supplier's owner calls hit the same router and setNodeDID names the supplier, because that is whose catalog is being published. The header says so, so nobody later reads the test as a claim about two live nodes. Verification: commerce + route suites 968 passed; typecheck and lint clean.

The catalog AppView, and the first real port (§10.2–§10.4, FR-A1–FR-A3, FR-A5–FR-A7)

Discovery is the WS-5 exit criterion — “a buyer with no prior supplier reference discovers and successfully quotes a live supplier” — and nothing on the AppView side existed. Three pure modules now do: catalog-verify (the §10.2 trust chain), catalog-projection (exact variants, never merged by name), and catalog-ingest-decision (what an arriving record means). 44 tests, 10 mutations, one of which survived and became a fix.

The interesting decision was how to share the contract. AppView deploys standalone with its own lockfile and cannot depend on @dina/commerce-protocol — the same boundary that makes the capability registry a byte-identical duplicate. Copying the module verbatim is not available here: commerce-protocol uses extensionless relative imports and AppView runs Node ESM, where those pass under vitest's bundler resolution and fail in production. Green tests plus broken production is worse than either alternative, so AppView carries an INDEPENDENT implementation, and the FROZEN VECTORS are what keep the two honest. The unit test reads conformance/vectors/catalog.json — the same bytes the protocol's own suite runs — and checks the digests and every chain refusal STRING. Those vectors were written so a port could be checked rather than trusted; this is the first port, and it works.

What is shared and what is not. Digest math and pointer-advance strings are frozen and identical. Composition-level refusals are NOT: Core's ingester says “catalog feed: …” because it is describing a fetch that lied, and AppView says “pointer: …” because it is describing a record that lied. Forcing one wording would make one of them inaccurate to its own operator. What must agree is the set of situations refused, and that is what the tests pin on both sides.

Three rules worth naming. (1) Identity comes from the identifier, never the name — an index that merged on name would answer a question about product A with product B's reviews, and nothing downstream could tell. (2) FR-A7 is enforced by the row SHAPE: there is no column for live stock or buyer authorization, so a future item carrying them cannot leak them into the index. (3) A snapshot no pointer names is never indexed, which is how a withdrawn or superseded catalog would otherwise come back to life; and both Jetstream delivery orders reach the same index through ONE decision function, because two copies of “which snapshot is current” would eventually disagree — the exact disagreement the pointer exists to settle.

The surviving mutation. The product key was separator-joined, and my “cannot be spliced into a collision” test used a pair that only LOOKED like a collision — the two keys differed by one character in a place no assertion looked at, so replacing the length-prefix encoding with join(':') passed. The real collision is {value:'A:B', issuer:'C'} against {value:'A', issuer:'B:C'}; both render custom:A:B:C:. Test corrected, mutation now dies. Separately, the file was first written with a raw NUL byte as the separator — which makes grep treat the source as binary and silently find nothing in it — so the tree was scanned again for control bytes (clean). Verification: AppView unit suite 1,969 passed across 94 files; the 5 pre-existing tsc errors are in an unrelated capability test and none are in these files.

Wired, not left as orphans. Three tables (commerce_catalog_pointers / _snapshots / _products), two zod record schemas, and two Jetstream handlers now carry a published catalog from the firehose to the index. The split across three tables is the design: the pointer is AUTHORITY, the snapshot is EVIDENCE keyed by content digest, and the products are the projection — which is what lets both delivery orders reach the same index without either record being able to make a snapshot current on its own. A withdrawal deletes the products and KEEPS the pointer row, because that row is what refuses a silent relaunch under the same catalog_id.

One subtlety the handler had to get right. A PENDING pointer is not the predecessor. It indexed nothing, so treating it as current would make the chain refuse the supplier's next publication for a gap they did not create — and they would have no way to see why. There is a test for exactly that, and a wiring test that asserts all three registries agree, because a record silently ignored looks precisely like a supplier who published nothing.

Adding two collections broke three census tests that hard-coded “20 collections”; they now say 22 and carry minimal records for both. That is the right kind of failure — the counts exist so a new collection cannot be half-registered. Verification: AppView 1,969 tests across 94 files, all passing; tsc unchanged at the 5 pre-existing errors. Still open in WS-5: the search xRPC (5.6), the relationship projection (5.8), and integration tests against real Postgres — the handler tests use a call-recording stub, which proves the ORDER of operations and not that the SQL is valid.

Discovery: search, and the relationship graph that must not merge (§10.5, §10.7, FR-A3–FR-A8)

WS-5.5 through 5.8 are done, which closes the WS-5 exit criterion: a buyer with no prior supplier reference can now find one. Search is a pure matcher behind an xRPC endpoint; the relationship graph is a second projection over the same rows.

The search score is recall, not a verdict. retrieval_score_bp says how sure the index is that a row is what was asked for. It is not permission and not the buyer's ranking — that happens in the buyer's own node against signed quotes. Conflating the two would let an index operator move commercial outcomes by tuning recall, which is the failure §10.6's multiple-AppView design exists to survive. Identifier weight exceeds category plus text plus region combined, because a GTIN match IS the product. Ties break on a key with no commercial meaning. Every candidate names WHY it matched, and the validator refuses an empty matched_fields: a result nobody can explain is indistinguishable from a paid placement.

SQL narrows, the pure matcher decides. Every row the query returns is re-checked by the matcher, so a WHERE clause that drifted from the scoring rules cannot silently change results. The narrowing is deliberately a SUPERSET — narrower would lose a candidate before the contract ever saw it.

The frozen §10.5 vector found a real bug on its first run. It is the first vector written for a CONSUMER rather than a publisher: the protocol side asserts its validator accepts the candidate, the AppView side asserts its projection produces it, and that pair is the only thing keeping a standalone deployment and a package it cannot import agreeing. It immediately rejected what AppView was emitting — regions as {scheme, code} under an invented iso3166-2 scheme, where §9.0's RegionRef is {scheme, value} over a closed vocabulary, and issuer_did dropped on a custom region. Regions are now carried verbatim and only flattened for comparison. Without the vector this would have shipped and been found by a client.

The relationship graph is a projection, never a merge. A merge is irreversible in practice: once two identities are one row, the reviews, the order history and the lineage of both are indistinguishable, and no later evidence separates them again. So exact variants stay separate documents forever and an edge only ever says "these two are related, here is who said so". Three ordered thresholds — show-as-related, inherit-standing, authorize-substitution — as separate constants, because one confidence number with three call sites drifts toward whichever constant was edited last. A model-suggested edge is arithmetically capped below the standing threshold whatever it reports, and the standing check tests the source again so a hand-built edge cannot route around the cap.

Disagreement is exposed, not resolved. Two claims naming different parents land on ONE edge marked disputed, with both claims kept, and a disputed edge never carries standing whatever its confidence. Deleting the loser is exactly the silent merge §10.7 forbids. Edges are DERIVED from stored claims rather than mutated in place, because a withdrawal has to be able to un-dispute an edge and an in-place update cannot. And first_party comes from the publishing repo, never from the record — a self-declared trust level is not evidence.

17 mutations across the two lanes, all killed. Worth recording: the first relationship batch reported nine mutations that had not landed, because the shell-embedded python driver silently no-op'd every replacement. Rewritten as a python driver with an explicit assert old in s — a substitution that does not land is a silent pass, which is the same shape of lie as a surviving mutation reported as killed. Two smaller fixes came from writing the tests: a stub that consumed two queue entries per query, and my own wrong assertion about a fixture's claim_id. Verification: AppView 2,014 tests across 97 files; commerce-protocol 311; repo typecheck clean. Recall EXPANSION is now wired too: a query by identifier reaches products across edges at or above the SHOW threshold, capped per query so a heavily-claimed product cannot turn one lookup into a graph walk. A row reached that way scores below category-plus-text — an edge improves recall without winning on precision — names related in its matched fields, and carries the claim ids that put it there so a buyer can see whose claim was responsible. A direct hit is never additionally paid for being related to itself, and expansion does not widen the freshness rule; four mutations pin all four of those. Open in WS-5: integration tests against real Postgres. The handler tests use a call-recording stub, which proves the ORDER of operations and not that the SQL is valid.

Approval and execution bind one payload (§15.2, §15.2b, FR-P5, WS-7.5)

The attack: a card says “500 chairs, ₹50,000, from ChairMaker”, the owner taps approve, and between the tap and the send something hands the executor a different order. Every downstream check still passes, because each one validates the order it was GIVEN. Nothing compared it to what was on the card. So approval now mints a payload covering every §15.2 field and a digest over it, and execution rebuilds the payload from what it is about to do.

Why a payload and not just the order digest. The order digest covers the order. It does not cover which install is about to send it, which capability, which manifest CID, which config revision, or which principal approved under what authority — and a swap of any of those is a different act by a different actor with the same paperwork. Five of the bait-and-switch cases change nothing but the actor, and an order-digest comparison would pass all five.

Its own digest domain, deliberately. The ten §9.12 domains are RECORDS that cross the wire between businesses. An approval payload never leaves the node — it is local evidence binding what a human saw to what the machine did — so widening the frozen list to hold it would put a private record in a public vocabulary and break the frozen vectors.

The builder refuses rather than filling gaps. The first version bound the empty string when a card supplied no label or product key, and two bait-and-switch cases passed while proving nothing: when NEITHER side of a comparison is supplied, the field binds to a constant and carries no information — the payload looks §15.2-compliant and binds nothing. It now returns the missing fields instead. `null` (the card showed no per-line price) and ABSENT (the card never said) stay distinct, because collapsing them is how a bound field stops being bound.

The supplier half is wired into the decision path, before the acknowledgement is recorded. A runner that answers `accepted` where the supplier's human approved `rejected` is refused and nothing moves — checking afterwards would leave the chain moved on something nobody approved, with only a compensating entry left as a remedy. The buyer half has nowhere to hang yet: the procurement routes deliberately plan and rank without sending, so Core contains no point where a buyer order is dispatched. It sits on the orphan ledger with that reason.

12 mutations, 10 killed, and both survivors taught something rather than being argued away. The payload KIND in the digest prefix was redundant — it is already a canonical field of both payload types — so it was removed rather than kept as decoration. The (digest) fallback branch is unreachable by construction, because the field walk stores the same canonical bytes the digest is taken over; the comment now says so and cites the surviving mutation as the evidence, instead of implying a test covers it. A third mutation exposed a weak assertion: substituting empty digests for a missing order also refuses, by mismatching, so the test that only checked the verdict could not tell the two apart — it now checks the message. Verification: core 6,987 (+39), core-server 2,841; typecheck and lint clean.

The buyer never sends it twice (§12.7, FR-P6, FR-P10, WS-7.7/7.8)

An order may have reached the supplier while the acknowledgement was lost. Everything in this item exists to stop one thing: the node deciding, on a clock or on a denial, that an effect which may have fired did not — because each of those decisions places a duplicate order for real goods.

The rule with teeth. `never_received` is the only outcome that authorizes resubmission, and §12.7 makes it legal only when the buyer presented no supplier-signed evidence: a supplier that cannot find the order but was handed an acknowledgement bearing its own signature must RE-ADOPT rather than deny. So the buyer checks the legality of the answer it received instead of trusting it, and stays parked when the answer is unsound. `received_unresolved` never authorizes resubmission however long it loops, and there is deliberately no buyer-side timeout converting it to a terminal state.

Durable, because the spec says so and because the crash that loses an acknowledgement is exactly when memory resets. Every transition is a value the caller persists, and `poll_count` is a column. The stored acknowledgement is RE-VALIDATED on read rather than parsed and cast — it is a supplier's signed commitment and the row is editable by anything with the database open. The static guard that forbids parsing a stored record outside the rehydration module caught this the moment the store was written, which is the guard doing precisely its job.

One projection, shared (FR-P10). The owner route returns the read model rather than raw state, so mobile and web cannot disagree about whether `outcome_unknown` means “failed”. That reading is what puts a second order on the wire, so nothing here says an order failed unless the supplier said so and was entitled to.

Eight mutations, seven killed, and the survivor was the interesting one: offering `resend` unconditionally on the `never_received` branch passed every test, because every path that reached that branch in a test happened to be authorized. The read model renders whatever is in the STORE and cannot lean on the state machine's invariants — a row written by an older build, or migrated, is exactly where the dangerous action would be worst. A test now renders that row directly. Verification: core 7,010 (+23), core-server 2,841; typecheck and lint clean. Open: nothing in Core submits a buyer order, so the state machine's write half sits on the orphan ledger with WS-3.6 named as its blocker — stated here rather than left to be discovered.

Commerce was server-only, and nothing said so (§23, WS-3.6)

The mobile boot never created a commerce runtime. It only tore one down — teardown has always called installCommerceRuntime(null) — so the code READ as though mobile had commerce, and every commerce path on the product surface found null and failed closed. That is the worst shape a gap can take: failing closed is exactly what a correctly-refusing node looks like, so neither a test nor a user could tell the difference. The mobile app is the product, so this was a real gap rather than a staging decision.

Both roots now compose the runtime the same way, reading identity and epoch through thunks that THROW rather than defaulting. §16.2 makes commerce signing fail-closed, and a ?? '' in either thunk would be the whole fence: a node signing under a guessed identity or a missing epoch produces commitments it cannot stand behind.

The guard is STATIC and compares the two roots, because a behavioural test would need a booted app and an assertion about one root would pass while the other quietly dropped the wiring. It asserts creation on both and teardown on mobile only — a phone signs out and back in inside one JS process, the server boots one identity per process — an asymmetry my first version wrongly demanded be symmetric, and the server failed it for a difference that is correct. Verification: mobile 3,298 passed across 225 suites; typecheck and lint clean.

The keystone, built (§9.9, §12.7, §15.2, FR-P5, FR-P6 — new WS-3.7)

The item below described three rules each ending at the same edge. It is now built: buyer_executor.ts is the one place a purchase order leaves a buyer node. It was not a named WBS row — WS-3.6 covers the composition root, WS-7 covers the surfaces, and every one of them assumed this component existed between them.

It still does not own transport. The sender is injected, because egress belongs to the service-query lane that already carries the four gates, signing and MsgBox; a second egress path inside Core would be a second place to forget one of them. What the executor owns is the ORDER of operations: refuse a duplicate, verify the §15.2 binding, RECORD then send, settle through the one §12.7 machine.

Step three is the argument. Recording before sending means a crash in between leaves a record for an order that never left — which reconcile resolves safely as never_received. Sending first means a crash after the send leaves NO record of an order that may exist, and nothing ever asks about it. The first mistake is recoverable by contract; the second is a silent lost order. The sender's three outcomes carry their obligations in the TYPE rather than in a comment: not_sent is a promise that nothing crossed the boundary and is the only one that authorizes a resend, so a sender reporting a timeout as not_sent would authorize a duplicate.

The journey now meets end to end. The retailer→manufacturer scenario runs its order step through this executor into ChairMaker's real provider ingress: Sancho's node verifies the binding, records, sends; the manufacturer's runner takes the job; Core signs the acknowledgement; the buyer settles from the SIGNED record rather than the runner's JSON, and the owner card offers “view acknowledgement” with no resend anywhere near it. Two shipped surfaces meeting, rather than one test calling both halves.

A surviving mutation found a real divergence from the spec. Swapping received_unresolved for received_processing changed nothing observable — because the state machine had collapsed both into outcome_unknown. §12.7 puts them in different states on purpose: processing means the decision has not reached the external boundary, so the buyer is still simply waiting; unresolved means the effect MAY have fired. Neither authorizes resubmission, so the collapse was not a safety hole — it was the loss of the only thing the owner can act on. Split, with the reason recorded. Three other mutations crashed as compile errors and were rewritten shape-preserving; all seven now die. Verification: core 7,025 (+15), core-server 2,841; typecheck and lint clean. Open: one line in each composition root — nothing binds the service-query sender yet, so the submit route answers 503, which is fail-closed and visible.

Both remaining buyer-lane gaps, closed (§11.2a, §12.7, §16.2)

The sender is bound in both roots. It runs over the same service.query egress every other outbound capability uses, so the four gates, signing and MsgBox apply without a second path to keep in step. The mapping from transport result to executor outcome lives in tested code rather than as a lambda in two composition roots, because the two directions of error are not symmetric: getting it wrong safely costs a needless reconcile, and getting it wrong the other way authorizes a duplicate order for real goods.

So a THROW is reported as AMBIGUOUS, not as “not sent”. A transport that throws has not told us whether anything left — the outbox may already hold the message, or the socket may have closed after the write. not_sent is reached only when a gate names itself. And a SUCCESSFUL dispatch is also ambiguous, because the acknowledgement arrives later through the response bridge: that is the honest description of an asynchronous lane, and it is exactly the state §12.7 was written for rather than a shortcoming.

The re-poll is a sweep, not a timer per order. A per-order timer is state to leak, to double-fire after a restart, and to forget to cancel when the order settles; the sweep re-reads the store each pass, so a settled order simply stops appearing. It ASKS and does not DECIDE — every answer goes back through applyReconcileResult, so the poller cannot drift from the submit path about what the same answer means. It never authorizes a resubmission of its own accord however long it loops, a transport failure is not treated as an answer, and an order it can no longer describe is SKIPPED rather than asked: asking without a digest and an idempotency key invites the one answer that must never arrive by accident.

Verification: core 7,035 (+10) · core-server 2,841 · mobile 3,298; typecheck and lint clean. The buyer lane no longer has a ledger entry. What remains on it for §12.7 is a scheduler TICK that calls the sweep, which is a scheduler concern rather than a commerce one and is named as such.

The comparison card, and a stale row (§18.4, WS-7.4 / WS-7.6)

§18.4 asks for something unusual and the implementation takes it literally: the result must stay useful on the GENERIC CardSpec fallback. So every baseline field is an ordered label/value line a renderer with no commerce knowledge can print, and an absent value reads “not stated” rather than leaving a blank where a number should be. A card that only reads well inside its own custom view reads badly everywhere else — which is exactly the device an owner is holding when the new block has not shipped yet.

It explains rather than re-decides. The “why” lines are the ranking's own components, and they report CONTRIBUTION rather than the raw factor score: an owner asking why this offer won is asking what moved the total, not how it did on a factor in isolation. The incomparable list is read off what the ranking RECORDED as missing — a first draft compared the offers itself and worked out which fields were stated unevenly, which is precisely the second opinion that would eventually contradict the score it claims to explain. Every excluded offer is named with its reason, never counted: “2 offers excluded” is the sentence that hides the one the owner wanted to see. The verb is “Review order”, never “Buy now”.

The card is built INSIDE the choose route so it travels with the decision. Leaving each client to render from the raw ranking is how one of them ends up offering a buy button.

WS-7.6 was a stale row. The supplier-side approval payload was built and wired with 7.5 and the WBS still listed it as TODO — corrected, because a plan that understates what is done is as misleading as one that overstates it. Two fixture bugs surfaced while testing, both mine: a hand-built evidence fixture used meanRatingBp: 0 where the module treats null as “nothing known”, so the headline claimed exact evidence nobody had — the same confusion the module refuses to make downstream — and a two-argument Jest assertion that does not exist. Verification: core 7,046 (+11); typecheck and lint clean.

Settings are policy, so they live where policy is enforced (§18.2, §18.3, §19 — WS-7.2 / WS-7.3)

A fan-out ceiling stored on a phone is a ceiling that stops applying when the server acts, and a blocked-supplier list only the UI knows is a list nothing enforces. So buyer and supplier settings live in Core beside the engines that read them, and both are now WIRED rather than stored: `/procurement/plan` takes its ceiling and block-list from settings and honours a smaller request but never a larger one, and the provider ingress consults the listing policy before any probing budget is spent.

Refused, not clamped — and the two are not in conflict. The fan-out planner clamps a policy it is handed, because refusing at dispatch time turns a bad config into a failed purchase. The settings validator refuses, because it runs where the OWNER TYPES the number: silently turning 50 into 8 leaves them believing something about their node that is not true, and they would only find out when a supplier they expected to hear from never answered. Both rules are correct at their own layer, and the comment at each site says so.

A supplier both preferred and blocked is refused, not resolved by precedence. Either answer guesses what the owner meant, and the guess that prefers a blocked supplier sends them business they said they did not want. Every finding is reported rather than the first: an owner fixing a settings screen one refusal at a time gives up on the third round trip.

Credentials cannot reach a settings record. §18.3 asks for credential STATUS — whether it works, when it was checked — and a settings record is the most-read, most-exported, most-synced object a node has. A connector entry carrying an api key, secret, token, password or private key is refused, and the finding names the FIELD without echoing the value: reporting a leak by repeating it turns one leak into two.

The listing check runs before the probing budget. A paused listing that still answered would be a supplier ignoring their customers rather than one who is closed, and spending a peer's budget on a question this node was never going to answer would penalise them for the owner's decision. It returns the SAME refusal code as a budget refusal, because §14.3 wants one refusal for every reason — a prober who can tell them apart reads the catalog from the shape of the “no”.

Wiring the ingress check broke five existing tests, and the fix was in the TESTS: their runtime doubles were casts that omitted a field the type promises. A double that claims to be a `CommerceRuntime` and is not is a lie the cast hides, and adding a `?.` in production to survive a shape only tests produce would have been the wrong repair. Settings are also validated on READ, because the row is editable by anything with the database open and a tampered `quoteAccess` on a paused listing is a supplier answering customers they closed the door on — that made the store trip the stored-record parse guard, which is the guard working: it parses and immediately re-validates through the same validator the write path uses. Verification: core 7,069 (+23) · core-server 2,841; typecheck and lint clean.

The supplier inbox, ordered by what it costs to ignore (§18.6, WS-7.3)

A projection over state that already exists — undecided orders, listing and connector settings, catalog freshness — assembled into the seven things §18.6 says an operator sees. One function rather than seven screens' worth of queries, because the item nobody built a screen for is the item nobody sees, and the two candidates for that are exactly the ones that cost money: an order sitting undecided past its deadline, and an external outcome nobody resolved.

Ordered by consequence, not recency. A stale catalog is a slow leak; an order about to time out is a customer who ordered and heard nothing. Sorting by arrival buries the second on a busy day, which is the day it matters. The urgency values are spread out rather than 1/2/3 so a future item can be inserted between two without renumbering the rest — renumbering is how an ordering silently changes for items nobody touched.

Two distinctions the inbox refuses to blur. An order whose effect phase is `effect_started` is reported as UNRESOLVED rather than waiting: the external boundary was crossed, so deciding now could double the work. And a closed listing ranks BELOW a failing connector on purpose — a closed listing is usually a decision, and nagging an operator about their own choice is how an inbox becomes something people stop reading.

Reading the undecided list required adding `listReserved()` to the CommerceOrder AGGREGATE rather than reaching into the repository. That is ARCH-0's whole point, and the alternative — a caller going around the aggregate — is what the boundary guard forbids; exposing a READ costs nothing the aggregate was protecting, because the state-changing mutators stay hidden. Verification: core 7,081 (+12) · core-server 2,841; typecheck and lint clean.

“Both” means two installs, and that is a safety rule (§18.1, FR-P1, WS-7.1)

§18.1 is one sentence and it is the whole item: “Both” creates two installs/consent decisions, not one superset install. It reads like a UX preference and is not. A superset install gives one consent record authority over both sides of a trade — revoking selling would revoke buying, a compromise of the supplier runner would carry buyer authority with it, and an owner reviewing “what did I agree to” would read one list covering two businesses.

The plan refuses three ways that could blur: two roles sharing a manifest (the superset in disguise), “both” collapsing to one install, and a manifest carrying the other role's capability. The last is CHECKED rather than assumed, because the manifests are data a pack author edits — giving the buyer manifest an order_status capability is exactly the change that would silently hand a buyer install a supplier's authority, and nothing else in the system would notice.

The read side matches: roleIsInstalled answers per role, and the inbox route uses it, because “is any commerce install present?” is precisely the shortcut a superset install would have made correct. And it PLANS rather than installs — the install machinery owns repo proofs, pending expiry and activation, and a route that did both would put a consent decision behind one tap.

A pleasant side effect: the two reference packs stop being documentation-only and become the manifests this installs, so they leave the orphan ledger. Verification: core 7,090 (+9) · core-server 2,841; typecheck and lint clean. WBS now 46 DONE, 26 PART, 19 TODO — and every one of the 19 is in WS-8 (multi-tenant hosting), WS-9 (external connectors), WS-10 (plural AppViews and third-party packs) or the infrastructure test rows, which is stated plainly in the closing note below rather than left as a list that looks like ordinary backlog.

I was wrong about what needed infrastructure (§7.2, §7.3, WS-8.4)

I grouped WS-8 as “multi-tenant hosting, needs a control plane” and stopped. Re-reading the rows, 8.4 is nothing of the kind: staff authority and the acting-for chain are pure authorization logic, they are security-critical, and they needed no infrastructure at all. The grouping was mine and it was wrong; the row is now built and wired.

Correction, added later. “Built and wired” was half true and the wrong half. It was built. It was not wired: the evaluation hung off an OPTIONAL argument no route supplied, so none of what this section describes ran on a real order. See The rule that had a caller and still never ran below for what was actually wrong and what closed it. I have left the original paragraph standing rather than editing it quiet, because a note that silently corrects itself is no more trustworthy than the claim it replaced.

§7.2 says caller-supplied body fields establish no identity. This module cannot verify that on its own, so it does the next best thing: the chain is a VALUE the caller assembles from authenticated sources, and a gap in it is a refusal rather than a default. A function that accepted an order and dug the principal out of its body would make the violation invisible — which is exactly how it would happen.

§7.3 says the contract must not encode “one phone equals the organization”. So there is no owner branch that skips evaluation. The pilot's single approver is a CONFIGURATION with one grant in it, and an owner with no grant record is not an owner. The contract still expresses buyer spend ceilings, category buyers, branch authority, supplier sales and delegation that expires on its own.

Three places the arithmetic fails toward refusing. A ceiling in another currency does not convert — converting would make an authority decision depend on a rate nobody approved. A category buyer must cover EVERY category, not any, because an order spanning an authorized and an unauthorized one is not half-authorized. And an amount this code cannot read exceeds every ceiling: an unreadable total that slipped UNDER a ceiling is the one arithmetic bug that spends money.

Quorum takes the SET of approvers rather than a count — two approvals from one person is one person approving twice — and it distinguishes “a second person would permit it” from a flat refusal, because those send an operator to completely different places.

Verification: core 7,118 (+28); typecheck and lint clean. One core-server run showed a single failure that named no test in its output and did not reproduce across four subsequent full runs (2,841 passing each time). I cannot name it, so I am recording it rather than calling the gate unqualified — it is the same unreproduced flake class noted earlier in this file.

Three items blocked on the same missing piece (open question — RESOLVED above)

Worth surfacing rather than leaving in three separate ledger entries: the buyer-side approval binding (§15.2), the buyer reconciliation write half (§12.7), and the procurement send step all wait on the same thing — a component that DISPATCHES a buyer order. Core deliberately has none: the procurement routes plan and rank without sending, because egress belongs to the service-query sender that already carries gating, signing and transport.

That component is not a named WBS row. WS-3.6 covers the composition root, WS-7 covers the surfaces, and the executor between them is assumed by both. It is the keystone for an end-to-end retailer→manufacturer run on a real node, and I would rather flag it than keep landing items that each end with the same sentence.

The credential broker has no read API, and that shapes everything (§8.3, FR-P4, WS-9.3)

§8.3 says two things, and the second is the one that designs the module: connector secrets must go through a Core-owned broker, and a generic secret read API is forbidden. If a runner could ask “give me the ERP token”, every other control is decoration — the secret is then in a runner’s memory, its logs, and whatever it sends next. So a caller does not GET a credential. It asks the broker to PERFORM a named operation, and the broker is the only thing that ever sees the material.

Three consequences worth stating, because each was a choice:

  • The store is the one authority for the material AND the permission. An earlier shape took the grants as a constructor array; that made a revoked credential keep working until the process restarted, because the array was policy sampled at boot. Grants are read per call, so forget() stops the next one.
  • Credential-shaped params are refused, not stripped. A caller that put a token in params has a bug, and quietly removing it would leave that bug shipping secrets everywhere else it also passes them. §8.3 names “workflow payloads” explicitly.
  • §18.3 status is derived, never typed. lastResult comes from real brokered calls. A credential status somebody typed stopped being true the moment the other end rotated theirs, which is exactly when an owner needs to know.

Rotation is a replace, not a version, and it resets the verdict: keeping the old ok would tell an owner their new credential works before anything has used it. The rotation route answers {ok: true, resource} and nothing else — no prefix, no length, no hash, because each narrows a guess and none helps an owner decide anything.

The lease is built and deliberately unwired (§8.3, WS-9.3)

§8.3 permits a narrowly scoped credential lease when a connector genuinely cannot work through a typed operation, bound to tenant, install, resource, operation and expiry. All five are required and all five are compared — a lease checked on four of them works for a fifth thing nobody consented to.

Nothing calls it, and it sits on the orphan ledger with that reason. Both connectors that exist go through broker.perform, so adding a route that issues leases would put a credential-leasing endpoint on the node with nothing to redeem them — attack surface bought with no working feature. The check is written now so the connector that finally needs one gets the five-way binding rather than inventing a fourth.

“Connector replacement does not change capability semantics” is structural, not tested (§24, WS-9.1)

That is §24’s acceptance sentence for the connector work, and the honest way to satisfy it is to make it impossible to violate rather than to write a test that compares two importers. So catalog_import.ts was split: parseCatalogCsv and catalogRowsFromRecords READ, and importCatalogRows — one function — decides what a catalog item is.

A second importer for JSON would have passed its own tests and disagreed with the first about a duplicate identifier, an out-of-vocabulary unit, or a variant whose parent is missing. Nobody would have noticed until a supplier switched backend and their catalog quietly changed. The tests still compare the two paths, but on EQUIVALENT-not-identical inputs — a CSV and a JSON feed carrying the same data — because feeding both the same bytes would only prove one function is deterministic.

One detail that looks fussy and is not: JSON values are stringified rather than passed through. The normalizer’s checks are written against text, and a raw number reaching them would store a number where every other path stores a string — two connectors, two shapes, one of them wrong. A nested value (a localised {en: "Oak chair"}) reads as ABSENT rather than [object Object], so a row whose identifier is nested is refused instead of published under a nonsense identity.

The credential must not cross an origin, and the URL policy would have let it (§10.3, WS-9.1)

This is the finding I would most want reviewed. fetchUnderPolicy follows redirects and re-validates every hop — scheme, literal address, connected address, content type. A redirect from the supplier’s ERP to some public CDN passes all of it. Without a further rule, the Authorization header would have travelled with it.

So the executor attaches the credential only when the hop’s origin matches the configured endpoint’s, compared through the URL parser rather than by prefix (https://a.com and https://a.com.evil.net share a prefix). There is no query-parameter auth option at all: a query string reaches proxy logs and browser history, and offering the option is how somebody picks it.

Mobile gets no networked connector, on purpose (§6.5, §10.3, WS-9.1)

§10.3 requires re-checking the address actually connected to, which is the defence against a hostname that resolves to a private address after passing the URL check. React Native’s fetch cannot report it, and a transport that answered with a separate DNS lookup would read as if the check had happened when it had not. So the mobile composition root supplies no credentialExecutors, every networked brokered call there refuses no_executor, and §6.5’s base no-code supplier — a CSV the owner uploads — works in full. The server uses node:https rather than fetch for the same reason, plus manual redirects.

Changing backend is a consent question, and widening is the test (§6.5, WS-9.1)

§6.5: selecting a different backend that widens the declared domains, credential resources or operations is a new install or re-consent event, never an ordinary config edit. classifyConnectorChange compares MEMBERSHIP, not counts — swapping one ERP host for another at equal breadth is still a host the owner never consented to. Narrowing is always ordinary: an owner may give a plugin less, and what a connector may actually reach is read from the current declaration at every call.

Treating every edit as a consent event was the alternative, and it is worse than not asking: it trains owners to click through consent screens.

What the mutation round found (WS-9.1 / WS-9.3)

38 shape-preserving mutations, 38 killed — but only after four survivors exposed real gaps and five mutations turned out not to compile (a “survivor” that is really a crash, which the harness now distinguishes by reading the suite line as well as the test line):

  • An unreadable operations_json was tested only through the JSON.parse THROW. Valid JSON of the wrong shape ({"read_catalog": true}) took the other branch and no test covered it — a corrupted row could have widened what a secret was good for.
  • A nested JSON value in a catalog row had no test at all.
  • The unparseable-URL branch of the origin check is genuinely unreachable through an executor (the fetch policy refuses a malformed URL first), so sameOrigin is now exported and driven directly. A rule nothing can reach is a rule nothing can check.
  • One mutation was a no-op I had written badly — a reminder that a surviving mutation is a finding about the MUTATION as often as about the tests.

A separate trap cost a full sweep: grep "^Tests:" matches nothing because jest emits ANSI bold before the word, and zsh does not word-split an unquoted variable, so nine test paths in a shell variable reached jest as ONE pattern that matched zero files. Both failures read as “every mutation survived”.

A declaration is not evidence, and that is the whole of WS-9.4 (§15.5)

§15.5 ends: “No layer may declare idempotency merely because Dina deduplicates its own task row. The real external order system must deduplicate the effect; until a connector proves that with the same key and retention window, automatic resubmission stays disabled and ambiguity resolves through 12.7.”

So what the node accepts is an OBSERVATION, not a setting. The evidence route is deliberately not a switch: an owner records a probe — the same key sent twice — and the verdict follows from what the external system did. Five ways to fail it, each a real one:

  • No probe. The “merely because” case, and the default for every connector nobody tested.
  • A second order was created, or the two attempts resolved to different references. An EMPTY reference counts as a mismatch too: a connector that reports nothing has not shown the attempts converged, it has shown it cannot tell.
  • The two attempts were under a minute apart. An in-flight request lock satisfies that, and an in-flight lock is exactly the mechanism that stops working at the moment a retry matters — after a crash.
  • The window is shorter than §15.5’s sum (quote validity + reconciliation + commercial retention). An ERP that deduplicates for 24 hours is an honest NO to a retry after a two-day outage.
  • The observation is older than 90 days. A vendor can change their deduplication window in a release nobody told the supplier about; evidence with no expiry would let one probe authorise retries for the life of the install.

Open question. §15.5 names the sum but not the numbers, and the numbers decide whether any real connector can clear the bar. The default is 7 + 30 + 90 days = 127 days of key retention. That is demanding — many ERPs keep idempotency keys for 24 hours to 90 days — and §15.5’s own wording (“the CONFIGURED commercial-retention period”) says this is policy rather than protocol. Please confirm the three components, or tell me the commercial-retention period you actually want; the requirement is already a parameter, so lowering it is a config change rather than a code one.

The order of two writes is the safety property (§9.9 step 3, §15.5, WS-9.4)

mark effect_started → cross the boundary → record what came back, and never any other order. A crash between the mark and the call leaves a row saying “the effect may have fired”, which §9.9 refuses to time out — costly, and correct. The reverse would leave an executed external order behind a record that still looks safe to expire, which is how a supplier delivers goods against an order they believe they never accepted.

Two consequences fall out of it. An order that is ALREADY effect_started is never attempted again, even with proven evidence — crash recovery is not a retry of a known-failed attempt, and the honest answer there is reconciliation. And a broker refusal decided BEFORE any network I/O (wrong install, undeclared operation, no credential) is reported as refused_before_sending, because the broker settles every authorization check before it opens the secret store. operation_failed is deliberately absent from that set: a throw, a timeout or an error answer may still have created the order.

Every attempt carries the key the order was ADMITTED under, read from the record. A retry with a fresh key is not a retry; it is a second order wearing the same intention. The route ignores an idempotency_key in the request body entirely, and there is a test that passes one to prove it.

The fulfilment seam proposes; it never signs (§12.7, §9.11, WS-9.5)

The naive version of this seam reads the warehouse system’s status and signs it. That makes an external tool an authority over a chain buyers rely on: a mis-scan briefly reporting delivered would produce a SIGNED claim of delivery, and §9.11 has no way back from a signed state.

So reconcileFulfilment returns advance | unchanged | needs_attention and the caller signs. Three rules, each preventing something real: never backwards (a preparing webhook landing after a dispatched one would make a buyer watch their delivery un-happen); never a jump the §9.11 graph forbids; and an unchanged state is not an update (polling returns the same state constantly, and signing each one costs a signature apiece for a record that says nothing).

A refusal is needs_attention, not silence. A backwards or illegal report is evidence the two systems disagree, which is precisely the §12.7 case an operator has to see; dropping it would keep the disagreement invisible until a buyer complained.

Deviation worth stating. Fulfilment arrives by POLLING through the broker, not by webhook. A webhook would be an inbound path with no authentication this node controls; polling reuses the one outbound lane that already carries the credential rules and the §10.3 fetch policy. It is slower, and it is the only version whose trust boundary I can state in a sentence.

Two more findings from the mutation round (WS-9.4 / WS-9.5)

24 mutations, 24 killed — after two survivors and four that did not compile. The survivors were the same class as last time: a query with no direct test.

  • listWithExternalRef was exercised only through a route double, so both the SQL != '' filter and its in-memory twin could be removed without any test noticing. The empty string is what the effect executor records when an external system answers without a reference, and an empty reference cannot be looked up any more than a missing one — a sweep including it would ask the ERP about nothing and report the order missing. Now parity-tested against real SQLCipher and the double, in the harness that already existed for exactly this.
  • Four mutations were “survivors” that were really compile failures, and one was a no-op I wrote badly (a guard I meant to disable that still fired on the test fixture). Both are reminders that a surviving mutation is a finding about the MUTATION at least as often as about the tests.
