§10.7 forbids an implementation by name, so the replacement had to be justified (§10.7, FR-A8, WS-10.3)

“Dina preserves the exact variant results and exposes material grouping disagreement instead of silently choosing whichever merge produces the highest score.” Taking the best answer is also the obvious implementation — ask three directories, keep the strongest, move on — and it is banned because the strongest answer is the one most likely to be wrong in the direction that costs money: a substitution authorised by whichever index was most enthusiastic.

So consensus is the floor, not the peak. An edge every consulted view reports carries the LOWEST confidence any of them gave it, which means a threshold is crossed only when all of them agree it is. An edge only SOME report is kept — §10.7 explicitly allows weaker semantic edges to improve recall — but capped below the standing bar, so it can be shown and can never inherit standing or authorise a substitution.

A contested edge authorises nothing whatever its number. That is stricter than the threshold alone, and deliberately: an edge two directories disagree about is exactly the edge that must not quietly change what a buyer receives. It is still SHOWN, with its dispute — hiding it would be the silent choice the spec forbids, made in the other direction.

Not every difference is material. Two views at 3100 and 3300 basis points agree about everything a caller can do with the edge, and reporting that would train an owner to ignore the display. A disagreement is material when it CHANGES AN ANSWER: the views land on different sides of a threshold, or they name different objects for a relationship that has one.

Duplicated thresholds, on purpose. An AppView deploys standalone and cannot import from @dina/core, and Core must not trust an index to say where the substitution bar is — a compromised one could lower it by reporting its own number. The three constants are therefore copied, and a test reads the AppView source as TEXT and compares. (Importing it fails: the AppView is ESM with .js specifiers Core's resolver cannot follow, and a guard that fails for a resolver reason is a guard somebody deletes for being flaky.)

A bug the mutation round found, in production rather than in a test (WS-10.3)

Five mutations survived, all in the “contested” path, and writing the test that should have killed them exposed a real defect: conflicting objects were collected into a map keyed by AppView, so a SINGLE directory recording two competing parents overwrote itself and the conflict vanished. That is the worst case to miss — a directory that lists both candidates is the one that NOTICED the ambiguity. §10.7 cares about the disagreement, not about who is holding it. Conflicts are now detected across views and within one, and a view naming two parents shows as two positions.

The other four survivors were test gaps of one shape: every contested edge in the suite was ALSO capped for partial support, so the !contested check was never the thing doing the work. Removing it changed nothing the tests measured. The fix was a unanimous, high-confidence, upstream-disputed edge — where contested is the only thing standing between it and a substitution.

Promotion is a gate, and the additive rule is the half that bites (§11.1, §11.3, WS-10.6)

§11.1 is the argument for taking §11.3 literally: the existing official price_check uses a FLOATING-POINT price and permissive additional properties, and order_status has no pinned schemas at all. Both were promoted at some point by somebody who felt the semantics were stable, and commerce v1 consequently cannot reuse either.

So the seven proofs are checkable rather than asserted. “Stable semantics across multiple independent providers” is a count of distinct provider DIDs (not rows — one provider cannot be two by appearing twice) answering ONE pinned schema hash, at least twenty times each, over at least thirty days. “Interoperable failure codes” means every provider expressed both refused and unavailable: “you may not” and “not right now” lead a buyer to different next steps, and a provider that only ever emits one has not shown it can distinguish them.

The additive rule is checked FIRST, before any of that, because a promotion that would reinterpret an existing capability is refused however good its evidence is. What a promotion may do is ADD an official id and record the custom one as an alias; the custom id keeps working, because retiring it would break every install already calling it. The read side matters too: resolveCapabilityId reports via_alias, so a caller can SEE its id was translated rather than have the substitution hidden from it.

One survivor here was worth fixing: when providers disagree about the schema, the “stable” hash is the empty string rather than one of the competitors. Returning a competitor would hide the additive finding behind schema_not_stable, and an operator would fix the providers, re-promote, and only then discover the promotion also reinterprets. Both findings now surface together.

Open question. §11.3 says “multiple independent providers” without a number; I chose two. Three would be safer and would make promotion unreachable for capabilities only two suppliers in a category offer. Similarly the twenty-answer and thirty-day floors are mine. Tell me if you want them higher.

Thirteen transaction boundaries, scattered through three domain classes (§9.9, §12.7, §16.2 — WS-0.2b / WS-0.3b)

Before this pair of rows, this.deps.tx(() => …) appeared thirteen times inside CommerceAdmissionEngine, CommerceLifecycleEngine and CommerceEpochService. Every one was correct. Together they meant nobody could answer “what is atomic here?” without reading 2,500 lines, and nothing stopped a fourteenth from being added wrong.

Two ways it goes wrong, and both are silent until production. A NESTED transaction fails on mobile only — op-sqlite implements transactions with a raw BEGIN and cannot nest, while the reentrant server test runner calls straight through and passes. The codebase already carried two hand-written comments warning callers about that, which is the shape of a rule with no enforcement. A FORGOTTEN transaction lets the second of two writes fail alone, which is how an acknowledgement comes to exist without its hold settlement.

So the boundary became a thing with a name. CommerceTransaction owns the one runner and refuses a nested atomically with a message naming BOTH operations, on every platform, in every test. The engines hold no runner at all and expose only …InTx methods; CommerceAdmissionService and CommerceReconciliationService decide where a transaction begins.

What the refactor is NOT (WS-0.2b / WS-0.3b)

It is not a unit of work, an identity map, or a place to accumulate changes — those would be a layer for its own sake over code that does not need one. The coordinator runs a function inside a transaction and knows whether one is open. The domain classes kept every line of their logic; what they lost is the decision about where a transaction starts, which is an application concern and was never theirs.

The service method names are the engine's OLD ones, deliberately. Every caller — routes, the ingress bridge, the decision path, six test suites — already speaks that vocabulary, and renaming them would have made a transaction refactor look like a behaviour change in the diff. The one asymmetry: admitOrder's shape checks (wire validation, sender binding, protocol version) touch no storage and stay OUTSIDE the transaction, because opening one to reject a malformed document takes the write lock for a request that was never going to write.

§12.8's genesis seam is deliberately not wrapped. Acceptance and its status genesis must commit together, so admission calls the lifecycle ENGINE's method from inside the transaction it already opened. Adding a service wrapper for it would offer a second, non-atomic way to do the one thing that must be atomic — so the service exposes the engine through a getter for that single caller and wraps everything else.

The guard found the engine I had forgotten (WS-0.3b)

Two static guards keep the boundary where it moved: TxRunner or .tx( may appear only in transaction.ts and runtime.ts, and every public method on an engine must be named …InTx or …Record.

The first one failed on its first run, naming epoch_service.ts — a third engine with a transaction I had not counted, because it publishes the §16.2 restore epoch rather than handling orders and did not come up when I mapped “the two engines”. That is the guard doing exactly what the orphan ledger does elsewhere: turning “I looked and thought I had them all” into a fact the suite checks.

A naming collision was the other finding worth recording. The lifecycle engine already had PRIVATE signGenesisInTx and signStatusUpdateInTx helpers, so renaming the public methods collided with them — which confirmed the suffix already meant exactly this in the codebase. The private pair became …Record, which is a better name anyway: they take an already-resolved reference and head rather than looking anything up.

Tradeoff I considered and rejected. A smaller change would have kept deps.tx in the engines and only wrapped it in the coordinator for nesting detection — a tenth of the diff, most of the safety. I did the full extraction because WS-0.2b's DoD is literally “engines stop owning the transaction”, and because a coordinator the engines still called would leave them deciding WHEN a transaction starts, which is the half that makes composing two operations atomically impossible.

The fifth journey: a retailer buying from a manufacturer with a real ERP behind them (WS-9 / WS-10 arc)

Four journey tests already existed — the spine, the plugin lane, discovery-to-approval, and a restore. All four predate the connector work, and none of them touches a credential, an external effect, or a fulfilment update. Six modules built this session landed in that gap, each written alone and gated alone, which is this codebase's signature defect.

So there is now a fifth: ChairMaker configures their ERP connector, stores its credential, fails to prove idempotency, proves it on the second attempt, and fulfils Sancho's order through it. The owner steps go through the real router, because once /v1/commerce/credentials, /idempotency, /orders/effect and /orders/fulfilment/sweep exist there are two ways to reach the same rule, and a journey taking the shorter one keeps passing while the shipped surface disagrees.

The sequence asserts, in order: a connector refuses before its credential exists; the stored material is unreadable through every route on the surface; the catalog loads through the broker and the credential's last_result becomes ok from that real call; a probe showing a SECOND order leaves resubmission manual_only; one buyer alone cannot commit €4,500 but a buyer plus the owner can; an ambiguous ERP timeout produces exactly ONE call without evidence; the boundary once crossed is never crossed again even after the ERP recovers; and with proven evidence a retry happens with the same idempotency key both times — the assertion that would catch a second purchase order in a real ERP.

Four more cases cover the fulfilment side: legal progress advances, a repeat report is not an update, a dispatch with no lines is refused, and a preparing webhook arriving after dispatched becomes needs_attention rather than making a buyer watch their delivery un-happen.

Two bugs, both in my own test. The first expectation asserted the effect route's no_such_credential shape against the catalog-load route, which reports the BROKER's refusal instead — the route was right and the expectation was wrong. The second was worse and worth recording: the scripted ERP's failure switch was wired into submit but not into read_catalog, so a “refusing ERP” still served a catalog and the inbox test would have passed for the wrong reason. A scripted double is code, and it gets the same scrutiny as the code it stands in for.

What it does not claim. No real ERP — the broker's executor is the injected boundary, which is the architecture rather than a mock — no D2D socket, no AppView, no PDS. WS-9.2 and WS-11.6 stay open for exactly those reasons, and this test does not pretend to close them.

Three of §3.4's four typed host operations were never written (§3.4, FR-P9 — WS-3.5)

I had been reading TODO rows and treating the 28 PART rows as done-enough. They are not: PART means “built with a recorded defect against the spec”, and WS-3.5's recorded defect was “only the search operation exists — D2D send, publication candidate and connector broker are not written; no boot registers an executor for it”. That is three quarters of a spec section missing, sitting under a status I had skipped past.

One rule runs through all three. The authority comes from the install and the node, never from the params. So each executor REFUSES the field rather than ignoring it: a params from_did on a D2D send, a supplier_did on a publication candidate, an install_id on a broker call. Silently overriding would leave the runner believing it chose, and that belief survives until something depends on it.

  • D2D send checks the recipient against the install's permitted set. Without it, one approved send is a channel to every DID the node can resolve. Everything decidable is decided BEFORE the send; after that a throw means the bytes may have left, and the dispatcher's outcome_unknown is the honest settlement.
  • Publication candidate validates through the §12.1 leakage gate and returns validated, not published. An operation that did both would let a runner advance a chain buyers follow, which is the authority §12.1 keeps on the owner's side. It reports EVERY finding, because an operator fixing a catalog one refusal at a time gives up on the third round trip.
  • Connector broker is the seam that lets a runner reach §8.3's broker. What it adds is the install id coming from the PROPOSAL rather than the params — the one check the broker cannot make for itself, since it trusts whatever install id it is handed. Its pre-network refusals map to failed and its wire failures to outcome_unknown, because “you may not” and “it may have happened” lead an operator to opposite next steps.

The server boot now registers the connector-broker and publication-candidate executors. Before this, a permitted proposal for either answered no_executor — the honest failure for an operation this build cannot do, and the wrong answer for one it can.

Both gaps are now closed, and one of them was worse than the note said. The mobile boot composed NO host runtime at all: the server had one and the phone did not, so a permitted extension proposal on mobile had nothing to execute it and the whole §3.4 lane was silently absent on the platform that is the product. It is not the kind of thing a test finds, because everything fails closed and a node with no plane looks exactly like a node correctly refusing. I found it by wiring the server and then asking what the other boot does — the same question that has turned up a defect every time it has been asked in this session.

d2d_send's permitted-recipient set turned out not to need a new policy at all. §8's grant constraints already carry a bounded resources allowlist — “matched against the dispatch's resource tag”, capped at 64 tokens — and a D2D recipient IS a resource the grant names. Deriving from it means the channel policy is the one the owner already sees on the consent screen, rather than a second list beside it that would drift. Two rules fall out and both are the fail-closed reading: an unconstrained grant names NOBODY (reading it as “every DID” would turn one approval into a channel to the whole network), and a row flagged constraintsCorrupt names nobody either — treating it as unconstrained would be exactly the fail-open that flag exists to prevent.

One test-double bug worth recording: the publication-candidate helper used options.supplier ?? default, and ?? collapses “not specified” with “explicitly null” — so the null case silently tested the default and the fail-closed assertion never ran.

A node signing into a wall looks exactly like a node nobody is buying from (§16.2 — WS-2.4)

§16.2 has one sentence that had no implementation: “Signing nodes also re-verify the live epoch on a bounded interval, so a forgotten pre-restore node converges”. The scenario is concrete. A supplier node is running. Somebody restores the same business identity on another machine, which publishes epoch 2 and voids the backup's quote capacity. The first node knows nothing: it cached epoch 1 at boot and goes on signing quotes and status updates at epoch 1 for ever. Counterparties reject those records — that is the hard enforcement and it lives elsewhere — but from inside, a node signing into a wall is indistinguishable from a node nobody is buying from.

Converging means stopping, not adopting. The obvious reading of “converges” is that the stale node picks up the higher epoch. That would be wrong twice over. It would resume signing beside the node that actually restored — two generations under one business identity, both “current” — while still holding the pre-restore capacity counters the fence had already voided on the other. And if observing a higher epoch made a node increment, two live nodes would supersede each other for ever. So supersession stops signing and waits for an operator; the legitimate route to a higher epoch stays establishAfterRestore(), which publishes its own increment and voids capacity in the same commit.

The refusal is what makes it enforcement. A revalidation whose only effect is a log line changes nothing about what the node signs. So the cached epoch carries a validated-at time and currentEpoch() refuses once it has gone unverified past a bound — which is the one part of this that is a judgement call, and it is recorded as an open question below.

Five distinct stop reasons, each a different repo fault: superseded; rolled back below what this node signed at; the record deleted (absence is not consent — this node published or adopted an epoch, so an empty repo means somebody removed the fence); the record replaced in place at the same number (the digest covers activated_at, so that cannot happen by accident); and a record that turns out to belong to another business. An unreachable repo is deliberately NOT one of them on a single failed read — a blip must not take a healthy supplier off the market — but the staleness bound turns a long run of them into a refusal, because “I cannot check” for long enough IS the forgotten node.

The phone had no epoch service at all. Asking what the other boot does found it again: getCommerceEpochService() was READ in two places on mobile and SET in none, so currentEpoch() threw on every commerce operation there. Invisible, for the same reason as every gap in this class — a node refusing everything is what a correctly fail-closed node also looks like. The repo protocol (CAS on swapRecord, an unreadable record refusing rather than reading as absent, the restore-fence decision) moved from the server app into @dina/home-node, and the server keeps only the genuinely server-shaped part: where the credentials come from.

A comment that described the intent while the wiring did something else. The §9.9 admission-recovery sweep was started inside the shared workflow plane, under a comment saying both boots got it from one place. They did not: the phone composes its own background work and never calls that plane. So on the product's primary surface no abandoned reservation ever timed out, every abandoned order held its quote capacity indefinitely, and the buyer was answered received_processing for ever. Both commerce ticks now start through one startCommerceSweepers per composition root, and a boundary guard fails if either root stops starting or stopping them — stopping matters as much, because a phone that kept the epoch tick running after an identity switch would go on re-reading the previous identity's repo.

The orphan ledger had a hole, and I found it while assuming the opposite. I read the admission sweeper as unwired, checked the ledger, and it was not listed — which looked like the guard failing. It was not: the sweeper was wired, in a package my grep had not covered. But the check I ran to prove it exposed a genuine weakness. The ledger counts a symbol as internally consumed when its own file names it more than once, and a class that names ITSELF in an error message (throw new Error(`CommerceAdmissionSweeper: intervalMs must be > 0`)) meets that bar with no call at all. Any such class could sit unwired and off the ledger. String literals are now stripped from the same-file count, with template interpolations kept — an interpolated symbol IS a reference, a message is not.

Three mutations were killed by the compiler rather than by a test, and that is a real result rather than a gap: the state machine is a discriminated union, so removing the unestablished or stopped guard makes validatedAtMs unreachable and the file stops building. The other nineteen were killed by tests.

The spec forbade the design I was about to build (§3.4 — WS-3.4)

I had the route mapped out — POST /v1/plugins/host-operations, plugin callers only, install resolved from the authenticated device — before reading §3.4's own sentence on the subject: “a runner requests an operation only by completing its current claim with a typed proposal ... the request rides the existing plugin task envelope and claim-token discipline — there is no separate in-process callback surface”. The route would have been exactly that surface: a second door into the same authority with none of the claim-token, idempotency, cancellation or lease discipline the task lane already enforces, and a runner holding one could ask for effects outside any claim it held.

So the lane is a completion interceptor. The runner completes its claim with a typed proposal; Core recognises it, brokers it, and the verified result reaches the runner as its NEXT task. Four rules follow and each is enforced rather than documented: the runner names an operation and nothing identity-shaped (a params install_id is REFUSED, not ignored); the capability comes from the pinned manifest AND the consent record, because a manifest capability with no consented scope hash is one the owner declined or has not yet seen; a PAUSED install cannot act on a claim it held while active, which is the window §14's pause creates; and a retry of the same idempotency key is never re-decided, so a runner that lost a response cannot spend a second permit for one asked-for effect.

The permit decision is the existing action plane, and that was also a spec instruction rather than my preference. §3.4: an effectful operation routes through the canonical plane “exactly like any other plugin effect: authority-domain evaluation, approval or standing-grant check ... and the deny-by-default safety floor”. So the decision runs evaluatePluginIntent over the operation's action_class — the same table, floors and first-N rule that govern every other plugin effect. A second policy here would have given the owner two consent surfaces that disagree, which is worse than either alone.

Two of my tests were wrong and the gatekeeper was right both times, which is worth recording because both were my misunderstanding of a rule I then went and checked. First: I set a standing approval in the test defaults and expected a card — but §3.4 says “approval OR standing-grant”, so a standing grant past the first N is supposed to run silent. Second: I expected a read to run silent and it carded, because §8 rule 5 says a CUSTOM capability id never floors below MODERATE (“the declared class is a consent label, not proof”). The fixed tests assert both halves of each rule instead of one, which is more than the originals would have caught.

A real defect fell out of building the sweep. settle read the proposal's state and then wrote without a compare-and-swap. Two settlers racing — the dispatcher finally hearing back, and the recovery sweep giving up on the same row — both passed the read and both wrote, last one winning. The loser could be the one holding the TRUE outcome: a swept outcome_unknown overwriting a verified completed turns a fact into a question, and an operator then chases an effect that was fine. finish now CASes on executing.

The sweep settles and never retries, and the deadline costs something I should name. A row in executing means the effect may have crossed the wire, so re-running it is how one purchase order becomes two; the sweep's whole job is to convert an open question into the terminal state that says so. But a host operation that is slow rather than dead gets swept, and its real result is then refused by the new CAS — the truth is lost and the owner sees an unknown for an effect that was fine. I picked that over the alternatives deliberately: no deadline leaves crashed rows open for ever, and a mutable terminal state would let a late settler overwrite a decision the owner has already acted on. Fifteen minutes is more than an order of magnitude beyond any operation this node ships, so a sweep of a live operation means something was already wrong.

The seam is now wired and the chain runs end to end. WorkflowService.complete() notices a typed proposal, DETACHED like the response bridge: the completion has already been CAS-confirmed, brokering is async, and a caller of complete() must never block on a network effect. A failure anywhere in the handler is reported, never thrown back — it cannot unwind a completion that is already durable.

The verified result reaches the runner as its NEXT claim on its own lane, in a new host_operation envelope block. That block is deliberately not params: the consented params_schema governs params, and an operation's result is not that shape, so putting it there would either fail validation or force the schema wide enough to accept both. It carries the terminal STATE verbatim, because a runner that saw only a result could not tell a refusal from a failure from an outcome nobody knows, and those three lead it to opposite next steps. A refusal is delivered too — a runner left waiting on a task that will never come is worse than a no. settleOwnerDecision gives the owner-approved route the SAME follow-up path as the auto-permitted one, so a runner cannot tell whether a human was involved.

Wiring the phone found the same divergence a third time. Mobile's WorkflowService never had ingressResultTransformer either — the §9.9 seam that answers a buyer with the acknowledgement Core signed instead of the runner's own JSON. The server plane has passed it since WS-3.8; the phone composes its own service and did not. So a supplier running on a phone was returning a runner's answer to a buyer. Both are now passed at both roots, through one factory.

One mutation survives and stays. The lane refuses on !decision.allowed || decision.mode === 'blocked', and every path in evaluatePluginIntent that sets mode: 'blocked' also sets allowed: false — so no test can reach the second clause and deleting it changes nothing today. It stays, documented in place, because the state it guards is the one where a permit would be worst: a decision whose two fields disagree must refuse rather than pick the permissive reading. Saying that plainly is more honest than contriving a test that fakes the contradiction.

The survivor hunt also paid for itself twice. It found a REAL gap — nothing completed a NON-plugin task through a service with a handler installed, so the envelope check was untested on the overwhelmingly common path — and a fixture bug: a capability without action_class builds an envelope the parser rejects, which is the parser working exactly as designed. The first version of the suite silently produced no follow-up because of it, and read as a broken seam.

Revocation, and the difference between two teardown APIs (§16.3, §16.4 — WS-11.3)

§25.6's manual journey ends "revoke and prove no further work", and the in-process half of that is now on the retailer↔manufacturer lane. The interesting part was not writing the scenarios; it was that my first version reached under the real door.

`markRevoked` is a PENDING-only tombstone. Used on an ACTIVE install it silently changes nothing, so my first three scenarios reported a revoked pack that carried on trading — and would have passed as soon as I "fixed" them to match. `remove` is closer but still only ONE step of the teardown. The real `uninstall` is the operation with the rules in it, and driving it turned three weak assertions into five that say something:

  • the uninstall is refused while an order is open, because every lifecycle capability a buyer needs — status, cancel, reconcile — answers through that install's binding, so tearing it down leaves the records intact and unreachable;
  • it is still refused after delivery. That is a recorded design decision now asserted rather than trusted: delivered is deliberately not terminal, because a buyer inside the dispute window can still dispute, and those are exactly the orders an uninstall would damage most;
  • once nothing is open it succeeds and fences the runner by revoking its paired device — which is what stops it acting, not the row's absence. A teardown that deleted the row and left the device paired would leave a runner that still authenticates;
  • a new order afterwards is refused install_unavailable, not no_plugin_binding: the listing still NAMES the install, so the honest report is that the named install is gone rather than that the listing was never bound. The two send an operator to different places;
  • a paused install HOLDS its queued order instead of losing it. §16.3's pause is a hold the owner can lift, and reading it as a teardown would drop orders they meant to resume.

Both times my expectation and the code disagreed, the code was right. That is the third time this session, and the pattern is worth naming: a scenario test written against what I assumed the API did is a scenario test that certifies my assumption.

The registry was empty, so the whole §3.4 lane was closed from the declaring side (§25.2 — WS-11.2)

WS-3.5 added three typed host operations and registered their EXECUTORS at both boots. WS-3.4 built the proposal broker and the completion seam. What nobody had done was register the operations in the ExtensionOperationRegistry — which both boots created EMPTY. The two halves answer different questions: the dispatcher says what the node can DO, the registry says what a capability may DECLARE and pins the schemas a proposal is checked against. With an empty registry every operation was refused operation_unregistered before reaching an executor, so the executors were unreachable and the lane was open on one side and closed on the other. Invisible for the usual reason: a node that refuses every brokered operation looks exactly like a node nobody asked.

The action classes are the policy, and they are the fail-closed reading. publication_candidate is read, because it validates and does not publish — classing it write would card an operation that changes nothing. d2d_send and connector_broker are write: the first puts bytes on the wire under this node's identity and cannot be taken back, and the second can submit a purchase order to an ERP under the same name. Classing an operation by the weakest thing it might do is how the strongest gets through silently.

I nearly changed a deliberate design to make a test pass. §25.2 says "Reference Buyer cannot call AppView or send D2D except through the typed Core host operation", and the reference Buyer declares no host_operations at all. My first reading was that the pack was incomplete, and I added d2d_send to two of its capabilities. That broke reference_manifests.test.ts, which pins "asks for no authority it does not use" — and the pin is right: the buyer's quote fan-out and order submission go through Core's OWN service-query egress, where the four gates and signing already apply. The pack needs no brokered authority, so §25.2's requirement is satisfied more strongly by declaring none. I reverted the manifest and moved the test. A failing test is a question about which of two things is wrong, and the answer is not always the older one.

Two more findings fell out. A PROVIDER-only capability cannot ride the tool lane — a hand-built tool task naming one is terminalized stale_authority rather than served, because the consented KIND is authority and the two lanes carry different context. And the empty-list case in the scope hash is deliberate rather than an oversight: host_operations enters the hash only when non-empty, so a pack declaring an empty list — which grants nothing either way — keeps its existing hash and its frozen vector.

Two strings of the same type, meaning opposite things (§16.4 — WS-4.5)

An uninstall refuses while commercial obligations are open, and the count was NODE-WIDE because an order recorded which manifest served it and not which install. On a node running two commerce plugins that made an operator resolve one pack's orders to remove the other — safe, and wrong. The fix is a second column, and keeping BOTH is the point: a plugin update moves the CID and keeps the install, so "is this install still on the hook" is a question the manifest CID cannot answer.

The admission seam now carries them as one object. Two string parameters side by side, meaning opposite things, is a transposition the compiler cannot see. Bundling them into { manifestCid, installId } costs one line and makes the mistake impossible to write.

Scoping the count needed a join the aggregates deliberately cannot make: the status-chain store does not know which install served an order. It happens at the ROOT, which is where the existing comment already said a cross-store question belongs. A chain whose order reference is gone counts as OPEN — an obligation whose provenance we cannot establish is not one to dismiss on a teardown. Orders admitted before the column existed carry '' and block nobody, which is the right trade for a pre-release schema: the alternative is every legacy row refusing every teardown for ever, with nothing an operator can do about it.

Two bugs of mine, both caught by the suite rather than by reading it back. The INSERT gained the VALUE but not the column NAME, so every reservation failed and reported itself as a "concurrent reservation race" — a message that sends you looking at transactions. And the SELECT list never gained the column, so String(row.serving_install_id) produced the literal text "undefined": a value that compares equal to no install, is not empty, and reads like data in a dump. The read is now ?? '' precisely so a missing column can never become a five-letter install id again.

An answer that was true and useless (§12.7, §16.2 — WS-2.3)

A supplier that lost an order re-adopts it from the buyer's held, supplier-signed acknowledgement rather than answering never_received — because answering "never received" against its own signature would invite a duplicate order. It then reported received_accepted, which is TRUE and useless: it hands the buyer back the document they just presented, and it tells a party who is about to wait for status updates that the order is live. It is not. Re-adoption rebuilds a REFERENCE, not the order's lines, its quote context, or its external state, and chain creation stays barred until the owner runs the §16.2 ceremony.

It now answers received_unresolved — §12.7's own phrase for "this supplier holds the order but cannot report where it is" — with the longer retry that suits something needing a human. Deliberately NOT a new outcome: the set is frozen with conformance vectors, and a fourth value would be a wire-major change to say what an existing one already says. The answer is the same on the poll that performs the re-adoption and on every poll after it, and it CLEARS: once the ceremony makes the order describable again, the same question returns received_accepted. The scenario test now walks that whole arc.

The scenario had been contradicting itself in writing. The sentence directly beneath expect(...).toBe('received_accepted') read: "The order is back, but ChairMaker cannot describe it… So it is frozen." Two assertions and a comment, all in view of each other, disagreeing. That is worth naming because it is not a subtle bug — it is what happens when an assertion is written to match what the code did rather than what the system should say.

I also got the state machine wrong mid-change and had to correct a comment I had just written: I described re-adopted orders as left reserved, and they are decided — the re-adoption path calls decide with the held record. The first version of the fix was aimed at a contradiction that did not exist; the second is aimed at the one that did.

The open item said "just needs a tick". It does not. (§12.7 — WS-7.7)

§12.7's buyer-side re-poll — the loop that asks a supplier again about an order whose outcome is unknown — is built, mutation-tested and unreachable. Both the WBS row and the orphan ledger recorded the reason as "no scheduler tick calls the sweep yet", which reads like ten minutes of wiring. It is not, and I only found that out by trying to do it.

sweepReconcilePolls takes ask: (…) => Promise<OrderReconcileResult | null>: a request that resolves to its own answer. The requester lane does not work that way. Every outbound service.query in this system is fire-and-forget, the answer arrives later as a correlated service.response, and there is no await-capable requester helper anywhere in Core — I checked for one before concluding it. Hanging a tick on this signature would mean inventing a correlate-and-await layer for ONE caller, sitting beside the durable correlation everything else already uses. That is the shape of a workaround.

The real work is a contract change, and it is worth stating precisely so the next pass does not repeat my hour. Split the sweep in two: ASK fires the order_reconcile query, advances nextPollAtMs and records that it asked; APPLY runs applyReconcileResult from the inbound response, exactly as the buyer's other lanes already do. The state machine and its rules — only never_received authorises resubmission, and only when no supplier-signed evidence was presented — are unchanged and already correct. It is the transport shape that is wrong, and I have corrected the record in all three places it was stated wrongly rather than leaving a note that makes the job look smaller than it is.

The re-poll, wired — and the second contract change nobody predicted (§12.7 — WS-7.7)

The note above was right about the split and wrong about it being the whole job. askReconcilePolls now fires the order_reconcile query and advances the clock; ReconcilePollSweeper runs it on a tick both composition roots start. That part went as described.

Then the boot wiring would not compile, and the reason was substantive. The sweep needs to REBUILD the question, and the buyer's record could not describe its own order. poll_count is a column precisely because §12.7 requires the loop to survive a restart — and after a restart the buyer knew it should ask and could not say what about. So order_digest, idempotency_key, protocol_version and service_rkey now live on commerce_buyer_orders beside the counter, written when the order is sent. Durability of the counter without durability of the question was durability in name only.

The rkey is not bookkeeping. A supplier may offer commerce on a non-default listing, and a service.query with no service_uri is checked against the default one — so leaving it out would have been refused by exactly the suppliers who run more than one listing, which is the population most likely to be running a real business. A row written before these columns existed reads as UNDESCRIBABLE and is skipped rather than asked: a supplier's honest answer to a question with no digest is never_received, and that is the one answer that must never arrive by accident.

I also dropped the orderDetails callback I had just added. It made the description a second source of truth beside the stored order, and the two would disagree exactly when it mattered — a reader's job is hardest for an old order, and an old order is what the re-poll is for. The request is now built from the record, so ask and apply cannot disagree about what was asked.

Building the APPLY half found the bigger hole: the buyer lane had no inbound side at all (§12.7, §9.10)

submitApprovedOrder parks an order as ambiguous and documents why: "the acknowledgement arrives later through the response bridge". Nothing on this node was that bridge. A supplier could accept an order, sign the acknowledgement, answer the reconcile — and every one of those answers reached the D2D ingress and stopped there. The buyer would go on asking for ever.

Invisible in the usual way. An order that never settles looks exactly like a supplier who has not answered yet, and the loop that hides it is the same loop that exists to survive a supplier who really has not. No test failed, because every test that sent an order also handed the executor the answer directly.

commerce/buyer_response.ts is that seam. Four decisions in it are worth recording:

  • One seam for two capabilities. A submission's answer and a reconcile's answer carry the same document, and a second reading of an acknowledgement would eventually disagree with the first about a kind one copy knew and the other did not. For the same reason acknowledgementToResult moved out of the executor and into the state machine.
  • Called directly from the receive pipeline, not injected. This departs from how the supplier-side seam (ingressResultTransformer) is wired, and deliberately: that one shipped wired on the server and unwired on the phone, and the phone is the product. A defence each composition root must remember to pass is one a root eventually forgets. The function is inert without a commerce runtime, so a node that does no commerce pays one string comparison per inbound service response.
  • The supplier is the transport-authenticated sender. The record is keyed on (supplierDid, purchaseOrderId), so binding to the acknowledgement's own supplier_did field would let any peer settle somebody else's order by naming it. A test pins it.
  • A non-success status is not an answer. unavailable is a fact about the supplier's runner, not about the order; settling on it would turn an outage into a commercial outcome. The order stays parked and the re-poll asks again.

Both payloads go through the commerce-protocol validators before anything moves, so a tampered digest or a fourth outcome value is refused rather than believed — this is the one place a counterparty's bytes become a commercial state on this node.

What the mutation sweep taught this time (22 mutations, all killed — three only after new tests)

Three survived the first pass, and each named a test that was weaker than the code it covered.

Dropping any single field from the describability check changed nothing. The test built its undescribable record with newBuyerOrder(id) and no description at all, so every field was empty and any one check caught it — three of the four conditions were doing nothing a test could see. A partial description is the dangerous case, not the total absence of one, and the check is now driven field by field, one record missing exactly one thing.

Swapping two adjacent bind parameters in the durable store changed nothing either. Every suite reached the buyer lane through the in-memory double, which stores whole objects and therefore cannot disagree with itself about columns. That is precisely the bug class that already cost this workstream an afternoon: a column added to the VALUES list and not to the column list, and a SELECT that never learned the column and wrote back the literal text "undefined". There is now a real-SQLCipher round trip that asserts field by field, covers listUnsettled separately (the sweep reads through it, and an undescribable order is SKIPPED, which is silence rather than an error), and reads a pre-migration row to prove it comes back empty rather than as a digest-shaped string.

A fourth defect I found by reading rather than by mutating, and it is the one worth remembering: my first ask-advance expressed every record as received_unresolved. That would quietly move a submitted_unconfirmed order — the decision has not reached the external boundary — into outcome_unknown, which means the effect MAY have fired. Reclassifying an order as possibly-committed because the BUYER asked about it inverts whose fact that is. The advance is now expressed as the outcome the record is already in, and a mutation swapping the two states is killed.

The command half: the projection is the authorization (FR-P10 — WS-7.8)

The read half shipped as one projection so mobile and web could not disagree about whether outcome_unknown means "failed". The command half keeps that promise by asking the same function: what an owner may DO with an order is describeOrderForOwner(record).actions, and the route checks membership in that list rather than re-deriving the rule from the state name.

It is a small thing to write and it is the whole design. Two readings of "may the owner resend this" will eventually disagree, and the disagreement that matters runs one way: a command permitting what the card never offered, on the single state where the wrong answer puts a duplicate order for real goods on the wire.

Two of the four actions turn out not to be commands. wait is the absence of one; view_acknowledgement is a read the projection already answers. Both are refused rather than given an invented side effect.

reconcile_now ignores the backoff on purpose. The interval exists to stop the automatic loop spinning against a supplier who is merely slow. It is not a limit on what the owner may do on their own node, and treating it as one would make the button lie. The ask still advances the clock, so one tap does not become two questions.

resend is a MODE of the existing submit, not a second function. The authority check, the §15.2 binding rebuilt from the order actually about to go, and record-before-send are identical; a parallel resendOrder would be a second place for one of them to be dropped. The only difference is what an existing record means — normally it refuses a duplicate, here it is required and must carry an authorization the supplier's own answer earned. Two details are load bearing: the approval is REBUILT rather than reused (the order is unchanged, but which install, capability, manifest CID and config revision are about to send it may not be), and the authorization is SPENT before the send, so an owner tapping twice on a slow network gets a refusal rather than a duplicate.

Two mutations survived here for the same reason as in WS-7.7, and the fix is the same shape. Swapping create for put in the record-before-send changed nothing observable — twice over. The settle at the END of the submit writes the record anyway, so the difference is invisible unless you look at the store from INSIDE the send; and the in-memory double upserts where real SQL updates, so a first submission written with put would have left an order on the wire with nothing recording it and no test would have said so. There is now a test that reads the store from inside the sender, and a real-SQLCipher one that drives the executor.

One more defect, in my own test rather than in the code, worth writing down because it will recur: it.each([undefined, 'brain', …]) against a helper with a DEFAULT parameter silently tested the OWNER for the undefined case. A default parameter swallows an explicit undefined, so the one caller shape that matters most — Brain's in-process transport, which carries no callerType at all — was the one case never exercised, in a boundary test whose entire job is that list. The default is gone.

The update flow: consent bound to content, not to a boolean (§9.13, §16.5 — WS-3.7)

UpdateRebindCoordinator has been correct and unreachable since it was written. Its ledger entry named the reason exactly — "the owner-facing update discovery + consent flow is still unbuilt and is the real owner of the boot wiring" — and that is what this is.

The publisher comes from the install, never from the caller. It is one line and it is the most important line in the file: a caller-named publisher would let anyone re-point an install at their own pack and inherit the consent the owner gave to somebody else's. A release carrying a different plugin_id is refused for the same reason one level down — applying it would keep the install's identity, its grants and its listings while running other code.

The gates are not re-implemented. I extracted vetReleaseManifest out of finishBegin so the update door runs exactly the install door's list. A second copy of that list is a second thing to keep in step, and the copy that fell behind would be the one letting a manifest this build cannot serve past the door. A parameterised test asserts the update path answers with the same CODE an install would — the property, not the branch.

The §16.5 re-consent binds to content. This is the decision worth reviewing. The coordinator used to hard-refuse any widening, which meant a widening update could never be applied at all — §16.5 says the owner must be asked again, not that the answer is always no. The obvious implementation is a boolean "the owner accepted", and it is worthless: it says nothing about what they accepted TO. The failure it permits is concrete — the owner approves a raised context ceiling, the operator re-prepares, and a capability that places orders applies under the same yes.

So confirmUpdate echoes back the findings the owner saw and the coordinator compares them, as a SET, against what it detects itself. Both directions are refusals: fewer is the obvious failure, more is a consent given against a different release. Five mutations exercise it — accept-anything, partial, same-count-wrong-content, right-capability-milder-escalation, compare-ignoring-the-target-value — and finding them took two rounds, because my first widening fixture widened on ONE axis and a partial consent is not expressible when there is only one thing to be partial about.

What is deliberately not built: automatic discovery. A release rkey is content-derived (§5), so the next version's key cannot be computed — finding it needs a repo LISTING, which Core has no transport for. The owner supplies the rkey; a lister added later feeds this same prepareUpdate and changes nothing else.

Tradeoff recorded: the prepared candidate is in memory. A durable row would survive a restart between the review and the tap, saving the owner a second prepare. It would cost a table holding a fetched third-party manifest as consent-shaped state — the kind of row that outlives the reason it was written. Losing it costs a re-prepare, which re-fetches and re-verifies. The failure mode of forgetting is "ask again", never "apply something the owner did not see", so forgetting is the safe direction.

Two nodes in one process, and the defect only two nodes could find (§12.7, §16.2 — WS-11.3)

Every journey so far carried an order INTO ChairMaker and stopped where the response bridge hands bytes to a socket. Every buyer-side test then STARTED by handing the executor an answer. The gap between those two points is where a whole missing lane lived, and neither side's tests could see it, because each was complete on its own terms.

So this journey runs both: two identity databases, two commerce runtimes, and the bytes ChairMaker's bridge produces are sealed, signed, and driven through Sancho's real receiveD2D. The only stand-in is the socket, which a jest process cannot have. installCommerceRuntime is a module global, so which node is acting is written down (asChairMaker / asSancho) rather than assumed — a test that left the wrong runtime installed would assert a buyer's rule against a supplier's store and would probably still pass.

It found two defects. The first was mine, in the test, and it is worth recording because it is the shape of a false green: I pushed ctx.resultJSON straight onto the wire, which is the RESULT and not the service.response envelope the bridge builds. Every delivery silently did nothing. The fix was to use the real makeServiceResponseBridgeSender — a hand-built wire body is a wire body production never emits.

The second was in the code. Held evidence was a PER-CALL argument. The re-poll sweep could be given a reader; the inbound apply runs in the D2D ingress and had no way to obtain one. §12.7's legality rule is about what the buyer PRESENTED — so a node that asks with its evidence and then judges the answer as though it had presented none accepts a never_received it was entitled to refuse, and authorizes precisely the duplicate order the section exists to prevent. One half knowing and the other not is worse than neither knowing.

installHeldEvidenceReader makes it node-level; the ask and the apply both default to it, and the sweeper no longer accepts one at all. Null stays the honest default and both halves agree on it. This is the same correction as the order description in WS-7.7 — two callers of one fact, so the fact belongs in one place rather than in each caller's arguments.

Built and unproven is a different problem from missing (§9.9 — WS-2.2)

This row's open clause named two things: the request-receipt / projection-digest binding, and store-integrity failure frozen as a durable wire rejection. Both turned out to be implemented. That is worth saying plainly rather than quietly ticking the row, because "built and unproven" and "missing" look identical from a status column and need opposite work.

The binding is real and the mutation that matters is the yardstick: swap the retained request's projection for the ORDER's own and every order passes, because an order compared against itself always matches. That mutation is killed. The retained request is rehydrated through validateQuoteRequest, which re-derives the request digest — the one corruption a shape check cannot see.

The freeze was believed and never asserted, and the old test said so in a comment: "a second call would be a replay of the first refusal, which is correct behaviour". §9.9's sentence is "an admission answer, once given, is frozen … never a retroactive acceptance", and the integrity path is exactly where that is easiest to get wrong, because the condition HEALS. An operator restores the receipt from a backup and the order would now bind perfectly. A node that re-evaluated would hand the buyer an acceptance for a proposal it has already told them was refused — after the buyer has moved on, possibly by ordering elsewhere.

So: repair the store byte for byte, replay, and the recorded rejection comes back — validated by the same validator the wire uses, because that is what the buyer receives. And the frozen rejection REFUNDS the hold, so a different order against the same quote still reserves; otherwise the supplier's own disk fault silently spends their offer.

Keeping a boundary instead of crossing it (§10.2/§10.3 — WS-5.1)

ingestCatalog sat on the orphan ledger with an accurate reason: it fetches, and Core makes no outbound HTTP. The tempting fix is to give the route a fetch. That would put an outbound request behind an owner endpoint where the egress gates cannot see it — the ledger entry was not describing an inconvenience, it was describing the design.

So the transport is INSTALLED by the composition root, which is the half that owns transport, and the route only asks whether one exists. Core keeps the part worth having on its side of the line: pointer advance, snapshot digest, per-page proof, page order, and one shared byte and time budget across the whole ingest. A node with no transport refuses rather than reaching for a global fetch, and that is the first test in the file, because it is the entire reason the registry exists.

The server sends no headers, and the empty object is the decision. A catalog is a public publication: there is nothing to authenticate with, and a credential sent to a supplier's feed URL is a credential given away.

Pages are addressed by a template, because a wire cannot carry pageUrl(index). A template with no {index} slot is refused up front — the ingest WOULD catch it, since every page carries its own index and is checked, but it would report an operator's own typo as "the supplier served the wrong page", which sends them to argue with their supplier.

Mobile installs nothing, deliberately. §10.3 re-checks the address actually connected to, and a phone cannot report it — the same reason the connector executors refuse no_executor there. A phone that ingested without that check would be doing the fetch and skipping the defence, which is worse than not ingesting.

One more fixture lesson, the same as the round-trip journey's: my hand-rolled snapshots and pointers were refused by every validator, because a fixture that guesses at digest domains and required fields tests the guess. They are built by the real buildCatalogSnapshot now — the thing a supplier actually runs.

Publish order is a failure model, not a data model (§10.2 — WS-5.1)

The routes built the catalog records and handed them back; nothing wrote them. Same split as the ingest half — Core owns the ORDER, because the order is the rule; the composition root owns the WRITE, because writing a repo is I/O.

Snapshot first, pointer last, and no pointer at all if the snapshot did not land. The snapshot is immutable and content-addressed, so writing it twice writes the same bytes and a crash after it costs nothing. The pointer is the mutable head: publishing it names a snapshot as current. A pointer naming a snapshot that is not there is the one failure a consumer cannot work around — it fetches, gets nothing, and cannot tell a supplier mid-publish from a broken one.

The reverse order is what you get from writing to the DATA model instead of the FAILURE model: the pointer is the smaller record and the obvious first thing to update. Every test in the file is about that ordering.

Two smaller decisions follow from the same reasoning. The snapshot is not CAS'd — it is content-addressed, so a CAS would make a retry fail on success. The pointer is, on the expected head, which is what stops a publisher racing itself into a fork; and there is one pointer key per catalog, because it IS the head and a second key would be a second head.

Building and publishing stay tellable apart. A node with no repo still gets the records — that is the useful half for an operator publishing by hand — and is never told they went anywhere.

One mutation survived the first pass and the reason is worth keeping: the test published against a NULL expected head, which makes "the snapshot is not CAS'd" and "the snapshot is CAS'd on the same value" the same bytes. A non-null head makes the claim testable at all.

Two things the full suite caught that the focused one did not. One mutation survived the first pass because the test published against a NULL expected head, which makes "the snapshot is not CAS'd" and "the snapshot is CAS'd on the same value" the same bytes; a non-null head makes the claim testable at all. And I had quietly changed the status of a BUILD refusal from 200-with-ok:false to 409 while adding the publish outcome — a contract change smuggled in with a feature, which two existing tests correctly refused. Build refusals keep their shape; only the publish outcome introduces a new status.

Our own packs go through our own door (§25.6 step 1 — WS-3.9)

The reference manifests ship as product artefacts rather than fixtures, on the argument that a manifest is a published contract and a pair living only in a .test.ts is one nothing can install or review. That argument is worth nothing unless the packs actually install — and until now they were validated (a pure gate) and then used as fixtures by journeys that skipped straight to an activated install by writing rows.

A reference pack is what a third-party author copies. If ours cannot pass our own door, the instruction "publish a manifest like this one" is wrong, and the first person to find out is somebody outside this repo. So each pack is now published at the content-derived rkey §5 requires and driven through beginInstall → pairing bind → confirmConsent.

Writing it found a gap in the test, not the code. My first version skipped the pairing ceremony and read as though consent alone activated a runner install. It does not: the device must have been bound to that pending during pairing. The test now asserts the refusal BEFORE the bind, and that a different device cannot activate the one bound.

Two mutations I could not kill, recorded rather than papered over. The device-binding guard's clauses are mutually redundant at runtime — on an unbound pending, "the install has no device" and "the presented device is not the bound one" each refuse on their own — so no single shape-preserving mutation is observable. Removing the whole disjunction is compiler-killed, because the undefined check is what narrows the type for the device-verifier call below it. That is defence in depth plus a type-level dependency, not a coverage hole, and the honest thing is to say so rather than invent a test that pretends otherwise.

Giving prose a compiler (§14.2 — WS-6.5)

This row's open clause was "the §14.2 surface is only as strong as 0.4", and 0.4 shipped. A stale clause is not evidence: it says the dependency landed, not that the surface holds. So both halves of §14.2 are driven now.

The first half is ordinary and already worked: six plausible per-customer price columns are each refused by the publication gate for the same reason — the closed public vocabulary does not need to have thought of the name. A published LIST price still goes out, because §14.2 protects customer-SPECIFIC pricing and a list price is the supplier's own advertising; refusing it would make the gate useless rather than careful.

The second half is a claim about COPY, and it is the one that rots. §14.2 is unusually explicit: a private quote is "confidential, not anonymous", the supplier necessarily sees the authenticated buyer DID, and "the UI and consent copy must not imply that D2D signing hides the requester". Nothing in a codebase enforces prose. So this file enforces it: every string literal in the commerce surface is scanned for a promise of anonymity, and the owner-facing order card is rendered and checked.

Scanned rather than listed, because a test naming today's strings passes for ever while a new screen ships the forbidden sentence. Literals only, so a doc-comment DISCUSSING anonymity — as §14.2 itself must — is not a promise the product makes.

Two of the three mutations inject the forbidden sentence into a real owner-facing headline, and that is the point of running them: a copy scanner that cannot be made to fire is worthless, and it would look exactly like a passing one.

The quote surface, and a join that belongs in one place (FR-P10 — WS-7.8)

Orders got the one-projection treatment first. The quote surface has the same shape and its own version of the risk: a quote's state is a JOIN over the voided flag, the validity window and the spent capacity, and a client computing "live" from any one of those alone is wrong in a specific, expensive way. An owner told a voided quote is live will honour terms this node has already refused to stand behind.

The precedence matches QuoteFamily's own refusal order, and that is the design rather than a coincidence. A card that ranked expiry above voiding would tell an owner a quote merely lapsed when this node disowned it — and they would reasonably re-offer the same terms, which is exactly what §16.2's restore-voiding exists to prevent.

Two smaller judgements. void is offered on one state only, because it is the only one where voiding changes anything; offering it on an expired quote invites an owner to cancel something already over. And an unreadable max_uses reads as ONE — the protocol default — rather than NaN (which reaches somebody's screen) or unlimited (which keeps offering a quote whose capacity nobody can count).

Two mutations survived the first pass, and both are the same lesson. The stable-tiebreak mutation survived every test of the projection, because a projection cannot see the order it is given. The capacity-join mutation survived because my route test STUBBED the aggregate and did the join itself. The test now uses the real QuoteFamilyStore — the same correction as using the real bridge sender in the round-trip journey and the real publisher in the catalog tests. A stub that reimplements the thing under test is a test of the stub.

The catalog card, and the node becoming the authority on its own publication history (§10.2, FR-P10 — WS-7.8)

The card itself is the small half. describeCatalogForOwner offers withdraw on a live catalog and NOT on a tombstone, because withdrawing a tombstone republishes it at a new sequence and tells every consumer the catalog was retired twice. Everything else on that surface is a fact, and inventing buttons for facts is how a card starts offering to un-retire something.

Building it surfaced two defects underneath, and neither was the card.

First: the caller was the authority on this node's own chain. The publish route took expected_pointer_cid — the compare-and-swap the repo enforces — out of the request body, and took the predecessor from there too. That is a fact only this node knows. A caller that omitted the predecessor published a GENESIS at sequence 1 over a live chain; a caller that got the CAS wrong lost a race it had no way to understand. The node now records its own head when the repo accepts the write, and supplies both from that record. A body previous that names a different point is REFUSED (409 stale_predecessor) rather than ignored — a client that believes the chain is at sequence 4 while this node publishes 9 would succeed and then show its user a catalog nobody published. expected_pointer_cid is no longer read.

Second: the withdrawal was never written. The route BUILT a tombstone and handed it back. Nothing published it, so the live catalog stayed live, consumers kept fetching the previous head, and the owner believed they had stopped selling. The gap between those two beliefs is filled with orders. This is the same defect class the orphan ledger exists for, hiding one level down: the function was reachable, its output was correct, and the output went nowhere. Withdrawal now publishes through the same publishCatalogRecords path with the same three outcomes as publication — published: null for an operator with no repo, 200 written, 409 refused.

The store reads rows back through the consumer's validator. rehydrateCatalogPointer runs validateCatalogPointer — the same check a buyer runs against a pointer fetched from the repo — so a row this build cannot read the way a buyer would is reported ABSENT rather than believed. That distinction is not cosmetic here: JSON.parse('null') as a pointer would read as "nothing published yet" and invite exactly the genesis-over-a-live-chain publication the first defect allowed.

The one CACHE claim, stated plainly. The row is a cache of our own writes, not a claim about the repo. The repo stays the authority: a CAS that fails means this row is stale and the honest response is to re-read, not to argue. What the row buys is that the ordinary path — publish, look at what you published, publish again — needs no network round trip and no caller bookkeeping.

Two guards caught my own violations before the suite did. The boundary test flagged a JSON.parse outside rehydrate.ts (which is what moved the read there, and made it better), and flagged the in-memory double missing from the orphan ledger. A third correction came from a mutation that SURVIVED and should not have counted: my first version cast null to the record type, which changes no runtime value because both callers already handle null. That is a bad mutation, not a coverage gap. The honest version returns a record with an empty pointer, and it dies on five assertions.

A fixture also contradicted the protocol: the first tombstone literal set withdrawn: true while keeping the snapshot fields, which the validator rightly refuses. The fixtures now come from buildCatalogSnapshot and buildCatalogWithdrawal — the real producers — and walk the chain forward rather than editing a sequence number in place, because the link fields have to agree with it.

Turning prose claims into guards, and what that found (WS-3.7, WS-7.5, WS-7.3, WS-1.9)

Four rows described complete work and carried no recorded gap, which under this WBS's own legend means their PART marker was stale. Flipping a status is the wrong way to close one of those — the rule this session has been holding to is to PROVE the property executable first. Doing that found real things in three of the four.

WS-3.7: "the one place an order leaves this node" was prose. Nothing stopped a second dispatch being added later, which is exactly the break the row was designed against: two send paths eventually disagree about what a failed send means, and one of those readings authorizes a duplicate order for real goods. The capability NAME is the chokepoint — any second path has to name submit_order to reach a supplier — so the guard is a four-file allow-list with a reason each. Both roots installing the sender is a second guard, and while writing them the EXISTING repository guard turned out to have a hole: it matched new SQLiteCommerce\w+Repository, so SQLiteBuyerOrderRepository and SQLiteCatalogPointerRepository were exempt by naming convention rather than by rule. A guard that matches the convention instead of the thing certifies whatever happens to be named differently.

WS-7.5: exactly two call sites, not at most two. A cap alone would pass a build where one half stopped checking the approval binding; a floor alone would pass a third caller nobody reviewed. Both directions are mutation-checked.

WS-7.3: a comment that overclaimed, and a route with no test. clear's comment said it was "reported rather than left to items.length === 0" while being exactly that. The distinction it wanted is real but lives one level up, at the route's 503. In a codebase where the comments are load-bearing, a comment that overclaims is a claim someone will later rely on, so it now says what is true. The route itself had NO test: the projection suite drove the builder directly and one journey walked past. That left all three of the endpoint's answers unchecked, and all three are DISTINCTIONS BETWEEN EMPTY LISTS — 200 clear, 503 no commerce, 409 not selling. Rendered as an empty list they read identically, and two of them are wrong in the direction that reassures an operator whose node is not even asking.

And the fixture mistake again, in a new costume: I invented { role: 'supplier' } for an install record. roleIsInstalled reads pluginId against the reference manifest and ignores role entirely, so the 409 case passed for the wrong reason and its sibling failed. Using SUPPLIER_REFERENCE_MANIFEST.plugin_id also means the test breaks if that id ever moves.

WS-1.9: the last four conformance categories. Product NORMALIZATION is the issuer rule — an identifier is a signed assertion by its issuer, and two manufacturers using the same internal SKU is ordinary rather than exotic, so a port accepting a scoped scheme unattributed would let one supplier's catalog answer for another's part number. EXACT-VARIANT projection is what stops a 12-pack shipping against a quote for a 6-pack because the GTIN matched, and the asymmetric case is pinned too: a variant on one side and absent on the other is not a match, because "unspecified" is not a wildcard.

Schema evolution needed a decision recorded rather than merely tested, because the unknown-field law is easy to get backwards and the two halves point in opposite directions. Canonicalization walks whatever it is given, so an unknown field CHANGES the digest — a receiver may not strip what it does not recognise and still expect the signature to verify. Validation, meanwhile, TOLERATES it — a record from a newer minor is not invalid merely because it says more than this build reads. Accept and preserve; never accept and rewrite. The vector pins the canonical BYTES rather than only their equality, because a port canonicalizing both sides the same wrong way would satisfy an equality-only assertion. Four of the thirty new cases were perturbed to prove the file gates.

Dual review — commerce remediation round (6 findings)

Artifact version 39ddba6b71ad9164. All six fixed, each mutation-verified; full gate green afterwards.

The two that mattered most

An acknowledgement was never bound to the order it settled. The buyer validated its SHAPE and its own digest — both of which pass for a perfectly well-formed acknowledgement about a DIFFERENT order — and then stored it as the settled commercial evidence. A supplier answering the question about order A with the acknowledgement for order B, a different order_digest, a different buyer, or an accepted_quote_digest naming terms the owner never approved was recorded as "Accepted", and §16.2 re-adoption would later present it back as proof of what was agreed.

The protocol already had the rule — verifyAcknowledgementForOrder — and it had NO production caller, for a reason worth writing down: it demanded a whole PurchaseOrderProposal, and a node checking an answer that arrives later does not still hold the order. It holds a record of what it sent. So the parameter is now the six fields the check actually reads, the record carries them, and the check runs in applyReconcileResult — the ONE place the inbound seam, the reconcile sweep and the executor's own send outcome all converge. A mismatch PARKS the order with a protocolFault rather than settling or rejecting it: the supplier said something this node cannot attribute to this order, so the real outcome is still unknown.

The §9.9 and §11.2 gates depended on how a capability was spelled. They matched hand-listed strings — the bare name and the UNDERSCORE NSID. The reference manifests spell their ids with HYPHENS (com.dinakernel.commerce.submit-order), which is also the natural key to publish a listing under, and a listing key is free-form. A CONFORMING supplier could therefore create an order with no admission at all — no idempotency lookup, no capacity hold, no reservation — and expose order_status and cancel_order with no subject authorization, which is any peer reading or cancelling another buyer's order. One of the four sets already carried the hyphen form, which is what makes it a slip rather than a decision. There is now one normalizer, the sets hold one spelling, and BOTH identities are consulted — the wire label and the bound manifest id — because gating on either is the fail-closed direction.

The four smaller ones, and what each taught

swapRecord: null is not "no CAS". In the PDS contract it means "only if NOTHING is there"; a blind overwrite is the property being ABSENT. The snapshot write passed null while its comment said "no CAS", so the documented recovery from a lost pointer write — the snapshot is durable, just write the head again — was rejected by the repo as InvalidSwap and reported as snapshot_write_failed, a message that would have been false. The writer type now has three states and the test asserts absence rather than null; the boot writer forwards the key only when it is present, because { swapRecord: undefined } still puts the key in the object.

The pointer store had no way back from divergence. The module's own comment said "a CAS that fails means this row is stale, and the honest response is to re-read the repo" — and nothing implemented the re-read, so divergence was terminal: supplying the true head was refused as stale, omitting it CAS'd on a value the repo never issued. POST /v1/commerce/catalog/adopt is that path, and it is the one place a caller may legitimately be the authority — an explicit recovery action, with the pointer run through the same validator a consumer uses. Separately, an unreadable row used to degrade to "nothing published", which authorizes a GENESIS over a live chain; the repository now answers has as well as get, and the route fails closed on the difference.

Two cards offered buttons that could only fail. The withdrawn-catalog card offered republish and told the owner the chain moves forward from here — verifyCatalogPointerAdvance refuses every successor of a tombstone, so §10.2 says the opposite and the copy was actively misleading. The quote card offered void, and nothing performs an owner-initiated void of a single quote: the aggregate has only voidPreRestore, §16.2's blanket disowning. Both actions are gone. The quote one is a real product gap and is recorded as such rather than papered over with a button.

The orphan ledger certified more than it checked. Its comment claimed "reachable from somewhere real rather than from a sibling in this directory"; the code only checked that the naming file was a DIFFERENT file, so a ring of mutually-referencing dead modules passed — on the exact defect class the ledger exists to surface. Fixing it needed two attempts. Excluding the whole directory was too strict and reported thirty symbols, because runtime.ts is the legitimate in-directory composition point and cross-package callers reach commerce through the barrel. The rule that works is a SYMBOL-level closure: a file is reachable when one of its exported names appears in a file that is already reachable, seeded from outside the directory. Proven by planting a two-module ring that references only itself — it is reported, and nothing legitimate is.

Dual review — Codex round (18 findings)

Same artifact version. Every finding verified in the source before any fix; every fix mutation-checked. Ten closed so far, eight open.

The wrong notion of "the same product"

verifyQuoteLinesAnswerRequest carried a LOCAL sameProduct comparing scheme and value. The two fields it omitted are the two that decide §9.4: issuer_did — the same SKU under a different manufacturer is a different product, and two manufacturers reusing an internal SKU is ordinary — and variant_digest, which is what stops a 12-pack shipping against a quote for a 6-pack. So a supplier could answer with another issuer's part, or another variant of the right one, and pass the check that exists to refuse exactly that, against a request saying acceptable_substitutions: "none".

The frozen vectors pinned productRefsEqual the whole time. A second, weaker notion of product identity living beside them is how a gate passes its own tests while disagreeing with the contract it enforces — and the vectors I added this session made that worse, because they exercised the helper and not the gate.

A field name that never existed

Drain reauthorization read cap.effects_idempotency. The validated manifest stores cap.effects.idempotency; dispatch.ts and the consent digest both read it correctly. So every capability declaring supported was reauthorized as unsupported during an update, and the claim guard terminalizes a task whose envelope disagrees with its authorization — work queued before an update DIED instead of draining. The test fixture carried the same invalid shape, which is the whole reason it survived: the fixture and the code agreed with each other and with nothing else.

Adoption had to read the repo, not be told about it
