The recovery route I added earlier this round took the pointer AND its CID from the request. Codex found the consequence: a caller who pairs the live CID with a fabricated high-sequence pointer gets a CAS that SUCCEEDS while publishing a successor to a record that never existed. "An explicit recovery action" is not a reason to trust content — it is a reason to go and look.

So there is now a CatalogRecordReader, injected exactly like the writer, and adoption reads the live pointer itself: the caller names only the catalog. The answer is validated by the consumer's own validator, checked to be about the catalog that was asked for and to name THIS supplier, and the CID recorded is the one the repo reported.

A fence consulted where it could not yet have failed

§16.2 was checked once, before the awaited snapshot write, and never again — so a node superseded during that round trip still advanced the head. The fence is now asked again immediately before the pointer CAS, and abandoning there is safe by construction: the snapshot is immutable and content-addressed, so consumers still see the previous head and a retry only rewrites the pointer.

Withdrawal is now fenced too, and the earlier decision has to be corrected rather than quietly kept. It was left unfenced when it only BUILT a tombstone — fencing it then would have removed an owner's ability to see how to stop selling at the moment they most want to. It WRITES now, and a superseded node writing an irreversible tombstone into a repo it no longer owns is precisely what §16.2 is for. The build stays open; the publication does not.

A whole owner surface missing on the phone

Only the server installed the catalog record writer, so publish and withdraw answered no_record_writer on a deployment that ships a PDS session and already publishes its restore epoch through that very client. The §10.3 reasoning that keeps a phone from FETCHING a supplier's feed does not transfer: this is a write to the node's OWN repo, not an outbound connection whose address a phone cannot report. Both roots now build the pair from one helper in @dina/home-node, so the identity re-check — the load-bearing part, since a session can rebind between boot and write — exists once. The phone clears both with its ticks, because a writer surviving an identity switch would publish the new owner's catalog into the previous owner's repo.

Three smaller ones

A cast turned a store-editable row into the content of a SIGNATURE: lifecycle genesis read its acknowledgement with JSON.parse … as rather than rehydrating it. Recording an order before sending it only helps if something later ASKS, and the pre-send record left nextPollAtMs null — invisible to both the sweeper and the poller. And my new conformance vectors were not reproducible from the generator, with one case labelled "explicitly undefined" while storing JSON null; both are generated now, and undefined (dropped) and null (a value that survives) are pinned apart.

C-09 — every buyer-side write was load, await, then clobber

The buyer order store had no swap value, and every write to it is load → AWAIT → write: the executor sends, the poller asks a supplier. Without a revision the last writer wins, and the last writer is the SLOWEST one. Three consequences, all of them the expensive kind:

  • Two orders on the wire. The duplicate check at step 1 is a READ; two concurrent submissions both pass it. Only the insert is atomic — and the executor ignored its answer and sent anyway. A lost create now refuses.
  • A settled order un-settled. A send completing after a re-poll already recorded an acknowledgement wrote its own outcome_unknown over it, putting the order back into the state that invites a duplicate.
  • One authorization spent twice. "One authorization, one resend" is only true if the consume is atomic; two workers that both read the flag each sent.

The resolution is one function, settleBuyerOrder, because four call sites deciding what a lost race means would eventually disagree. A lost race is NOT "retry with my value" — my value is older than what is already there. If the live row is terminal, leave it; if it is not, re-apply the answer on top of the live row so the newer poll count survives and the answer is not dropped. One retry, never a loop: a second conflict means a third writer, and spinning on an order lane is worse than losing the write.

The terminal-wins branch first looked unkillable, and the reason is worth recording: the state machine ALSO refuses to move a terminal record, so preserving the state proves nothing about the resolver. What the guard actually saves is the WRITE — a fall-through re-applies and rewrites identical values at a new revision, on a settled order. The test now asserts the revision is unchanged, and the mutation dies.

C-03 — the reconcile gate demanded a field the protocol has never had

Provider ingress authorized order_reconcile by comparing params.buyer_did to the authenticated sender. OrderReconcileRequest carries no such field — the protocol type has none and buildReconcileRequest sends none — so EVERY conforming reconcile was denied and §12.7's disaster-recovery path was dark. The tests around it passed because they invented the field.

Worse than a bug, had the field existed: a sender-signed inner body is not authority. query.fromDid is the relay-authenticated identity and is already what flows on as authorizedBuyerDid. A payload that DOES name a buyer must still agree, so the check survives as a conditional rather than a requirement.

The fix also corrected a test that contradicted its own sibling: order_reconcile sat in the "denies a peer who does not own the order" group beside a case asserting the opposite. Status and cancellation are entitlement-by-EXISTENCE; reconcile is entitlement-by-EVIDENCE, and for a peer who owns nothing the honest answer is never_received. The grouping only looked right because the vacuous check denied everything.

C-10 — one conversation pins one version

§9.13 is explicit: "the quote chain and any order built on it use the version of the originating request; a counterproposal cannot silently upgrade the conversation". Nothing enforced it. Every validator ran checkProtocolVersion, which answers a DIFFERENT question — may I parse this at all — and a 1.0 participant CAN parse a 1.7 document, because minor is additive. So a 1.0 request accepted a 1.7 quote, and the terms each side believed it had agreed were written under different field sets.

verifyConversationVersion now runs at four binding gates: quote-answers-request, revision-extends-held (as an immutable field, which is exactly the counterproposal clause), order-against-quote, and acknowledgement-against-order. The last takes the version OPTIONALLY, because a node checking a late answer holds a record rather than the proposal — demanding it would put that function back out of reach of the one place that most needs it, which is how it shipped with no caller at all.

Two fixtures turned out to describe states the protocol forbids, and passed only because nothing checked: a lifecycle test seeded a 1.7 order against a 1.0 quote, and the buyer fixtures produced acknowledgements carrying no version.

C-02 — a policy that was stored and never read

orderAcceptance: 'auto' | 'review' IS §15.2b's question — does accepting an order need a human — and nothing in production read it. A supplier who chose review still had their runner's accepted recorded and SIGNED without anyone seeing it, because the approval-binding check only runs when an approval is supplied and the inbound plugin lane supplies none. The setting existed, the UI could set it, and it governed nothing.

ACCEPTANCE ONLY. A rejection commits this business to nothing and a counterproposal is an offer the buyer must still take up; gating those would stop a supplier declining work while their approver is asleep, which is the opposite of protecting them. accepted is the one answer that reserves stock and creates an obligation.

A settings row that does not VALIDATE fails closed. "I cannot read your policy" must not read as "you said auto" on the single decision that spends money. An ABSENT row is a different thing and is allowed through — a node that never configured commerce has not chosen review, and refusing there would break every default deployment.

The refusal is its own code. approval_required and approval_binding_failed send an operator to different places: one is a card waiting on their desk, the other is a mismatch to investigate.

C-12 — seven named widenings against a twenty-field consent digest

detectUpdateWidening compared capability ids, action and privacy class, context ceiling, categories, personas and idempotency. The canonical consent projection covers twenty fields. So an update could add a NETWORK DESTINATION, swap the runtime ISSUER or pinned ARTIFACTS — a different party and different code receiving the owner's data — widen the brokered-operation allowlist, change the interaction mode, add a lane it may serve, or raise the verification budget, and be classified as "no widening" while the consent hash it is measured against moved.

The rule is now INVERTED for everything the named kinds do not own: compared through scopeHashInput — literally the projection the consent hash is computed over, so it cannot drift — and any movement is a widening by default, naming the fields that moved.

Two exclusion sets, each for a stated reason. The four fields the typed rules own are skipped because those have a DIRECTION the code can rank, and reporting them here too would turn every NARROWING into a re-consent prompt — an owner prompted for everything reads nothing. And params_schema / result_schema are skipped because §9.13 says minors are strictly additive and a same-major update REBINDS while in-flight tasks complete against their pinned schemas: prompting on every minor release would defeat the drain mechanism that exists to absorb it. The pinned-schema hash still binds each task at the claim guard, which is a different guarantee.

My first attempt excluded nothing and turned four narrowing tests red — which is how the direction distinction got written down rather than assumed.

C-13 — scope was gated, behavior was not

§16.5 lists them together — "Manifest CID, behavior hash, schemas, network domains, issuer, execution mode, or data scope changes follow the generic plugin update and re-consent rules" — and §20.12 names "behavior and scope hashes" side by side among the controls against a malicious update. The review carried perCapabilityScopeHashes and no behavior comparison at all, so material executable behavior could change under an existing install with nothing to compare and nothing to show.

The review now reports the hash BEFORE and AFTER rather than a boolean: an owner asked to approve a behavior change is entitled to see what they are approving, and an audit entry recording only "it changed" cannot be checked afterwards. The confirm must echo the reviewed hash — content-bound for the same reason the widening list is, because a re-prepare in another tab would otherwise make one tap apply a different release's behavior.

It is NOT folded into the widening list. Nothing here can rank executable behavior, and a card forced to invent a direction for it would be guessing; behavior_change_unreviewed is its own refusal beside requires_reconsent, which is about scope.

C-11 — the catalog records were checked for emptiness, not shape

Every other record in @dina/commerce-protocol validates DIDs through validateDid, timestamps through validateIsoUtc, digests through validateHex64 and versions through validateProtocolVersionShape. The catalog snapshot and pointer asked only whether the strings were non-empty — so this producer could publish, and rehydrateCatalogPointer could adopt as its own head, records a stricter consumer rejects. Producer/consumer divergence on a PUBLIC record is the expensive kind: the supplier believes they published and the buyer sees nothing.

Two judgements worth recording. snapshot_rkey is validated as an ID, not a digest — this producer happens to use the snapshot digest as the key because the record is content-addressed, but that is a producer choice, and pinning it here would put one implementation's convention into the contract others must satisfy. And the genesis-coherence check enforces ONE direction (sequence 1 may not name a predecessor) using the CHAIN VERIFIER'S OWN STRING verbatim; the mirror case belongs to the chain verifier, which sees the pair. Two wordings for one fault would make a single incident look like two in an operator's log, and the refusal strings are frozen precisely to stop that.

C-18 — continuity authority was never released, and the counter was the wrong one

Two defects, and the second only becomes dangerous once the first is fixed. releaseContinuity was written, tested, and called by NOTHING; continuity authorizations carry expiresAt: null on purpose, because no clock knows when an order ends. So a prior plugin CID kept authority over this node's lifecycle lane for ever, and every update left another one behind.

The counter it would have released on — countReservedByServingManifest — answers "is anything waiting to be DECIDED". An accepted order is decided the instant the supplier answers, while dispatch, delivery and any dispute come after. Wiring the release to that counter would have revoked authority the buyer still needs, which is a worse failure than the one being fixed. The counter came first, and unfinished is now defined by the STATUS CHAIN.

Delivered still counts as unfinished. It is terminal only after its dispute window, which SQL cannot evaluate — so the query errs toward "still working". The cost is a prior CID kept alive slightly too long; the cost of the other direction is a buyer locked out of an order inside their window.

The release is a SWEEP rather than an event, because the moment a lane becomes releasable is the moment its last order goes terminal — inside the lifecycle engine, a commerce concern that must not reach into the plugin registry to revoke authority. The coordinator re-reads the count itself and refuses while work remains, so the sweep can only ever be LATE, never early.

Dual review — four rounds

Two independent reviewers: a Claude continuity subagent and Codex via the openai-codex plugin. R1 8+17, R2 5+12, R3 7+15, R4 6+15. No version has yet had both PASS.

The counts did not fall monotonically, and the reason matters: R1 reviewed the original code; R2 mostly reviewed the snake_case conversion R1 demanded (1,400 renamed sites); R3 mostly reviewed R2’s fixes and their tests; R4 reviewed the wire change and the round-3 fixes. Each round found a different, shallower layer rather than the same defects recurring.

R4 produced no reviewer disagreements — the first round with none, because the scope ruling above was given to both as a frozen rubric. Four findings were flagged independently by both. Where severities differed the higher was taken.

Test quality was the dominant recurring theme. Seven tests were caught passing for the wrong reason: a missing verifyHeldEvidence default; hand-built acknowledgements rejected before the branch under test; a guarded if that asserted nothing; receipts.put ignoring conflicts so in-place corruption silently no-oped; admission returning reserved with no acknowledgement; an assertion comparing a value to itself; and an unwrapped evidence fixture. Every guard added in rounds 2–4 is now mutation-verified — reintroduce the defect (asserting the code is present first), watch the test go red, restore. Two mutations were themselves caught as no-ops by that assertion.

X-11 — §12.7 re-adoption is inert in production, and its two halves disagree

The most consequential finding of this session, and the one I most want you to read. §12.7/§16.2's held-evidence re-adoption does not run at all on a real node, and it fails in a way where each half looks safe alone.

  • The buyer's installHeldEvidenceReader is called only in tests. Neither composition root installs one, so a real buyer presents NO evidence, ever.
  • The supplier's verifyHeldEvidence is also unwired at both boots. It fails closed, so re-adoption is refused even when evidence does arrive.

Individually each reads as cautious. Together: a supplier that lost state answers never_received; the buyer, having presented nothing, treats that answer as LEGAL; and never_received is the one answer that authorises a resubmission. That is the duplicate order the whole of §12.7 exists to prevent, reachable on any node today.

Why it cannot simply be wired up. Chasing it turned up a contract disagreement — the fifth comment/code divergence this session and the load-bearing one:

  • commerce-protocol/reconcile.ts: "signature is hex-encoded Ed25519 over the record's canonical JSON."
  • core/lifecycle_engine.ts's verifier: "Ed25519 over the retained D2D envelope."

One field, two contracts. A buyer cannot satisfy both, and because NEITHER side is wired, nothing has ever exercised the disagreement. This is why the gap survived every green suite: the tests install a reader that returns whatever the test wants, so the question of what a real signature IS never arose.

Design decision (reversible; tell me if you disagree). I am taking the ENVELOPE reading, for three reasons that are facts about the code rather than preferences:

  1. A supplier emits no per-record signature. OrderAcknowledgement has no signature field; authenticity comes from the signed D2D envelope that carried it. So the canonical-JSON reading asks the buyer to retain something the supplier never sends, and would need a wire-format change and a protocol major bump.
  2. The evidence vocabulary already models the envelope: receiptEvidence.receivedFrom takes {fromDid, observedAt, envelopeId, keyId, signature}. It was designed for exactly this and the buyer simply never called it.
  3. The verifier that will actually judge the evidence — Core's — already says envelope.

The signature is available where it is needed: receive_pipeline.ts verifies signatureHex before the buyer seam and just does not pass it on.

Built. And the envelope reading needed one correction to be true.

Building it exposed a hole in my own decision. An envelope signature is checkable only against the envelope's bytes, and I had planned to retain {envelopeId, signature} — an id and a signature, with the signed bytes thrown away. No supplier could ever have verified that. It would have replaced one unverifiable pair with another and looked wired the whole time.

So the buyer retains the message. HeldEvidence now carries a RetainedEnvelope — the six fields the D2D signature covers, in the shape the wire builder serialises: id, type, from, to (array), created_time (number), body (plaintext, pre-base64). The types are load-bearing: storing the timestamp as a string emits "created_time":"177…" where the signature covers "created_time":177…, and every verification fails for a reason no error message would name.

The pairing is the new attack, so Core binds it. {record, envelope, signature} invites presenting one message's genuine signature beside another message's record. verifyHeldRecord checks, in compiled code: the message came FROM this supplier; it went TO the buyer now presenting it; and the record's content digest appears among the signed body's own string values (walked over the parsed JSON, not substring-matched, so a digest inside an unrelated blob is not a match). The record's own validator recomputes that digest from its content, so digest-in-signed-body means the supplier put its name to a message carrying exactly this record. Only the cryptography is delegated to the injected callback — a boot that implements it loosely cannot drop the binding.

Both halves are built in Core (makeHeldEvidenceReader, makeHeldEvidenceVerifier) and installed at both composition roots. The reader being test-only for its whole existence is the argument: a defence each root must remember to install is one a root eventually forgets, so there is now one implementation to be right or wrong, phone and server alike.

Answer shape, worth knowing. Unverifiable evidence is refused non-disclosingly — not answered never_received. That is stronger than it first reads: never_received is the one outcome authorising a resubmission, so once evidence is presented the only safe answers are "verified, here is the decision" and "I cannot tell". "It never happened" is off the table.

What proves it. Seven unit cases drive the real verifier over a real keypair (wrong key, genuine signature over other bytes, tampered body, unresolvable key, malformed signature, and the honest control). The two-node journey — ChairMaker restores a backup from before Sancho's order, Sancho presents what he kept, ChairMaker re-adopts — now runs the real verifier rather than () => true. Its counterpart drives three forgeries (wrong key, addressed to another buyer, signed body that does not carry the record) and asserts none of them re-adopt. The commerce boundary test caught the stale "not yet wired" annotation on its own.

Open question, narrowed. The envelope reading is now implemented, so this is a question about what to do next rather than a blocker. Per-record supplier signatures remain the more robust design — evidence would survive outside the transport that carried it, and a buyer would not need to store message bodies — at the cost of a protocol major, a signing key threaded into the commerce runtime (it has none today), and a signature field on three record types plus their digest-exclusion rules. Say the word and I will do it; nothing built here forecloses it.

X-06/X-07 — a drained task skipped the one consent check that mattered

The update/rebind coordinator turned out to be built and wired at both boots: it snapshots the prior capability's schemas, creates the bounded drain and the unexpiring lifecycle_continuity entries, rebinds listings, and releases continuity only when the relevant orders are terminal. What was wrong was on the claim side, and it was an authority hole rather than a gap.

The shape of it. The claim guard deliberately skips its whole consent block for a drained task, and that is correct: after an update the capability may have left the current manifest entirely, so the drain entry is the consent proof. But one check inside that block was not bookkeeping — §11.2a's rule that an ingress task may dispatch only a capability consented as provider, and every other plugin task requires tool. Skipping the block skipped that too, and the drain row recorded no kinds to check against. So a continuity lane opened for a provider capability admitted a tool envelope on the same capability id, and the reverse.

The fix is to make the row able to answer. DrainAuthorization now carries authorizedKinds, taken from the PRIOR capability for the same reason the schemas are — the current manifest cannot vouch for what was approved under the old CID. The guard computes the required kind once and applies it on both lanes.

An empty list is a refusal, not a wildcard. Rows written before the column existed default to [], which reads as "this row cannot say which kinds were consented". Cannot-say has to refuse: the alternative is admitting an envelope onto a consent no row can show covered it. Same posture as the held-evidence verifier, and for the same reason.

Tests. Three cases — provider lane refuses a tool envelope, tool lane refuses a provider envelope (with a matching-lane control so the refusals are the check working rather than the lane being shut), and a no-kinds row refuses. Mutation-checked: stubbing the predicate to false fails exactly those three and nothing else. The type change also flushed out every hand-seeded drain row in the suite, which is the point — each now has to state which kind it authorized.

And the major check, which both sides already had the facts for

buildContinuityEnvelope stamps prior_version from the row; the row records priorVersion from the manifest the install stopped running. Nothing compared them. A lane retained to serve major 0 admitted an envelope declaring major 2, and the runner would answer the buyer under a contract their order was never opened under.

The check applies to continuity entries only, and the asymmetry is the design. A drain entry covers work that ALREADY EXISTED at the rebind: those envelopes came from the ordinary builder, which does not stamp the field. Requiring one there would terminalize exactly the in-flight tasks a drain exists to let finish. A continuity task is created AFTER the rebind by a builder that always stamps it, so there the field's absence is a fact about the envelope rather than about when it was made — and it is refused. Compared by MAJOR, since §9.13 retains a lane per major and makes minors additive.

Writing this exposed that the existing fixture built continuity-lane tasks with buildPluginEnvelope, which production never does for that lane — so the suite had been exercising the drain lane's rules while claiming to test continuity. The fixture now stamps the field the way the real builder does.

Still open on this row. Binding a continuity claim to a specific NON-TERMINAL ORDER. The envelope does not name the order it continues except inside params, so the check needs a field the wire does not carry yet. Not done, and not counted as done.

X-08 — the archive preflight measured everything against an unverified yardstick

The structural half of §16.2's fail-closed reconstruction was already there and runs before a single row is written: required tables present, row shapes sound, no dangling order/quote/ receipt references, no duplicate keys, and the whole import refused rather than partially applied. What it did not do was ask whether the receipts were real.

Why that is the load-bearing gap. Every other check asks whether the archive agrees with itself — and a self-authored archive agrees with itself perfectly. archive.ts already states the payload is attacker-influenced. Receipts are what the order refs, quote heads and status heads are all measured against, so a forged receipt is not one bad row: it is a corrupted measure that makes every other check pass.

The fix. Each receipt's stored record is re-derived under its own domain and must produce the digest it is filed under. restore_fence_event — the one domain with no digest field of its own — is filed under the EPOCH record's digest and stores {record, voidedQuotes}, so it is verified as an epoch record read out of that envelope. Skipping it would have left the one receipt kind a restore itself writes as the unchecked way in. A domain with no digest rule is refused: cannot-derive has to mean no, or "verified" would mean "verified, or filed under a domain someone invented".

A forged row stops counting as a receipt. It is excluded from the set the cross-references resolve against, so whatever named it is reported dangling too. I wrote the test expecting the opposite and the code was right — admitting a forged row as a valid reference target would let the structural checks pass against a record nobody signed, which is the exact problem this removes.

Two fixtures were unrepresentative and are now real. The preflight suite filed an invented digest under the domain order_status, which is not in the vocabulary at all — so it validated an archive shape production cannot emit. The export round-trip inserted 'c'.repeat(64) with record_json: '{}'. Both now carry a record and the digest that record actually produces.

Still open on this row. Force-restore CLEARS a commerce table the archive did not supply, which is data loss on a partial archive rather than a forgery. The preflight refuses a missing table outright, so the reachable case is narrow, but the clearing behaviour itself is untouched. Not done, and not counted as done.

ARCH-0b/0c — two things a restore could stand on that it should not

0b: the post-restore re-offer ran inside the write transaction

A live defect I introduced. rejectVoidedFamily asked the injected resignVoidedQuote seam for a replacement quote from INSIDE the admitOrder transaction. A seam backed by a supplier plugin or an ERP connector would then have done plugin dispatch or a network round trip while SQLite held the write lock — on mobile that is the single connection, for as long as the counterparty took to answer. Nothing in production wired it, so the cost was latent rather than observed; the shape was wrong either way.

Three steps that cannot overlap now: admission refuses quote_voided in a short transaction that performs no I/O; the supplier composes a replacement with NO transaction open; CommerceAdmissionService.registerReplacementQuote validates and registers it in a second short transaction. The engine loses the seam entirely, which is the point — an I/O hook has no business on a domain engine whose methods all run inside someone else's transaction.

Behaviour change worth your attention. Admission no longer answers quote_superseded with a fresh head inline; it answers quote_voided and the buyer requests a new quote. That is what the fall-through already said and what CMR-1 settled (Core refuses, never invents terms), but it is one fewer round trip saved for the buyer. Say so if you want the inline answer back — it would mean an async admission path, which is a larger change than this one.

Also checked and already correct, contrary to the row's description: settleUse's boolean is not ignored. All three settlement call sites go through QuoteFamily.settle(), which throws CommerceIntegrityError on a failed CAS, so a decided order with an unsettled hold rolls back rather than leaking capacity.

0c: fence selection took the tallest receipt and asked nothing else

A restored supplier fast-forwards to what the buyer can prove, so which receipt it lands on decides the chain's whole future. The selection was reduce to the highest sequence plus two checks against the local head. Two things got through:

  • A gap. Present the head's successor and then a receipt six sequences later, and the supplier jumps past records nobody showed it. Every status names its predecessor precisely so the chain has no gaps; skipping one discards that at the moment it matters most.
  • A fork in the evidence itself. Two authentic receipts at the same top sequence with different digests mean the supplier signed twice at one height. reduce kept whichever came first in the array — the buyer chose the winner by ordering its own request. The existing same-height check only compared against the LOCAL head, so two receipts both above it never triggered it.

selectFencePredecessor now walks head+1 upward: every sequence present, every record naming the one before it, one record per height. Rooted by DIGEST rather than by requiring the head record itself — a buyer presenting only what is above our head is being economical, and what matters is that its lowest record names our head.

Both are refusals rather than repairs, because which branch is real is exactly what a restored node lost and cannot re-derive. The rule lives in status_chain.ts because it is a chain rule; signature verification stays with the caller, and the function assumes every receipt it is handed is already proven authentic.

Ten cases, mutation-checked. Neutering the link comparison outright crashed the suite (Tests: 0 total is a crash, not a pass); chaining the walk to the wrong field failed the contiguous case, which is the assertion that matters.

Also verified as already fixed, contrary to the row: signStatusUpdate no longer treats the previous head receipt as optional — it fails closed when the record cannot be read, so a lost receipt can no longer let Core sign a regressing fulfilled quantity.

ARCH-2 — the gate was already split; the case that proves it was not tested

The ingress gate already separates the two questions the row asks it to: order_status and cancel_order require an owned reference (speaking about someone else's order is the attack), while order_reconcile authorizes on sender identity and delegates existence to the lifecycle engine. The comments there record why, including that an earlier version of the gate silently disabled the recovery path it was meant to protect.

What was missing was the test that joins it up. Every reconcile case in the ingress suite answered from a STUB lifecycle, so the lane was proven to reach reconcile and nothing more. Three things had been built and tested separately — the gate that authorizes reconcile without requiring existence, the engine's §16.2 re-adoption path, and X-11's held evidence — and nothing exercised the lane between them.

There is now an end-to-end case: a supplier accepts an order on one node, a second node stands up over empty stores (what a restore from a pre-order backup leaves), and the buyer's reconcile arrives through the real ingress lane carrying evidence that is really signed and verified by the production verifier. The answer is received_unresolved — re-adoption — where a bare reconcile gets never_received.

The control taught me something. My first version reused one supplier for both halves and the control failed: the bare reconcile also answered received_unresolved. That is the feature working — the first reconcile RE-ADOPTED the order, so the node genuinely held a record by the time the second arrived. It just makes that supplier useless as a control, so the control now uses a second restored node that never saw the evidence.

ARCH-3 — the cast was in the wrong place, and my first fix did nothing

Three wire bugs shipped through the record builders: a camelCase key in the draft, and an as CommerceOrderStatus / as OrderAcknowledgement on the way out telling tsc to accept the result anyway. The success criterion for this row is concrete — reintroducing a camelCase key in any wire-record builder must fail tsc — so I checked it rather than assumed it.

My first fix failed that check, and the failure is the useful part. I removed the trailing cast and annotated the RESULT. Then I injected supplierOrderId into the draft and tsc stayed silent. Two rules combine to allow it: an object literal with no type annotation gets no excess-property check, and a SPREAD is never excess-property checked. So the stray key rode through the unannotated draft and then through the spread into the typed result, untouched. Annotating the result is exactly the wrong end.

The fix is to annotate the DRAFT — Omit<CommerceOrderStatus, 'status_digest'> and its two siblings — so the key is checked on the line that writes it. Re-injected, all three builders now fail with the right message: "'supplierOrderId' does not exist … Did you mean to write 'supplier_order_id'?"

One subtlety worth recording. OrderAcknowledgement is a discriminated union, and Omit distributes across it by keeping only the fields every member shares — so reason_code vanished and the literal was checked against the wrong shape. The annotation extracts the variant the builder actually emits (Extract<…, { kind: 'rejected' }>) instead.

Also in this row: loadHeadStatus now reads through rehydrateOrderStatus rather than a local parse-and-cast. The checks were equivalent, but written twice — and a second copy of a rule is a place for the two to drift.

And a defect that had already been fixed differently. The row says decisionOutcome has no default arm. It has one, and every caller reaches it through a validator that enforces the closed kind set — so the arm is unreachable. It was answering received_unresolved, which tells a buyer to keep polling a record that can never resolve: an impossible state dressed as a business outcome. It now delegates to a never-typed helper, so the compiler proves the three cases cover the union and adding a fourth kind without a case fails the build.

Not done, and not counted. The row asks for three explicit layers at the seam (structural validation, signature/identity verification, domain construction) as a named structure. What exists is those three things happening in that order at each seam, enforced by the boundary test and now by the draft annotations, rather than a shared abstraction. Eight as-casts remain outside the builders; each is a narrowing after a validator has run, which is the safe form, but I have not audited every one.

A commerce order now crosses the real D2D transport

Counting properly, there were SEVEN in-process journeys, not the six I reported — I had missed procurement_journey.test.ts, which walks discovery to purchase through the real routes. Every one of them hands the supplier a body some test constructed. None had an order cross the transport.

That is this subsystem's recurring defect one layer out. The transport is well tested and commerce is well tested; the seam where a commerce order meets the transport's own rules was not. A field the ingress needs but the envelope drops, or a sender binding that reads the body instead of the authenticated envelope, would pass every existing suite.

two_node_d2d_order.test.ts: two keypairs, real sealMessage, real receiveD2D. Sancho's order is sealed and signed; ChairMaker unseals, verifies the signature against Sancho's registered key, binds the sender from the ENVELOPE rather than the body, and admits. Four cases — the happy path, a forged signature dropped, a sealed replay refused by the transport's own cache, and an unconfigured capability declining to bypass.

The limit, stated because the claim is easy to overstate. Two Cores in ONE PROCESS. No socket, no MsgBox relay, no two machines — §25.6's two-machine journey stays manual. This proves an order survives the crypto and the receive pipeline, which is more than "the engines agree" and less than "it works between two nodes".

I also deleted the SQLite scaffolding I had opened in this file's setup and never read. Dead setup in a test is the same shape as an unwired engine in production, and I had just spent the session removing those.

§9.13 — a continuity claim now names the order it continues

The last gap I had recorded rather than closed. A continuity lane is retained so a SPECIFIC set of in-flight orders can finish under the contract they were opened in, but the claim check was per-lane: an authorized lane admitted any newly created task on the prior CID for that capability, including one for an order that had already gone terminal. A runner answering for a closed order, under a manifest the install no longer runs.

It needed a wire field, so I added one. PluginTaskEnvelope.continuity_order_id, stamped by buildContinuityEnvelope from the value the INGRESS GATE authorized — the same handover discipline as authorizedBuyerDid, for the same reason: a runner that could name the order would be choosing which order its lane covered. The buyer comes from the envelope's authenticated service_ingress.from_did, so the claim is bound to buyer AND order, which is what §9.13 asks.

The predicate is shared, deliberately. "Unfinished" already had a definition — the SQL behind countUnfinishedByServingManifest, which the continuity-release sweep uses to decide when a lane may be retired. I narrowed that same query to one row rather than writing a second reading of the word. Two spellings would eventually disagree, and the pair that disagreed would be the release sweep and the claim guard: a lane released while a claim still believes the order is live, or the reverse.

Fails closed on every uncertainty — no order named, no commerce runtime to ask, or a finished order. Three new cases plus a mutation check; neutering the store lookup fails the already-finished case, which is the one that carries the property.

One existing test changed meaning and I did not force it back. The "tool-only lane" control used to assert that a tool envelope on a tool-authorized lane is ADMITTED. It cannot be any more: order_status is a lifecycle capability, its continuity lane is per-order, and a tool-shaped envelope carries no ingress and therefore no order. Production never builds that shape — the rebind coordinator opens continuity lanes only for the lifecycle capabilities, and those are answered on the ingress lane. The test now asserts the refusal and says which check produced it; the "matching lane admits" control lives in the provider case, where the shape is real.

Force-restore no longer empties a table the backup cannot speak for

force means "make the target look like the backup", and for a table the backup DESCRIBES that is right — an empty list is a statement and clearing honours it. But the import cleared every allowlisted table before checking whether the payload carried one, so a key that was ABSENT — an archive written before that table existed — also cleared. That is data loss dressed as a faithful overwrite.

The commerce set is exactly that shape. A backup taken before commerce existed carries none of its tables — the preflight already names the state predatesCommerce — and a force-restore from it wiped every order reference, quote head, use counter and status head on a live trading node. Fixed on the identity and persona paths both.

The evidence is weaker than the fix, and I would rather say so than imply otherwise. The test added alongside proves the reachable half: a table carried as empty still clears. It passes under the OLD behaviour too — I reverted the guard and checked, rather than assuming. The absent-key branch has no end-to-end test because this build cannot produce an archive with a missing key: dumpTable returns [] for a table it cannot read, and the exporter writes a key for every allowlisted table. The only producer of that shape is an older build, and there is nothing in the current API to construct one from. The guard is defensive and correct; the test beside it is a regression guard on what I changed, not a proof of what I fixed.

Two mutation attempts on this one produced Tests: 0 total before I found one that compiled — a crash is not a survival, and reading it as one is how a fix gets believed without evidence.

The consolidated test plan, and what it measured

docs/COMMERCE_TEST_PLAN.md. Built by reading the suites and the spec rather than by recalling what was written, so the gaps in it are measured.

What the retailer/manufacturer coverage actually is: 47 journey cases across five files, Sancho as retailer and ChairMaker as manufacturer throughout, all driving the real engines. procurement_scenario runs the whole set twice — in-memory and SQLite — so a rule that depends on storage semantics cannot pass in one and fail in the other.

Threat-model coverage was measured by behaviour, not by section tags. A raw §-reference diff claimed 53 uncovered sections, which is misleading: §4.x is vocabulary and §3.x is ownership, neither testable. Checking §20 by what the tests actually do gives 14 of 16 threats covered.

The two that are not, and why that is scope rather than omission. §20.3 (reputation whitewashing by variant churn) and §20.14 (Sybil suppliers) name controls that live in the AppView and PeerLens layers — quotas, retrieval diversity, reputation inheritance, identity age. What Core owns for them is the signed primitive those controls read: ProductRelationshipClaim lineage assertions and historical catalog snapshots, both validated and vector-frozen and tested. The enforcement belongs to §25.4's AppView tests. I am recording this rather than writing a Core test that would only assert the primitive exists.

Four gaps are stated in the plan rather than closed: continuity claims are not bound to a specific non-terminal order (needs a wire field); force-restore still clears a commerce table the archive did not supply; ARCH-3's three seam layers are an enforced ordering rather than a shared abstraction, with eight un-audited narrowing casts outside the builders; and there is no live two-node commerce run over MsgBox — every cross-Dina journey here is in-process.

WS-8.1/8.2/8.3/8.5 — built, as @dina/managed-runtime

I raised the concern that a hosting product does not belong in the commerce WBS; the owner reaffirmed "complete all pending items". So it is built — in its own package, which satisfies both the instruction and §17's opening line. Nothing in it imports a vertical, and nothing in it knows what a quote or an order is.

The design is one rule, made structural. §17.2 permits sharing the control plane, AppView, MsgBox, Brain workers, the package cache, supervision, metering and backup — then constrains all of it: shared workers "never receive a reusable tenant master key or unscoped database handle". Every decision below follows from refusing to let that be a convention:

  • TenantRecord has NOWHERE to put a key. Not a nulled field, not an optional — no field. A design where the key could be stored and merely should not be is a design where one day it is.
  • ManagedBlobStore has no decrypt, not even an injected one. A hook the vendor could populate is a key the vendor holds. A tenant's export is decrypted by the tenant with the recovery phrase that already opens its vault, which is what makes "tenant-owned" a fact rather than a promise.
  • A CellLease names ONE tenant and one purpose and expires. There is no all-tenants lease and no way to derive a second tenant's from a first. LeasePurpose is a closed set, because a free-text reason would let a caller invent a purpose no cell knows how to refuse.

Cells register SEALED (§17.3), and lease REFUSES a sealed cell rather than waking it. Waking means key material becomes resident, so it is the tenant's own act — modelled as a separate method precisely so the two cannot be confused at a call site. A worker that could wake a cell by asking for work would make idle sealing decorative.

Judgement calls worth your eye:

  • A zero quota means "do not serve", not "unlimited". The fail-closed reading of an unset or suspended plan, and the classic way a suspended tenant keeps being served.
  • Fleet suspension is one call, not a loop over bindings. When a hosted runner is compromised the operator needs a stop that cannot half-apply; a loop failing partway leaves a subset of businesses served by a runner already judged hostile.
  • Refusals are ordered least-to-most specific. unknown_runner says nothing about which tenants exist; not_bound_to_tenant nothing about which installs do. A probe learns the least that is still true.
  • The blob store copies on write and re-digests on read. A caller reusing its buffer would otherwise rewrite stored history, and the digest check would then report corruption for a store that did nothing wrong. A blob failing its digest is REFUSED, never returned with a warning — it is exactly the object a tenant would restore from without looking.
  • Idle sealing is a sweep, not a timer. The caller owns the cadence, so a deployment schedules it and a test runs it at an instant of its choosing.

How this relates to Core's claim guard, which stays the last word. HostedRunnerRegistry.admits returning true is permission to TRY, never to claim — Core's guard then decides. It adds only the binding a single Core cannot see: which tenants a vendor identity is admitted to at all, and whether the fleet is suspended. Both are control-plane facts. Nothing here can make a claim succeed that Core would refuse, which is what keeps a compromised control plane from becoming a compromised tenant.

24 tests, all four isolation mutations killed (lease tenant check, zero-quota reading, vendor-to-any-tenant admission, store copy-on-write). Gate: 18,928 across nine packages, typecheck 0. One mobile flake (recovery-phrase idle timer) appeared and passed on re-run and in isolation twice — the same one recorded earlier, not a regression.

Still honest about scope. This is the hosting CONTROL LAYER: scheduling, leasing, binding, metering, tenant-owned storage. It is not a deployment — no process supervisor, no real cell process, no billing integration. Those are operational build-out, and I would rather ship a correct control layer with its invariants proven than a half-running system whose isolation nobody has tested.

WS-8 / §17 — what the spec actually asks commerce to do here

The owner was right that WS-8 is code, not a blocker, and reading §17 changed what that code is. §17 opens: "Commerce depends on the generic managed Dina runtime but does not define it. The following requirements are vertical integration constraints." Building a multi-tenant control plane, hosted runner supervisor and metering system inside the commerce pack is precisely what that sentence forbids — it would put the hosting product inside a vertical.

So the honest reading is that WS-8's four rows are the GENERIC runtime's work, and what commerce owes is the one constraint it can enforce and prove: §17.4's claim binding.

The audit. §17.4 requires every claim bound to seven things. Mapped against the guard: install ID ✓ (envelope-to-lane match), paired instance identity ✓ (install.deviceDid plus auth), exact lane ✓ (server-forced, client runner_filter ignored, exact-match-only selection), capability ✓ (consent map) and manifest CID ✓ (divergence admitted only through a live §9.13 drain authorization), claim token ✓ (claim_id CAS), authority snapshot ✓ (scope hash + config revision, both terminalizing). Tenant is structural: §17.1 keeps the single-writer Home Node model authoritative per tenant, so a tenant boundary is a whole Core with its own encrypted store and there is no tenant column to get wrong.

What was missing was not a check but a PROOF. The suite covered install isolation inside one database; nothing exercised the threat §17.4 actually names — "a vendor-wide identity alone cannot claim any tenant's task". Now four tests stand up TWO tenants as two encrypted stores with their own install repositories and workflow queues, and drive a single vendor device identity across them:

  • it MULTIPLEXES where paired into both — which §17.4 permits and is the point of hosting;
  • it cannot claim in a tenant it holds no install in, and leaves that tenant's task untouched for its own runner (nothing claimed AND nothing terminalized — a guard that killed the row would be a denial of service dressed as isolation);
  • the identity alone is not enough where the install names a different device;
  • a task does not cross tenants even when both install ids collide and pluginLane is therefore identical — the pathological case a shared control plane could really produce.

Why two databases and not two rows. Two installs in one store tests install isolation, which was already covered, and says nothing about the property §17.4 is about. Two adapters is the shape a hosted runner really faces.

A fixture correction worth recording: the first version used the SUPPLIER pack, whose capabilities are all declared provider-kind. A task with no service_ingress requires tool consent, so every claim terminalized on the kind check and the tests would have "passed" the isolation assertions for entirely the wrong reason. §17.4 names a hosted BUYER runner in the same sentence; the buyer pack's track-order is a real tool capability, which is what the tests use.

Mutation-verified: removing the device-pairing check or the server-forced lane each kill tests. Full gate: 18,904 tests, typecheck 0 errors.

Open question. WS-8.1/8.2/8.3/8.5 remain open as GENERIC runtime work — hot and cold cells, hosted runner supervision, managed catalog storage, metering. I have not built them here and I do not think they belong in this pack. If you want them built as part of this effort, say so and I will treat them as a separate vertical rather than smuggling a hosting product into the commerce WBS.

WS-9.2 — the connector could only GET, so it could not talk to an ERP

Probing for a live ERP found something better than a demo instance: the connector executor accepted a URL and headers and nothing else. Odoo speaks JSON-RPC over POST, a NetSuite RESTlet takes a POST body, an OData function import the same — so the connector lane could read a spreadsheet and almost nothing a business actually runs on. §24's "one real ERP connector" was unreachable by design, not by circumstance.

fetchUnderPolicy now takes an optional FeedRequestBody, threaded THROUGH the §10.3 policy rather than around it, so an RPC endpoint gets the same scheme, literal-address, connected-address, content-type and byte checks a feed URL does. Absent is a GET and behaves exactly as before, which is why every existing connector is untouched.

A request with a body does not follow redirects. Three options and two are wrong. Replaying the body at the new location sends a supplier's query — and, on a hop whose origin matches, its credential — to a host the operator never configured, and a redirect is exactly how an attacker chooses that host. Following as a GET, which is what a browser does to a 301 POST, silently drops the body and returns something unrelated that this node would then record as the ERP's answer. So it refuses and names the reason: an endpoint that redirects its RPC path is one whose URL the operator should correct once, rather than one this node guesses about on every call.

The body is CONFIGURATION, never a caller's argument. That is what keeps the lane safe to expose: a brokered call chooses whether to ask, never what to ask. A caller-supplied body would turn a read connector into an arbitrary RPC channel into a supplier's system of record with the owner's credential attached. For the same reason method is the literal 'POST' rather than a string — PUT, PATCH and DELETE are all WRITES to a system of record, and adding one should be a decision somebody argues for, not a value somebody passes.

Settings validation refuses a body without a content type or a content type without a body (endpoint_request_incomplete): the first is a request most servers misparse, the second a half-configured RPC endpoint that would silently GET.

Open question for the owner. No live round trip yet — both public demos were down when I tried (demo.odoo.com/start 500s, ERPNext's 404s). The tests drive the real Odoo call_kw request shape against a scripted server, which proves the request and the refusals but not a real ERP's answer. Point me at an instance — yours, or a demo when one is up — and the round trip is a config row, not a build.

Mutation-verified: removing the redirect refusal, sending the credential on every hop, and dropping the configured body each kill tests. Worth recording that my first M3 run reported all-green and I nearly believed it — the patch had not applied (wrong indentation). Confirming the edit landed before trusting the result is the same discipline as watching the suite total.

Full gate: 18,900 tests, typecheck 0 errors.

WS-11.4 / 5.4 — the AppView was never blocked, and its migrations were broken

I had this wrong and the owner corrected me. I recorded WS-5.4 and 11.4 as blocked on "infrastructure that does not exist here". It does exist: test-pds, test-appview and test-mailbox are live and answering, Postgres is installed locally, and the AppView ships drizzle migrations, a vitest suite and five commerce catalog test files. Nothing was blocked. The same correction applies to WS-8 — a multi-tenant control plane is code to write, not a service to wait for — and to 9.2, where Odoo's demo exposes real XML-RPC.

Running it locally found a deploy-breaking defect and four stale-test classes, taking the integration suite from 36/430 to 430/430, and the whole AppView from a bare database to 2,449 passing.

The deploy defect (two migrations, 0021 and 0022). The Docker deploy runs drizzle-kit migrate — chosen deliberately over push, which "still prompts on renames". But catalog_snapshots and all five commerce_catalog_* tables existed ONLY in the Drizzle schema, with no migration. install_appview.sh runs push, so that path worked and hid it. A Docker-deployed AppView therefore had no commerce catalog storage at all: com.dinakernel.commerce.searchCatalog is registered in the router and every commerce module queries those tables, so the entire commerce discovery lane fails at its first query. Migrations 0019 and 0020 exist for exactly this drift; these are its third and fourth instances.

DDL taken from what the schema PRODUCES (push into a scratch database, then pg_dump -s), not transcribed from the TypeScript — transcription invites a default or a nullability that differs and is noticed only when a row is rejected in production. Hand-written rather than generated, because drizzle-kit generate cannot diff this schema without asking whether admin_audit_log is a RENAME of trust_edges, and answering that wrongly emits a destructive migration.

A real runtime defect: graph timeouts never degraded. withGraphTimeout caught err.code === '57014', but Drizzle wraps the driver error — the pg error carrying that code sits on cause. The fallback matched nothing, so a timed-out graph query threw all the way out and a resolve response lost its whole body because one optional panel was slow. Now walked through the cause chain, bounded to five hops so a cycle cannot hang the error path.

Four stale-test classes, each the same shape — a copy of a rule that moved:

  • cleanAllTables truncated a hand-written list naming trust_policies, a table the schema dropped. One stale name failed 260 tests in beforeEach. Now asks pg_tables, which cannot drift.
  • A test helper recomputed subject ids as sha256('did:'+did) while the resolver moved to v3, which prefixes a version into the hash. The module's own doc calls that formula "a frozen wire format" — and a test re-implementing a frozen format is a second implementation of it, of which only one gets bumped.
  • The search cursor became an opaque base64url envelope, deliberately, so a strategy change could reject mid-pagination cursors loudly. Two tests still split on '::'; one compared every row against NaN. They now decode with the endpoint's own decoder and assert BOTH cursor components — the composite claim the fix was for, rather than its encoding.
  • IT-E2E-004 asserted Tier 2 name subjects are author-scoped. The resolver deliberately made them SHARED ("two reviewers of 'Aeron Chair' land on ONE subject row") — the test was asserting that reviews stay siloed, the opposite of what PeerLens is for. Its second half claimed to test Tier 1 convergence and passed no identifier at all, so its comment and its assertion contradicted each other. It now sends a real URI, with different display names, so convergence-by-identifier is actually exercised.

One assertion replaced rather than repaired: expect(tables.length) .toBe(34), whose only possible fix is to bump the number — a ritual that teaches the next reader to bump it without looking. Now a named set difference, so the failure says WHICH table appeared and the fix is an act of description.

My own mistake, recorded: I ran drizzle-kit push against the test database, which dropped the generated search_vector column the SQL migrations create, and spent a cycle chasing an FTS failure I had caused. Migrate-only is the correct local setup and is what the final run uses.

Result — from a dropped database: migrate clean, then 118 files / 2,449 tests passing, integration 430/430. The monorepo gate is unchanged at 18,897.

§12.5 — the review that nothing could close

finalizePendingCancellation was written, tested at the engine, exposed on the service, and called by nothing. resolveCancellation refuses to leave pending_review on purpose — the review closes because the OWNER decided, never because the buyer resent the request — so with no route the state was a trap. An order whose external effect had fired stayed non-terminal for ever, holding quote capacity and blocking both continuity release and plugin uninstall.

Two halves were missing, and only building both makes either useful: an operator could not SEE the parked review, and could not settle it. Now listPendingReviewCancellations scans the receipt store per order, the inbox offers finalize_cancellation only where one is actually parked, and POST /v1/commerce/cancellations/finalize settles it.

Design decision — a scan, not an index. The pending_review result IS a receipt; a second table listing them would be a projection that can disagree with the evidence it summarises. The scan is bounded to one order and its caller already holds the short list of unresolved orders.

Design decision — the allowed kinds are a named list, not "anything except pending_review". A future kind added to the protocol must not silently become an owner decision nobody designed a card for.

Two test weaknesses this exposed, both mine. The inbox route test passed with a runtime that had no lifecycle at all — the route's catch (there so one corrupt receipt cannot cost an operator the whole inbox) was hiding the wiring, and every test would have gone on passing with the feature disconnected. And the "stops listing once decided" assertion survived deleting the check it was written for: with a single cancellation it passed on receipt ordering rather than on the rule. Two cancellations on one order, one parked and one decided, cannot be answered by ordering — that version kills the mutation.

Still outstanding: an effect_started order with NO cancellation attached still has no command. It is told, not actionable, and the card says so rather than offering something that does nothing.

§9.11 buyer-side fork detection — the check the spec puts on the receiver

§9.11 ends on a sentence the implementation had not answered: "Receiver-side chain checks remain fork DETECTION for a misbehaving supplier." Supplier-side CAS is run by the party a buyer is worried about, so it is no defence at all — the buyer's own check is the whole of the protection, and verifyStatusSuccession and verifyRestoreFence were written, tested, and called by nothing. A supplier could roll a chain backwards, emit two conflicting successors, or re-point one at another order, and every one landed as ordinary progress.

Built: a buyer-side chain store keeping EVERY accepted record (a §16.2 fence may name a strict ancestor, so a head-only store would have to either refuse every fence or accept any), the verification seam, ingestion on the response lane, an owner check_status action, and the supplier half that attaches what it signed.

A chain, not a head — found by the journey test, not by reading. Succession is checked link by link, so a buyer holding sequence 2 and handed sequence 5 can neither verify it nor honestly call it a fork. The answer now carries every record from the buyer's stated since_sequence through the head. It REFUSES to truncate: a gap wider than MAX_STATUS_CATCHUP yields no chain rather than a partial one, because a partial chain is exactly what a receiver cannot verify — it would arrive looking like a fork.

Three production bugs fell out of building it, none visible from a static read:

  • The provider-ingress dedup key omitted the CAPABILITY. Commerce uses the purchase order id as the correlation id on every lane, deliberately — but "will you take this order" and "where has it got to" are two questions, and the second collided with the first. A buyer's very first order_status query was refused ingress_key_conflict, permanently. The status lane could never have run at all.
  • The same key ignored the request BODY, so a second status poll deduped onto the first completed task — answered once, then silently never again. A resend still dedups, which is the property that matters: §12.7's one authorized resubmission carries the identical order.
  • signed_status had to become signed_status_chain, and since_sequence had to be DECLARED in the manifest params — Brain strips undeclared fields before dispatch, the same defect CANCEL_PARAMS already records.

A test that proved nothing, caught by mutation. "Refuses a status signed by a supplier that is not the sender" was answered by the ORDER-binding check, not the transport one — so the confused-deputy guard could be deleted with every test still green. The case that isolates it uses an order row written before that column existed, where the authenticated sender is the only thing standing between a peer and somebody else's chain.

A test whose premise was wrong. "Refuses a chain from a supplier that rewrote the order" expected a binding refusal and got none: the order id is inside status_digest, so a rewritten field breaks the record's own digest and is refused a layer earlier. The digest does the work. No fault is recorded for that case, and that is right — an unreadable frame does not establish that the SUPPLIER misbehaved; anything on the path could have mangled it.

Full gate — core 7,945 · brain 3,569 · mobile 3,308 · core-server 2,841 · protocol 724 · commerce-protocol 349 · brain-server 116 · home-node 30. 18,882 tests, typecheck 0 errors.

C7-08 (part 2) — a continuation that knew its CID but not its contract

The authorization split landed last round: continuity only across a major, only for the three lifecycle handlers. The DISPATCH half was still wrong. A drain row recorded WHICH CID was authorized and nothing about WHICH CONTRACT it speaks, so a prior major's continuation reached the current adapter and the runner had no way to know it was answering for an older major.

DrainAuthorization now carries priorVersion, read from the INSTALL — the manifest about to stop running — never from the update being applied. That direction is the whole point of a drain row: it describes the PRIOR contract, and taking the version from the incoming update would describe the wrong one. buildContinuityEnvelope passes it through as an optional prior_version, set only when the row recorded one, so a row written before the column existed stays silent rather than claiming a version it does not know.

What this buys: a runner that understands one major can now REFUSE work addressed to another instead of answering it wrongly. Core cannot make that judgement for it — only the pack knows what its code can honour — so the honest move is to tell it and let it decide.

The trap, caught by a count and not a red mark. Adding the field to the envelope interface was not enough: KNOWN_ENVELOPE_FIELDS is an EXACT key set that fails closed, so every envelope carrying prior_version was quarantined and parsePluginEnvelope returned null. The signal was core dropping from 7,894 to 7,808 tests while typecheck showed four unrelated fixture errors — a suite that would not load. Same shape as the earlier service_runtime incident, and the same habit found it: compare the total against the previous run rather than reading the word "passed". This is the third variant of "a new wire field needs every validator updated, not just the type" in this codebase.

Mutation: writing '' instead of the install's version kills the new test. Full gate — core 423 / 7,894 · brain 190 / 3,569 · mobile 226 / 3,308 · core-server 132 / 2,841 · protocol 30 / 724 · commerce-protocol 14 / 349 · brain-server 17 / 116 · home-node 5 / 30. 18,831 tests, typecheck 0 errors.

C7-05 (part 2) — a declined order had no chain, and that blocked uninstall

§12.8's genesis ran for accepted only. A rejected or countered order got no status head at all — and two consequences follow from a missing head, both silent.

The one nobody would guess: countUnfinishedByServingManifest reads a NULL head as unfinished. So every declined order counted as open work for ever, holding a prior manifest's lifecycle authority and blocking plugin uninstall — the identical failure the delivered-order dispute-window fix addressed, arriving by a completely different route. Two separate defects, one symptom, and fixing only one would have left suppliers still unable to uninstall.

The one the buyer feels: §9.11's chain is where a rejection becomes a record Sancho can verify. Without it the rejection existed only inside an acknowledgement he was handed — a claim, not evidence.

Design decision: a counterproposal opens a REJECTED chain. The terms move to a fresh quote family, so the order that was countered is over — it is a rejection of THIS order, not a pending state. Modelling it any other way would leave a chain that never closes for an order nobody is working on.

createAcceptedGenesisInTx hard-coded 'accepted' as the genesis state; it now takes the state to open at, defaulting to accepted so no existing caller changes meaning. Admission calls it for EVERY resolving decision, and the rollback guarantee is unchanged: the genesis commits with the decision or the decision does not stand.

The scenario asserts both halves — the chain exists at rejected, AND the manifest's unfinished count drops to zero. Mutation: restoring the accepted-only guard kills the decline journey. Gate — core 423 / 7,893 · core-server 132 / 2,841 · mobile 226 / 3,308 · brain 190 / 3,569. Typecheck 0 errors.

C7-10 — the §15.2b lane, built rather than refused

I had asked whether to build this or ship with the setting refused. The instruction is explicit — "don't wait for me for different phases" — so the question was mine to answer and I was wrong to hold it open. Built.

Everything §15.2b needs except the lane itself already existed: the approval payload, the binding verifier, the policy gate. Nothing could supply a SupplierDecisionApproval, so orderAcceptance:'review' had exactly one reachable outcome — rejection at the decision deadline, without asking anyone.

Design decision: a store, not a workflow approval task

Every other thing an owner must answer on this node is a WorkflowTaskKind.Approval, and this deliberately is not. A workflow approval asks permission to START work; this holds a decision the runner ALREADY made until a person agrees to it. The difference shows at recovery: a lost approval task costs a prompt, while losing this row strands a reserved order with a buyer waiting. So it sits beside the other durable commercial memory as commerce_pending_decisions.

It also keeps the execution plane out of the domain. Giving the commerce runtime a task handle so it could raise a card is exactly the coupling runtime.ts exists to prevent — commerce owns stores and services, not the workflow engine.

The rule that makes the card trustworthy

The runner's bytes are held VERBATIM and replayed VERBATIM. The owner approves the decision the pack actually proposed, not a re-derivation of it — so a pack that revises its answer between the card being raised and the owner tapping cannot have the new answer signed under the old consent. The card is also idempotent by order, and the FIRST answer is the one kept: a second is a pack revising a proposal the owner may already be reading.

Only approval_required raises a card. It is the one refusal that names a person rather than a fault. result_unreadable and its siblings mean Core could not record the decision at all, and a card there would ask an owner to approve a runner answer nobody could parse.

What the owner can now do

  • POST /v1/commerce/orders/decide — owner-only. approve is a required boolean both ways: a missing value is not consent and not a refusal, it is a malformed request, and guessing either way decides an order on the owner's behalf.
  • Declining clears the card and moves nothing. The order lapses at its deadline like any undecided order. Core does not invent a rejection the owner never worded.
  • A failed settlement KEEPS the card. Clearing it would lose the runner's answer and leave the owner with nothing to approve on retry, on an order still reserved.
  • GET /v1/commerce/orders/pending-decisions — the list. It deliberately does NOT project the runner's raw answer: that is an unsigned proposal, and a surface rendering it invites an owner to read it as the decision.

The buyer learns the outcome through §12.7 reconcile. Their submission was withheld and the order is now decided, so a reconcile returns the recorded acknowledgement — no need to reopen a D2D response that has already been answered.

Proven as a journey, not just wired

Two scenarios now drive the lane through the real Sancho↔ChairMaker harness AND the real HTTP routes — CoreRouter with registerCommerceRoutes, owner capability and all — so the owner's half is exercised the way an owner exercises it.

  • ChairMaker reviews before accepting. Sancho orders, the pack answers accepted, and NOTHING goes to Sancho. The owner is told through GET /pending-decisions, and the test asserts the runner's supplier_order_id does not appear in that projection — an unsigned proposal must not be rendered as a decision. The owner approves, the order stops being reserved, and the card is gone.
  • Declining signs nothing. The card clears, the order stays reserved and lapses at its deadline, and nothing reaches the wire. Core does not invent a rejection the owner never worded.

The journey asserts the whole arc that was broken three different ways in sequence: before the seam fix the pack's answer was signed with nobody seeing it; after the withhold fix it vanished and the order lapsed; only with the card does it wait for a person.

Mutation: leaving the card in place after approval kills the first journey. A second attempt — inverting the settlement branch — was a BAD mutation (Tests: 0 total, a compile error from narrowing) and was discarded rather than counted.

Deep review of this item, and what it caught

Reviewing my own work against the three questions — is this the correct way, are there code smells, are the tests sound — surfaced a real defect I had just written.

The route built the approval payload with installScopeHash: '' and configRevision: ''. §15.2's binding exists precisely so that a swap of WHICH install is about to act fails the comparison, and the approval-payload module documents what empty strings cost: "when the card supplies neither side of a comparison, the field is bound to a CONSTANT and carries no information — the payload looks §15.2-compliant and binds nothing." I had reproduced, in new code, the exact defect that module was written to prevent. The verifier would have compared constant to constant and passed every bait-and-switch.

Fixed by reading the real values off the install record. And where the buyer-side builder returns a union and REFUSES a card missing its fields, the supplier-side builder does not — so the refusal has to happen at the route: an install uninstalled since the card was raised yields 409 serving_install_gone rather than an approval bound to nothing.

Worth stating plainly: the tests were green before this fix. Nothing failed, because a constant-to-constant comparison succeeds. It was found by reading the code against its own documented rule, which is the only thing that finds this class.

Two things un-refused, now that they are true

validateSupplierSettings accepts orderAcceptance:'review' again, and the supplier inbox offers accept and reject again. Both had been turned off precisely because nothing served them; the pairing recorded at each site — "restore these in the same change that builds the lane" — is what made this safe to reverse. §18.6's counter stays absent and stays OUTSTANDING: a counterproposal needs replacement terms this card cannot collect, and offering it would repeat the mistake those comments record.

Also still open: finalizePendingCancellation remains without a caller, so a pending_review cancellation still has no owner route. The store and route built here are the shape that will serve it.

A SECOND FLAKE, recorded not hidden: core-server reported one failure in a full run and 2,841/2,841 on the next, with no code change between them. That is the second flake this session (the first was a recovery-phrase idle-timer test). Neither is in commerce, but two in one session is worth someone looking at.

Mutation: pointing the card at a different refusal kills both new tests. The orphan ledger caught the new in-memory double immediately, which is the guard working. Gate — core 423 / 7,891 · brain 190 / 3,569 · mobile 226 / 3,308 · core-server 132 / 2,841. Typecheck clean.

C7-08 — one mechanism doing two jobs: drain versus prior-major lifecycle

authorize created a non-expiring lifecycle_continuity row for EVERY capability on EVERY update, and never compared versions. Its own comment claimed to be "serving prior-major lifecycle" while nothing established that the major had changed.

§9.13 draws a line the code did not. A SAME-MAJOR update is a compatible swap: tasks already created drain against their pinned schemas, and anything new belongs to the current runtime. Granting the prior CID open-ended authority there meant continuations ran under stale code a compatible release had just replaced. A MAJOR change is different — the old major must keep serving the orders it took until they are terminal — but only through the three lifecycle handlers §9.13 names: order_status, order_reconcile, cancel_order.

Quoting and ordering are deliberately excluded from continuity. A superseded major that could still quote or take orders would not be draining, it would be running. That is the substantive judgement in this fix, and it is the one the old code got backwards by retaining everything.

The existing tests encoded the defect, exactly as Codex said. The fixture bumped 0.1.0 → 0.2.0 — same major — and demanded continuity for BOTH order_status and request_quote. Those tests are now the same-major story and assert drain-only; three continuity-specific tests drive a real major bump through a majorUpdate variant; and one new test states the major rule positively. One surviving assertion expected request_quote to keep continuity across a major, which is precisely what must not happen — corrected with the reason in place.

Not claimed: Codex also asks for the drain row to carry protocol version and handler identity so a major's continuations DISPATCH to versioned code rather than the current adapter. DrainAuthorization still stores schemas and authority only, and buildContinuityEnvelope still targets the current install lane. The authorization side of §9.13 is now right; the dispatch side is not, and that is recorded rather than glossed.

Mutation: removing the major-and-lifecycle guard kills four tests. Gate — core 423 / 7,888 · core-server 132 / 2,841 · mobile 226 / 3,308. Typecheck clean.

Retailer↔manufacturer scenario coverage for the newly wired lanes

Wiring a lane and proving a retailer can use it are different claims, so the cancellation work is now driven end to end through the existing Sancho↔ChairMaker harness — real SQLite, the real commerce runtime, a real PluginRunner, and the SAME ingressResultTransformer the composition roots wire, so what the buyer receives in the test is what a buyer receives in production.

Sancho cancels an order he placed with ChairMaker. ChairMaker's pack is asked and answers {verdict:'cancelled'} — and that shape never reaches the wire. What Sancho receives is Core's persisted CancellationResult, and the test asserts the two bindings that make it evidence rather than a claim: result_digest, so the record digests itself, and status_digest_at_resolution, so a ruling made at one chain position cannot be replayed at another.

A competitor cannot cancel Sancho's order. The same well-formed request from a different authenticated sender is refused at the §11.2 subject gate before any runner is asked, and nothing reaches the lane.

The fixture was wrong first, and the code caught it. My initial helper invented cancellation_digest: 'd'.repeat(64); Core refused it and withheld, which is the behaviour working and a test proving nothing. Rebuilt on commerceRecordDigest, the real producer.
