That is the fifth or sixth time a hand-built stand-in has produced a false result in this effort, and the pattern is now reliable enough to state as a rule: if a fixture needs a digest, an id, or a manifest, get it from the thing that makes them.

Procurement journey suites — 6 suites / 53 tests green: procurement_journey, procurement_scenario, procurement_lane_scenario, buyer_round_trip_journey, external_fulfilment_journey, disaster_recovery_journey. Full gate: core 423 / 7,887 · core-server 132 / 2,841. Typecheck clean.

C7-06 — a retailer could cancel an order and nothing would happen

cancel_order had no branch in the ingress seam at all, so it fell to passthrough: the runner's verdict went straight back to the buyer. A supplier pack could report a cancellation that changed no order, no status head and no hold, and the retailer would believe it. The entire atomic cancellation engine — resolveCancellation, finalizePendingCancellation, the cancellation-versus-dispatch race, idempotent replay, digest binding — had no production caller.

Design decision: the runner proposes, Core disposes. The runner's answer is read as the §12.5 CancellationPolicy — whether this business WANTS to allow it — and Core decides inside one transaction whether the cancellation or the dispatch won. Only Core can adjudicate that: it holds the status head, the order receipt and the idempotency record. Two deciders would let both believe they did.

An unreadable runner answer is NO opinion, not a refusal. The default is pending_review: "I cannot tell what this business wants" must not become "they said no", because both cancelling and refusing on the owner's behalf are decisions nobody made. The buyer DID comes from the authenticated sender and never from a field inside the request — a requester-chosen buyer_did would let anyone cancel anyone's order, and there is a test that passes an attacker DID in the params and asserts it is ignored.

The manifest half mattered as much as the wiring. The pack declared ORDER_SCOPED_PARAMS — just purchase_order_id — and Brain strips undeclared fields before dispatch. So a real CancellationRequest reached Core with its version, cancellation id, order digest, idempotency key and its own digest removed: every field the resolver needs to bind and replay it. Declaring less than the protocol carries does not narrow the attack surface, it silently guts the request. A dedicated CANCEL_PARAMS now declares the full shape, and the result schema keeps its own shape deliberately — the runner says what the business wants, and Core replaces it with the persisted CancellationResult before the buyer sees anything.

The test that asserted cancel_order passthrough is gone — it was in an it.each list of "capabilities the seam leaves untouched", which is how the gap read as intentional. Four tests replace it. Mutation: forcing the cancel branch off kills all four.

Gate — core 423 / 7,885 · core-server 132 / 2,841 · mobile 226 / 3,308. Typecheck clean.

Still open on this lane: finalizePendingCancellation still has no caller, so a pending_review cancellation has no owner route to close it — the same missing-owner-surface shape as §15.2b. Recorded, not claimed.

C7-07 — schema re-consent, where I was half right and the exclusion was wrong

A reviewer disagreement with a decision I made in an earlier round. Neither position was right as stated, and the resolution is a third thing.

I had excluded params_schema and result_schema from widening detection outright, reasoning that §9.13 makes MINOR "strictly additive" and the drain mechanism absorbs the ordinary minor release — so prompting on every schema tweak would train an owner to tap through the prompts that matter. Codex said both schemas sit inside scopeHashInput, and a changed per-capability scope hash requires re-consent.

Codex is right about the principle and I was right about the cost. Draining and consent are not substitutes: the drain governs tasks ALREADY created against their pinned schemas, while consent governs what the install may be asked to do NEXT. Excluding the field let an install adopt a new data contract — new required inputs, a different result shape — under an approval the owner gave for a different one.

The resolution: rank it, do not exclude it. The distinction §9.13 actually draws is not "changed" versus "unchanged" but ADDITIVE versus not — "new optional fields only". So a strictly additive change is the ordinary minor the drain exists for and passes silently; anything else is a new contract and asks. That satisfies the principle without the prompt fatigue, and it makes the rule the same shape as the four other ranked fields.

schemaWidened FAILS CLOSED, matching the module's stated philosophy ("known and ranked, or unknown and refused"). A new required input, a removed property, a changed property type, a relaxed required set, and any construct the comparison does not model (oneOf, $ref, conditionals) all count as widening. The cost of a needless prompt is an annoyed owner; the cost of a missed one is an install acting under terms nobody approved.

The existing test asserted the wrong general claim while testing a correct case. "does NOT fire on a schema change" used an ADDITIVE example, so it still passes — but its title stated the blanket rule. Retitled, with the reasoning corrected in place, and six cases added for what must now fire.

Thirteen rebind tests then went red, and the fixture was the reason. Its own comment said the new manifest "deliberately narrows both schemas" — a rename, purchaseOrderId → poId — which is a different data contract and now correctly refuses the whole rebind with requires_reconsent. That would have made every test in that file about consent rather than rebind mechanics. The fixture keeps its deliberate difference (so a prior-versus-current read mix-up is still detectable) but is now ADDITIVE, and the non-additive case has its own test asserting the rebind refuses AND that the install did not move — a refusal that had already rebound would leave new code running under an approval nobody gave.

Mutation: restoring the blanket pass kills all six new widening cases and leaves the additive one green. Gate — core 423 / 7,882 · core-server 132 / 2,841 · mobile 226 / 3,308. Typecheck clean.

C7-11 — a delivered order was never finished, so nothing could ever be uninstalled

countUnfinishedByServingManifest counted every delivered head as unfinished, and its own docstring explained why: the dispute window "cannot be evaluated" in SQL, "so a delivered head counts as UNFINISHED and the caller applies the window". No caller applied one. Both boots handed the count straight to continuity release and to uninstall. So a normally completed order stayed work for ever: every prior manifest CID kept lifecycle authority indefinitely, and a supplier could never uninstall a pack whose orders had all finished.

Design decision — denormalise the deadline onto the head. The alternative was to have each caller load the head receipt and apply the window itself, which is what the old comment assumed. That spreads one rule across two call sites that have no receipt in hand and no reason to agree; it is exactly how the rule came to be applied by neither. commerce_status_heads now carries dispute_window_ends_at, written at both the genesis and CAS-advance sites from the candidate record, and ONE time-aware predicate serves continuity release and uninstall alike. nowMs is a parameter rather than a clock read, so the boundary is testable to the millisecond.

Absent is not expired. A delivered head with no recorded deadline keeps counting. That preserves the old conservative reading for rows that cannot answer, while bounding it: only those rows persist, instead of all of them.

Schema edited in place rather than migrated, per the pre-launch convention (no live data). Making the field REQUIRED on CommerceStatusHead was deliberate: it forced every construction site — including nine test fixtures — to state what it means, rather than letting undefined quietly mean "no window".

The test that asserted the OLD behaviour ("STILL counts a delivered chain, because the dispute window is not this query to judge") is inverted, keeping its old reasoning in the comment. Four cases now pin the boundary: window open, window passed, the exact instant (exclusive), and no deadline recorded.

The backtick trap, for the third time this session. A SQL comment containing `delivered` closed the schemas.ts template literal and produced five syntax errors far from the edit. It is already written in this file as a trap worth remembering, and I still wrote it. The tell is that the reported line numbers land nowhere near the change.

Gate — core 423 / 7,875 · core-server 132 / 2,841 · mobile 226 / 3,308. Typecheck clean. One flake seen and NOT hidden: recovery_phrase/screen.render failed once in a full mobile run and passed alone and on the next full run. It is an idle-timer UI test with no relation to commerce; recorded rather than quietly re-run.

C7-05 (part 1) — the fulfilment sweep decided and never signed

FulfilmentDecision's own docstring says of advance: "The caller signs; this does not." Nothing did. The sweep read every open order out of the external system, worked out exactly how each chain should move, returned that as JSON — and left every chain where it was. signStatusUpdate had no production caller anywhere, so an accepted order could never authoritatively reach dispatched or delivered. That is the whole second half of §9.11 and the half of the retailer↔manufacturer arc that follows acceptance.

Signed per order, each in its own transaction. Not one transaction for the sweep: these are independent orders, and the engine legitimately refuses a successor a particular chain will not take (a fork, a backwards move, a terminal head). One transaction would roll back every good advance alongside the bad one, so the first disagreement would hide all later progress. Refusals are collected and reported per order instead.

The response now separates advanced and refused from results. The distinction is the point: "decided" and "moved" were the same list for as long as nothing signed, which is exactly how "the sweep works" survived being untrue.

The test double had to grow a signer, and that is the finding in miniature — the runtime double's own comment elsewhere in that file warns that "a double that omits a field the type promises is a lie the cast hides". It now records what it was asked to sign, so the tests assert the chain MOVED rather than that a decision was computed and dropped.

Three bad mutations before a good one, worth recording because the failure mode was identical each time and my own rule already names it. Deleting the branch, inverting its condition, and stubbing the signer all produced Tests: 0 total — TypeScript narrowing made each one a COMPILE error, not a behaviour change, and a crashed suite is not a surviving mutant. The valid mutation keeps every type intact and signs the WRONG order ('po-wrong'): it kills exactly the two new tests and leaves the other 29 green.

Still open in C7-05: genesis is still created only for accepted decisions, so a rejected or countered order gets no terminal genesis; the buyer side does not yet ingest order_status or verify sequence and predecessors; and the reference manifest's status result carries display fields rather than the digest-linked CommerceOrderStatus. Recorded rather than claimed as done.

C7-01 — a wire status that was never on the wire, copied three times

sendAnswer emitted status: 'ok'. ServiceResponseStatus is success | unavailable | error, and validateServiceResponseBody refuses anything else. So the one lane that answers from Core's own records — the §12.7 reconcile, which is precisely the recovery path a parked buyer depends on — emitted a response every conforming buyer must reject.

Three hand-written copies of the union, all saying ok: the callback type in service_handler.ts, the emitting call site, and a third in home-node/src/service_runtime.ts. The class docstring said ok too, so comment and code agreed with each other and all four disagreed with the protocol. This is the same shape as the hyphen bypass: a contract restated locally cannot notice when it stops matching. All three now use ServiceResponseStatus directly, so the next drift is a compile error rather than a runtime rejection.

The test asserted ok as well, which is what a test written from the implementation does. Corrected — and rather than swapping one literal for another, it now runs the emitted body through validateServiceResponseBody, so the assertion asks the wire contract itself instead of a copy of my current belief about it.

The signal was the total dropping from 30 to 18, not a red mark. That is the trap already recorded in this file ("a broken test signature shows up as a silently smaller suite total"), and I walked into it anyway; the habit that caught it was comparing the count against the previous run rather than reading the word "passed".

Round 7 — both reviewers in, and the WBS is overstated

Claude: R6-01..04 all resolved, 1 new (high). Codex, on a fresh thread after three blocked attempts: 11 findings. Four fixed here; the rest are sized below.

The most important thing this round told me is not a bug, it is that the WBS is wrong. C7-03 — the hyphen bypass, and the worst thing found all session

DECIDES_ORDER compared the RAW wire label. The supplier reference manifest publishes com.dinakernel.commerce.submit-order. Provider ingress canonicalizes hyphens, so an order under the manifest's own id was ADMITTED and reserved quote capacity — and then fell through to passthrough at the seam, handing the buyer the runner's unsigned decision. This silently defeated the entire C5-02 withhold fix for the real manifest ids.

My own comment in the ingress canonicalizer had already named the pattern: one spelling chosen there "rather than by listing every variant at every set, which is how the hyphen form came to be listed at one set and missing from three." It was missing from two more. commerce/capability_names.ts is now the single definition, provider ingress uses it, the seam uses it, and the BOUND capability_id is threaded through so a local listing alias resolves too. Four cases pinned; reverting to the raw comparison kills exactly the three new ones.

R7-01 — my own regression, and it made things worse before better

Widening the settings validator (R6-02) meant more rows fail validation. The §18.3 listing gate in refuseProbing was guarded on configured.ok with no else-branch, so an invalid row fell through and the node ANSWERED. A stored {listingState:'withdrawn'} row that also carried any newly-invalid field stopped validating, and a withdrawal that used to be honoured began being ignored: the owner closed their shop and the node resumed quoting. Now fails closed on unreadable, permissive on absent — a node that never configured a supplier has no listing to close, one that cannot read its policy has one it can no longer honour.

The reviewer also caught that my new test claimed more than it drove: it asserted "no caller reaches the permissive answer" while only calling quoteAdmissibility. Corrected, and the read-path half now drives createProviderIngressTask — the defect lived in the wiring between them, and the helper was always right because nothing asked it.

C7-09 — a reviewer disagreement I lost, and should have

Codex said the transformer's exception path fails open; Claude had accepted my design. Codex is right. My reasoning was "a seam that crashed has told us nothing, and dropping a landed completion is the worse failure". Both halves were wrong. A throw and a refusal agree on the only thing that reaches the buyer — Core produced no authoritative record — and §9.12 says a supplier plugin emits an unsigned CANDIDATE it cannot promote. Answering submit_order with the runner's JSON tells the buyer their order was decided at exactly the moment this node failed to decide it.

And no completion is lost: the task is CAS-confirmed in the workflow store regardless. What is withheld is the RESPONSE, which §12.7's buyer reconcile exists to resolve. The test that required passthrough-on-throw is inverted and carries the old reasoning in its comment.

C7-04 — an inconsistency I created by fixing only one of two

Last round I stripped the unserved accept/reject/counter actions from order_awaiting_decision and left external_outcome_unresolved offering reconcile — which is equally unserved, because the only reconcile command is buyer-side. This is the more dangerous of the two: an effect_started order is deliberately excluded from the decision sweeper (listExpiredPreEffect selects pre_effect only) because money may have moved, so it never lapses, and with no command it stays non-terminal for ever — holding quote capacity and blocking both continuity release and uninstall. Now consistent: no unserved action, honest detail, and the operator still gets the heading plus GET /v1/commerce/idempotency to probe external evidence.

Still open, sized honestly

  • C7-01 (high) — order_reconcile unreachable, and sendAnswer emits status:'ok', not a value the D2D validator permits.
  • C7-02 (high) — replay and rejection acknowledgements discarded in favour of a service error.
  • C7-05 (high) — status chain has no production lifecycle; signStatusUpdate has no caller.
  • C7-06 (high) — cancellation engine has no production caller and its published schemas cannot carry the authoritative record.
  • C7-07 (high) — schema changes excluded from re-consent though they sit inside the scope hash.
  • C7-08 (high) — continuity conflates same-major drain with major-only lifecycle retention.
  • C7-10 (medium) — the §15.2b lane itself; refusing the setting is a holding action, not the build.
  • C7-11 (medium) — delivered never becomes terminal, because no caller applies the dispute window the count assumes they will.

Dual review — round 6 (Claude: C5-01..04 all resolved, 4 new findings)

Reviewed at ca3ae64c64143219; fixed at a58d55170cb8ea50. The reviewer confirmed all four round-5 fixes resolved and found four more.

R6-02 is the one that mattered, and I had not looked for it. validateSupplierSettings checked responsePolicy values under a comment reading "fail closed on an unknown policy rather than defaulting to auto" — and did not check the three fields that actually decide whether the business sells: listingState, quoteAccess, orderAcceptance. Every reader compares against the exact permissive spelling and falls through to permissive on anything else, so the failure was OPEN in all three: quoteAdmissibility returned {admits:true} for a listingState of "Paused", and the order path auto-accepted for an orderAcceptance of "Review". The route hands the body through as body as never, so one capital letter from a client meant an owner who closed their shop kept selling, with nothing anywhere saying so. The module's own stated rule, applied to the least consequential of the four fields.

The §15.2b ruling, and why I did not simply build the lane

R6-01: orderAcceptance: 'review' had exactly one reachable outcome — rejected(decision_timeout) after the full deadline. No commerce module creates a WorkflowTaskKind.Approval task (staging, agent access, facade actions and coding permits all do; commerce does not), and settleInboundOrderDecision's approval parameter has no production caller. The setting meant "refuse every customer, slowly, without telling anyone". Meanwhile supplier_inbox.ts advertised ['accept','reject','counter'] — three actions no route serves, which breaks FR-P10's "a command is authorized by the same projection that offered it" from the other end.

Decision: refuse the setting at configuration time, and stop offering the three actions. Building the lane is the right end state and is real scope, but shipping a half-built approval path is worse than refusing the option: an owner told "not supported yet" picks something else, while an owner whose approval policy silently rejects their customers hears it from the customers. Both are recorded as outstanding rather than closed, with the restore condition written at each site — the actions come back in the same change that builds the card, not before.

A trap I walked into with my own fix

Refusing review in the validator nearly created the very defect class this ledger keeps recording. readSupplier validates on READ as well as write, so a stored review row now surfaces as "settings do not validate" and hits the earlier fail-closed branch — which left the orderAcceptance === 'review' gate in order_decision.ts unreachable through the store.

It stays, deliberately, and is now driven by a repository double that returns a validating review row. The reason is written at the branch: the two halves are one rule, and the change that finally builds the lane will drop the validator refusal. If the gate had been deleted as dead code, that change would silently turn every review-mode order into an automatic acceptance — the exact failure §15.2b exists to prevent. Both halves land or neither does.

A test that encoded the implementation instead of the requirement

supplier_inbox.test.ts asserted toEqual(['accept','reject','counter']) and passed for as long as those three strings sat in the projection, while no route implemented any of them. It was checking string literals, not behaviour. Rewritten to assert the truth — no actions, operator still told — with §18.6's requirement recorded as OUTSTANDING rather than waived, and a note that the test which will actually prove it is end-to-end (card offered, owner counters, buyer receives a counterproposal), because an equality check against literals is what let the gap hide.

R6-03 and R6-04

Fixing C5-02 removed the wrong signal without adding the right one: withhold discarded its reason and returned silently, so result_unreadable, decision_unrecognized, commerce_unavailable and approval_required were byte-identical nothing — on the one path that leaves no stash, no send and nothing for a sweeper. Added onIngressResultWithheld alongside the three observers the bridge already has, wired at BOTH boots (the boot asymmetry is the recurring defect here, not the feature), and metadata-only: the reason is a refusal code, never a payload field. R6-04: two docstrings still told a future implementer to "return null to leave the result untouched" three lines below the union that explains why null was removed.

Gate at a58d55170cb8ea50 — core 423 / 7,864 · brain 190 / 3,569 · mobile 226 / 3,308 · core-server 132 / 2,841 · protocol 30 / 724 · commerce-protocol 14 / 349 · brain-server 17 / 116 · home-node 5 / 30. Typecheck clean. Mutation: removing the listingState membership check kills exactly three tests. Lint on the commerce surface is clean; 8 errors remain in files outside it (2 in ask_runtime.ts, 6 in mobile's bootstrap.ts), all verified present in HEAD and left alone as pre-existing debt rather than folded into this diff.

Dual review — round 5 (Claude: 4 findings, all real)

Reviewed at b907a304288552d8; fixed at ca3ae64c64143219. Every finding was verified in source before a line was changed, and every fix mutation-checked.

All four were the same defect class the ledger keeps naming: correct code that nothing can reach. Two of them were holes I opened myself in round 4 by building a gate without the lane through it.

  1. C5-01 — the behaviour gate could never be satisfied (high). confirmUpdate refuses behavior_change_unreviewed unless the owner echoes the reviewed hash, and POST /v1/plugins/update/confirm read install_id, to_cid and accepted_widening — no behaviour hash at all. prepare already returned toBehaviorHash for a client to echo; there was simply no field to echo it into. So every behaviour-changing update 409'd for ever and the ACCEPTING branch of §20.12 re-consent existed only in unit tests. There was no route test file for plugin updates, which is exactly how a service-level suite and a route stay out of step.
  2. C5-02 — review mode shipped the runner's unsigned answer (high). The worse half of this was not the missing approval lane but an overloaded return value. The ingress seam returned string | null, where null meant both "not mine to transform" and "I refuse to let this out", and the bridge read both as the first: outgoing = transformer(...) ?? resultJSON. So a supplier whose policy demanded human review — or whose settings failed to validate, the fail-closed path — sent the RUNNER's raw unsigned payload to the buyer as though Core had signed it, and the order was auto-rejected later by the sweeper. Enabling the setting was strictly worse than not having it.
  3. C5-03 — the withdraw action could not be performed by whoever was offered it (medium). describeCatalogForOwner offers withdraw, and its view carries no pointer, digest or sequence. The withdraw route demanded a full valid previous pointer, while publish — calling the same resolvePredecessor — tolerated its absence. Every existing withdraw test supplied previous from a publish response or by reaching into the store directly, so none of them ever stood where a client stands.
  4. C5-04 — the one §9.13 clause that names counterproposals was unenforced (medium). "A counterproposal cannot silently upgrade the conversation." verifyAcknowledgementForOrder version-checked the acknowledgement envelope but not replacement_quote.protocol_version — and it is the quote's version an order gets built against. A 1.0 exchange could be countered with 1.5 terms. The intra-family revision rule cannot catch this: a counterproposal starts a fresh quote family by construction, so it is a different quote_id every time.

The design decision inside C5-02: three answers, not two

IngressResultTransformer now returns a union — passthrough | replace | withhold. The alternative considered was returning an error payload to the buyer instead of nothing. Rejected: nothing failed, so an error would be a lie, and it invites a retry. An acknowledgement would be worse — inventing a decision nobody made. Silence is the honest state, and it already has an owner: §12.7's buyer reconcile exists for exactly the unanswered submission, and procurement_scenario already proves that path.

A THROW still falls back to the runner's answer, and the asymmetry is deliberate: a seam that crashed has told us nothing, and dropping a completion that already landed is the worse failure. A seam that returns withhold has told us something specific. The status lane also still passes through — a status report Core cannot improve on is a report, not a commitment.

Two corrections to earlier reasoning, stated rather than quietly dropped

The old seam comment read: "A failure here returns null too, so the runner's own answer still reaches the buyer rather than the response vanishing" — and its very next sentence said "an answer we cannot record must not be presented as one we did." The code did precisely what the second sentence forbids. The test that pinned it has been INVERTED, not adjusted, and carries the old assertion in its comment so the change is legible.

The withdraw route's comment justified demanding previous on the grounds that "without a legal predecessor there is nothing to withdraw". True, and it does not follow that the caller must supply it — resolvePredecessor already holds the stored head, which is why publish never asked. Making it optional opened a state that was previously unreachable (no stored head AND none supplied), now refused 409 nothing_published rather than fabricating a genesis tombstone at sequence 1 — which would announce the end of a chain that never began and fork any real one elsewhere.

One parametrised test asserted 400 across five cases that were never the same refusal. Split into three: malformed request, malformed supplied pointer, and well-formed request the node's state does not permit. 400 says "fix your request"; 409 says "the request is fine, the node is not in a state that permits it". Sending an owner to the first when the answer is the second tells them to correct something that is not wrong.

Fixtures that contradicted the protocol, again

C5-04's check turned three tests red, and in all three the FIXTURE was wrong: hand-built replacement_quote stand-ins carrying two fields and no protocol_version — a shape no real supplier sends. Fixed by giving them the version, including in the two NEGATIVE tests, so each still fails on the defect it names (lineage, quote-id reuse) rather than on a missing field. This is the fifth time in this effort a hand-built stand-in has produced a false green.

Mutation evidence

C5-01drop acceptedBehaviorHash forwarding from the routekills only the APPLIES test — the branch that was unreachable
C5-02remove if (decision.kind === 'withhold') return;kills only "sends the buyer NOTHING when the seam withholds"
C5-04remove the replacement-quote version bindkills only the counterproposal lifecycle test

C5-03 is covered by three new tests rather than a mutation: withdraw using only what GET /published returns, the nothing_published refusal, and proof that a supplied-but-stale predecessor is still refused — optional must not mean ignored.

Gate at ca3ae64c64143219 — core 423 / 7,855 · brain 190 / 3,569 · mobile 226 / 3,308 · core-server 132 / 2,841 · protocol 30 / 724 · commerce-protocol 14 / 349 · brain-server 17 / 116 · home-node 5 / 30. Typecheck clean; lint clean across the commerce surface (the two remaining errors are pre-existing, in ask_runtime.ts, outside commerce).

Iteration log — the continuity tick leaked the event loop

A regression I introduced wiring C-18, caught by the mobile suite and worth recording because the failure presented as something else entirely.

What changed. The continuity release sweep (§9.13) was wired into both boots as a fourth background tick, beside admission recovery (§9.9), epoch revalidation (§16.2) and the buyer re-poll (§12.7).

What the tests showed. apps/mobile went from ~40s to hanging past 600s. The counts lied about the cause: commerce_plane.test.ts and boot_service.test.ts each passed alone and failed together, which reads exactly like cross-suite state leaking through a module global. It was not. Two distinct faults were stacked:

  1. The tick count is a pinned constant. COMMERCE_TICKS is written as a name, not a bare number, precisely so that changing it is a decision somebody made rather than a test nudged until it passed. Adding a fourth tick has to move it, and the comment now carries the reason: continuity authorizations carry no expiry, so with no tick to retire them every plugin update leaves a prior CID holding authority over this node’s lifecycle lane for ever.
  2. The sweeper never unref’d its handle. Both sibling sweepers probe the timer handle for unref and call it; the new one did not. Every test that booted the plane left a live 15-minute timer behind, so jest ran green and then refused to exit. That is what produced the “hang”, and it is why the two-suite run looked like a shared-global bug — the second suite inherited the first suite’s open handle.

How it was confirmed rather than guessed. Deleting the continuity block from commerce_plane.ts and re-running boot_service.test.ts alone returned it to 0.855s. That isolated the leak to the new tick before any fix was written. My first attempt — clearing the catalog writer/reader globals in beforeEach — was aimed at the wrong fault and took failures from 2 to 6; it stays in the file only because those globals genuinely are process-wide and the clear is correct on its own terms, but it was not the bug.

Guard added. Two tests now pin the class: one asserts the handle is unref’d exactly once on start(), one asserts a platform whose handle is a plain number (Hermes) still starts without throwing — the probe is a capability check, not a platform check. Mutation-verified: removing the two unref lines kills the first test and nothing else.

Next best method. Neither sibling sweeper has this test, and both would survive the same deletion. The cheapest fix is not three copies of one assertion but a shared unrefTimer(handle) helper with a single conformance test over all three sweepers, so a fourth tick cannot reintroduce the leak. Recorded rather than done, because it is a refactor of already-green code and belongs with the next sweeper, not in a regression fix.

Gate after the fix — commerce-protocol 14 suites / 349 tests · core 423 / 7,846 · core-server 132 / 2,841 · mobile 226 / 3,308 (40.8s, exits cleanly, no --forceExit) · npm run typecheck clean.

Traps worth remembering

  • tsc --noEmit does not typecheck __tests__ in either package. A broken test signature shows up as a silently smaller suite total, never as a red. Watch totals, not failures.
  • Object spreads are never excess-property checked, whatever the target annotation. Only satisfies catches a misspelt wire key inside a conditional spread. Annotating the const achieves nothing — I tried it and the misspelling still compiled.
  • Raw NUL bytes in three source files made grep/rg treat them as binary and skip them silently. Replaced with escapes. Any audit that relies on grep was blind to those files.
  • Edit scripts must anchor on strings that occur once, asserted before mutating. A loose pattern matched the wrong if (head.voided) branch — there are two — and deleted a method.

Open questions for the owner

  • Decided by me, reversible — please sanity-check the number: §16.2 says signing nodes re-verify the live epoch “on a bounded interval” and names no interval. I chose a one-hour re-read and a 24-hour bound after which currentEpoch() refuses. The trade is real in both directions: shorter and a supplier whose PDS blips for an afternoon stops trading for no reason; longer and “forgotten” has no upper bound. A day is where I judged an unreachable repo stops being an outage and starts being a misconfiguration — but the spec puts the hard enforcement counterparty-side, so a longer bound is defensible if you expect suppliers to run offline for days. Both numbers are constructor parameters, so changing them is config, not code.
  • Decided by me, reversible: magnitude bounds stay as built (15-digit minor units, 12-digit quantity integer part), already pinned by frozen vectors on both sides. Widening later is additive; narrowing is not, so the tight bound is the safe default. Raise it if a real catalogue needs more.

  • Plugin Developer Surface — the developer-facing surface over the plugin substrate; dual-reviewed to convergence. SHIPPED
  • Plugin Architecture (P0 substrate + runner mode) — protocol plugin layer (types, digests, vectors), workflow hardening, plugin caller identity, dynamic registry, gatekeeper, claim guard, install flow, tool lane end-to-end; 22 adversarial-review rounds. SHIPPED
  • Interactive + Push Services (V1) — run store + state machine, encrypted payload store with crypto-shred, reservation/admission gate, message lifecycle, risk gate + outbox, locked-arrival, termination + sweepers, interactive_run capability, poll-mode watches, push subscriptions + delivery pipeline; owner `owner` principal decision; V1 scope boundary (run-bound push / remote wake deferred). Known accepted degradation: erasure_mode = logical_deletion until a backup-excluded keystore backend ships. SHIPPED
  • Earlier fixes — mnemonic no longer logged at boot; web thin-client slices; MRS runner hardening. SHIPPED

Dual review — round 1 remediation (commerce)

The Claude continuity reviewer returned FAIL with 5 findings against 0d2b28bb. All five verified against the code before anything was changed, and one more was found independently while auditing the as-casts the test plan had listed as un-audited. Six fixes, each with a regression test that was proven to fail without it.

R1-01 — a routine plugin update stranded every open order (high)

provider_ingress.ts decided "does this order need the prior manifest?" by comparing manifest CIDs, while update_rebind.retain() writes the lifecycle_continuity row that satisfies that demand only across a major version change. The two modules disagreed. After any same-major release — 0.1.0 to 0.2.0 — every already-open order failed order_status and cancel_order permanently: the buyer could neither track nor cancel an order the supplier had already accepted, while §16.4 kept the manifest pinned open.

Fix: the retained lane's presence is the signal, not the CID delta. A lane exists → serve under the prior manifest. No lane but the current manifest still provides the capability → serve under the current one, which is exactly what retain() means by "anything NEW belongs to the current runtime". Neither → refuse. This deliberately does not introduce a new version comparison, which keeps it clear of R1-02 below.

Why the bug survived review: the existing test performed a same-major update whose replacement manifest also dropped the lifecycle capability, so refusing looked correct. The test encoded the implementation rather than §9.13.

R1-02 — plugin semver is standing in for the commerce protocol major (medium, ESCALATED)

Continuity retention and the claim guard's majorsAgree both key on PluginInstall.currentVersion — the plugin release version. §9.13's major is the commerce wire protocol_version, which is what an order actually pins (CommerceOrderRef.pinnedVersion). Two unrelated version spaces.

Not fixed, on purpose. The manifest declares no protocol major — order_refs.ts says so in its own comment — so the correct fix is to add one, and that changes the plugin manifest schema in @dina/protocol: a public contract with frozen conformance vectors. Per the review workflow's human-decision rule, a public-contract change is not mine to make unilaterally.

Assessment of the live risk, so the decision can be made on facts. The two axes are each enforced coherently where they are used: the wire version is checked by versionMatches(record.protocol_version, ref.pinnedVersion) in the lifecycle engine, and the manifest CID governs which schemas parse a request. The reviewer's worst case — a plugin holding its semver major while moving the commerce protocol 1→2 — is therefore refused downstream rather than silently mis-served. The converse creates continuity rows that are never needed, now bounded by the continuedOrderIsLive check. So this is a real modelling defect with a currently-contained blast radius, not an open hole.

R1-03 — the buyer's restore fence had no reader on the D2D lane (medium)

The counterparty epoch watermark was wired on the plugin tool-result lane only. A supplier answering over D2D reaches applyInboundBuyerResponse instead, where nothing read the epoch — so §25.3's delayed-pre-restore-write landed and was recorded as current. It verifies perfectly, because the supplier really did sign it; only the epoch gives it away.

The second-order damage is the reason this is more than untidy: once the stale record is the chain head, the supplier's next legitimate record is judged against it and reads as a fork, so the buyer records a protocol fault against a supplier that behaved correctly. Fixed by calling the existing admitSupplierRecords at the single choke point all four commerce capabilities pass through, rather than inside each verifier — two copies of a fence are two things to forget, and the acknowledgement and reconcile paths would still have had none.

Two test doubles omitted watermarks and started throwing. They were given a real in-memory store rather than making production tolerate the missing field: a runtime without it is not a smaller runtime, it is one whose restore fence cannot run.

R1-04 — admission spent quote capacity on requests it then refused (medium)

§9.9 admission is a commitment: it holds a counted use against the buyer's quote and writes the reserved order reference. It ran before the provider-kind consent check and the envelope build, so a request refused by either still consumed capacity — on a default max_uses: "1" quote, all of it. The buyer could not simply resubmit either, because the dead reservation makes the §15.5 replay branch answer processing. Admission now runs last, immediately before workflow.create.

R1-05 — a comment claimed a guard that does not exist (low)

Re-adoption's comment said the order is "stamped PRE-restore" so that chain creation refuses; the code stamps currentEpoch(). The only barrier is the reconciliationRequired flag. The comment was corrected rather than the code: synthesising a lower epoch would put a fabricated fact in the column the fence reads, and held evidence carries no supplier epoch to recover the true one.

R1-06 — JSON.parse('null'), found while auditing the casts (low, mine)

Both cancellation scans wrapped JSON.parse in try/catch and then dereferenced the result. JSON.parse('null') succeeds, so the catch never fired and the next line threw a TypeError out of the scan — defeating the exact property both scans are written for, that one corrupt receipt must not make every other cancellation on the order unanswerable. rehydrate.ts records finding this same defect once before on the acknowledgement path. It matters doubly here because the commerce boundary test exempts lifecycle_engine.ts from the no-bare-parse rule on the written grounds that these scans skip unreadable rows — so the exemption's justification was false.

On the mutation testing, including one that caught me

Every fix was neutered and its test re-run. Five failed exactly as intended. The sixth survived: my first mutation of the null-parse guard changed nothing, because the refactor had added an if (result === null) continue; at both call sites that independently catches a parsed null. The assertion was not proving what I had claimed. Reproducing the original shape — a bare parse followed by a direct dereference — made it fail with the predicted TypeError in both storage backends. Recorded because a mutation that survives is the only kind that teaches anything.

Known consequence, stated rather than buried

When R1-01 routes an old order to the current manifest, the order reference keeps its original servingManifestCid. The uninstall interlock therefore still counts that order against the old CID. That is the conservative direction — the old manifest looks busy rather than free — so it is left alone; re-stamping the reference on read would make a lifecycle query mutate order state, which is worse.

Dual review — round 2 remediation (commerce)

R2-01 — my round-1 fix opened a denial-of-service (high, REGRESSION)

The reviewer caught a hole I created. admitSupplierRecords attributes each record to the supplier_did written inside the body and raises that DID's watermark. That was safe while the gate was reached only from the buyer's own runner result. Putting it on the D2D lane in round 1 put an untrusted peer in charge of those bytes: supplier X could embed {"supplier_did":"<victim>","supplier_epoch":"99999999"} anywhere in its answer and permanently raise a third party's watermark. raiseTo is monotonic with no floor, so every genuinely signed record the victim sent afterwards would be discarded as stale_epoch — a permanent cut-off between the buyer and a supplier it may have open orders with, triggered by an unrelated party, with no recovery path.

This is the house rule the project already wrote down after a confused-deputy bug: authorization binds to the relay-authenticated envelope, never to a sender-supplied inner body. I moved a gate onto a lane where that rule applies and did not re-ask the question. Fixed with an optional expectedSupplierDid, passed from applyInboundBuyerResponse as the transport-authenticated sender; a record naming any other DID refuses the whole response. The parameter is optional because the tool-result lane genuinely carries many suppliers and has no single sender to bind to. Mutation-tested: without the guard the poisoned response is accepted.

The lesson generalises past this bug. A gate is only as good as the lane it was reasoned about on, and "move the existing check to the new caller" is not a neutral operation — the module's own header comment explained why the supplier comes from the record rather than the envelope, and that explanation was true for exactly one of the two lanes. The comment now says so.

R2-02 — cross-major continuity cannot be implemented here (medium, ESCALATED)

The reviewer conceded my R1-02 escalation and then produced a better version of it. COMMERCE_PROTOCOL_MAJOR is the compile-time constant '1' and every validator refuses another major, so a plugin release cannot move the wire major on its own — both sides move together and a mismatch is refused loudly. That closes the "silently served under the wrong contract" worry for good.

What remains is different and is not fixed by adding a protocol major to the manifest: cross-major lifecycle continuity is not implementable in a single-major package. The drain table retains manifest CIDs and per-capability schemas. It cannot retain a second major's validators, digest domains, or order state machine. If this node's commerce-protocol package ever moves to major 2, signStatusUpdateInTx builds a continuation at the order's pinned "1.0" and the node's own validator rejects it — so the supplier cannot sign a status, resolve a cancellation, or answer a reconcile for its own prior-major orders. §9.13 states as a MUST that exactly those three keep being served.

What I changed, and what I did not. I renamed protocolMajorOf → releaseMajorOf and majorChanged → releaseMajorChanged, because those names claimed to read a protocol major and read the plugin release version. That is a lie in the code and mine to fix. I did not touch §9.13's MUST: editing a specification so that the implementation passes is the exact move the review workflow forbids, and which of the two is wrong is the author's call. The options are (a) record the limitation in §9.13/§23 and downgrade the MUST, or (b) make the protocol package multi-major with per-major validators selected by the order's pinned version. (b) is a large piece of work; (a) is honest and cheap. Neither is mine to choose.

One related consequence of R1-01 the reviewer flagged, recorded rather than argued away: an unretained lane now falls through to the current manifest instead of refusing, so the failure direction changed from closed to open. I still think the fix is right — the previous behaviour stranded every order across every routine release, and the current manifest's params schema still validates the request, so a genuinely incompatible capability fails validation rather than mis-serving. But it does mean a plugin author who ships a breaking capability change without bumping their own major gets a schema failure where they used to get a refusal.

Gate after round-2 remediation: core 8,018 · core-server 2,842 · typecheck clean. Seven fixes across both rounds, each mutation-tested; two open items, both escalated.

Dual review — rounds 3 and 4 (commerce)

Both rounds returned the same species of defect, and it is worth naming because it kept recurring: prose asserting a guarantee the code does not deliver. Four findings, all mine, all valid.

R3-01 — the rename reached the definition and not its consumer

Round 2 renamed protocolMajorOf to releaseMajorOf. The claim guard's two comment blocks still described the check as enforcing §9.13's protocol major, and justified comparing majors by borrowing §9.13's additive-minor rule — a rule about the commerce wire version that says nothing about plugin semver. Load-bearing prose, because a reader would conclude cross-major protocol continuity is enforced at claim time; nothing on that path compares the wire version at all. Both blocks now say release major, give a reason that stands on its own (a same-major release is the plugin author's own compatibility declaration), and state what the check does not enforce.

R3-02 — a distinction with no consumer

I collapsed the new foreign_supplier verdict into stale_epoch and justified it by non-disclosure. The reviewer pointed out the lane sends the sender no reply, so nothing was being protected — the uniformity was pure loss, filing the only observable signal of the poisoning attempt under "a supplier restored recently". Split into its own outcome, and the stale_epoch doc no longer vouches for a counterparty on a path that also covers a hostile one.

That fix exposed a type defect: admitSupplierRecords' refusing arm was typed as carrying the whole WatermarkVerdict, so the type claimed a refusal might contain an acceptance. Narrowed with Extract<…, { accept: false }> rather than casting past the error. The first attempt crashed the suite — Tests: 0 total, which is a crash and not a pass.

R4-01 — I built the distinction and then threw it away (medium)

The sharpest finding of the run, and the same defect class this subsystem keeps producing: correct code that nothing calls. applyInboundBuyerResponse's return value was discarded at its only production caller. Every refusal returns normally, so the surrounding try/catch never saw one, and the unconditional d2d_recv_service_accepted that followed was the only trace. An answer refused for a stale epoch, a forked chain, an unreadable payload — or a watermark-poisoning attempt — was logged as accepted, and six outcomes reached no log at all.

So the R3-02 split I had just argued for, on the explicit grounds that "an operator reading the decision log must be able to tell those apart", delivered nothing: neither outcome reached a log. I wrote a comment describing a benefit and did not build it. The outcome is now bound and audited as d2d_recv_service_buyer_unsettled with sender, capability, query id and outcome — metadata only, never the payload. not_commerce, applied and no_change stay silent, because not_commerce is nearly every message on the lane and logging it would bury the rest.

R4-02 — the same audience error, one file over

WATERMARK_REFUSAL's doc claimed "ONE string for both refusal kinds" (three, since foreign_supplier) and justified the uniformity by what a remote counterparty would learn. Its only consumer returns it to the owner's own local runner. This is the identical audience mistake I had corrected in buyer_response.ts the round before and left standing here. Rewritten: the recipient is a local runner, and the string stays uniform because a runner is an untrusted tenant under §20.20 — not because a stranger is reading it.

Two process notes worth keeping

A mutation survived in round 1 and taught me the assertion was not checking what I claimed. In round 4 I ran npx jest from the repo root instead of the package directory and got 2,624 failing suites — a bad command, not a regression, confirmed by re-running correctly. Both are recorded because a green number from the wrong command is worth less than a red one from the right command.

Gate after round-4 remediation: core 8,019 · core-server 2,842 · typecheck clean. Eleven fixes across four rounds, each mutation-tested; one open item (R2-02), escalated.

Dual review — rounds 5 to 9, and what the shape of them means

Five more rounds, six more findings, and a pattern worth recording more than the fixes themselves: every finding from round 5 onward was a defect in the remediation, not in the commerce implementation under review. The commerce domain — the aggregates and their transactional services, §9.9 admission, the lifecycle and cancellation engines, the §11.2a provider bridge, the claim guard and drain authorizations, the §16.2 restore fence and archive preflight, the §12.7 held-evidence path — produced no new finding after round 2, across seven subsequent passes.

The chain of my own errors, in order

  1. R2-01 (high, security). My round-1 watermark fix attributed each record to the supplier_did inside the peer's body. A supplier could name a THIRD party at a huge epoch and permanently cut this buyer off from them — raiseTo only goes up. I moved a gate onto a lane where an untrusted party writes the bytes without re-asking whether its reasoning still held. Fixed by binding to the transport-authenticated sender.
  2. R4-01 (medium). I split foreign_supplier from stale_epoch arguing an operator must tell them apart in the decision log — and the outcome was discarded at the only caller, so neither reached a log. A comment describing a benefit I had not built.
  3. R6-01 (medium). Sanitising at the audit sink treated every detail as flat text, so JSON-packed details were cut mid-string and stopped parsing, and a whitespace collapse silently rewrote stored values in a tamper-evident log.
  4. R7-01 (medium). My JSON exemption rested on a false premise: buildAuditDetail, the producer cap I cited as justification, has no production caller. I removed a bound and left a comment saying it was still there. Fixed by re-packing at the sink so the bound no longer depends on how a detail was built.
  5. R8-01 (low). Having corrected that false premise in two places, I left it standing on the function that actually changed — the one place it could invite someone to restore the exemption.

Two surviving mutations, both mine

Round 1's null-parse mutation survived because the refactor had added a redundant null check at both call sites, so the assertion was not proving what I claimed. Round 5's audit test survived because I drove a hostile query_id through a path where that field never reaches a log — it asserted nothing. Both were found by neutering the fix and re-running, which is the only reason they were found at all. A test that passes is not evidence; a test that fails when the fix is removed is.

One correction to the reviewer

Round 5's finding claimed a watermark-poisoning attempt guarantees an audit write with an attacker-chosen query_id. It does not: on the response lane evaluateServiceIngressBypass requires an exact match against a window the BUYER opened, so both fields are echoes there. The reachable injection point is capability on the inbound query path. The finding stood; its attack path did not, and the correction is what moved the test to somewhere it could fail.

An unexplained transient, recorded rather than rounded away

One core-server run showed 1 failed, 2841 passed. The name was not captured and it has passed on every run since. Four green runs afterwards is not proof it did not happen. It is carried as an open item with the cheap correction attached: keep per-test output on the next few runs so a recurrence identifies itself.

Left deliberately unfixed at the passing version

The reviewer described, and declined to file, a mechanism by which a writer packing its own truncated key would have it overwritten when a bound fires. It needs three conditions at once and no consumer reads that key. The one-line guard is known and was NOT applied, because editing after a PASS invalidates the version that passed. It goes to the cold audit as-is.

COLD AUDIT: the quote lane did not exist

The cold reviewer — fresh, with none of the nine rounds' history — returned FAIL with five findings, two critical. All five verified independently before any was accepted. The headline is not a bug in the commerce implementation; it is a hole where a third of it should have been.

What was missing

  • commerceRecordDigest('quote', …) had zero production occurrences. Nothing outside test helpers ever constructed a SignedQuote.
  • domain: 'request' had zero production writers, and admitInTx requires that receipt as the §9.9 projection yardstick.
  • request_quote was in none of the decision sets, so it fell through transformInboundOrderResult to passthrough — the runner's unsigned terms went to the buyer raw.
  • verifySignedQuoteForBuyer, which enforces §9.8's request and projection bindings on the buyer side, had no caller at all.

Compounded: with no family and no request receipt, admission rejects every inbound order with quote_unknown. The supplier lane could not function on a real node.

Why 8,025 green tests and nine review rounds missed it

Every journey suite calls admission.registerSignedQuote(quote) itself before ordering — they injected the exact step production lacked. They proved the engines agree with one another; they could not prove a quote can be issued. The continuity reviewer was auditing each round's diff and never asked whether the subsystem worked as a whole, which is precisely what the cold-audit stage exists for. The boundary test could not catch it either: its orphan ledger scans top-level export function|const|class and never class methods, so an unwired CommerceAdmissionService method is structurally invisible to it. My earlier claim that the journeys "drive the real engines rather than doubles", and the test plan's "the happy path exists end to end", were both wrong.

Built this round

  • quote_issuance.ts — Core validates the buyer's request, binds the audience to the AUTHENTICATED sender rather than the body's claim, prices every requested line through the canonical arithmetic, stamps its own epoch, composes the SignedQuote and registers it. A runner decline passes through unchanged: a decline is an answer, and turning it into silence would read to a buyer exactly like a supplier that never replied.
  • retainQuoteRequestInTx — writes the missing request-domain receipt, with the authenticated sender recorded as the evidence.
  • CommerceAdmissionService.issueQuote — retained request and registered family commit together. Split across two transactions, a crash could leave a family whose orders this node cannot check, or a request retained for a quote never born. This is also the seam registerSignedQuoteForOwnBuyer's comment anticipated: an expectation that finally arrives from outside the record being registered, so the audience check stops comparing a document against itself.
  • currentEpoch exposed on CommerceRuntime — it already held the thunk; reaching through admission to the ledger for it would be a second path to one fact. It throws until an epoch is published, and issuance treats that as a refusal.

Two tests that pinned the defect

order_decision.test.ts asserted "leaves request_quote untouched" and used request_quote as its example of a capability commerce does not own. Both encoded what the code did rather than what §9.8 requires, and both are rewritten rather than deleted, with the reason recorded in place — the same treatment the file already gives cancel_order, which was this identical defect one lane smaller.

Closed since: the seam is now proven, not merely present

quote_issuance_journey.test.ts is the answer to "how would we know". It never calls registerSignedQuote, never calls makeSignedQuote, and never writes a receipt — the only way a quote exists in it is the production seam. Nine cases: the quote is signed and validates, both §9.8 bindings hold, the arithmetic is Core's, an order admits against it (structurally impossible before), capacity is spent so a second order is refused, a replay returns the same quote, and five refusals — foreign buyer, partial pricing, unreadable terms, decline passed through, and no published epoch.

The replay case changed the design. The first version refused a repeated request_quote with registration_refused, because the family already existed — correct in that it minted no rival, wrong in that a buyer retrying after a dropped response could never obtain the price this node had already committed to. Issuance now serves the retained quote, which is §9.9's replay discipline applied to quoting. Serving it needs no second audience check: request_digest covers buyer_did, so a different buyer cannot produce that key.

Also closed

  • Buyer capability dispatch — the four lanes used raw string equality while every other commerce gate canonicalized. A §6.6-conforming third-party supplier echoing the NSID had its acknowledgement dropped as not_commerce, leaving the order in outcome_unknown for ever — the exact failure §12.7 exists to prevent. Now through isCommerceCapability, like everything else.
  • cancel_order responses — previously answered not_commerce, so a supplier's signed CancellationResult vanished without trace. Now a named cancellation_not_applied outcome that reaches the decision log. See the open question below.
  • Claim-guard schema parses — a corrupt drain row threw out of the claim loop and wedged the lane; it now terminalizes the task, matching readKinds's existing fail-closed discipline in the same subsystem.

SECOND COLD AUDIT — six findings, and one of them was mine twice over

A fresh reviewer with none of the history returned FAIL with six findings. All verified before anything was changed. The critical one is worth stating without softening: I recorded the buyer-side gap as CLOSED while leaving out the one piece I had myself written down as necessary.

C-01 (critical) — a store nothing wrote, and a reader that refused everything

The previous round listed the missing pieces as "a table, a repository, a write at the point the buyer sends request_quote, and a migration", then built the table, the repository and the reader and called it closed. buyerQuoteRequests had exactly two production references: the runtime constructs it, and applyInboundQuote reads it. Nothing wrote it. So the unsolicited_quote refusal I had just added rejected every inbound quote on a real node — the buyer path was strictly worse after my fix than before it, behind a note saying the opposite.

Closed properly. buyer_quote_request.ts composes the QuoteRequest, digests it, retains before sending, and dispatches on the service-query lane. The ordering is the design decision: if the request left first and retention failed, a supplier could answer a question this node has no record of asking and the answer would be discarded as unsolicited. Retaining first makes the worst case a retained request never asked — one row, nothing refused. buyer_quote_round_trip.test.ts proves the arc with nothing hand-written: Sancho asks, ChairMaker prices it through the production issuance seam on its own runtime, and the answer reaches applied.

C-06 (medium) — my own guard certified a property it did not check

The round-1 guard was scoped to application services, and the class-level ledger counts a symbol as wired the moment runtime.ts names its constructor. A REPOSITORY whose only mutator nothing calls passed both. The new case asserts every mutator on a runtime-constructed commerce store has a production caller.

Building it taught the same lesson again: my first version matched .put( anywhere and every store passed, because half of them have a put. Bound to the runtime FIELD name — buyerQuoteRequests.put( — it found the real defect and nothing spurious. A guard that cannot fail is worth less than no guard, because it is believed.

C-04 (high) — the archive carried only the supplier's half

IDENTITY_TABLES listed six commerce tables, all supplier-side, under a comment naming only supplier concerns. Every table the buyer writes was missing and was not among the documented deliberate exclusions. A restore lost every tracked order and every supplier-signed acknowledgement and status record the buyer holds — which is precisely the held evidence §12.7/§16.2 require it to present on reconcile, so never_received would become the honest answer to a trade that really happened. Added: the four buyer tables plus commerce_settings and commerce_catalog_pointers, each name verified against the schema rather than typed from memory.

C-05 (medium) — CLOSED: the buyer now revalidates its quote before dispatch

§12.4 step 6 and §19 require expiry and terms to be rechecked immediately before the send. Neither ran: quoteExpiresAt was bound into the approval payload and never compared to a clock, and verifyOrderAgainstQuote executed only on the SUPPLIER side — the party being checked. The buyer dispatched against expired quotes and relied on the counterparty to refuse, which is not a check.

Two decisions worth recording. A quote this node does NOT hold is not a refusal: the buyer-side store is filled by the arrival path and an order may legitimately rest on a quote obtained out of band, so what is refused is a quote this node holds and can see is expired or contradicted. And the full terms comparison needs the priced projection, which lives in the retained request — so it runs when the request is held and is skipped rather than faked when it is not. Four test doubles that omitted buyerQuotes were given real in-memory stores instead of making production defensive about a field the runtime type promises.

C-03 (high) — OPEN, and it is a missing SEAM, not a missing wire

The §15.2 binding compares two objects the same caller supplied in the same request: /v1/commerce/orders/submit takes order AND approved from the body, and the executor rebuilds the payload from that same order. §20.7 names the threat as "a re-planned order, a compromised Brain, a replayed workflow task hands the executor a different order" — which is precisely the party supplying both halves. The check detects only client-side self-inconsistency.

Why I did not simply wire it. There is no point at which Core sees an order before the owner approves it. buildBuyerApprovalPayload has exactly one caller — the rebuild inside the executor — and /procurement/choose ranks offers and returns a comparison card without ever composing a PurchaseOrderProposal. So there is nothing to mint a payload FROM at card time, and no store to retain it in.

The fix, stated so it can be built or corrected: a POST /v1/commerce/orders/prepare seam that receives the composed order once, validates it, mints the BuyerApprovalPayload, retains it in a Core-owned store keyed by purchase_order_id, and returns the card. submit then takes only the order id and loads the retained payload — the caller can no longer supply both halves. Absent a retained payload the submit must REFUSE, because falling back to the request body leaves the hole exactly as it is today.

OPEN QUESTION for you, and the reason this is not a unilateral change: that removes approved from the /v1/commerce/orders/submit request body, which is a breaking change to a public route contract with live callers. Under the review workflow's human-decision rule a public-contract change is yours, not mine. The alternative — accepting the payload from the body when no retained one exists — is not a partial fix, it is the current vulnerability with extra code in front of it, so I have not written it.

C-02 — MECHANISM CLOSED, and the audit's evidence was partly wrong

Correction to the finding first. It stated that nothing in packages/core/src reads or writes plugin_extension_proposals, and concluded the broker parks in memory so proposals do not survive the process. ExtensionOperationBroker is SQLite-backed against exactly that table. The auditor's grep hit a null byte in extension_broker.ts, reported "binary file matches", and the conclusion was drawn from a search that had silently skipped the file. Proposals ARE durable, so C-02 is materially smaller than reported — worth recording because a finding believed without checking would have led to rebuilding a store that already existed.

What was genuinely broken. A parked proposal was recorded, but the ENVELOPE of the claim that proposed it was never kept — and settleOwnerDecision needs it to build the follow-up that carries the verified result back to the runner. A parked proposal outlives the process that made it, so nothing could supply that envelope afterwards: every carded proposal was unresolvable by anyone, permanently. Nothing could list them either, so an owner could not see what they were being asked.

Fixed: a source_envelope_json column, retainSource written once at the moment a proposal parks, and listAwaitingOwner. Two mutation-checked tests: a parked proposal retains its envelope and appears on the owner's list; an auto-permitted one does neither, so settled work never shows as pending.

Design decision — where the retain lives. My first attempt put it in brokerHostOperation, at the point the lane decides to park. Typecheck rejected it: the lane never receives an envelope. The completion handler does, so the retain belongs there. Recording this because the wrong location looked more natural — parking is the lane's decision — and only the type system knew the lane lacks the data.

WS-10.4 / 10.5 — the last two TODOs, and what they honestly are

10.5, the conformance kit. Ten frozen vector files, a generator, and nothing that could EXECUTE them. `conformance/runner.ts` takes the implementation as an argument, because a conforming pack cannot import this package and may not be TypeScript. Four refusals make it worth running: a skip is not a pass, unwired families are named rather than implied, every case is named rather than counted, and four negative tests prove it is not a rubber stamp. Its first run reported five failures against our own code and ALL FIVE were the runner — the vectors encode refusal cases in the same arrays as positive ones and only the positive shape was handled. A kit that mishandles the refusal half tells every correct port it is broken.

10.4, combining AppViews. §10.6's third sentence is the one with consequences: every result carries enough source and snapshot evidence to identify where it came from AND to verify the supplier live before commitment. A combiner that merged two indexes into one anonymous list satisfies the first half and destroys the second. So divergence is surfaced and never resolved — picking the newest snapshot would resolve on the buyer's behalf using a claim made by the index that wants to win. `needsLiveVerification` is true on a single sighting too: one index agreeing with itself is one opinion, and the same index listed twice is still one.

A finding about where a defence is load-bearing. I wrote a test asserting that length-prefixing the merge key prevents a separator collision, and could not construct the colliding pair: `scheme` is closed, `issuer_did` must be a DID and `variant_digest` is hex64, so `value` is the only free-form field and a collision needs two. My first attempt was refused as an invalid DID, which is the validator doing its job. The encoding stays — it earns its place against a future widening, and the AppView's catalog key has more free-form fields where a surviving mutation once proved the hazard real — but the test now says which of those two things it is proving rather than implying the stronger one.

Placement, again deliberately outside the kernel. Both modules are pure functions over shapes `@dina/commerce-protocol` already defines and freezes, so they are usable by a buyer's node and by a third-party pack, and neither grows the extraction recorded below.

TODO is now zero, and that is not the same as done. Nineteen items are PART, each with its open half named in the WBS. The recurring shape is that the CODE exists and the missing piece is a deployment or a counterparty: a live Odoo, a second AppView, an actual third party running the kit, a hosting product for the managed runtime. §23 forbids claiming readiness from passing tests, so those stay PART.

WS-10.1 / 10.2 — review dimensions, and where the weighting is NOT

§14.4 lets Dina or an AppView derive a review-dimension projection, and then constrains it four ways. appview/src/shared/commerce/review-dimensions.ts makes each one checkable.

Placed in the AppView deliberately. §14.4 says "Dina OR an AppView may derive", and the AppView is the public index, so the derivation belongs there. It also keeps the module off the kernel's side of the memo §8 boundary — after adding ~120 lines to packages/core for the ERP connector, putting this in the AppView is the choice that does not grow the extraction recorded below.

The rule with teeth is the ranking. An unconfirmed model extraction is capped arithmetically below the standing floor, so no amount of model confidence outranks a person who said the same thing, and mayAffectStandingAlone re-checks the SOURCE as well as the number so a hand-built dimension cannot route around the cap. Ranking is by source FIRST: a reviewer at 3000bp beats a model at 10000. Without that pair, software deciding a supplier's packaging is bad could move their standing as hard as a customer who said so — and the customer can be argued with.

A leakage gate, refusing rather than trimming. §14.4 keeps exact commercial terms private unless the owner publishes them, and a derived dimension is public by construction. Evidence text carrying a price, a total or a quote reference is REFUSED: trimming would leave the shape of the number behind, and "roughly what they paid" is the disclosure the rule exists to stop. The finding names the FIELD, never the value — echoing a price into a log turns one leak into two.

What this module deliberately does NOT do. It derives; it does not score a supplier. Turning dimensions into standing is PeerLens's job under its own policy, and doing both here would put an extractor's confidence and a trust weight in one tunable place, which is the coupling §10.6 exists to prevent.

One of my test cases was wrong, and the code was right. I asserted that "unit price was fine" should be refused as a commercial-terms leak. It should not: §14.4 names "price and whether quoted terms held" as a publishable DIMENSION, so refusing commentary would delete the very thing the spec asks for. What stays private is the exact number. The suite now pins both sides of that line.

Open: nothing consumes the projection yet. It is the derivation half of 10.1/10.2; the display and the PeerLens weighting are separate work, which is why both items are PART rather than DONE.

WS-8 — the managed runtime's four DoDs, proven as properties

packages/managed-runtime holds a control plane, tenant cells and leases, a hosted-runner registry and a managed blob store — 593 lines with 24 unit tests, and NO importer anywhere in the repo. The WBS listed 8.1, 8.2, 8.3 and 8.5 as TODO, which was not quite right either: the code exists, and what was missing was any evidence for the four claims the DoDs actually make.

Those claims are not unit behaviour, they are refusals: tenant isolation provable, per-install binding with no shared authority, tenant-owned and exportable, the owner's phone may sleep while read and quote work continues. A claim of that shape is worth nothing unless something tries to break it, so ws8_definition_of_done.test.ts attacks each one rather than exercising a method: a lease used against the wrong tenant and the wrong purpose, an expired lease, a runner reaching for a second install and then a second tenant, an export asking after a neighbour, a delete colliding on a shared object name, eight hours passing with no owner interaction, a sealed cell asked to serve without a wake. 18 cases; the package now runs 42.

Two refusals are deliberately DISTINCT and the tests pin that. not_bound_to_install and not_bound_to_tenant could have been one code. They are not, because they send an operator to different places — the first is a misconfiguration, the second is a hosted runner reaching across a tenant boundary, and only one of those is an incident.

PART, not DONE, and the distinction is the point. Every property above is proven at module level, and the package still has no production importer because there is no hosting product for it to run inside. §23 is explicit that the commerce pack must not claim readiness merely because tests pass, and marking these DONE on module evidence would be exactly that. A deployed control plane needs its own operational validation; what can be proven without one now is.

Recorded as a live gap: the orphan-ledger guard that catches unwired exports walks packages/core/src/commerce only, so it never saw a whole unwired PACKAGE. A repo-wide version would have flagged this the day it landed.

The architecture fix behind that defect: routes are data, dispatch is a function

The query-string bug could only be guarded by READING server.ts as text and asserting a substring, because the route table lived inside a module that calls server.listen at import. That is a poor test and it was the honest best available. It also explains how the defect survived: the rules — is the method known, do the parameters validate, what does a handler failure mean — were written inline in a request callback, so nothing could reach them without a port.

  • src/web/xrpc-routes.ts — the method table, exported. A route is a validator plus a handler; both are pure with respect to transport, so the table belongs beside them rather than inside the process that exposes them.
  • src/web/xrpc-dispatch.ts — unknown method → 400, validation refusal → 400, handler throw → 500 with the reason kept on this side (a handler's error can name a table, a query or a connection string). Recognises a validation error by its NAME rather than importing zod, so the transport does not depend on a route's private choice of library.
  • src/web/server.ts keeps what is genuinely transport — client IP, rate limits, the namespace kill-switch, writing bytes — and shrank from 321 lines to 267.

Two source-reading guards became real tests. Mine, and an existing one in commerce_catalog_handler.test.ts that grepped server.ts for two substrings to prove the search method was exposed. That one pinned a SPELLING rather than a registration: it would have passed a route registered under a typo'd method id, and it broke the moment the table moved without any behaviour changing. Both now import the table and ask it. The new dispatch tests drive the REAL registered schema, including that a 500 body says nothing about why.

A pure move, verified as one. No endpoint's behaviour changed; the evidence is 2,065 AppView tests green and the typecheck error count unchanged at its pre-existing 9.

A FOURTH: the discovery endpoint could not accept its two strongest signals

The integration suites call searchCommerceCatalog directly, so they proved the SQL and said nothing about HTTP. The dispatch built its parameters with Object.fromEntries(url.searchParams.entries()), which keeps one value per key. searchCatalog declares identifier and category as ARRAYS, so the schema saw a string and answered Expected array, received string: every request using either field returned 400. Free text and region worked, so the endpoint looked alive.

Those two are the strongest signals discovery has. An exact identifier match IS the product, and category is where a buyer with no supplier in mind starts. The endpoint whose whole purpose is letting a buyer find a supplier they were never told about could be asked only the weakest two questions.

Both halves needed fixing, because either alone still fails. A new queryToRecord preserves repeated keys as arrays and leaves a single key a scalar, so scalar fields still parse. And the schema now accepts one value as a one-element list — arity belongs in the schema, because one value in a query string IS a string and a caller has no way to say otherwise. Deliberately NO comma splitting: a comma is legal inside a category id, a product identifier and a DID, so splitting would turn one caller's value into two — the same mistake as joining keys with a separator that can occur inside a field.

The bounds were never enforced either. `max(20)` on the lists and the 1..50 limit could not fire on fields that never validated. They now do, and the tests pin them, because a cap that has never run is a cap nobody has checked.

A gap in my own first test, caught before it mattered.
