The suite imported queryToRecord and the schema directly, so reverting server.ts would have restored the defect with everything still green — the identical correct-thing-nothing-calls shape. A static guard now asserts the dispatch calls the helper and no longer flattens the query string.

Open, and honestly named: src/web/server.ts calls server.listen at module scope and does not export its route table, so the real dispatch cannot be imported and tested — hence a source-reading guard rather than a request through the router. Extracting the route table is the right fix and is larger than this one; recorded rather than done, since it touches every xRPC endpoint.

A THIRD, and the worst: the relationship edge key could never be stored

appview/tests/integration/commerce_relationship_ingest.test.ts failed all eight cases on its first run with invalid byte sequence for encoding "UTF8": 0x00. The edge key was built as `${subjectKey}\0${relationship}` — a LITERAL NUL character, embedded in the source file. Unambiguous in a JavaScript Map, and impossible in Postgres, whose text type rejects 0x00 outright.

§10.7 was entirely non-functional against a real database. Not degraded — every relationship claim failed to index, and the product relationship projection is the feature that decides whether one product may inherit another's standing. Its stub suite was green throughout, because a Map stores a NUL without complaint.

A second cost, quieter. The NUL made relationship-projection.ts read as BINARY to grep, so grep -c '' on it returns nothing at all. An earlier cold audit in this project hit exactly that and drew a wrong conclusion from the silence. A control character in source is not only a storage problem; it disables the tools used to review the file.

The fix is the encoding already used everywhere else. encodeParts — length-prefixed, which is why the product key survives a custom value containing any separator — is now exported from catalog-projection.ts and used for edge keys too. ONE key encoding in the index rather than two, and no unprintable characters. No migration concern: the table could never have held a row.

Three defects, one method. Category search, region search and the entire relationship projection were all dead against Postgres, all green against stubs, and all three surfaced within an hour of pointing the same tests at a real database. The common shape is worth stating plainly: a stub records that a statement was issued, never that it would run. Where a module's correctness is expressed IN SQL, a stub test is not weak evidence — it is evidence about something else.

One of my own test expectations was wrong again, in the same direction as before. I asserted mayInheritStanding as a stored column; it is a derived predicate over the row. The test now calls the real function, which is also the version that cannot pass spuriously if someone later adds a column by that name for another reason.

TWO PRODUCTION DEFECTS in catalog discovery, found by running the SQL

appview/tests/integration/commerce_catalog_discovery.test.ts publishes two manufacturers through the real Jetstream handlers and then searches for them with the real xRPC — the claim WS-11.3 names as unreachable in-process, a buyer finding a supplier they were never told about. On its first run, nine of eleven cases failed, and two of those were the code rather than the fixture. Both are paths that had never worked in production, and neither is visible to the existing stub suite, because a stub records that a select happened rather than whether it runs.

1. Category search was dead. The narrowing built lower(c) = ANY(${params.category.map(...)}). Drizzle spreads a JS array into separate placeholders rather than binding one array parameter, so Postgres received ANY($1) with $1 = 'furniture.seating', tried to read it as an array literal, and answered malformed array literal (SQLSTATE 22P02). Every category-filtered discovery query failed at the database. Now IN (...) with one placeholder per value — parameterised, no array parsing, no interpolation.

2. Region search was dead. The clause read lower(r->>'scheme' || ':' || r->>'value'). In PostgreSQL ->> and || sit in the same precedence class and associate LEFT, so that parses as (((r->>'scheme') || ':') || r) ->> 'value' — a text on the left of ->>, which is operator does not exist: text ->> unknown (42883). Now parenthesised. This one is worth dwelling on: the expression READS correctly and only a database disagrees.

Tech debt removed while there. The identifier clause built its array literal with sql.raw and hand-rolled quote doubling. A product key can carry a custom value — an arbitrary bounded string out of a stranger's published catalog — and hand-escaping is the wrong side of that boundary to stand on. Replaced with real placeholders and a ::text[] cast.

Three of my own test expectations were wrong, and the code was right each time. The item fixture named secondary identifiers secondary_products where the projection reads identifiers; the region query form is scheme:value (documented, and used by BOTH the SQL narrowing and the pure matcher) rather than a bare value; and isFresh is validUntil > now, so a row read exactly AT its deadline is already stale.

Open question on that last one. "Valid until T" excluding T is a defensible reading and it is what the code and its DoD both state, so the test now pins it rather than arguing. The direction is at least the safe one — a stale row dropped costs a buyer one discovery result, a stale row served costs them a quote round trip against terms the supplier has already retired. Worth a confirmation if suppliers would read it the other way.

Note on the gate. The AppView typecheck reports 9 errors both before and after this change, all in test files unrelated to commerce (17-end-to-end-flows, capability_registry). Pre-existing, recorded rather than claimed clean.

WS-5.4 CLOSED — the AppView catalog ingest now meets real SQL

The unit suite drives both Jetstream handlers against a call-recording stub and states its own limit in its header: it proves the ORDER of operations, not that the SQL is valid. Those are different claims and only one survives without a database. A delete the stub records as delete:products might be missing a WHERE clause, might name a column the schema does not have, or might violate a constraint on the way back in — each passes a stub and fails in production, where the symptom is a supplier's catalog either vanishing or refusing to update.

appview/tests/integration/commerce_catalog_ingest.test.ts runs the real handlers in real transactions against dina_commerce_test, and every assertion is a query against the tables rather than a recorded call. 8 scenarios: both delivery orders reaching the same index; a snapshot no pointer names staying evidence and never searchable; a pointer whose snapshot has not arrived waiting rather than publishing an empty catalog; full-state replacement actually deleting the discontinued product; a replayed OLDER pointer leaving the live catalog alone; withdrawal deleting the products and KEEPING the pointer tombstone; a withdrawn catalog not reviving when its snapshot is re-delivered; and both record kinds being re-deliverable without duplicating a row.

Mutation, targeted at the gap. Removing the delete that makes replacement full-state kills two cases. That is precisely the claim a stub cannot make — it records delete:products whether or not the statement would have matched a row.

The first run failed for a reason worth recording, and it was mine. Seven of eight cases failed because my fixture SPREAD the snapshot at the top level of the record, where the handler reads it from a nested snapshot key. Nothing was stored and nothing was indexed. It is the same shape of mistake the notes keep recording — a fixture that guesses at the wire shape tests the guess — and it is why the suite is worth having: the handler refused a malformed record exactly as designed, silently, and only a read-back caught that nothing had landed.

Environment note. The AppView test helper defaults DATABASE_URL to a dina_trust database that does not exist on this machine; the migrated database is dina_commerce_test. The suite header records the command rather than changing a shared default that other suites may rely on.

WS-11.6 steps 13-15 — a restart that actually restarts

__tests__/commerce/acceptance_restart_journey.test.ts. Every other commerce journey runs inside one process lifetime; none of them closes the two databases mid-trade. Step 13 of §25.6 is "restart both sides", and the WBS calls it unskippable for a reason — a commerce claim that only holds while the process is up is not a commerce claim.

What makes it a real restart. Both SQLCipher files are close()d and reopened with the same passphrases, and both nodes are rebuilt by the same composeBothNodes() the first boot used — so the second boot cannot be handed something the first one kept in a closure. The workflow store is SQLite rather than the in-memory double the sibling journeys use: an in-memory lane would make the restart look cleaner than it is by losing the evidence that could contradict it.

Mutation, and the only one worth running here. Pointing the reopen at EMPTY databases kills all five cases. Without that check the suite could have been re-reading live objects and asserting nothing.

A finding that turned out to be the code being right. The replay case first failed with no outbound frame at all, which read like a buyer waiting for ever on a resend. It is the opposite: createProviderIngressTask returns a TWO-ARM union, and a replayed order comes back as { ok: true, coreAnswerJson } rather than { ok: true, taskId }. Core recognises the replay from the receipt it kept and answers inline — the plugin lane is never involved, so a supplier pack gets no second chance to decide differently about a sale already committed. The test now narrows on that union, which is a stronger assertion than comparing two wire frames: no task id means no plugin work.

WS-9.2 — the connector lane can read a real ERP (live run still owed)

The REQUEST half already spoke an ERP's language: an owner-configured JSON-RPC body, POSTed through the feed policy, with a credential that never crosses an origin. The ANSWER half was missing, and two owner-DECLARED fields close it. Declared, never inferred — the row reader refuses an unrecognised wrapper on purpose, because guessing which field holds the catalog is how a connector silently publishes a page of metadata as products.

  • rowsAt — one field NAME, never a path. A dotted expression is a small query language, and a query language over a supplier's response is a second way to decide what a catalog is. A declared-but-absent field REFUSES rather than falling back to the envelope: Odoo's error replies carry no result, and treating that as an empty catalog would publish a withdrawal of every product the supplier sells.
  • fieldMap + price — a rename, plus the one thing a rename cannot carry. ERPs quote a major-unit decimal and the catalog carries integer minor units (§9.1), so the price declaration is three named values: which field, what currency, how many decimals. The currency is configuration because a bare number has not said which currency it means, and guessing that publishes a catalog at a hundredth of its price. Rounds rather than truncates; an unmapped field passes through so the importer still names it rather than this code hiding it.

A test caught a real bug in my own implementation. The first version used Number(value). Odoo answers false for an unset field, and Number(false) is 0 — as are Number(null) and Number('') — so an unpriced product would have been published free. Only a number, or a string that is entirely one, is a price.

Open, and honestly open: everything above is tested against the documented Odoo JSON-RPC shape, not a live server. The item is not DONE until a real catalog load runs against the demo Odoo instance. Also note this is ~120 more lines on the plugin side of the §8 boundary, inside the kernel — it grows the extraction recorded below.

DEVIATION (owner decision, 2026-08-10) — the commerce pack is kernel code, not a plugin

The finding. docs/COMMERCE_PLUGIN_DECISION_MEMO.md §8 draws the authority boundary explicitly. Plugins may normalize products and units, calculate and compare offers deterministically, propose catalog publications, and talk to a separately consented business backend. Core must retain signing, vault access, consent, staff authority, approval decisions, D2D sending, repository publication, durable workflow, idempotency, and audit receipts.

Measured against that line, packages/core/src/commerce is 22,244 lines — 12,340 code, 8,566 comment (41%), 1,338 blank — and roughly 2,629 lines are on the plugin's side of the boundary but living in the kernel: catalog_import.ts (534), connectors.ts + connector_executors.ts + catalog_ingest.ts (875), offer_ranking.ts + comparison_card.ts (531), catalog_publisher.ts (228), reference_manifests.ts (461).

And there is no plugin artifact at all — no manifest file, no runner package. reference_manifests.ts is plugin manifests written as TypeScript inside Core, which is the tell: the thing we called a plugin was built as kernel code with a plugin-shaped name. This is a "Kernel, not Platform" violation, not a naming quibble.

The decision. The owner chose to KEEP BUILDING and extract later. Recorded as a deviation rather than a defect, because it is now a known, priced choice: later connector and ranking work (WS-9.2, WS-10) will also land in the kernel and make the eventual extraction larger. The correction, when it happens, is to move those modules into a commerce pack with a signed manifest and a runner, leave Core with the substrate, and add a boundary guard so the kernel cannot reabsorb them — the same shape as the guards in __tests__/commerce/boundary.test.ts, which already catch this class inside src/commerce but say nothing about which side of the plugin line a module belongs on.

Also unwired: packages/managed-runtime (641 lines, 8 files) has no importer anywhere in the repo. It went in with the WS-8 groundwork while WS-8 is still TODO. The orphan-ledger guard only walks src/commerce, so it did not see this.

C-02 — CLOSED: the owner can now see a proposal and answer it

packages/core/src/server/routes/host_operations.ts, registered from core_server.ts. GET /v1/plugins/host-operations lists everything parked; POST /v1/plugins/host-operations/decide answers one. Both are owner-only: a proposal is a request for authority the runner does not have, so letting the runner's own lane answer it would make the card a formality. Eleven tests in __tests__/server/routes/host_operations_route.test.ts, driving the ROUTES rather than the engines beneath them — the engines already had tests and were never the problem.

No accessor was needed after all. The note above predicted an installCommerceRuntime-style accessor touching both composition roots. Wrong: getPluginHostRuntime() already exposes the broker and the dispatcher. I wrote a second accessor before finding it, then deleted mine. Recording the false prediction because the reflex — "a route needs a new seam" — cost real work on a seam that existed.

What travels, and what does not. The params travel: an owner cannot judge "may this plugin search the AppView" without seeing what it wants to search for. The retained envelope does not — it is Core's own dispatch record, and shipping it would put a claim token on a screen. The list also carries a resolvable flag, because a proposal parked before its envelope was retained cannot be settled and saying so beats offering a button that fails.

Deviation from the first draft — a second fail-closed gate. The route originally settled whenever the retained source parsed and the install was alive, taking the workflow store as whatever getWorkflowService() returned. That is wrong: the runner learns BOTH answers — a permitted operation's verified result and a refusal — as its next task on its own lane. With no workflow store there is nothing to enqueue, so settling would spend the decision and leave the runner waiting on a lane that never fills. The route now answers 503 and leaves the proposal in proposed, answerable once the node is whole. Same reasoning as the retained-source refusal, one step later.

Both new behaviours are mutation-checked. Deleting the follow-up enqueue inside settleOwnerDecision and deleting the workflow guard each killed exactly one test and no others. The approve case asserts the whole chain, not the status code: the proposal reaches completed, the result JSON holds what the executor returned, and a follow-up task is claimable on pluginLane(installId) carrying that result.

Test-harness findings worth keeping. Three fixture shortcuts each produced a green-looking test against a world that does not exist, and the row hydrator caught all three: {kind:'publisher'} is not a valid trust anchor; a manifest carrying only capabilities is quarantined because plugin_id / version / execution.mode must agree with the scalar columns (round-12 #8); and a hand-written envelope stub cannot survive parsePluginEnvelope. The suite now builds a real install through SQLitePluginInstallRepository, a real envelope through buildPluginEnvelope, and a real WorkflowService. Note in passing that host_operation_completion.test.ts still uses the invalid {kind:'publisher'} anchor — harmless there because that fixture never round-trips through the database, which is exactly why it went unnoticed.

C-02 — the original finding, kept for the record

A host-operation proposal that cards as awaiting_owner can never be resolved. settleOwnerDecision and applyOwnerDecision have no production caller, and two docstrings claim an owner surface — "the owner already answers plugin effects through the approval inbox" — that nothing connects proposals to.

Why it is not a one-line wire. settleOwnerDecision needs the proposal id, the SOURCE ENVELOPE of the claim that proposed it, and the install. None of that is retained: plugin_extension_proposals exists in the schema and nothing in packages/core/src reads or writes it — the broker parks in memory, so a parked proposal does not survive the process, let alone reach an owner. Closing it needs the repository behind that table, the broker wired to it, the envelope and install retained with the proposal, a list surface, and a decide route.

Because neither composition root injects capabilityKind, publisherRing, standingApproval or priorInvocations, every registered operation evaluates at custom/unverified with no standing approval and returns mode: 'card' — so all three host operations park, and §3.4's brokered lane is inert in practice. A Buyer or Supplier runner cannot perform an AppView search, a D2D send, or a connector call.

Still open from this audit, unstarted and not disguised

  • C-02 (high) — host-operation proposals card as awaiting_owner and can never be resolved: no route, no inbox record. §3.4's brokered lane is inert, and two docstrings claim an owner surface that does not exist.
  • C-03 (high) — the §15.2 approval binding compares two objects supplied by the same caller in the same request. Nothing Core-owned mints or retains the payload, so §20.7's bait-and-switch check detects only client self-inconsistency.
  • C-05 (medium) — the buyer never revalidates quote expiry or runs verifyOrderAgainstQuote before dispatch, though it binds quoteExpiresAt into the approval payload. §19 says an expired quote must force a requote.
  • No route for requestQuote — the composer works and is exercised end to end, but an owner cannot yet ask for a price from the product. Ledgered with that exact reason.

Gate: core 8,039 · typecheck 0.

The buyer's half, and a guard that now finds this class by itself

The open question above is CLOSED, and the diagnosis was bigger than stated

It was not a missing store. commerceRecordDigest('request', …) had zero production occurrences: the buyer never composed a QuoteRequest at all. The outbound quote lane did not exist, which is why verifySignedQuoteForBuyer had no caller — it had no precondition. Built:

  • commerce_buyer_quote_requests and its repository — the requests this node sent, retained as the yardstick §9.8 measures an arriving quote against.
  • The row checks itself. parseRetained runs validateQuoteRequest, which ends in verifyCommerceRecordDigest, so the digest is recomputed from the body rather than compared to a neighbouring column. A row edited after writing — body and digest changed together — is caught. Without that, a tampered yardstick would quietly redefine the question the buyer holds the supplier to.
  • verifySignedQuoteForBuyer wired into the buyer's arrival path, with two new outcomes: unsolicited_quote (a quote for a request_id never sent) and quote_not_our_question (§20.4 — right address, wrong question).

Design decision: audience is judged before question

A quote addressed to another buyer returns quote_fork, which already covers "offered to somebody else", rather than quote_not_our_question. My first ordering had the binding check answer first, which reported every misaddressed quote as one that answered the wrong question — a different accusation about a different mistake.

Three errors caught by checks rather than by luck

  • Backticks in a SQL template literal — the seventh occurrence, and my own notes warn about it. Broke the schema module with errors 300 lines from the edit.
  • I used run().rowsAffected for first-writer detection. run() returns a constant 1 in the base adapter — the exact hazard behind a past mobile CAS bug. Replaced with read-then-insert inside one transaction.
  • A test double narrowed its own put arity, which would have let it stop standing in for the real repository at the call sites that matter.

The guard that would have caught all of this

The orphan ledger scans top-level export function|const|class and never class METHODS, so an unwired method on a wired class was invisible — which is exactly how registerSignedQuote hid through nine review rounds. The new boundary case asserts that every public method on an application service has a production caller, scoped deliberately to the services rather than to every method: a whole-codebase version would need an allow-list of internal helpers big enough to hide the next one in.

It found four more instances immediately — registerSignedQuote (now superseded by issueQuote and marked for deletion once the quote-ledger tests migrate), and the three §16.2/§12.7 recovery-ceremony methods that have no operator surface yet. Each is now named with a reason instead of being invisible. That is the point: the defect class this whole review chased is now found by a test rather than by an auditor.

docs/COMMERCE_TEST_PLAN.md corrected

Its "the happy path exists end to end" claim was false and is now marked as such, with a caveat naming why: every journey suite hand-registers its quote, so they prove the engines agree and not that a quote can be issued. quote_issuance_journey.test.ts is the one that proves the latter.

Gate: core 8,034 · core-server 2,842 · typecheck 0.

SUPERSEDED — the open question below is answered above, kept for the record

verifySignedQuoteForBuyer enforces §9.8's buyer-side clause: that request_digest matches the request the buyer RETAINED and priced_delivery_projection_digest matches the projection the buyer SENT, plus line correspondence and substitution authority (§20.4 bait-and-switch). It has no caller, and it cannot be given one as things stand: its BuyerQuoteContext requires the retained request and the sent projection digest, and this node keeps neither — there is no buyer-side outbound-request store at all.

So this is not a wiring omission, it is a missing component: a table, a repository, a write at the point the buyer sends request_quote, and a migration. Until it exists, a supplier can return a quote that prices a different projection or answers a different request and the buyer will record it. COMMERCE_WBS row 1.5 is marked DONE and should not be — it is true of the protocol function and false of the product. Recorded here rather than quietly wired against a half-built context, which is the failure mode this whole review found.

State: commerce + plugins suites green (1,847), typecheck clean. Still open from the cold audit: the buyer-side verifySignedQuoteForBuyer wiring (high), the buyer capability dispatch and missing cancel_order branch (medium), two unguarded JSON.parse calls in the claim guard (low), extending the boundary ledger to class methods, correcting the test-plan claim, and a journey that issues a quote without hand-registering one.

Final continuity state: Claude PASS at 5b613c6a with zero findings — core 8,025 · core-server 2,842 · typecheck clean. Sixteen findings across nine rounds, fifteen fixed, one escalated (R2-02, §9.13 cross-major continuity). The Codex reviewer never ran, so this artifact has been checked by exactly one independent reader; convergence in the two-reviewer sense was never reached and is not claimed.

Dual-review remediation — DR-1: the rule that had a caller and still never ran

The dual review's first finding is the one worth reading, because the defect class beat every guard this repo has. evaluateStaffAuthority — spend ceilings, category and branch authority, quorum, time-bounded delegation, all of §7.2 and §7.3 — was correct, had 28 passing tests, and never ran on a single real order. It hung off an OPTIONAL authority argument on submitApprovedOrder, guarded by if (args.authority !== undefined), and neither order route supplied it. Every test that exercised the rule handed the function its argument directly.

Why the orphan ledger missed it. That guard matches symbol names across production files, and buyer_executor.ts names evaluateStaffAuthority — imports it, calls it, mentions it in three comments. The ledger's question is “does anything reference this?” and the answer was yes. The question that mattered was “does this run?”. A rule can have a caller and still never run, and no name-matching guard can tell the difference.

The fix is three changes that only work together. A BuyerAuthorityProvider seam (commerce/buyer_authority.ts) that Core resolves at submit and at resend. authority promoted from optional to REQUIRED, so the compiler now refuses a caller that omits it — a type is the only guard that cannot be forgotten. And both composition roots installing a provider. Any one alone leaves the hole open: the seam without the required type is another optional argument, and the required type without the roots is a node that cannot buy.

Null is a refusal, not a default. No provider installed returns 503; a provider that holds no record for this principal returns 403. The composition roots read the node DID and refuse when it is null rather than passing an empty principal. §7.3's pilot shape is the single-approver CONFIGURATION — one owner grant evaluated by the same code as any other — rather than an owner branch that skips the check, because “the owner is obviously allowed” is the exact reasoning that produced the dead path.

A second dead rule found while fixing the first. The executor built its AuthorityRequest with regionValue: null hardcoded. covers refuses a location grant outright when the request names no region — so §7.2 BRANCH authority was not merely unwired, it was unsatisfiable: a business whose staff model is “this branch buys for this state” could not buy at all. The region now comes off the order's own delivery projection as scheme:value. From the ORDER, never the caller-supplied context, so nobody names the region that grants them authority — and matching is on scheme:value rather than the bare value, because a postal area and an admin area can carry the same digits.

Both fixes were mutation-checked, not just run. Reverting regionValue to null fails the branch case; making the null-provider branch permissive fails the 503 case. An earlier attempt at the second mutation printed Tests: 0 total — which is a compile crash, not a pass, and is the third time this session that reading it as a pass would have been wrong.

Verification: core 433 suites / 8,096 tests green (8 new), typecheck 0. WBS 8.4 rewritten to record that it read DONE while the section was dead, rather than quietly re-marked.

Dual-review remediation — DR-2 to DR-5: four more rules that bound nothing

DR-1 was a rule with a caller that never ran. The next four are its cousins: checks that ran, and compared a claim against itself.

DR-2 — §15.2 bound whatever the caller said it was

POST /v1/commerce/orders/prepare took the whole approval context out of the request body and retained it, so §15.2's last line — “plugin install, capability, manifest CID, scope hash, and config revision” — bound the caller's own claim. The binding the send later verifies then agreed with itself, because both halves came from the same sentence. It is the confused-deputy shape the D2D receive path already learned once: a field is authority only when the party that produced it is the party being bound.

commerce/acting_install.ts resolves the five from Core's own registry. The body SELECTS an install and a capability; this node says what they are. A body that states a DIFFERENT manifest CID or config revision is refused rather than corrected — the surface showed the owner something, and if that is not what would run then the owner approved a description of a different act. Silently substituting the true value would make the card and the send agree while the human and the card do not.

DR-3 — the card and the authority check meant different listings

The card bound context.serviceUri. The routes carried a separate service_rkey, out of the body, defaulting to self — and that rkey is what reaches the acting-for chain, so a listing-scoped grant could be evaluated against a listing the owner never saw. On a node with one listing the two agreed by accident, which is why it survived: §10's multi-listing model is exactly where that stops being true. The rkey now comes OUT of the bound URI; a stated rkey that disagrees is refused, and so is a URI whose authority is not the order's supplier.

DR-4 — half of the §12.1 value scan

§12.1 step 10 asks for “the existing structured-identifier PII patterns (phone, email, account and ID number shapes) PLUS a secret-shaped-token detector”. Only the token detector existed. The patterns were already in Core, running on the egress path — so this was one import, and nothing looked missing because the module it belonged in was full of careful work on the other half.

A separate refusal code, on purpose. personal_identifier_value rather than reusing secret_shaped_value, because the two send an operator to different places: one means rotate that credential, the other means a person's details are in your catalog export.

The false positive that shaped the rule, recorded because it is a real tradeoff. detectPII's US phone pattern is not anchored at its start, so it finds a ten-digit window inside any longer digit run — including the GTIN in this module's own test catalog. Product number fields (value, sku, mpn) are therefore scanned only for classes with non-alphanumeric structure, on the principle that a product number is a bag of digits and letters by design and a pattern whose only signal is length cannot tell one from a phone. Free text keeps the FULL scan: a description reading “Model 9506000134352” will be refused, and that is the accepted cost — moving the code into sku takes seconds, a published customer phone number is public for ever. The alternative was a gate that refuses honest catalogs, and a gate that refuses honest catalogs gets switched off, which is the outcome that actually leaks.

DR-5 — discovery told every buyer to knock on the same door

Every §10.5 candidate answered service_uri and service_rkey as self. That is where a buyer sends the quote request. §10.2 identifies a catalog by catalog_id and gives a supplier no way to name the listing that serves it, so AppView had nowhere to learn it and asserted a fact it had not been told.

CatalogPointer.service_rkey is the missing sentence — optional and additive, so no catalog published before today became invalid. It had to be added in TWO places: the protocol type and AppView's own copy of the interface, which AppView keeps rather than importing. Adding it to one would have left discovery quietly answering self for ever, and the compiler would not have said a word. self survives as the documented convention for a primary listing, used where the supplier has not said rather than asserted as known.

A listing rkey carrying a URI separator is refused at INGEST. The buyer's parseAtUri would refuse the spliced URI anyway, which fails closed — but it fails closed by dropping the supplier's own products from every result, with no explanation reaching either party. Refusing the publication tells the supplier what is wrong with it.

Verification: core 433 suites / 8,122 tests (+26 over DR-1); AppView 123 files / 2,532 tests against real Postgres; commerce-protocol 389. Typecheck 0 across the monorepo. Every fix mutation-checked — reverting the check fails the case, and one earlier mutation attempt printed Tests: 0 total, which is a compile crash and not a pass. WBS rows 7.5, 3.7, 5.3 and 5.6 carry the corrections.

One AppView run needs a caveat. The first full-suite run showed a single failure in the PeerLens friend-boost test — column "search_vector" does not exist. That was my scratch database, provisioned with drizzle-kit push, which does not apply the raw-SQL generated columns in fts_columns.ts. Applying those four statements turned it green. Not a regression, and recorded rather than quietly re-run.

Dual-review round 2 — the reviewer found that two of my five fixes were half-fixes

The continuity reviewer returned FAIL with twelve findings against 964dcc99bd8e: DR-1, DR-2, DR-3 and DR-6 resolved, DR-4 and DR-5 unresolved, and eight new. It was right on every one I checked, and the two unresolved ones are the more useful lesson.

DR-5 was the read half of a fix with no producer

I added service_rkey to the pointer type, to AppView's copy of that type, to the ingest, to the product row and to the candidate projection, tested it end to end against real Postgres, and never noticed that no Dina node could emit the field. The publish route accepted no such value and neither pointer builder set one, so every catalog the shipping implementation publishes still had a null listing and every candidate still fell back to self — the original symptom, unchanged, behind a fix that read as complete.

It survived a round of review because my tests wrote the pointer by hand. A read path with no producer is the same defect as a rule with no caller, one layer out, and hand-built fixtures hide both in exactly the same way.

DR-4's exclusion list had grown past the principle that justified it

I stated a broad rule — only patterns with non-alphanumeric structure carry information in an identifier field — and then excluded more classes than that rule covered. BANK_ACCT is \b\d{16}\b and no GTIN scheme is sixteen digits; CREDIT_CARD is Luhn-validated. Neither can collide with a product number, so both exclusions bought nothing and cost the one case where a false negative is unrecoverable. My own test asserted that a valid card number in a sku column published clean. The rule is now stated as the ONE collision it actually avoids (the unanchored US phone pattern reading a ten-digit window inside a GTIN), because a rule stated as a principle invites the next reader to extend it by analogy.

NEW-1 — the §15.2 defect moved rather than closed

Worth recording carefully, because I would have called this fixed. DR-2 stopped the caller supplying both halves of the binding comparison. What replaced it was ONE STORE ROW supplying both halves: the approval store rebuilds its payload from its own order and context on every read, and the executor rebuilds it again from those same two values. Two derivations of one stored value cannot disagree, so verifyApprovalBinding could no longer fail on any field — while buyer_executor.ts still promised that "a swapped install between the tap and the send is refused rather than dispatched".

A card lives thirty minutes. Nothing re-read the install registry in that window, so a plugin could be paused, revoked, reconfigured or updated and the order would still go out bound to the facts as they stood at the tap. Submit and resend now re-resolve the install against the registry, which puts live state on one side of the comparison and is what makes the check able to fail at all.

The other five

  • NEW-2 — the pointer-first ingest path dropped the listing when rebuilding a held pointer, so DR-5's read path worked on one Jetstream delivery order and silently failed on the other. My DR-5 tests published snapshot-first only. Both orders are now driven.
  • NEW-3 — context.principal was still caller-supplied after DR-2, and chainGaps only checks the authority domain is non-empty. I had judged this acceptable on the grounds that a node has no staff directory to check a principal against; the supplier half of the same file disproves that one function away, naming the owner and a Core-side domain constant. The buy side now does the same, and refuses a body that states otherwise — including one claiming policy approved what a person tapped.
  • NEW-4 — category_buyer authority was evaluated against a category list the request body supplied and the digest does not cover. The same shape DR-1 fixed for the region, one line above it. There is no Core-side category derivation to replace it with, so the request now carries an empty list and covers refuses category grants outright — a rule this node cannot enforce should refuse, not be satisfied by the caller.
  • NEW-5 — nothing checked that the acting install was the BUYER pack, so a card could bind the supplier pack as the plugin that placed a purchase order. roleIsInstalled(…, 'buyer') already expressed the rule and had no production caller: the dominant defect class again, in the module written to fix it.
  • NEW-6 — DR-2's "the business is this node" rule was applied to the buyer half only; the supplier decision route took whatever the settings row said. An asymmetric rule is a rule with a hole in it.
  • NEW-7 — the approval store read service_rkey back from a column the digest does not cover, reintroducing the second source of truth DR-3 removed, for the one field DR-3 was about. It is re-derived on read now, and a disagreeing row reads as absent.
  • NEW-8 — DR-6 guarded "the implementation did not claim a family" and not "the caller supplied no vectors for it", so a CI job fetching a truncated artefact got a clean pass over the money arithmetic it never checked.

Verification: core 433 suites / 8,139 tests · core-server 2,842 · commerce-protocol 393 · AppView 123 files / 2,534 against real Postgres · typecheck 0 · commerce lint clean. Every fix mutation-checked. New artifact version b80529cee0a3.

Two review results, one reviewer. Codex was asked twice and is BLOCKED both times: its harness starts a background job and returns a task ID, and the only way to collect the result is /codex:status, which only the human can invoke. I declined to run the wrapped binary directly, since doing exactly what a disabled command does is circumventing the restriction rather than respecting it. So this artifact has been read by one independent reviewer. Convergence in the two-reviewer sense has not been reached and is not claimed.

Dual-review round 3 — all fourteen closed, six more found, one of them mine twice over

The reviewer marked DR-1 to DR-6 and NEW-1 to NEW-8 all resolved, and raised six more. The most instructive is NEW-9, because it is the same rule being wrong in the opposite direction.

NEW-9 — a rule that has now been wrong both ways

Round 1 excluded BANK_ACCT and CREDIT_CARD from product-number fields on a general argument, and a valid card number in a sku column published clean. Round 2 admitted both back on the reasoning that "CREDIT_CARD is Luhn-validated" — which settles BANK_ACCT and does NOT settle CREDIT_CARD, because that pattern accepts 13 to 19 digits and a GTIN-13 is thirteen. GTIN check digits are mod-10 with 3/1 weighting, Luhn is 2/1, so the two are independent. Measured rather than argued: of five real GTIN-13s, 5901234123457 and 4901234567894 both pass Luhn and would have been refused as credit cards. The module's own fixture happens to fail Luhn, which is exactly why the suite stayed green while about a tenth of honest catalogs became unpublishable.

The rule is now a list of measured collisions with their boundaries — PHONE always in these fields, CREDIT_CARD only below fifteen digits — rather than a principle. A principle invites the next reader to extend it by analogy, which is how it went wrong twice.

NEW-11 — the honest answer to a question I asked

I asked the reviewer whether verifyApprovalBinding could still fail on anything other than the install fields. The answer is that it cannot fail on anything: the store computes the approved payload by calling the same pure builder the executor rebuilds with, over the same two objects, so the comparison is byte-identical by construction. The real protections are elsewhere and adequate — the digest recomputation on hydrate, and the live install recheck — but the exported seam's docstring still promised all three, and the recheck was a private route helper that a third send path could simply not call.

So the recheck moved INSIDE submitApprovedOrder, for the same reason authority is a required argument: a guard a caller can forget is a guard that eventually gets forgotten. The docstring now states which layer refuses each of the three cases instead of claiming them all for the one that provably cannot.

Four test harnesses failed the moment that landed — they registered the supplier install and then submitted buyer orders under a hand-written install id that named nothing. That is the check working, and the harnesses now mint a real buyer pack in the registry they already own.

The other four

  • NEW-10 — the DR-5 producer half only half reached the routes. service_rkey was read from the request body and nowhere else, so a routine republication that did not restate it silently reverted every buyer to self; and buildCatalogWithdrawal's new parameter had no caller that ever supplied it. It is now inherited from the predecessor unless explicitly restated, and carried onto the tombstone. This route already refused a MISSING items array on exactly that reasoning.
  • NEW-12 — refusing a category grant was right, but the refusal said "holds no live grant" when the principal holds one this node cannot evaluate, sending an operator to their grant table on a money path. Named properly now.
  • NEW-13 — allowedCategoryIds survived NEW-4 as a dead field still documented as an authority input, and the prepare route spread the body's whole context into the retained row where the digest does not bind it. Field deleted; the retained context is now built from named fields.
  • NEW-14 — third iteration of one hole. DR-6 caught "the implementation did not claim a family"; NEW-8 caught "no vectors were supplied" by checking the KEY was present; a present-but-empty family still passed. The predicate now measures what EXECUTED.

Verification: core 433 suites / 8,149 tests · core-server 2,842 · commerce-protocol 396 · AppView 123 files / 2,534 against real Postgres · typecheck 0 · commerce lint clean. Artifact version e92bb05f4bff.

Dual-review round 4 — three findings, and the leakage rule was wrong a third time

Twenty prior findings all resolved. Three new, and the important one is NEW-15: I asked the reviewer to assume I had got the leakage rule wrong again, and I had.

NEW-15 — a third collision, in the rule that has now been wrong three times

AADHAAR is twelve digits that do not start 0 or 1. GTIN-12 — UPC-A — is twelve digits. So every honest UPC-A with a number-system digit from 2 to 9 was read as an Aadhaar number and refused the whole publication. Measured, not argued: 712345678904 is a valid UPC-A and detectPII returns ["AADHAAR"] for it. Number-system 2 is variable-measure retail and 3 is drug codes — exactly the catalogs a small supplier uploads as a CSV.

The PHONE exclusion did not rescue it, which is why it needed its own line: inside a twelve-digit run the phone rule matches a ten-digit window, resolveOverlaps drops the shorter match, and the AADHAAR span survives. The fix keys on the SEPARATOR — a bare twelve-digit run cannot be told from a UPC-A, while a real Aadhaar written 2345 6789 0123 keeps its spacing and stays refused. Both directions are now tested.

Three errors in one rule, and the pattern in them is the same each time: I reasoned about a class instead of measuring it. The comment now carries the measured boundary per class and states what the card boundary knowingly gives up (a Diners Club number is fourteen digits, so a 13- or 14-digit card in a product-number field is not scanned), because the previous version read as though the boundary were free.

NEW-17 — a distinction the NEW-11 move quietly collapsed

The route helper the recheck replaced returned 503 for an unreadable install registry and 409 for everything else. Folding it into the executor mapped every outcome to one refusal at 409 — so a node with no registry told a client "the install changed since approval, retry", inviting a retry loop against a node that will refuse identically for ever. Every other not-configured condition on these routes answers 503, and prepare never stopped doing so. Worth recording as a class: moving a check to a safer place can still lose something the old place was carrying.

NEW-16 — the WBS stated a principle the code had abandoned twice

Row 5.3 still carried the round-one wording for the leakage rule, which the code contradicted in round two and deleted in round three. A row describing behaviour the code no longer has is the failure this whole review was chartered on, in the document the brief calls the work breakdown. Rewritten to the measured list, with the history in it.

Verification: core 433 suites / 8,153 tests · core-server 2,842 · commerce-protocol 396 · AppView 123 files / 2,534 against real Postgres · typecheck 0 · commerce lint clean. NEW-15 and NEW-17 mutation-checked. Artifact version 247e4df1d391.

The reviewer also checked the four test harnesses I repaired last round — the move that hid DR-5 from it for a round — and confirmed they mint through the real repository and the real reference manifest rather than being edited green. One cosmetic divergence it recorded rather than filed: buyer_orders_sqlite still names a principal no production path can emit after NEW-3.

Dual-review round 5 — one finding, and the enumeration that ended the leakage rule

Twenty-three prior findings resolved, one new. I had asked the reviewer to stop confirming my exclusions and instead ENUMERATE what detectPII can match inside a bounded alphanumeric string, because three errors in one rule meant checking my own list again would only confirm my own blind spot.

NEW-18 — two classes the measuring method could never reach

The enumeration came back with exactly two live cases, and the reason they were missed is the useful part. The first three collisions were MEASURABLE because what they compete with is a GTIN: a closed set of lengths with a check digit, so a boundary can be derived and checked. PAN and IFSC compete with sku, mpn and custom values — supplier-chosen strings with no length bound, no vocabulary and no check digit. There is nothing to measure, which is why three rounds of measuring never surfaced them.

The collision is entirely ordinary. Verified before deciding: CHAIR2024B is refused as an Indian tax ID (five letters, four digits, one letter) and ACME012345X as a bank branch code (four letters, a zero, six alphanumerics). Both are unremarkable part numbers.

Excluded, and recorded as a CHOICE rather than a measurement. A supplier cannot renumber their catalog, so a false positive makes an honest SKU scheme permanently unpublishable; a genuine PAN in a sku column is a deliberate act rather than the ERP-export accident this scan exists for. §12.1 is explicit that this layer is defence in depth and the closed vocabulary is the primary control. Free text still scans both at full strength, which is where a leaked contact block actually arrives. Four tests pin both directions, so reversing the choice means saying which case you are changing.

The other half of the finding was two comment blocks in the same file disagreeing about how long the exclusion list was — one saying "the two collisions", the other "THREE CLASSES". In a file whose comments ARE the decision record and have been wrong three times, that is how the fourth error gets made. One list now, in one place, with the history and the measured boundary per class.

What the reviewer named as out of scope rather than filing

Recorded here because an accurate boundary is worth more than a longer list: whether PERSONAL_IDENTIFIER_TYPES is the right vocabulary for §12.1 step 10 at all (a spec question); the did:plc:sanchoowner fixture divergence in buyer_orders_sqlite, which hides nothing because only non-emptiness is checked; the conformance runner measuring coverage across four family names when there are eight sub-families, which the EXECUTED: line makes visible; and the WS-8 managed-runtime rows carrying PART with no production importer, which the WBS states plainly.

Verification: core 433 suites / 8,158 tests · core-server 2,842 · commerce-protocol 396 · AppView 123 files / 2,534 against real Postgres · typecheck 0 · commerce lint clean. Mutation-checked. Artifact version de6e64c28e29. WBS row 5.3 carries the fifth and sixth exclusions and the measurement-versus-choice distinction.

Continuity review closed — PASS at de6e64c28e29, zero findings

Six rounds. Twenty-four findings raised and closed: DR-1 to DR-6 from the first pass, NEW-1 to NEW-18 found while fixing them. The reviewer's closing line is the one that matters: the defect class that started this — a correct rule nothing calls, or one that compares a claim against itself — is closed at every place reachable from the routes and the two composition roots.

What the rounds actually cost, stated because the shape is the lesson. Of the eighteen NEW findings, eleven were defects in work I had done in the immediately preceding round. Two of my five original fixes were half-fixes (DR-4, DR-5). One fix moved a defect rather than closing it (NEW-1). One fix quietly dropped a distinction the code it replaced was carrying (NEW-17). And one rule — the §12.1 leakage scan — was wrong three separate times, in both directions, before an enumeration ended it. A fix is not a fix because it was written carefully; it is a fix when someone else has failed to break it.

The one observation the reviewer recorded rather than filed

PRODUCT_NUMBER_FIELDS matches on the LEAF field name, so value also covers regions[].value and variant_axes.value, not only product.value. All five exclusions therefore reach two fields the comment does not name. It predates this round, none of the excluded classes is a plausible leak in a region or axis code, and narrowing it by path would add a mechanism to maintain for no case anyone can point at.

Deliberately not fixed, and the reason is procedural. A PASS applies to one exact artifact version, and this file's whole lesson is that its comments ARE the decision record. Editing the comment would invalidate a verification that took six rounds to earn, for a note. It is recorded here instead, to be folded into that comment the next time the file is opened for a real change.

DUAL_REVIEW_EVIDENCE

ARTIFACT: packages/core/src/commerce + packages/commerce-protocol + appview/src/shared/commerce
             + appview/src/api/xrpc/commerce-catalog-search.ts + the commerce routes
ARTIFACT_VERSION: de6e64c28e29
PHASE: REVALIDATION
CLAUDE_STATE: PASS_CURRENT
CLAUDE_FINDINGS: 0
CODEX_STATE: BLOCKED
CODEX_FINDINGS: NOT_RUN
OPEN_FINDINGS: 0
PROVISIONALLY_FIXED: 0
VERIFICATION: PASS — core 433 suites / 8,158 tests; core-server 2,842;
             commerce-protocol 396; AppView 123 files / 2,534 vs real Postgres;
             typecheck 0; commerce lint clean; every fix mutation-checked
NEXT_ACTION: the human runs /codex:status task-msmp32te-iafqls to collect the
             second reviewer, which is the only step that can raise this
TERMINAL_STATE: BLOCKED

This is BLOCKED, not SUCCESS, and the difference is not cosmetic. The dual-review contract requires two independent readers reaching PASS on the same unchanged version. One reader reached PASS. Codex was asked twice and could not complete either time: its harness starts a background job and returns a task ID, and the only way to collect the result is /codex:status, which only the human can invoke. I declined to run the wrapped binary directly, because doing exactly what a disabled command does is circumventing the restriction rather than respecting it.

So: convergence in the two-reviewer sense was never reached and is not claimed, and no cold audit was run, because a cold audit is defined against a CANDIDATE_CLEAN artifact that two continuity reviewers have passed. What this artifact has is one thorough independent reader across six rounds. That is worth a great deal and it is not what the contract asks for, and those two facts are stated together on purpose.

WS-11.3 discovery closed — and it found the defect that mattered most

WBS 11.3 named its own remaining gap: "every journey here starts from a supplier the buyer already names". Discovery — a retailer FINDING a manufacturer nobody told them about — was the one step no in-process journey could reach.

The join had to be the bytes, not an import. AppView takes no @dina/* dependency on purpose; it deploys independently, which is exactly why it keeps its own copy of CatalogPointer and why a field added on one side and not the other went unnoticed for a round (NEW-2). So Core's real publisher writes ChairMaker's catalog to a fixture — regenerate-and-compare, so it cannot drift from the publisher that claims to have made it — and AppView ingests those exact bytes through its real handlers into real Postgres.

That also fixes something about the discovery suite I had already written: it built its catalog records with AppView's OWN catalogPageDigest and catalogSnapshotDigest and then asserted AppView accepted them. Both sides of that check were AppView's — the same defect class as everything else this review found, sitting in my own test. Mutating the fixture's digest now kills the whole suite, which is what makes it a join.

What it found on its first run

Core's publisher refused ChairMaker's catalog: 17 item field(s) may not be published (§12.1). The leakage vocabulary had been written from the spec's prose — category, regions, list_price, pack_size — while @dina/commerce-protocol defines the published item as category_ids, fulfilment_regions, indicative_price, pack.

Two self-consistent vocabularies that had never met, so Core's own publication gate refused every item shape AppView's ingest requires. A supplier publishing a real CatalogItem could not publish at all. Neither side could see it: Core's publisher tests used a flat {sku, name} CSV shape and AppView's ingest tests hand-built CatalogItems and never called Core. Each half was green about a different thing.

Fixed at the root rather than by widening a list. The vocabulary now covers the wire type AND the flat CSV lane; attributes keys are supplier-chosen per §9.5 with their values still scanned; and catalog_leakage_vocabulary.test.ts drives a MAXIMAL CatalogItem through the real validator and asserts every field it can carry is in the list, in both directions. That test is the fix; the list is data it checks.

And my reconciliation immediately got it wrong once. Replacing category with category_ids dropped the flat CSV column and broke every spreadsheet import. An existing test caught it, which was luck rather than design — nothing said both lanes had to be covered. There is now a case that names the pair together, because "reconcile A to B" is exactly the edit that silently drops the half nobody is looking at.

The scenarios

Sancho finds ChairMaker by category, by free text, by a GTIN read off a physical product, and by a scoped secondary SKU — then reads the listing to send the quote request to, from the pointer Core signed. Deliberate fixture choices: the GTINs are 5901234123457 (a real GTIN-13 that passes Luhn) and 712345678904 (a valid UPC-A), and the SKU is CHAIR2024B — all three are values the §12.1 scan refused at some point during this review. A fixture that avoided them would publish cleanly while real catalogs did not.

Two of my assertions were wrong about the CONTRACT rather than the data, and both are recorded in the test: a scoped manufacturer_sku needs its issuer (two manufacturers may both call something CHAIR2024B), and region is a §10.5 recall SIGNAL rather than a hard filter, so an unserved region removes the region match from a candidate rather than the candidate.

Verification: core 435 suites / 8,168 tests · core-server 2,842 · commerce-protocol 396 · AppView 124 files / 2,547 against real Postgres · typecheck 0 · commerce lint clean. Fixture mutation-checked. WBS 11.3's discovery gap is closed; a live PDS stays open, and the boundary is stated plainly — a PDS is the transport for these bytes, and the bytes are what both sides are now checked against.

WS-8 — the honest answer was a boundary, not a hosting product

All four WS-8 rows carried the same gap in the same words: "no production importer". A whole package nothing calls is the defect class this review spent six rounds on, so the obvious move was to build the importer — a hosting service that composes the control plane, the tenant cells, the runner bindings and the managed store.

That would have been wrong, and §17's first line says so: "Commerce depends on the generic managed Dina runtime but does not define it. The following requirements are vertical integration constraints." Building a hosting product here would be building what the spec explicitly excludes; wiring a ControlPlane seam into Core for a deployment that does not exist would be speculative generality — the same orphan defect in new clothes, which is exactly what the goal asks me to avoid rather than manufacture.

So the WS-8 rows now state the boundary instead of implying an oversight, and what commerce actually owes §17 is asserted for the first time.

§17.5 and §17.1, as structural properties

Checked against the SOURCE rather than against behaviour, because that is the only way to check a negative that has to hold everywhere — a test that called one function and found no LLM would prove nothing about the next one. No reasoning import and no model client anywhere in the aggregate; ranking and the comparison card free of randomness and of the clock, because a ranking that moved between two runs makes §18.4's "why did this win" unanswerable; money as integer minor units and never a float; and no commerce module holding the runtime's own apparatus, which is isolation by ignorance — a module that could resolve a tenant is a module that could resolve the wrong one.

What it found

transformInboundOrderResult read Date.now() inside its logic while every neighbouring module takes nowMs as an argument. That made the D2D ingress the one path where a test could not put the world at a chosen instant — so quote expiry and the age of a withheld decision were untestable there specifically. Now injected, with the default at the seam and nowhere deeper.

Two of my five assertions were wrong on first run, in opposite directions, and both are recorded in the test. Banning tenantId outright flagged credential_broker.ts, where it is one of §8.3's five REQUIRED lease bindings — the scope that makes a narrow credential lease safe, and the opposite of a violation. And a blanket ban on clock reads flagged four legitimate composition defaults because my regex was written from memory and missed the codebase's actual idiom, ?? ((): number => Date.now()). That is the third regex rule of mine this session that was narrower than the code it judged. The lesson is the one the reviewer taught about the leakage classes: measure the shape, do not recall it.

Verification: core 436 suites / 8,175 tests · typecheck 0 · commerce lint clean. The clock rule is mutation-checked. WS-8 rows 8.1, 8.2, 8.3 and 8.5 rewritten from "no production importer" to the actual spec boundary.

Two PART rows were stale — the WBS has now been caught lying in both directions

Re-reading the remaining rows rather than trusting them found two whose stated gap had closed and nobody had gone back:

  • WS-1.8 read "the AppView side (Jetstream ingest, PostgreSQL projection, xRPC search) is unbuilt". All three ship, and this session drove the whole chain end to end against real Postgres with Core's own bytes. → DONE.
  • WS-7.7 read that §16.2's evidence "would be a buyer-side STATUS RECEIPT store, which does not exist". SQLiteBuyerStatusRepository.evidenceChain exists, createCommerceRuntime builds it, and makeHeldEvidenceReader is installed at BOTH composition roots over the runtime's own repositories. → DONE.

That is the same defect this review has spent its whole length on, pointing the other way. WS-8.4 claimed DONE while the rule was dead; these two claimed a gap that was filled. A work-breakdown that can be wrong in both directions is not a record, it is a note — and the only fix is to re-read it against the code rather than read it as the code.

What WS-7.7 was missing after all: a guard

The store and the reader are wired, and nothing failed if either root stopped wiring them. The regression is on file in reconcile_poller.ts: installHeldEvidenceReader was once called only from tests, so every real node presented NO evidence — which makes a supplier's never_received legal, and that is the one answer authorizing a resubmission. §12.7's re-adoption path was inert end to end.

So boundary.test.ts now asserts both roots install it AND build it through makeHeldEvidenceReader, since installing a hand-rolled stand-in is how a node comes to present less than it holds. Mutation-checked against each root independently, which is the point: a behavioural test on one boot passes while the other does nothing.

My first attempt at that guard was itself the defect. I added an exported getHeldEvidenceReader so a boot test could ask "did anything install this" — an export whose only consumer is a test, which is precisely the orphan shape the ledger exists to catch. The ledger caught it within one run. Removed, and replaced with the static both-roots check the sweeper guard beside it already uses. The fix for "is it wired" must not itself be unwired.

Verification: core 436 suites / 8,176 tests · core-server 2,842 · typecheck 0 · commerce lint clean. WBS now 81 DONE / 17 PART / 0 TODO.

WS-10.1 / 10.2 — the §14.4 projection had no consumer, and now does

review-dimensions.ts held the whole of §14.4 — the closed dimension vocabulary, the model-extraction cap, the reviewer-confirmed floor, the commercial-terms scan — with 33 passing tests and NOTHING in AppView calling it. WBS 10.1 said so plainly ("nothing consumes it yet") and 10.2 said nothing at all.

Thirty-three unit tests could not see it, and that is the point worth keeping: unreachability is never visible from inside the thing that is unreachable. It is the same defect as the staff authority, the review dimensions and the catalog listing — a rule that is correct and cannot be asked.

What a buyer actually asks

Not "is this supplier good". A number a buyer cannot interrogate is the thing PeerLens exists to replace, so com.dinakernel.commerce.getSupplierDimensions answers PER DIMENSION: each with its own provenance, its source, and its own mayAffectStandingAlone, plus the spread behind the winner and every refused claim NAMED. An index that silently dropped an out-of-vocabulary category would look identical to a supplier nobody has reviewed.

It computes no score, and a test asserts the response keys to keep it that way. §10.6's argument is that an extractor's confidence and a trust weight tunable in one place is the coupling to avoid; an endpoint returning one blended number would put both dials in one hand. The day someone adds a score field, that test fails and asks them why.

The identity mistake it would have been easy to make

An attestation names a subject ROW; the DID lives on that row. Reading attestations.subject_id as though it were a DID would answer about whoever happened to share the string — the §9.4 identity mistake, one table over. The query joins through subjects, and there is a test that publishes a rival's review and asserts the supplier's answer stays empty.

Verification: AppView 125 files / 2,557 tests against real Postgres; typecheck clean. Mutation-checked — removing the projection kills the three §14.4 rule cases while the plumbing tests stay green, which is the right shape: the rules and the route fail independently.

Two fixture corrections along the way, both mine: subjects requires a name and a type, and attestations requires an author DID, a CID, a raw subject ref and a creation time. Filling them with what a real record carries rather than the minimum that compiles is what makes the test a test of the route.

WS-11.4 route layer and WS-10.5 product conformance

The route layer, because the repository has the scar

Every commerce AppView test so far called a handler with an already-parsed params object, skipping the two things a caller actually meets: the route TABLE, which decides whether the method exists, and the ZOD PARSE, which decides what a query string means. That distinction is not academic here — a catalog-search test was once written against the parse HELPER rather than the server, and reverting the server would have left it green.

commerce_xrpc_route_layer.test.ts goes through the real dispatchXrpc over the real XRPC_ROUTES, with a method id and a query string. Ten scenarios, including the one that matters most: a bound is REFUSED rather than clamped, because silently serving 50 to a caller who asked for 5000 makes a truncated page look complete. And a retailer's whole question end to end over the wire — discovery answers WHO, dimensions answer WHAT IS SAID.

Product identity in the conformance kit

Four of ten families were wired; the fifth is now product. §9.3/§9.4 is the family that decides whether two suppliers are selling the same thing, and a port that gets the arithmetic right and this wrong prices the correct total for the wrong product — a worse failure than a refusal, because nothing downstream notices.

23 cases: equality, variant distinction, scoped-reference acceptance AND refusal, and the frozen refusal STRINGS. The strings are part of the contract rather than decoration — two ports that describe one fault differently make one incident look like two in an operator's log.

My first mutation of this silently did nothing, and I nearly recorded a pass. I searched for a.variant_digest === b.variant_digest; the code reads (a.variant_digest ?? null) === (b.variant_digest ?? null), so the edit never applied and the suite stayed green for the most boring reason there is. A mutation that changes nothing is indistinguishable from a test that catches nothing, and only checking that the anchor existed told them apart. Redone: removing the variant comparison fails same_identifier_different_variant_is_NOT_a_substitute, which is precisely the §9.4 substitute-for-the-ordered-thing failure.

Verification: core 436 suites / 8,176 tests · commerce-protocol 396 · AppView 126 files / 2,567 against real Postgres · typecheck 0. Both mutation-checked. WBS 83 DONE / 15 PART, with 10.5 and 11.4 narrowed rather than closed — five conformance families and the hosted-identity claims remain, and both rows now say which.

WS-10.5 closed — the last five conformance families, and a frozen vector only one implementation could run

The kit went from five wired families to all ten: relationship, search_candidate, schema_evolution, catalog and malformed. Each carries a rule a port can get wrong silently, which is the only kind worth freezing:

  • §10.3 relationship decides what an edge in the product graph MEANS. A port admitting manufactured_by pointed at a product rather than an operator builds a graph whose edges mean two different things, and every traversal after that is arithmetic over a category error.
  • §10.5 search_candidate is the one vector written for a CONSUMER rather than a publisher. A buyer's node reads these from indexes it does not run and cannot audit, so the shape is the whole boundary between a stranger's projection and a buyer's screen.
  • §9.13 schema_evolution is the most consequential. A port that drops unknown fields before canonicalising digests a document nobody sent, and then verifies a signature over something the signer never signed. It looks like tidiness and it is a forgery seam.
  • §10.2 catalog has two digest levels — a page digest over items, a snapshot digest over page digests — and a chain whose refusals are the load-bearing half. A gap is a publication FAULT, not a warning: an index applying sequence 3 after sequence 1 serves a catalog no supplier ever published, assembled out of two that they did. Every record in it is genuine, which is what makes it hard to notice.

The finding: half of malformed was executable by exactly one implementation — ours

Its quote, status and held_evidence cases are MUTATIONS — "set total.minor_units to 1 and expect a refusal" — and the vector file carried no record to set it on. Our own suite built one with makeSignedQuote(), so a port in Go or Rust had nothing to run. A frozen vector only its author can execute is a fixture, not a contract, and the unreachable half was the half that decides whether a tampered quote is caught.

Fixed at the source: the generator now emits the base records into malformed.json, additively — one new base key, every pre-existing case byte-identical (checked, not assumed). The dotted-path mutation is applied by the KIT rather than by each port, because path semantics are exactly the sort of thing two implementations read differently and then disagree about a case neither of them got wrong.

Deviation: two tools were fighting over the frozen bytes

Before regenerating anything I checked that the generator reproduces the committed vectors. It does not — four files drift. The difference is purely formatting (the generator expands short arrays, prettier collapses them; every VALUE is identical, verified per file), but the consequence is not cosmetic: "regenerate and compare" is the check these files exist to support, and it reported a difference every time, so a real divergence would have arrived as noise nobody reads. Worse, catalog_interop_fixture.test.ts compares its fixture byte for byte — it is the only join between Core's publisher and an AppView that takes no @dina/* dependency — and prettier claimed that file too.

Added a .prettierignore for both generated-artefact directories, so their bytes belong to their producer. I did NOT touch the 975 pre-existing prettier failures elsewhere in the repo: that is unrelated debt, and npm run format is not a green gate today independently of this change.

Two test-hygiene fixes this round found in my own work

The required-family list was pinned in three separate expectations, so wiring a family meant editing three assertions that had nothing to do with it. An expectation updated mechanically to make a suite green is an expectation nobody is reading, so REQUIRED_VECTOR_FAMILIES is now exported and pinned once, in the test that exists to pin it; the coverage assertions derive from it.

Added a per-family executed-case COUNT. missingVectorFamilies catches a family that ran zero cases; nothing caught a family that ran one of nine. A wiring bug that iterated the wrong key would report the family as executed and certify a rule it never checked — this codebase's signature defect wearing a green tick.

And one assertion I had to invert. The test read expect(formatReport(report)).toContain('NOT EXECUTED') — correct while six families were unwired, and quietly wrong the moment the last one landed. A test asserting the presence of a caveat passes hardest when the caveat is permanent. It now asserts the caveat is ABSENT, with a separate test keeping the reporting mechanism honest against a synthetic report that does have a gap, because the next family added to vectors/ arrives unwired.

My first mutation of the quote family was wider than its own description. I suppressed any refusal containing "recomputation", which also swallowed the terms-digest refusal ("does not match the canonical terms recomputation") — so it failed three cases while the comment claimed two. I measured the six real refusal strings rather than reasoning about them again, and narrowed it to the exact §9.1 arithmetic clause. A mutation whose blast radius exceeds its description tests something other than what it says it tests.

What the negative tests now cover

Eight, one per rule, each pinning the SPECIFIC case that must fail rather than "something failed". The two sharpest: a port that verifies a quote's signature and trusts its arithmetic — a tampered total travelling under a valid signature, so every downstream party holds cryptographic proof of a number nobody computed — and a port that accepts held evidence with no signature, where a record plus its content digest proves nothing (the digest is a hash of the record, so anyone holding or inventing it computes one) and the recovery path becomes forger-drivable.

Verification: core 436 suites / 8,176 tests · commerce-protocol 407 · AppView 126 files / 2,567 against real Postgres · typecheck 0 · commerce lint clean. All five new families mutation-checked. WBS unchanged at 83 DONE / 15 PART, and deliberately so: 10.5 stays PART even though every buildable part of it is now finished, because what remains is that no actual third party has run the kit. Marking it DONE would be reading readiness off our own tests, which is the one thing §23 forbids. The row now states the gap as an external dependency rather than as work.

Row 3.8 was the FOURTH stale row — and the third stale in the same direction

I flagged 3.8 two rounds ago as possibly stale because its cell showed no stated gap, and said I would read it against the code before believing it either way. It is stale. The cell describes a complete implementation and ends on "2 further mutations killed" — it names nothing outstanding at all, and was still marked PART.

Verified against the code, not the row: serving_manifest_cid, buildContinuityEnvelope, lifecycle_continuity_unavailable, countReservedByServingManifest (present in BOTH repos, as claimed) and transformInboundOrderResult all exist; the transformer is wired in packages/home-node/src/workflow_plane.ts, the shared composition helper, so both boots get it; and admission.ts carries a real counterproposal branch rather than a mention of one. continuity_release.test.ts and provider_ingress.test.ts are 80 tests green.

A stale PART is not harmless. It is indistinguishable from work somebody still owes, so it sends the next reader off to re-derive a conclusion that was already reached — and it makes the remaining count wrong in the direction that hides real progress. Three of the four stale rows found so far lied this way; one lied the other way, claiming DONE over dead code, which is worse. Both are the same defect: a record of the work that nothing checks against the work.

WBS now 84 DONE / 14 PART / 0 TODO. Of the 14: four are stated boundaries rather than unfinished work (8.1/8.2/8.3/8.5 — §17 excludes hosting from commerce — plus 5.1 and 11.5); four need infrastructure or a decision from you (9.2 demo Odoo, 3.6 a provisioned PDS, 10.4 a second live AppView, 11.6's real-ERP half); 10.5 needs an external party; and three have buildable work left (11.2, 11.3, 11.4).

Row 11.4's gap was the FIFTH stale claim — and the WBS now has a test

11.4 listed "relationship-claim ingest against Postgres" as open. tests/integration/commerce_relationship_ingest.test.ts already does exactly that: it drives commerceRelationshipClaimHandler.handleCreate/handleDelete against the real database, 8 scenarios, including §10.7's rule with teeth — two claims naming different parents land on ONE edge marked disputed with BOTH claims KEPT, because deleting the loser is the silent merge the spec forbids, and a merge is irreversible in practice: once two identities are one row, the reviews, order history and lineage of both are indistinguishable and no later evidence separates them. Verified by running it, not by reading the filename. 11.4's only residual is the hosted-identity claims, which are the same WS-8 boundary as 8.1/8.2/8.3/8.5.

Five stale rows is not five mistakes, it is one missing mechanism. The WBS is the record a reader trusts when deciding what is left to build, and nothing has ever checked it against anything — the same defect the commerce code kept producing, one level up. So wbs_hygiene.test.ts now checks the two things that are actually decidable, chosen because they are the two failures observed: a PART row that names NO gap (row 3.8's exact failure), and a cited repo path that does not exist. Both mutation-killed: flipping a DONE row to PART with its notes blanked fails the first, renaming a cited file fails the second, and each fails its OWN test rather than both.

My first version of the gap rule flagged six innocent rows. It matched only Open:, and rows say "Still open:", "stays open", "out of commerce's scope". That is the FOURTH time this session a pattern of mine has been narrower than the thing it judged. The rule is deliberately broad now: a doc check that cries wolf gets deleted, and then nothing checks the document at all — so it catches the row that says NOTHING rather than policing how a gap is phrased. What it cannot catch is 11.4's failure, a gap stated but already closed; no mechanical rule reaches that, and pretending otherwise would buy a check that passes while the document drifts.

Also fixed while measuring: docs/conformance.md was cited three times and exists under BOTH packages/commerce-protocol/ and packages/protocol/. Now disambiguated, so the citation names the file it means.

WS-11.2 — "secrets never enter AppView", and the leak it found

Design decision: the claim had to be interpreted before it could be tested

§25.2's sentence cannot be taken at face value, and working out why is most of the value here. AppView does not ingest from Core — it ingests from a firehose, and anybody may publish anything to their own PDS. A guarantee that holds only when every publisher is honest is a guarantee about publishers, not about AppView. And AppView cannot classify a stranger's string as a secret without inventing a requirement nothing can meet.

So the sentence splits into two claims with different owners:

  • Dina never SENDS secrets — Core's §12.1 publication gate. This is the load-bearing half, and the only one that protects the OWNER's data. Already covered.
