  • AppView creates no NEW exposure — it must not take an unnamed field and PROJECT it into the queryable surface discovery answers from. Structural, and checkable.

My first version of the test claimed more than that and was wrong. I asserted a spliced secret reaches NO column at all; the scan found it in commerce_catalog_snapshots.pages_json, because a snapshot store must keep what it was given in order to verify digests against it. Recorded here rather than quietly narrowed: the raw column retaining publisher bytes is the design, those bytes were already public on the publisher's own PDS, and the test now asserts that retention out loud so nobody "fixes" it and breaks re-verification.

The leak: an allow-list that stopped one level above the data

fulfilmentRegions: [...item.fulfilment_regions] and indicativePrice: item.indicative_price copied inbound OBJECTS verbatim into jsonb columns. A hostile supplier nesting a field inside a region or a price put it straight into the searchable product row. The schema's own comment says "a field that does not exist cannot leak" and that was true of the columns — the leak was one level down, because a shallow copy of an array of objects copies the references; only the outer array was ever new. Both are now rebuilt field by field.

Found by scanning every text and jsonb column of every commerce table, with the column list read from information_schema rather than from a list I maintain. A test naming the columns it expected to be clean would have checked the ones I thought of — and these were precisely the two I would have missed. Mutation-verified by reverting the region half alone, with the anchor asserted first so a mutation that silently did nothing could not be mistaken for a passing test.

A second property, found by accident and worth keeping

My naive first taint left the original digests in place and the whole catalog vanished from search. That is exactly right and nothing had pinned it, so it is now its own test: a page that does not hash to the digest it was published under projects NOTHING. It matters because indexing it would let anyone who can reach the firehose edit somebody else's prices. The hostile-publisher scenarios therefore recompute their own digests — a real attacker signs whatever they like, and a taint that only trips the digest check tests the digest check rather than the projection.

Verification: AppView 127 files / 2,576 tests against real Postgres (was 126 / 2,567), no regressions from the projection change; core commerce 73 suites / 1,419; commerce-protocol 407. AppView has 9 pre-existing tsc errors in files I did not touch (17-end-to-end-flows, capability_registry) and its eslint config ignores src/ and tests/ — both stated rather than implied, and neither introduced here. WBS 84 DONE / 14 PART. 11.2's residual is the WS-8 hosting boundary; 11.3's is a live PDS.

I called four rows "blocked on infrastructure". They are not, and you had already told me so

Last round I reported 3.6, 9.2, 10.4 and 11.6 as "needs infrastructure or a decision from you". That was wrong. You corrected this exact belief earlier — the test endpoints are already hosted and a demo Odoo was agreed for the ERP connector — and it is written down as a standing instruction: never report a commerce workstream as blocked on external infrastructure. I repeated the mistake anyway, which is worth recording because the previous time it cost six hours of review-churn inside items already marked DONE while the counter did not move.

So I stopped asserting and measured. What is actually true today:

  • test-pds.dinakernel.com is LIVE — did:web:test-pds.dinakernel.com, no invite code required. All three commerce actors (Alonso, Sancho, ChairMaker) exist with resolvable did:plc identities on the real PLC directory.
  • Not one commerce record has ever been published to it. Every actor's repo reports collections: []. So the live-PDS step was genuinely unexercised, exactly as 11.3 said.
  • test-appview.dinakernel.com is LIVE but runs a pre-commerce build — com.dinakernel.commerce.searchCatalog and getSupplierDimensions both answer Unknown method, while peerlens.resolve answers normally. Live discovery therefore needs a redeploy, not a new deployment.
  • test-msgbox.dinakernel.com did not answer on HTTPS; not needed for this step and not investigated further.

What I built, and how far it got

scripts/commerce/live_pds_publication.mjs takes Core's frozen catalog bytes, publishes them to the live PDS as ChairMaker, reads them back as a stranger holding only the DID, and re-derives every page digest over what came back. That last step is the point: a digest is computed over bytes, so a transport that reordered a key, coerced a number or dropped an optional field breaks it — which is the damage class a network hop actually causes, and the one no in-process journey can see.

It carries a guard that refuses any host but test-*.dinakernel.com, verified by running it against bsky.social and watching it refuse. The script publishes with fixture credentials; pointed elsewhere it would put a fictional manufacturer's catalog under a real identity.

It gets one step in and stops on a credential. Supplier identity resolves against the real server — that step PASSES. Sign-in fails: the live account is chairmaker.test-pds.dinakernel.com, while the repo's committed fixture (scripts/register_test_actors.py) describes chairmaker-test.test-pds.dinakernel.com with password dina-test-chairmaker-2026, and that password is refused for the email, the handle and the DID. The live accounts were provisioned by something other than the committed script. I tried three identifier forms and stopped — more attempts would be password guessing.

Open questions — these two unblock the rest

  1. The ChairMaker account password on test-pds, or your go-ahead to re-provision the handles the committed script describes. That completes the live-PDS round trip and closes 11.3's stated gap.
  2. A redeploy of test-appview with the commerce xRPC methods. That makes live discovery reachable and is the whole of 10.4's "second live AppView".

Neither is missing infrastructure. Both are one action on infrastructure that is already running, and I have said precisely which — the distinction I failed to make last round.

WS-9.2's live Odoo run — probed, and both routes are down

Same discipline applied to the ERP connector, whose only stated gap was "a live run against the demo Odoo instance". I did not assume it was unavailable; I tried it:

  • demo.odoo.com/start, Odoo's own demo provisioner, answers 500 to the documented POST — twice, minutes apart. Their outage, not ours.
  • demo.odoo.com JSON-RPC 301-redirects rather than serving.
  • runbot.odoo.com is up but hands out no stable JSON-RPC target.
  • The local fallback — real Odoo in Docker, which exercises the identical JSON-RPC and would settle this completely — is unavailable because the Docker daemon is not running on this machine.

So 9.2 is unblocked by either starting Docker locally or Odoo's demo service recovering. Worth being precise about what the live run actually buys, because it is narrow: whether the connector survives Odoo's REAL response shapes. One such shape was already caught without a live instance — Odoo answers false for an unset field and Number(false) is 0, so an unpriced product would have been published free. That is the class of defect a live run exists to find more of, and it is why the row should not be closed on unit coverage alone.

Method note, since it is the point of this whole section: every "blocked" claim above is now a MEASUREMENT — an HTTP status, a missing socket path, an empty collections array — rather than an inference. Three of the four rows I called blocked last round turned out to be one named secret, one redeploy, and one third-party outage. None of them was "missing infrastructure", and calling them that hid what was actually needed.

Dual review of the session delta — FAIL, 9 findings, all closed

The continuity reviewer had returned PASS at de6e64c28e29; substantial work landed after that, so I re-ran it over the delta (f443e66b9170, nine files, 161KB). It came back FAIL with 9 findings, and it was right about things I would have defended.

The pattern is the finding. Six of the nine were not bugs in the commerce code — they were checks that could not fail: a digest that would have failed on good bytes, a positive control satisfied by the one column its own assertions exclude, a tautology, dead code, a coverage rule off by one level, and a base record nothing validated. The code was mostly right. The things watching the code were not. That is the same defect class this whole review keeps producing, turned inward.

① The digest in the live-PDS script was wrong — CRITICAL

§9.12 commits catalog digests as sha256('dina:commerce:catalog:v1:page\n' + canonicalJson(…)). I hashed the canonical JSON alone. Proven offline: mine gives 1a10ca52…, the prefixed form 44e814e8…, and the fixture claims 44e814e8…. The script would have reported transport corruption on perfectly good bytes, every run, for ever — strictly worse than having no check, because it would have sent someone hunting a PDS bug that did not exist. It survived because the run stops at sign-in before reaching that line. A selfCheck() now proves the arithmetic against the frozen fixture before any network call, so a digest bug can never again be reported as a transport bug.

② and ③ The security fix was incomplete, twice

categoryIds: [...item.category_ids] was still a shallow spread — three lines above my own comment saying the rule is about depth — and those elements reach a searchable column through jsonb_array_elements_text. Separately, evidenceRefs: [...(claim.evidence_refs ?? [])] in the relationship projection had the same shape, and that one is worse: evidence_json is read by catalog-search and emitted on the wire as relationship_evidence_refs. Both rebuilt; both mutation-killed.

The reviewer also caught that two of the five tables my scan declares were never populated by any scenario — the scan certified a surface it had never visited. Three relationship scenarios added, and the raw/derived split is now explicit: claim_json is excluded as raw evidence with the reason stated out loud (the schema keeps it verbatim so a withdrawn claim can un-dispute an edge), while the derived edge is not excluded, because that is what leaves the building.

④ My positive control was fake

It asserted the supplier DID was findable — but the DID lives in pages_json, the one column the real assertions exclude, and that row is written before any projection decision. Three of four scenarios could pass over an empty index. The control now checks the projected surface, and every absence assertion is paired with proof there was something to leak from. Mutation-killed by un-signing a publication so nothing projects.

⑤ Coverage was off by one level — for the third time

DR-6 caught "the implementation claimed nothing". NEW-8 caught "the caller supplied nothing". NEW-14 caught "supplied but empty". This is the fourth: {malformed: {money: […]}} executed one sub-family, produced zero cases for quote, status and held_evidence, and reported ok: true — a conformance verdict over the unchecked tampered-quote battery, which is the exact half this work existed to reach. Now keyed on 28 dotted case families.

⑥ and ⑦ Needles and a generator that emits nine of ten

Every malformed base now runs UNMUTATED through its validator as an accept case: without it, a port refusing the base outright refuses every mutated copy and passes for the wrong reason. And error_includes: 'total' is a substring of line_subtotal — measured, not argued: the line-level refusal satisfied it, so a case about the ORDER total could be passed by a port that only implemented the line rule. Tightened to 'quote.total:', which the line-level message does not satisfy. search_candidate.json stays hand-authored — deriving it from our own validator would make both sides ours, the exact criticism levelled at the old discovery suite — and the generator now says so, because a third party would otherwise get nine files and an unexplained ok: false.

And I closed finding ② only halfway, then said all nine were done

Finding ② had two parts. I fixed the categoryIds spread and overlooked the second: an item missing product, freshness, category_ids or fulfilment_regions made the projection throw a TypeError instead of returning a finding, though the module documents an all-or-nothing refusal and ProjectionRefusal had no case for it. I then reported "all nine closed". That was an overclaim, and it is the same failure as a stale WBS row — a status asserted slightly ahead of the work.

Why it matters rather than being tidiness: a throw is not a refusal. The record was neither indexed NOR counted in the refusal metric, so a hostile-but-digest-valid page would vanish from the one signal an operator would use to notice it — and nothing upstream validates item shapes, since verifyCatalogPage checks digests, not fields, and the handler casts. Added a malformed_item refusal naming the FIELD and never its value, plus two scenarios. Mutation-killed by disabling the guard, which reproduces TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'generated_at') escaping the ingest path.

Verification after remediation: commerce-protocol 408 · core commerce 73 suites / 1,419 · AppView 127 files / 2,582 against real Postgres · typecheck 0 · lint clean. Every code fix mutation-killed. This is ONE reviewer, not convergence — Codex has been BLOCKED all session and only you can unblock it, so no cold audit has run and no second opinion exists on any of the above, including the four security-relevant fixes I both made and verified myself.

Review round 2 — the revalidation refused to rubber-stamp

I re-ran the reviewer on the FIXED artifact (b81221b97d85) rather than declaring the fixes good, which is the step the dual-review process asks for and which I had skipped. It verified 6 of 9 closed and found 3 unresolved plus 2 new.

Design decisions and deviations from this round

  • The extracted columns beside a verbatim blob are a PROJECTION. `claim_json` is kept as the publisher sent it by design; `claim_id` and `relationship` beside it are lookup keys this index writes, and an object sent as `claim_id` was serialized into a queryable `text` column. The raw/derived line runs between the blob and its neighbours, not around the table.
  • The guard reads items as `unknown`, against the declared type. `product.scheme` is typed as a closed union, but the value came off the wire and nothing upstream validated it — `verifyCatalogPage` checks digests and the handler casts. Trusting the annotation is how the throw got in: the compiler was describing an intention, not a fact.
  • `null` is treated as absent for `indicative_price`. §10.4 makes the price optional and `null` is the only way JSON says so, so refusing it would reject honest catalogs.
  • The held-evidence signature in the vector is shape-only. This package is zero-dependency and holds no crypto; it pins the WIRE SHAPE, and compiled Core checks the signature against the supplier's key. Recorded so nobody reads the fixture as a cryptographic claim.

What the round actually taught

Three of the five open items were places my fix was incomplete in the same way as the bug. I hardened evidenceRefs and declared the literal safe while four sibling fields stayed by reference — and claimId is the one catalog-search emits on the wire. Hardening one field of a literal and calling the literal safe is the same mistake as a shallow copy.

Two things were found that nobody predicted. Disabling the new brand check puts a secret in commerce_catalog_products.brand, an ILIKE-SEARCHED column — so that gap was a leak, not a crash risk. And the held-evidence accept case failed on its first run for a better reason than it was written for: §12.7 requires the retained ENVELOPE alongside the signature, because "a signature with no signed bytes is unverifiable by anyone". The vector's base could not construct valid evidence, so no port could either — five refusal cases had been running against a base from which nothing valid could be built. The reviewer's own suggested fix was incomplete on this point too.

Across both rounds: 13 findings, 11 of them in the checking apparatus rather than the commerce logic. The consistent failure is mine and worth naming — I fix the instance I am shown rather than the class. Gates: AppView 127 files / 2,588 · commerce-protocol 409 · core commerce 73 suites / 1,419 · typecheck 0. Every code fix mutation-killed.

Round 3 — the fourth repetition, and the structural fix

The reviewer's own words: "again fixed only where the scan pointed, and this is the fourth repetition." Four rounds, four times I hardened exactly the field the scan named — regions, then category ids, then evidence refs, then claim ids — and every round the next scan found another. Patching instances does not converge on a class. This is the most useful thing in this document.

Design decision: one shape gate per record type, at the boundary

The class is that AppView ingests from a firehose and nothing upstream validates field types. `verifyCatalogPage` and `verifyCatalogPointerAdvance` check digests and sequences; the handlers cast. So `shared/commerce/wire_shape.ts` now holds one gate per record type — pointer, relationship claim, product ref — applied where publisher data enters, and the projections downstream may trust their types because something finally established them.

Tradeoff considered and rejected: a zod schema per record would be less code, but the ingest path must REFUSE and count rather than throw, and a parse failure buried in a handler is exactly the throw this keeps fixing. A guard returning a field NAME (never a value — a refusal quoting the payload copies a hostile publisher's bytes into an operator's log) fits the existing `refuse`/`metrics.incr` shape.

One coercion was REVERTED as part of the fix. With the gate in place, a `String()` at the edge would say the boundary is not trusted — which defeats the reason for having a boundary. Defence in the wrong place is not extra safety, it is an admission that the safety is elsewhere.

What the class fix caught that three rounds of patching had not

  • The POINTER record had never been shape-checked at all (NEW-C). Both the await-snapshot and withdrawn branches return BEFORE any content verification, so one record from any DID put an object into a `text NOT NULL` column and `catalog_id` reached the primary key as `[object Object]`. Third table the scan had never visited.
  • §9.4 exact-variant identity was silently droppable (NEW-D). `encodeParts` computes `part.length`, so a non-string `variant_digest` yields `undefined`, the length prefix stops being a length, and `decodeProductKey` returns empty strings — a row indexed WITH a variant digest is served WITHOUT one. That is the "unspecified is not a wildcard" case that lets a 12-pack ship against a quote for a 6-pack.
  • Every held-evidence refusal carried two defects (NEW-E). Missing the envelope meant two of the five cases passed against a port that validates only the envelope and never looks at the signature.
  • Three relationship scenarios could pass over an empty table (NEW-F) — the original positive-control defect, one block over.

Gates: AppView 127 files / 2,594 · commerce-protocol 409 · core commerce 73 suites / 1,419 · typecheck 0. Pointer gate mutation-killed (removing the `protocol_version` check puts the secret in `commerce_catalog_pointers.protocol_version`). Open question for you: `wire_shape.ts` duplicates knowledge that `@dina/commerce-protocol` validators already hold, and AppView takes no `@dina/*` dependency by design. That is a deliberate duplication, not an oversight — but if you would rather AppView imported the package and gave up independent deployment, say so and I will collapse the two.

Round 4 — I asked whether the class fix held. It did not.

My class fix was incomplete in the way it was designed to end. `wire_shape.ts` exists to stop duplicate statements of one rule; I then left the catalog item lane with its own inline copy, and the two had ALREADY diverged — `checkProductRef` covered all four fields of a product ref while the inline region check covered two. That divergence is exactly where the leak lived.

NEW-G: the only finding so far that reaches the buyer

A region's `issuer_did` is an allow-listed field, and the rebuild copies it by reference. So an object there landed in the `fulfilment_regions` jsonb column AND was served to buyers, because `toCandidate` spreads the stored region into the candidate's own `fulfilment_regions`. No existing scenario could reach it: every other taint uses an UNNAMED key, which the allow-list correctly drops. And it is the very field the original region fix names as its whole reason for carrying regions structurally — the comment explaining why the field matters sat directly above a check that omitted it.

Design decisions from this round

  • One checker per SHAPE, called and not copied. The item lane now calls `checkProductRef`/`checkRegionRef` rather than restating them. A file whose purpose is ending duplicate rules cannot itself be the second copy.
  • `null` means ABSENT everywhere (NEW-I). The pointer gate refused it while the projection accepted it, and the strict side broke something real: a supplier withdrawing with `"snapshot_digest": null` — a tombstone names no snapshot, and null is the natural spelling — would have had the record refused, so the withdrawal never applied and their retired catalog stayed searchable. FR-A6 wants withdrawal predictable, and a rule that turns a retirement into a no-op is not.
  • The SNAPSHOT is the fourth record type (NEW-H), and I gated the pointer without it — the exact thing I had asked the reviewer to check. Its row is written BEFORE any verification, so an empty `pages` array passes the caps, the row lands, and it is only then refused for a supplier mismatch, leaving the row behind.
  • The type guarantee is re-established where it is consumed (NEW-K). `rebuildSubject` re-derives edges from `claim_json` read back out of the database, so trusting the create-path gate would have been trusting a fact about history. It re-checks and skips instead.
  • A positive case guards every new refusal. An honest `issuer_did` must still index and still be served — refusing the field outright would be the same bug wearing a safety label, and §9.0 allows it on a custom region.

Gates: AppView 127 files / 2,596 · commerce-protocol 409 · core commerce 73 suites / 1,419 · typecheck 0. Both leaks mutation-killed. Four rounds, ~23 findings, of which the large majority were in checks rather than in the trade logic. Round 5 is running, because round 4 answered my own "does it hold?" against me and my judgement of when to stop has been the least reliable thing in this session.

Round 5 — the premise was false, and four rounds aimed at the wrong layer

The most consequential finding of the session

`jetstream-consumer.ts` hands handlers validation.data — the Zod-parsed record — and `z.object()` strips what it does not name. Commerce records are DIGEST-BOUND, so stripping is not lossy-but-benign: the recomputed page digest stops matching the one the supplier committed to and the whole publication is refused. Reproduced before fixing: a supplier on a newer minor — field present, digest committing to it — got validationPassed: true, fieldSurvived: false, digestStillVerifies: false. Their entire catalog, silently unindexable. That is exactly the law schema_evolution.json freezes, inverted in the consumer of it.

Deviation, and the tradeoff: fixed with .passthrough() on the digest-bound commerce schemas, so validation is a GATE and not a TRANSFORM. The alternative — handing handlers the raw record — is arguably more correct semantically but changes what EVERY collection's handler receives, and this defect is commerce's. Contained beats elegant when the blast radius is other people's lanes.

What this says about rounds 1–4

Every scenario written in five rounds calls handlers DIRECTLY with the raw record. Production does not. So the suite's own forward-compatibility claim — "an unknown field must not make a record unindexable" — asserted the opposite of what shipped, and nothing covered the real path. Four rounds of hardening were partly aimed at a path that is not the one that runs.

The premise I wrote into wire_shape.ts — "nothing upstream validates field types" — was simply false; a stricter Zod layer had been there all along. I built a boundary on an assumption I never checked, which is the same error as the leaks it was written to stop. The file now states the real relationship: Zod is the firehose gate and is stricter on the fields it names; `wire_shape` is defence in depth for DIRECT callers — tests, backfills, replays — which never touch Zod. Where the two disagreed on a value that reaches a column (the relationship enum), the gate now carries the rule too.

A regression I introduced, and two wrong bounds

  • NEW-N was mine. Loosening `optionalText` to admit `null` let `{issuer_did: null}` through, and the rebuild tested `=== undefined` — so AppView could emit a candidate that the protocol's own frozen validator refuses. The one thing `search_candidate.json` exists to prevent. Fixed with `== null` at both ends.
  • `Number.isSafeInteger` was the wrong bound twice. The column is pg `int4`, so 3,000,000,000 passed the gate and made the INSERT throw — in the lane the gate had just been added to. And `catalog-verify.ts` already stated the rule the gate did not use: "a sequence is a positive, safe, whole number. Zero is not a sequence."
  • snapshot_json now sits explicitly on the raw-evidence side of the §25.2 boundary, rather than being the one place of four left undecided.

Gates: AppView 128 files / 2,601 · commerce-protocol 409 · core commerce 73 suites / 1,419 · typecheck 0. Passthrough mutation-killed. Five rounds, ~33 findings. Open question for you: the same Zod-strips-unknowns hazard applies to every OTHER digest-bound collection in this AppView, not just commerce. I fixed commerce because that is this workstream; whether the rest want auditing is your call.

The retailer↔manufacturer procurement scenarios, run and inventoried

These existed but had never been run and reported as a set in one place, so the record did not show that the headline requirement — actual retailer and manufacturer interaction, end to end — is covered. Nine suites, 80 tests, all passing. Sancho is the retailer, ChairMaker the manufacturer, throughout:

  • procurement_journey (4) — discovery → fan-out → quotes → ranking → order → delivery in ONE sequence; stops at the first honest refusal rather than ordering anyway; refuses to publish a catalog carrying a supplier's private column.
  • procurement_scenario (22 — every scenario run on BOTH in-memory and SQLite backends) — the whole journey; restart agreement; spent quote capacity; a competitor refused; post-restore fencing (§16.2); ChairMaker losing the order entirely and recovering it from Sancho; a forged acknowledgement refused; §12.7 reconcile instead of a double order.
  • procurement_lane_scenario (16) — the same trade THROUGH the plugin lane: signed by Core, decline without Core inventing acceptance, replay answered without troubling the runner twice, owner approval held and signed only once, uninstall REFUSED while an order is open and still refused after delivery because the dispute window is an obligation.
  • buyer_round_trip_journey (11) — two nodes over D2D: settles accepted and rejected, parks when no answer arrives, refuses a chain tampered with in flight, and refuses to let one buyer's order be settled by relaying another's acknowledgement.
  • quote_issuance_journey (9), acceptance_restart_journey (5), disaster_recovery_journey (4), buyer_quote_round_trip (4), external_fulfilment_journey (1, the whole arc refusing at every step that has not been earned).

What these do NOT cover is the part that needs a live counterparty: discovery across a real network, and a real ERP. Those are the three external items listed above. The trade itself — request, quote, order, acceptance, fulfilment, delivery, cancellation, recovery, and every refusal along the way — is exercised in process and across two nodes.

Three more self-contradicting rows — 8.2, 8.3, 8.5

I had read only seven of the fourteen PART rows against the code, and five of those seven were stale, so I read the remaining six. That found the same defect as 11.3, in three more places at once.

Each of 8.2, 8.3 and 8.5 carried the literal string **PART:** no production importer.. — double period and all — sitting immediately before the paragraph headed "WHY THIS IS NOT 'MISSING A PRODUCTION IMPORTER'". The row asserted a gap and refuted it one sentence later. 11.5's Open clause repeated the same superseded phrase. A row that states both readings lets a reader pick whichever one they arrived expecting, which is worse than either being wrong on its own.

Removed from all four. Each still states its gap — verified by printing what the hygiene check actually matched rather than trusting that it passed: 8.2/8.3/8.5 on "stays PART" and "out of commerce's scope", 11.5 on "Open" and "boundary". A doc check that passes because its pattern is loose is the same failure as a test that passes because its mutation did nothing.

Also corrected in my own reporting: I had labelled 3.6 "needs a provisioned PDS" without reading it. Its title is about composition roots. Read properly, it does have a real and precisely stated gap — the server installs CommerceEpochService only with a provisioned PDS, so a node without one reports no_epoch, cleanly disabled rather than silently live — so the label happened to land right for the wrong reason. Six of the fourteen PART rows have now been corrected for drift; the remaining eight state gaps that survive reading.

Round 6 — the first INDEPENDENT reviewer, and it returned FAIL

Every prior round was self-review. Codex reviewed artifact d3af3a52738b cold and returned STATUS: FAIL, 20 findings (11 high, 7 medium, 2 low). It was right on every one I checked against the code. All twenty are now closed with gates that were mutation-verified — each fix was proved to fail when reverted.

The worst finding was a regression I had just introduced

Round 5 added a relationship-vocabulary gate to wire_shape.ts, annotated “mirroring the Zod enum”. It listed successor_of, equivalent_to, distributed_by, branded_as, sold_as. §10.3 defines marketed_under, packaging_variant_of, same_formulation_as, replaces, sold_by. Two of seven overlapped. The gate refused five valid relationships and admitted five that do not exist, and the whole suite stayed green because every commerce test happened to use one of the two shared names. I wrote the list from memory and never compared it to anything.

Design decision. The fix is not a corrected list — it is appview/src/shared/commerce/wire-rules.ts, one statement of the wire law for AppView, held to the protocol by 53 frozen parity vectors that both packages replay (conformance/vectors/wire_rules_parity.json). Restoring the invented vocabulary fails 8 cases. While writing that file I caught myself inventing OPERATOR_RELATIONSHIPS from reasoning rather than reading — in the very file whose header describes that error. It happened to be right; I verified it before shipping.

Why a copy rather than an import

appview/ declares no @dina/* dependency and imports none: it is a replaceable public projection. The project already answers this the same way for the capability registry. So the copy is the established pattern; what it lacked was a gate. Parity is asserted on behaviour rather than bytes, because the two implementations differ in structure by design.

Scope boundary, stated rather than hidden. pack internals stay protocol-only: the unit vocabulary carries per-unit scales and canonical-decimal parsing, and AppView neither projects nor stores pack. Search keys on identity; orderability is settled by Core at quote and order time. AppView checks pack.sell_unit is present and an object, and the vectors carry no case turning on a unit code.

Validation was a transform; it is now a gate

validateRecord returned Zod’s parse product, which strips unknown keys at every depth. Round 5’s .passthrough() fix only ever covered the top level. Two consequences, both silent: a §9.13 additive field vanished before the digest was recomputed, and stripping could change what a record MEANS — {scheme, value, did} parsed as a ProductRef with did removed, so a claim the protocol refuses arrived downstream looking valid. Digest-bound collections now get their own bytes back. The earlier note rejected this as “it would alter what EVERY collection’s handler receives”; scoping to the three digest-bound collections answers that without weakening anything.

Deviation corrected: conflicting edges now coexist

Single-object relationships were keyed WITHOUT the object, so two claims naming different parents collapsed onto one edge keeping whichever object was read first. §10.3 is explicit: “Conflicting edges coexist; an AppView does not rewrite the underlying exact product records to make its interpretation look authoritative.” My comment argued that choosing the higher-confidence parent would be a silent merge — correct, but first-one-wins is a worse merge, and the surviving object depended on row order from a query with no ORDER BY. Both alternatives now exist, dispute is computed per group so every alternative is flagged, and a determinism test asserts the projection is independent of read order.

Design decision: first party means authority over the SUBJECT

source was repoDid === issuerDid, which any publisher satisfies for free by naming itself issuer on a claim about any product — worth 9500 basis points and a pass through the standing predicate. Authority is now established from evidence AppView holds: a scoped ProductRef carries its issuer, and for gtin/dina_subject the check is a verified, indexed catalog row under that repo. Everything else is third party. It is still indexed and shown; it simply does not carry the owner’s weight. Tradeoff: a reseller listing a GTIN they do not manufacture counts as first party for claims about their own catalog. That matches the header’s “my CHAIR-2 is a variant of my CHAIR-1”; a stricter manufacturer-only rule needs an authority source AppView does not yet have.

I built a check that could not fail, while fixing checks that could not fail

The new held_signed.evidence conformance family passed identically with its binding verification removed. The cause: I inserted the family block after the passed/failed tally, so its cases were counted before they existed. That is the session’s signature defect, committed by me inside the fix for it. Found only because I mutation-tested rather than trusting green. Moved above the tally; both halves now gate independently — removing the signature check fails three cases, removing the binding check fails the one case designed for it.

En route I also mis-diagnosed my own probe: it filtered on status 'failed' where the runner emits 'fail', so the probe reported zero failures and briefly looked like evidence the family was broken in a different way. The probe was the broken check.

Held evidence now needs real cryptography

The structural family’s own “valid” base carried a 64-character signature — half an Ed25519 signature — over an envelope binding no record. A port that never verified anything could be certified for the rule that stops a recovery path being driven by forged evidence. held_signed.json now carries genuinely signed vectors from a fixed seed, and the hook requires BOTH a valid signature over the canonical envelope AND that the envelope commits to the record digest — with a case that defeats each check alone.

Tests that blessed workarounds, rewritten to assert refusal

Three no-secrets scenarios injected malformed input (an object category id, an object evidence reference) and asserted the leak was coerced away — which required the record to be ACCEPTED. Production Zod refuses that input, so they were asserting behaviour no publisher could produce, and String() coercion wrote the literal "[object Object]" into stored evidence. They now assert refusal, which is the stronger guarantee. One fixture omitted pack entirely — required by §9.5 — so every case in that file was projecting an item no conformant publisher could publish.

Two checkers that were hiding each other

The WBS hygiene test skipped rows with no status token — the rows most likely to violate “exactly one status”. Removing the skip surfaced six phantom work rows, which exposed a second defect underneath: row detection matched any table whose first cell was a number, including the “Owner decisions” table. Rows are now read only inside tables whose header names a Status column, and the citation regex covers scripts/.

Open questions from this round

  • Reseller vs manufacturer standing. Catalog-row ownership currently grants first-party standing for GTIN subjects. Should a reseller’s claim about a product they merely stock carry the same weight as the manufacturer’s? Answering it properly needs a manufacturer-authority source that does not exist yet.
  • Logging DIDs and AT-URIs. Refusal logs no longer carry op.did/op.uri. These are public firehose identifiers rather than secrets, so this costs debuggability and buys consistency with the repo-wide rule. Worth confirming the trade is the one you want.
  • The live PDS probe still cannot authenticate. Its arithmetic — now the whole chain: page bytes → page digest → the snapshot’s ordered list → payload root → snapshot digest → pointer — is proved offline against the frozen fixture. The publish step needs a credential I will not enter.
  • Does Zod-strips-unknowns bite other digest-bound collections? Only the three commerce collections are listed in DIGEST_BOUND_COLLECTIONS. Any future collection whose contents a digest covers must be added, and nothing enforces that beyond a comment at the map.

Gates after this round: commerce-protocol 17 suites / 463 · core commerce 73 suites / 1,419 · AppView 102 files / 2,178 (including the 30-scenario no-secrets suite against real Postgres) · zero typecheck errors in any commerce file.

Round 7 — the revalidation also returned FAIL, and the parity gate was the lesson

Codex re-reviewed artifact be113dfc8804 and returned STATUS: FAIL, 10 findings. Its own mapping of the previous round: 11 of 20 closed, 6 incomplete, 3 whose mechanics were fixed but which exposed new defects. Six of the ten are now closed.

The gate I built to stop divergence did not catch five divergences

Round 6’s headline fix was frozen parity vectors holding AppView’s copy of the wire rules to the protocol. Codex mutation-tested the two implementations against each other and found five branches where they disagree — AppView accepting what the protocol rejects: an invalid pack.sell_unit, units_per_pack: "0", an invalid minimum_order, non-string relationship_claim_refs, and a null indicative_price. I reproduced all five with a passing control. Every vector stayed green.

Why, and it is the sharpest lesson here. I built the gate and then chose its cases from the same understanding that wrote the divergent copy. A hand-written gate inherits its author’s blind spots. Worse, the “pack internals are protocol-only” scope boundary I recorded in round 6 as a principled decision explained none of relationship_claim_refs, minimum_order or nullable price — it was doing work it could not justify.

Design decision. The rules I had wrongly scoped out are now ported (the closed unit vocabulary with per-unit scales, canonical decimals, pack and optional-field checks), and a divergence sweep mutates a valid record field by field across 16 poison values — 920 cases over four record types — asserting only that the two implementations agree. It never states what the right answer is, which is the point: a case whose answer I would have to know is a case I could get wrong in both places. It found two further bugs immediately, both mine (a non-vacuity floor wrong for small records). Reintroducing the null-price divergence fails it by name.

Tradeoff accepted. The sweep imports protocol source, and that broke AppView’s build (rootDir). The file is excluded from the build tsconfig only; vitest discovers tests independently, so the gate still runs. Production code keeps its no-@dina rule.

Deviation corrected: my first-party rule was an explicit non-goal

Round 6 granted first-party standing to any repo holding a verified catalog row for the subject. §24 lists as a non-goal, verbatim: “Making public catalog presence equivalent to supplier verification.” A reseller would have spoken with the manufacturer’s authority about a product’s FORMULATION or what it REPLACES, at 9500 basis points, clearing the substitution threshold. I replaced one over-broad rule with another.

Standing is now relationship-specific. Issuing the identity (a scoped ProductRef whose issuer is the publishing repo) confers authority over the product, so first party for any relationship. A verified catalog row proves only that this repo sells the thing, so it supports sold_by and nothing else. Everything else is third party — still indexed, still shown, simply not carrying the owner’s weight.

Design decision: a dispute needs overlapping time AND two parties

Round 6 marked every distinct object in a (subject, relationship) group disputed. That asserts a cardinality §10.3 never states — that a product has exactly one manufacturer, ever — and produced two wrong outcomes: sequential manufacture (one plant until March, another after) became a permanent dispute, and co-manufacture declared by a single issuer became a disagreement with itself.

A disagreement now requires overlapping effective intervals (an absent bound reads as open-ended, so an unqualified claim conflicts with everything it touches rather than being quietly exempt) and two different issuers — a dispute is between people. Conflicting edges still coexist per §10.3; disputed gates only standing inheritance, which is the thing that must not flow along a contested lineage.

Two more of my own checks that could not fail

  • Duplicate pages passed verification. Per-page checks ask each page about ITSELF; nothing asked whether the pages together covered the snapshot. Serving page 0 twice passed while a committed page was never presented — and it lands under the global digest key where onConflictDoNothing blocks the correct bytes from replacing it. Fixed in the shared verifier and the pre-insert path, with the whole-publication item count. Writing the regression exposed that the fixture only ever emits ONE page, which cannot express the defect; a genuine two-page fixture was needed.
  • The WBS parser under-counted for the third time. Its ID grammar silently skipped the real composite row 5.9 / 7.1, and STATUSES omitted BLOCKED though the legend defines it. Row detection is now positional — the separator is the only non-row — and the count is pinned at 97 (it was capturing 96).

Logging, again

Round 6 fixed the one refusal path it was shown and left two others. And the reviewer’s point is sharper than mine was: an AT URI EMBEDS the publisher’s DID, so dropping did while keeping uri changes nothing. Every commerce log site now carries collection and reason only.

Open questions

  • Relationship cardinality is not normative. §10.3 does not say which relationships admit one answer. I treat variant_of, packaging_variant_of and manufactured_by as exclusive-at-a-point-in-time and everything else as many-valued. That is a judgement, and it belongs in the spec rather than in two implementations that must agree.
  • Does sold_by deserve first-party standing at all? A verified catalog row proves the repo lists the product, not that the sale is authorised by anyone upstream. It is the narrowest reading I could defend, and it may still be one step too generous.

Still open from this round

  • #4 — a live pointer carrying snapshot_digest without snapshot_rkey is admitted; the protocol requires both.
  • #6 — the “genuinely signed” held-evidence vector is not a valid OrderAcknowledgement, so it tests cryptography over arbitrary JSON.
  • #7 — the parse-round-trip family uses flat generic objects and cannot detect NESTED stripping, which is the defect it claims to prevent.
  • #8 — the live probe still compares only the pointer’s digest, so other pointer fields could change unnoticed.

Gates after this round: AppView 103 files / 3,103 · core commerce 73 suites / 1,420 · commerce-protocol 17 / 463 · zero typecheck errors in any commerce file. Codex could not run the Core or AppView integration gates in its sandbox, so its report treats those totals as unverified claims; they are run here.

Round 8 — the last four, and every one was a check that could not test what it named

Findings #4, #6, #7 and #8 closed. All ten revalidation findings are now closed. Gates: commerce-protocol 17 suites / 464 · conformance runner 33/33 · AppView 103 files / 4,437 · core commerce 73 suites / 1,420 · typecheck clean.

#4 — fixing the bug took two lines; the gap behind it took the rest

The stated defect: a live pointer carrying snapshot_digest and no snapshot_rkey was admitted, where the protocol requires both. But the real point was that the parity family covered neither the pointer record nor its COHERENCE rules — the ones that live BETWEEN fields rather than in any one of them. Field-by-field validation is structurally unable to see them, the same way per-page checks could not see the page SET.

So I extended the divergence sweep to pointers, snapshots and pages, and it failed 140 cases immediately. My guards were shape-only: they accepted supplier_did: "not-a-did", non-canonical timestamps, and page_digests entries that were not hex64 — every one refused by a conforming consumer. The protocol's own comment names the cost: producer/consumer divergence on a PUBLIC record is the expensive kind, because the supplier believes they published and the buyer sees nothing. The publication-record rules are now ported (including genesis coherence and the live/tombstone pairing) and the guards delegate. The sweep now runs 2,254 mutations across seven record types.

#6 — my “genuinely signed” vector was signed nonsense

The held-evidence record was not a valid OrderAcknowledgement at all: no acknowledgement_id, no issued_at, no kind. The family signed arbitrary JSON and called it held evidence, so a verifier correctly composing SCHEMA validation with signature checking would reject the case labelled valid — the implementation that passed was the one doing LESS work.

Two attempts, and the second is the point. I first hand-wrote a correct-looking record; that invites a third divergence when the digest already has a canonical derivation, so it now uses makeAcceptedAck, the builder every order test uses. Then, checking the refusal cases rather than stopping at green, I found the fix had BROKEN the case that matters: mutating purchase_order_id made the record fail its own digest, so a verifier checking only the schema would refuse it and never reach the binding check — passing for the wrong reason. The binding case is now a SECOND fully valid acknowledgement paired with the first envelope: schema passes, signature passes, and only the binding fails. Nothing but a binding check can refuse it.

#7 — a family that could not detect the defect it was written for

schema_evolution.parse_round_trip used flat generic objects, so it could only catch TOP-LEVEL stripping. AppView's schema was .passthrough() at the root and z.object() beneath, so an additive field survived at the top and vanished inside a page, an item, or a product reference — and the digest over the shortened record stopped matching what the supplier signed. The family would have certified that parser.

nested_unknown.json carries real records of a NAMED KIND with an unknown field at each depth, and the kind travels with the case so a port routes to its real parser instead of guessing at a generic one. The negative test asserts both halves: a parser that keeps top-level unknowns and strips nested ones FAILS the nested family and PASSES the flat one. That second assertion is deliberate — “the other family would have caught it” is the belief that let this through the first time.

#8 — the probe compared one field of the pointer

Everything except snapshot_digest sits OUTSIDE the snapshot digest, so a store could drop or rewrite service_rkey, snapshot_rkey, sequence, protocol version or published_at and pass every assertion. service_rkey is the one with teeth: it is the listing a buyer resolves, so losing it silently detaches the catalog from the service that serves it. The probe now compares canonical bytes of the whole pointer, and selfCheck first proves that comparison can DETECT a changed field — a comparison that cannot fail would make the live check vacuous.

Tradeoffs taken in this round

  • The divergence sweep imports protocol SOURCE, which broke AppView's build (rootDir). That one file is excluded from the build tsconfig only; vitest discovers tests independently, so the gate runs and production code keeps its no-@dina rule. A drift gate that cannot see both sides cannot detect drift.
  • AppView's pointer and snapshot sweeps assert a ONE-WAY property — anything the protocol refuses, AppView must refuse. The reverse (AppView stricter) is a false refusal rather than an interoperability break, and is reported separately instead of silently tolerated.

Open questions carried forward

  • Relationship cardinality is still not normative. §10.3 does not say which relationships admit one answer. I treat variant_of, packaging_variant_of and manufactured_by as exclusive-at-a-point-in-time. That judgement belongs in the spec, not in two implementations that must agree.
  • Does sold_by deserve first-party standing at all? A verified catalog row proves the repo lists the product, not that the sale is authorised upstream. It is the narrowest reading I could defend and may still be one step too generous.
  • The live PDS probe still cannot authenticate. Its whole chain is proved offline against the frozen fixture; the publish step needs a credential I will not enter.
  • No third party has run the conformance kit. Twelve families are wired and mutation-checked, but §25.1's real test is an independent implementation, which no amount of wiring settles.

The pattern, stated once

Across both review rounds, thirty findings closed. Almost every one was in CHECKING APPARATUS I had built and believed: parity vectors that inherited my blind spots, a conformance family inserted after the pass/fail tally so its cases were counted before they existed, a “signed” vector that was not a valid record, a probe that filtered on 'failed' where the runner emits 'fail'. The divergence sweep exists because of that pattern rather than because of any one bug, and it has since found 145 disagreements nobody thought to look for.
